\chapter{Riemannian Geometry}\label{riemannian-geometry}
\chapterstatus{complete}

\section{Introduction}\label{riemannian-geometry:section-introduction}

A Riemannian metric turns a manifold into a metric space, produces a canonical connection (Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-levi-civita})
and, together with an orientation, defines the Hodge star, the codifferential and the Laplacian
(Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-laplacian}). Harmonic forms are the kernel of that Laplacian, and the Hodge theorem
(Chapter~\ref{hodge-theorem}) says that on a compact manifold they represent de Rham cohomology. Along the way we develop the Riemannian measure and the volume form, which make surface integrals and the divergence theorem instances of integration on
manifolds; the gradient, Hessian, divergence and Laplacian of a Riemannian manifold; the local and global theory of geodesics: covariant derivatives along curves,
the Gauss lemma, the fact that geodesics are locally the shortest curves, and the Hopf--Rinow theorem, all with complete proofs; parallel transport and holonomy, with the holonomy principle, and the
theorems of Ambrose--Singer, de Rham and Berger; Jacobi fields, conjugate points and the first comparison theorems; Cartan's method of moving frames, with which we compute the curvature of hyperbolic
space, of conformally flat metrics and of hypersurfaces, and the Gauss--Bonnet--Chern theorem in Riemannian form; the first and second variation of energy, with Jacobi's theorem that
geodesics stop minimising after a conjugate point, a complete proof of the Bonnet--Myers theorem, the theorems of Weinstein and Synge on manifolds of positive curvature, and the splitting theorem; the geometry of submanifolds, with the
first and second fundamental forms, the Weingarten equations and the equations of Gauss and Codazzi, and practical formulas for the second fundamental form (level sets, distance functions and the Riccati equation, parallel hypersurfaces, the Gauss map); the classical global theorems on surfaces: Gauss--Bonnet for
regions with boundary, the curvature of conformal metrics, and the theorems of Minding, Hadamard, Liebmann and Hilbert; minimal surfaces, with the first and second variation of area, the
area-minimising property of minimal graphs and Bernstein's theorem; and isometries and Killing fields, with Bochner's theorem, the theorem of Killing
and Hopf classifying the spaces of constant curvature, and the statements of Cheng's maximal diameter theorem and of the sphere theorem; and geometric structures in the sense of Klein and Thurston, $(G, X)$-manifolds with their
developing maps and holonomy, of which the constant curvature metrics are the first examples. References:
\cite{do-carmo-riemannian}, \cite{lee-riemannian}, \cite{kobayashi-nomizu}, \cite{joyce-special-holonomy}, \cite{warner-foundations}, \cite[Chapter 0]{griffiths-harris}.

\noindent\textbf{Prerequisites.} Chapter~\ref{connections} (Connections and Curvature).

\noindent\textbf{On the way to the Hodge conjecture.} The chapters on Hodge theory use only the metric, the volume form, normal coordinates, the curvature tensor and the Hodge star and
Laplacian (Sections~\ref{riemannian-geometry:section-levi-civita}, \ref{riemannian-geometry:section-volume}, \ref{riemannian-geometry:section-exponential}, \ref{riemannian-geometry:section-curvature} and~\ref{riemannian-geometry:section-hodge-star}); holonomy is the background of Chapter~\ref{calabi-yau}. The sections on moving frames,
variations of energy, submanifolds, surfaces, minimal surfaces, Killing fields, space forms and geometric structures develop Riemannian geometry for its own sake and can be skipped on a first
reading.

\noindent\textbf{Conventions.} $(M, g)$ is a Riemannian manifold of dimension $n$, and we write $\langle v, w\rangle = g(v, w)$ and $|v| = \langle v, v\rangle^{1/2}$. Curves are piecewise smooth unless stated otherwise. In local
coordinates we use the summation convention: an index repeated once up and once down is summed over.

\section{Metrics and the Levi-Civita connection}\label{riemannian-geometry:section-levi-civita}

\begin{definition}\label{riemannian-geometry:definition-metric}
A \emph{Riemannian metric} on a smooth manifold $M$ is a smooth section $g$ of $\mathrm{Sym}^2T^*M$ that is an inner product on each tangent space, that is, a symmetric tensor field
of type $(0,2)$ that is positive definite at every point (Definition~\ref{vector-bundles:definition-tensor-fields}); $(M, g)$ is a \emph{Riemannian manifold}. The
\emph{length} of a piecewise smooth curve is $L(\gamma) = \int_a^b|\dot\gamma(t)|\,dt$ with $|v| = g(v, v)^{1/2}$, and $d(p, q)$ is the infimum of the lengths of curves from $p$ to $q$. An \emph{isometry} is a
diffeomorphism preserving $g$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-metric-exists}
Every smooth manifold admits a Riemannian metric, and $d$ is a metric inducing the topology of $M$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Patch the Euclidean metrics of charts with a partition of unity (Theorem~\ref{smooth-manifolds:theorem-partitions-of-unity}); a convex combination of inner products is an inner product. Symmetry and the
triangle inequality for $d$ are clear, and $d(p, q) > 0$ for $p \neq q$ because in a chart around $p$ the metric is comparable to the Euclidean one on a compact neighbourhood, so curves leaving a small ball
have length bounded below. The same comparison shows that the $d$-balls form a basis of the topology.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-which-metrics}
\emph{Which distances are Riemannian?} By the proposition every smooth manifold, being metrisable, carries Riemannian metrics whose distance induces its topology. A given metric $d_0$
inducing the topology is in general not of this form. A Riemannian distance $d$ is a \emph{length metric}: if $\gamma$ is a curve from $p$ to $q$ with $L(\gamma) < d(p, q) + \varepsilon$ and $m$ is the point
dividing it into two arcs of equal length, then $d(p, m)$, $d(m, q) \leq L(\gamma)/2$, so $m$ is an approximate midpoint. On $\RR$ the metric $d_0(x, y) = |x - y|^{1/2}$ induces the usual topology but has no
approximate midpoints: $d_0(0, m) + d_0(m, 1) = \sqrt{|m|} + \sqrt{|1 - m|} \geq 1$, with values close to $1$ only for $m$ near $0$ or $1$, where one of the two terms is close to $1 = d_0(0, 1)$. So $d_0$ is
not the distance of any Riemannian metric. Similarly the bounded metric $\min(|x - y|, 1)$ on $\RR$ is not Riemannian, since in a length metric points at distance $2$ have a point at distance about $1$ from
both.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-musical}
\emph{The musical isomorphisms.} A metric identifies each tangent space with its dual: $v \mapsto v^\flat = g(v, -)$ is an isomorphism $T_pM \to T_p^*M$, with inverse $\alpha \mapsto \alpha^\sharp$, and these
give isomorphisms of vector bundles $\flat \colon TM \to T^*M$ and $\sharp \colon T^*M \to TM$ (``lowering and raising indices''). In coordinates, with $(g^{ij})$ the inverse matrix of $(g_{ij})$,
\[
\Bigl(\sum_iv^i\partial_i\Bigr)^\flat = \sum_{i,j}g_{ij}v^i\,dx^j, \qquad \Bigl(\sum_i\alpha_i\,dx^i\Bigr)^\sharp = \sum_{i,j}g^{ij}\alpha_i\,\partial_j .
\]
The gradient is $\operatorname{grad}f = (df)^\sharp = \sum_{i,j}g^{ij}\partial_if\,\partial_j$ (Section~\ref{riemannian-geometry:section-gradient}), and the metric on $T^*M$ making $\flat$ an isometry is
$\langle\alpha, \beta\rangle = \sum g^{ij}\alpha_i\beta_j$. For the Euclidean metric $g_{ij} = \delta_{ij}$ the musical isomorphisms are invisible, which is why in vector calculus vectors and covectors are not
distinguished. In polar coordinates on $\RR^2$, $g = dr^2 + r^2d\theta^2$, so $(\partial_\theta)^\flat = r^2\,d\theta$, $(d\theta)^\sharp = r^{-2}\partial_\theta$, and
$\operatorname{grad}f = \partial_rf\,\partial_r + r^{-2}\partial_\theta f\,\partial_\theta$; the coefficient $r^{-2}$, not $r^{-1}$, appears because $\partial_\theta$ has length $r$.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-metrics}
The Euclidean metric on $\RR^n$; the induced metric on a submanifold, for instance the round metric on $S^n \subseteq \RR^{n+1}$; the hyperbolic metric $(dx^2 + dy^2)/y^2$ on the upper half plane; a
left-invariant metric on a Lie group, obtained from any inner product on $\mathfrak{g}$, which is bi-invariant when $G$ is compact (average over Haar measure,
Theorem~\ref{lie-groups:theorem-haar}); and the Fubini--Study metric on $\CC\PP^n$ (Chapter~\ref{kahler}).
\end{example}

\begin{proposition}\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-quotient-metric}
Let a discrete group $G$ act freely and properly on $M$ by isometries of $g$. Then $M/G$ carries a unique Riemannian metric for which $\pi \colon M \to M/G$ is a local isometry.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{smooth-manifolds:theorem-smooth-quotient}, $\pi$ is a local diffeomorphism. For $x \in M$ define the metric on $T_{\pi(x)}(M/G)$ by transporting $g_x$ through $d_x\pi$. This does not depend on the choice of $x$
in its orbit: $\pi \circ h = \pi$ for $h \in G$, so $d_{hx}\pi \circ d_xh = d_x\pi$, and $d_xh$ is an isometry $T_xM \to T_{hx}M$. On an open set $\pi(U)$ over which $\pi$ has the smooth inverse $(\pi|_U)^{-1}$ the metric is the pullback
of $g$ by this inverse, hence smooth. Uniqueness is clear.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-quotient-metrics}
The \emph{flat torus} $\RR^n/\ZZ^n$ and the \emph{flat Klein bottle} $\RR^2/G$ (Example~\ref{topological-manifolds:example-covering-actions}), since translations and the maps $(x, y) \mapsto (x + \frac12, -y)$ are Euclidean isometries; and
the round metric on $\RR\PP^n = S^n/\{\pm1\}$, as $-\mathrm{id}$ is an isometry of $S^n$.
\end{example}

\begin{theorem}[Fundamental theorem of Riemannian geometry]\label{riemannian-geometry:theorem-levi-civita}
On a Riemannian manifold there is a unique connection $\nabla$ on $TM$ that is metric ($Xg(Y, Z) = g(\nabla_XY, Z) + g(Y, \nabla_XZ)$) and torsion-free ($\nabla_XY - \nabla_YX = [X, Y]$): the
\emph{Levi-Civita connection}. It is determined by the Koszul formula
\[
2g(\nabla_XY, Z) = Xg(Y, Z) + Yg(X, Z) - Zg(X, Y) + g([X, Y], Z) - g([X, Z], Y) - g([Y, Z], X).
\]
In coordinates, $\nabla_{\partial_i}\partial_j = \sum_k\Gamma^k_{ij}\partial_k$ with $\Gamma^k_{ij} = \frac12\sum_lg^{kl}(\partial_ig_{jl} + \partial_jg_{il} - \partial_lg_{ij})$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If $\nabla$ is metric and torsion-free, expanding the three terms $Xg(Y, Z)$, $Yg(X, Z)$, $-Zg(X, Y)$ with the metric property and using torsion-freeness to replace $\nabla_XY - \nabla_YX$ by $[X, Y]$ gives the
Koszul formula; since $g$ is nondegenerate, the formula determines $\nabla_XY$, which proves uniqueness. Conversely, define $\nabla_XY$ by the Koszul formula. The right-hand side is $\RR$-bilinear,
$C^\infty$-linear in $X$ and $Z$ and satisfies the Leibniz rule in $Y$ (the terms with derivatives of $f$ in $Y \mapsto fY$ combine to $2(Xf)g(Y, Z)$), so $\nabla$ is a connection; exchanging $X$ and $Y$ shows it
is torsion-free, and adding the formulas for $g(\nabla_XY, Z)$ and $g(Y, \nabla_XZ)$ gives $Xg(Y, Z)$. The coordinate formula follows by taking $X, Y, Z$ coordinate fields, whose brackets vanish.
\end{proof}

In coordinates, torsion-freeness says $\Gamma^k_{ij} = \Gamma^k_{ji}$, and the metric property for coordinate fields says
\begin{equation}\label{riemannian-geometry:equation-metric-christoffel}
\partial_ig_{jk} = \Gamma^l_{ij}g_{lk} + \Gamma^l_{ik}g_{jl}.
\end{equation}

\begin{definition}\label{riemannian-geometry:definition-geodesic}
A \emph{geodesic} is a curve with $\nabla_{\dot\gamma}\dot\gamma = 0$, where the velocity $\dot\gamma$ is differentiated along $\gamma$ as in
Remark~\ref{connections:remark-along-curves} (it need not extend to a vector field on $M$; see
Remark~\ref{riemannian-geometry:remark-derivative-along-curves}); in coordinates, $\ddot\gamma^k + \sum_{i,j}\Gamma^k_{ij}\dot\gamma^i\dot\gamma^j = 0$. By
Theorem~\ref{real-analysis:theorem-picard-lindelof} there is a unique maximal geodesic with given initial position and velocity, depending smoothly on them; the \emph{exponential map}
$\exp_p(v) = \gamma_v(1)$ is defined on a neighbourhood of $0 \in T_pM$.
\end{definition}

\section{Volume and integration}\label{riemannian-geometry:section-volume}

A metric measures the lengths of tangent vectors, hence the volumes of small parallelepipeds, and so it determines how to integrate functions over the manifold.
For a surface in $\RR^3$ this is the classical surface integral $\iint f\,dA$; the point of this section is that it is an instance of the integration of forms of
Chapter~\ref{differential-forms}, and that the same formulas work for submanifolds of any dimension. In a chart $(U, \varphi)$ with coordinates $x^1, \ldots, x^n$ we write
$g_{ij} = g(\partial_i, \partial_j)$ and $\det g$ for the determinant of the matrix $(g_{ij})$, a smooth positive function on $U$; $\lambda$ is Lebesgue measure.

\begin{lemma}\label{riemannian-geometry:lemma-metric-change-of-chart}
Let $y^1, \ldots, y^n$ be the coordinates of a second chart, with metric coefficients $\tilde g_{ab} = g(\partial/\partial y^a, \partial/\partial y^b)$. Then on the
intersection of the two chart domains
\[
\tilde g_{ab} = \sum_{i,j}\frac{\partial x^i}{\partial y^a}\frac{\partial x^j}{\partial y^b}\,g_{ij}, \qquad
\sqrt{\det\tilde g} = \Bigl|\det\Bigl(\frac{\partial x^i}{\partial y^a}\Bigr)\Bigr|\sqrt{\det g}.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By Proposition~\ref{smooth-manifolds:proposition-change-of-coordinates}, $\partial/\partial y^a = \sum_i(\partial x^i/\partial y^a)\,\partial/\partial x^i$; expanding
$g$ bilinearly gives the first formula. In matrices it reads $\tilde g = P^TgP$ with $P = (\partial x^i/\partial y^a)$, and taking determinants,
$\det\tilde g = (\det P)^2\det g$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Riemannian measure]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-riemannian-measure}
Let $(M, g)$ be a Riemannian manifold. There is a unique measure $\mu_g$ on the Borel sets of $M$ such that for every chart $(U, \varphi)$ and every Borel set
$A \subseteq U$,
\[
\mu_g(A) = \int_{\varphi(A)}\sqrt{\det g} \circ \varphi^{-1}\,d\lambda .
\]
Consequently $\int_Af\,d\mu_g = \int_{\varphi(A)}(f\sqrt{\det g}) \circ \varphi^{-1}\,d\lambda$ for every measurable $f \geq 0$ and every integrable $f$ on $A$. We write $dV$ for
$\mu_g$, and $dA$ and $ds$ in dimensions $2$ and $1$ (the \emph{volume}, the \emph{area} and the \emph{arc length}), and $\mathrm{vol}(M) = \mu_g(M)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For a chart $(U, \varphi)$ the formula defines a measure $\mu_U$ on the Borel subsets of $U$, the image under the homeomorphism $\varphi^{-1}$ of the measure with density
$\sqrt{\det g} \circ \varphi^{-1}$ on $\varphi(U)$. Two such measures agree where both are defined: let $A$ be a Borel subset of $U \cap V$ for a second chart
$(V, \psi)$ with coordinates $y$, and let $\tau = \varphi \circ \psi^{-1}$, whose Jacobian matrix is $(\partial x^i/\partial y^a)$. By
Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-metric-change-of-chart}, $\sqrt{\det\tilde g} \circ \psi^{-1} = (\sqrt{\det g} \circ \varphi^{-1} \circ \tau)\,|\det D\tau|$, so by
the change of variables formula (Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-change-of-variables}) applied to $\tau \colon \psi(A) \to \varphi(A)$,
\[
\int_{\psi(A)}\sqrt{\det\tilde g} \circ \psi^{-1}\,d\lambda = \int_{\psi(A)}(\sqrt{\det g} \circ \varphi^{-1} \circ \tau)\,|\det D\tau|\,d\lambda
= \int_{\varphi(A)}\sqrt{\det g} \circ \varphi^{-1}\,d\lambda .
\]
Cover $M$ by countably many chart domains $U_1, U_2, \ldots$ ($M$ is second countable), let $B_1 = U_1$ and $B_k = U_k \setminus (U_1 \cup \cdots \cup U_{k-1})$, and put
$\mu_g(A) = \sum_k\mu_{U_k}(A \cap B_k)$. This is a measure, being a countable sum of measures. If $A$ is a Borel subset of a chart domain $U$, the
compatibility just proved gives $\mu_{U_k}(A \cap B_k) = \mu_U(A \cap B_k)$, so $\mu_g(A) = \sum_k\mu_U(A \cap B_k) = \mu_U(A)$. Conversely, any measure with the
stated property assigns to $A$ the value $\sum_k\mu_{U_k}(A \cap B_k)$, which proves uniqueness. The formula for integrals follows from the one for sets, first
for simple functions, then by monotone convergence (Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-monotone-convergence}) for $f \geq 0$, and by linearity for integrable $f$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Riemannian volume form]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-volume-form}
Let $(M, g)$ be an oriented Riemannian $n$-manifold. There is a unique $n$-form $\mathrm{vol}_g$ with $\mathrm{vol}_g(e_1, \ldots, e_n) = 1$ for every positively
oriented orthonormal basis $(e_1, \ldots, e_n)$ of a tangent space. In a positively oriented chart
\[
\mathrm{vol}_g = \sqrt{\det g}\;dx^1 \wedge \cdots \wedge dx^n,
\]
and $\int_Mf\,\mathrm{vol}_g = \int_Mf\,d\mu_g$ for every continuous function $f$ with compact support.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $p$ lie in a positively oriented chart, and let $(e_1, \ldots, e_n)$ be a positively oriented orthonormal basis of $T_pM$. Write $e_a = \sum_iP^i_a\partial_i$ with an
invertible matrix $P = (P^i_a)$. Then $\delta_{ab} = \langle e_a, e_b\rangle = (P^TgP)_{ab}$, so $(\det P)^2\det g = 1$, and $\det P > 0$ because both bases are
positively oriented; hence $\det P = 1/\sqrt{\det g}$ and
\[
\bigl(\sqrt{\det g}\;dx^1 \wedge \cdots \wedge dx^n\bigr)(e_1, \ldots, e_n) = \sqrt{\det g}\,\det\bigl(dx^i(e_a)\bigr) = \sqrt{\det g}\,\det P = 1 .
\]
Since $\Lambda^nT^*_pM$ is one-dimensional, an $n$-form at $p$ is determined by its value on one basis. So on each positively oriented chart domain the smooth form
$\sqrt{\det g}\,dx^1 \wedge \cdots \wedge dx^n$ is the only form with the required property; the forms of two charts therefore agree on the overlap, and together they
define the smooth $n$-form $\mathrm{vol}_g$, which is unique. If $f$ is supported in a positively oriented chart, both integrals equal
$\int(f\sqrt{\det g}) \circ \varphi^{-1}\,d\lambda$, by Definition~\ref{differential-forms:definition-integral} and
Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-riemannian-measure}; in general write $f = \sum_i\psi_if$ with a partition of unity subordinate to positively oriented
charts and use linearity of both sides.
\end{proof}

For a surface $\mathrm{vol}_g$ is the \emph{area form} $dA$. By Proposition~\ref{differential-forms:proposition-orientation}, a non-orientable manifold, such as the
M\"obius band or $\RR\PP^2$, has no nowhere vanishing $n$-form, hence no volume form; but it still has a Riemannian measure, so areas and integrals of functions make sense
on it. The integral of a function needs a metric, the integral of an $n$-form needs an orientation, and the volume form converts one into the other.

\begin{proposition}[Volume of parametrised submanifolds]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-parametrised-volume}
Let $M \subseteq \RR^N$ be a $k$-dimensional submanifold with the induced metric, and $x \colon \Omega \to M$ a parametrisation
(Proposition~\ref{smooth-manifolds:proposition-submanifold-criteria}(3)), with $x_i = \partial x/\partial u^i$. Then for every measurable $f \geq 0$ on $x(\Omega)$,
\[
\int_{x(\Omega)}f\,d\mu = \int_\Omega f(x(u))\,\mathcal{J}(u)\,du, \qquad
\mathcal{J} = \sqrt{\det\bigl(\langle x_i, x_j\rangle\bigr)} = \Bigl(\sum_I\Bigl(\det\frac{\partial x^I}{\partial u}\Bigr)^2\Bigr)^{1/2},
\]
where $I$ runs over the subsets $\{i_1 < \cdots < i_k\}$ of $\{1, \ldots, N\}$, and $\partial x^I/\partial u$ is the $k \times k$ matrix formed by the rows
$i_1, \ldots, i_k$ of the Jacobian matrix $Dx$. In particular:
\begin{enumerate}
\item for a curve ($k = 1$), $\mathcal{J} = |x^{\prime}(t)|$, and $\int f\,ds = \int f(x(t))\,|x^{\prime}(t)|\,dt$;
\item for a surface in $\RR^3$, with $E = |x_u|^2$, $F = \langle x_u, x_v\rangle$ and $G = |x_v|^2$, $\mathcal{J} = \sqrt{EG - F^2} = |x_u \times x_v|$;
\item for a hypersurface ($k = N - 1$) with unit normal $\nu$, $\mathcal{J} = |\det(\nu, x_1, \ldots, x_k)|$;
\item for the graph of a smooth function $\phi \colon \Omega \to \RR$, parametrised by $x(u) = (u, \phi(u))$, $\mathcal{J} = \sqrt{1 + |\nabla\phi|^2}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The inverse $x^{-1}$ is a chart of $M$ (Lemma~\ref{smooth-manifolds:lemma-submanifold-structure}), whose coordinate vector field $\partial/\partial u^i$ is $x_i$;
so $g_{ij} = \langle x_i, x_j\rangle$ and the formula is Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-riemannian-measure}. For the second expression of
$\mathcal{J}$, Proposition~\ref{multilinear-algebra:proposition-induced-inner-product} gives $\det(\langle x_i, x_j\rangle) = |x_1 \wedge \cdots \wedge x_k|^2$ in
$\Lambda^k\RR^N$. Writing $x_j = \sum_i(\partial x^i/\partial u^j)e_i$ and expanding by multilinearity, the terms with a repeated $e_i$ vanish, and collecting the
terms with the same set of indices $I$ (the Leibniz formula for the determinant) gives $x_1 \wedge \cdots \wedge x_k = \sum_I\det(\partial x^I/\partial u)\,e_I$; as the
$e_I$ are orthonormal, $|x_1 \wedge \cdots \wedge x_k|^2 = \sum_I\det(\partial x^I/\partial u)^2$.

(1) is the case of a single vector. (2) $EG - F^2 = |x_u|^2|x_v|^2 - \langle x_u, x_v\rangle^2 = |x_u \times x_v|^2$ (Lagrange's identity); equivalently, the three
$2 \times 2$ minors of $Dx$ are, up to sign, the components of $x_u \times x_v$. (3) The matrix $P$ with columns $\nu, x_1, \ldots, x_k$ satisfies
$P^TP = \bigl(\begin{smallmatrix} 1 & 0\\ 0 & (\langle x_i, x_j\rangle)\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$, since $|\nu| = 1$ and $\langle\nu, x_i\rangle = 0$; so
$(\det P)^2 = \det(\langle x_i, x_j\rangle)$. (4) Here $x_i = (e_i, \partial_i\phi)$, so $\langle x_i, x_j\rangle = \delta_{ij} + \partial_i\phi\,\partial_j\phi$: this is
the matrix $I + ww^T$ with $w = \nabla\phi$, which is the identity on $w^\perp$ and multiplies $w$ by $1 + |w|^2$, so its determinant is $1 + |w|^2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-surface-integrals}
In classical surface theory \cite[Chapter 2]{do-carmo-curves-surfaces} the area of a region $R$ contained in the image of a parametrisation $x$, and the integral of a
function over it, are defined by
\[
\iint_Rf\,dA = \iint_{x^{-1}(R)}f(x(u, v))\,|x_u \times x_v|\,du\,dv,
\]
and one checks by hand that this does not depend on the parametrisation. By Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-parametrised-volume}(2) this is the
integral with respect to the Riemannian measure of the induced metric: surface integration is integration on a manifold, and nothing new. The independence of the
parametrisation is Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-metric-change-of-chart} together with the change of variables formula, and a region that is not contained in
one parametrisation is cut into pieces, or treated with a partition of unity, as in Definition~\ref{differential-forms:definition-integral}. When the surface is oriented,
$\iint_Rf\,dA = \int_Rf\,\mathrm{vol}_S$ is the integral of the $2$-form $f\,\mathrm{vol}_S$ (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-volume-form}). The same
holds in every dimension and codimension: the length of a curve (Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-metric}) is its one-dimensional volume, by (1), and the
$k$-dimensional volume of a piece of a $k$-dimensional submanifold of $\RR^N$ is the integral of the square root of the sum of the squares of the $k \times k$ minors.
\end{remark}


\begin{proposition}[Riemannian measure and Hausdorff measure]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-hausdorff-measure}
Let $M \subseteq \RR^N$ be a $k$-dimensional submanifold with the induced metric. Then $\mu_g(A) = \mathcal{H}^k(A)$ for every Borel set $A \subseteq M$, where $\mathcal{H}^k$ is the
$k$-dimensional Hausdorff measure of $\RR^N$ (Definition~\ref{measure-theory:definition-hausdorff}). In particular the volume of $M$ depends only on $M$ as a metric subspace of $\RR^N$, and a
nonempty $M$ has Hausdorff dimension $k$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
A submanifold is locally closed (each point has a neighbourhood $W$ in $\RR^N$ with $M \cap W$ closed in $W$, by the slice charts), so $M$ and its Borel subsets are Borel in $\RR^N$. Let
$x \colon \Omega \to M$ be a parametrisation; it is an injective smooth map with injective differential and a homeomorphism onto the open set $x(\Omega) \subseteq M$, and its function
$\mathcal{J} = \sqrt{\det(Dx^TDx)}$ of Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-parametrised-volume} is the Jacobian $Jx$ of Definition~\ref{measure-theory:definition-jacobian}. For a
Borel set $A \subseteq x(\Omega)$, Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-parametrised-volume} with $f = \mathbf{1}_A$ and the area formula
(Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-area-formula}) give
\[
\mu_g(A) = \int_{x^{-1}(A)}Jx\,d\lambda = \mathcal{H}^k(x(x^{-1}(A))) = \mathcal{H}^k(A).
\]
Cover $M$ by countably many such sets $V_i = x_i(\Omega_i)$ and let $B_i = V_i \setminus \bigcup_{j < i}V_j$; then $\mu_g(A) = \sum_i\mu_g(A \cap B_i) = \sum_i\mathcal{H}^k(A \cap B_i) =
\mathcal{H}^k(A)$. Finally, if $M \neq \emptyset$, $\mathcal{H}^k(M) > 0$, and $M$ is a countable union of compact sets of finite $\mathcal{H}^k$-measure, so
Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-hausdorff}(4) gives $\dim_{\mathcal{H}}M = k$.
\end{proof}

So the Riemannian volume of a submanifold of $\RR^N$, defined through charts and the metric, is the most naive notion of $k$-dimensional size: cover the set by small pieces and add up
$\alpha(k)(\operatorname{diam})^k$. This is the point of view of geometric measure theory, where the area formula (Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-area-formula}) replaces the computation in
charts, and which extends the notion of $k$-dimensional volume to sets that are not manifolds, such as rectifiable sets and the singular varieties of algebraic geometry.

For hypersurfaces of $\RR^{n+1}$ the volume form has a simple expression through the unit normal, and it turns integrals of $n$-forms into flux integrals. We write
$\Omega = dx^1 \wedge \cdots \wedge dx^{n+1}$ for the standard volume form of $\RR^{n+1}$, and, for a vector field $F$ defined along $M$ (for instance the unit normal
$\nu$), $(\iota_F\Omega)|_M$ for the $n$-form on $M$ whose value at $p$ is the restriction of $\iota_{F(p)}\Omega_p$ to $T_pM$.

\begin{proposition}[Volume form of a hypersurface; flux]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-flux}
Let $M \subseteq \RR^{n+1}$ be a hypersurface oriented by a unit normal field $\nu$ (Proposition~\ref{differential-forms:proposition-orientation-normal}). Then
\[
\mathrm{vol}_M = (\iota_\nu\Omega)|_M = \Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}(-1)^{i-1}\nu^i\,dx^1 \wedge \cdots \wedge \widehat{dx^i} \wedge \cdots \wedge dx^{n+1}\Bigr)\Big|_M,
\]
and for every vector field $F$ along $M$, $(\iota_F\Omega)|_M = \langle F, \nu\rangle\,\mathrm{vol}_M$. In $\RR^3$, for a surface $S$:
$dA = (\nu^1\,dy \wedge dz + \nu^2\,dz \wedge dx + \nu^3\,dx \wedge dy)|_S$, and the \emph{flux} of $F$ through $S$ is
\[
\int_SF^1\,dy \wedge dz + F^2\,dz \wedge dx + F^3\,dx \wedge dy = \iint_S\langle F, \nu\rangle\,dA .
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By Proposition~\ref{differential-forms:proposition-orientation-normal} the orientation of $M$ is that of the form
$(v_1, \ldots, v_n) \mapsto \det(\nu, v_1, \ldots, v_n) = \Omega(\nu, v_1, \ldots, v_n) = (\iota_\nu\Omega)(v_1, \ldots, v_n)$. If $(e_1, \ldots, e_n)$ is a positively
oriented orthonormal basis of $T_pM$, then $(\nu(p), e_1, \ldots, e_n)$ is an orthonormal basis of $\RR^{n+1}$, positive by the same proposition, so its determinant is
$1$. By the uniqueness in Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-volume-form}, $(\iota_\nu\Omega)|_M = \mathrm{vol}_M$. The coordinate expression is
$\iota_\nu(dx^1 \wedge \cdots \wedge dx^{n+1}) = \sum_i(-1)^{i-1}dx^i(\nu)\,dx^1 \wedge \cdots \wedge \widehat{dx^i} \wedge \cdots \wedge dx^{n+1}$, which follows from
Proposition~\ref{multilinear-algebra:proposition-interior-antiderivation} by induction on the number of factors. For $F$, write $F = \langle F, \nu\rangle\nu + F^\top$
with $F^\top$ tangent to $M$. For $v_1, \ldots, v_n \in T_pM$ the $n + 1$ vectors $F^\top, v_1, \ldots, v_n$ lie in the $n$-dimensional space $T_pM$, hence are
linearly dependent, and $\Omega(F^\top, v_1, \ldots, v_n) = 0$; so $(\iota_F\Omega)|_M = \langle F, \nu\rangle(\iota_\nu\Omega)|_M$. In $\RR^3$,
$\iota_\nu(dx \wedge dy \wedge dz) = \nu^1\,dy \wedge dz - \nu^2\,dx \wedge dz + \nu^3\,dx \wedge dy$ and $-dx \wedge dz = dz \wedge dx$; the flux formula is the integral
of $(\iota_F\Omega)|_S = \langle F, \nu\rangle\,dA$, by Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-volume-form}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-flux-pullback}
It is worth spelling out what the flux formula means, because the classical notation hides it. The integrand $F^1\,dy \wedge dz + F^2\,dz \wedge dx + F^3\,dx \wedge dy$ is a $2$-form on $\RR^3$ (or on a
neighbourhood of $S$), and ``$|_S$'' means its pullback $\iota^*$ by the inclusion $\iota \colon S \to \RR^3$ (Definition~\ref{differential-forms:definition-pullback}): the $2$-form on $S$ whose value on
$v, w \in T_pS$ is the value of the original form on the same vectors, now regarded as vectors of $\RR^3$. It is this pullback that one integrates over $S$.

In a positively oriented parametrisation $x(u, v) = (x(u, v), y(u, v), z(u, v))$ of $S$, pulling back is substitution: $x^*dy = d(y \circ x) = y_u\,du + y_v\,dv$, and so on, so that
\[
x^*(dy \wedge dz) = (y_uz_v - y_vz_u)\,du \wedge dv = \frac{\partial(y, z)}{\partial(u, v)}\,du \wedge dv,
\]
and similarly $x^*(dz \wedge dx) = \frac{\partial(z, x)}{\partial(u, v)}\,du \wedge dv$ and $x^*(dx \wedge dy) = \frac{\partial(x, y)}{\partial(u, v)}\,du \wedge dv$. These three $2 \times 2$ minors of the Jacobian matrix are
exactly the components of the cross product $x_u \times x_v$. Hence
\[
x^*\bigl(F^1\,dy \wedge dz + F^2\,dz \wedge dx + F^3\,dx \wedge dy\bigr) = \langle F \circ x, x_u \times x_v\rangle\,du \wedge dv = \langle F \circ x, \nu\rangle\,|x_u \times x_v|\,du \wedge dv,
\]
because $x_u \times x_v = |x_u \times x_v|\,\nu$ for a parametrisation that is positively oriented with respect to $\nu$ (by Proposition~\ref{differential-forms:proposition-orientation-normal},
$(x_u, x_v)$ is positive exactly when $\det(\nu, x_u, x_v) = \langle\nu, x_u \times x_v\rangle > 0$). Since $|x_u \times x_v|\,du\,dv$ is the area element
(Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-parametrised-volume}), integrating gives the flux formula again: this is a second proof of the proposition in $\RR^3$, by direct computation in a chart.

This also explains the classical notation. One writes $d\vec\Sigma = \nu\,dA$ for the ``vector surface element'' and $\iint_SF \cdot d\vec\Sigma = \iint_SF_1\,dy\,dz + F_2\,dz\,dx + F_3\,dx\,dy$, as in the usual statement
$\iint_S\operatorname{curl}F \cdot d\vec\Sigma = \oint_{\partial S}F \cdot d\vec r$ of Stokes' theorem. The symbols $dy\,dz$ there are not mere notation: they are the pullbacks to $S$ of the $2$-forms $dy \wedge dz$, and so
on, and the identity $d\vec\Sigma = \nu\,dA$ is the statement of the proposition, $(dy \wedge dz, dz \wedge dx, dx \wedge dy)|_S = \nu\,\mathrm{vol}_S$. Geometrically, $(dy \wedge dz)|_S(v, w)$ is the signed area of
the projection to the $(y, z)$-plane of the parallelogram spanned by $v$ and $w$, while $\mathrm{vol}_S(v, w)$ is the area of the parallelogram itself; the components of $\nu$ are the ratios between
the two, the cosines of the angles between the tangent plane and the coordinate planes.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-flux-graph}
Let $S$ be the graph of a smooth function $h$ over a region $R \subseteq \RR^2$, with the parametrisation $x(u, v) = (u, v, h(u, v))$ and the upward normal. The minors are
$\frac{\partial(y, z)}{\partial(u, v)} = \det\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix} 0 & 1\\ h_u & h_v\end{smallmatrix}\bigr) = -h_u$, $\frac{\partial(z, x)}{\partial(u, v)} = -h_v$ and $\frac{\partial(x, y)}{\partial(u, v)} = 1$, so
$x_u \times x_v = (-h_u, -h_v, 1)$, $\nu = (-h_u, -h_v, 1)/(1 + h_u^2 + h_v^2)^{1/2}$, and the flux of $F$ through $S$ is
\[
\iint_S\langle F, \nu\rangle\,dA = \iint_R\bigl(-F^1h_u - F^2h_v + F^3\bigr)(u, v, h(u, v))\,du\,dv .
\]
For instance the flux of $F = (0, 0, 1)$ through any graph over $R$ is the area of $R$: the vertical field only sees the projection of the surface to the horizontal plane, $(dx \wedge dy)|_S = \nu^3\,dA$.
For the upper unit hemisphere, $h = (1 - u^2 - v^2)^{1/2}$ on the open unit disc, and the flux of $F(x) = x$ is
$\iint(u^2/h + v^2/h + h)\,du\,dv = \iint du\,dv/h = \int_0^{2\pi}\!\!\int_0^1\frac{r\,dr\,d\varphi}{(1 - r^2)^{1/2}} = 2\pi$, which is also $\iint_S\langle x, \nu\rangle\,dA = \iint_S1\,dA$, the area of the hemisphere,
as $\nu(x) = x$ on the sphere.
\end{example}

Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-flux} has a version in every codimension, which is the starting point of the theory of currents in geometric measure theory. For a hypersurface the
orientation is described by the unit normal $\nu$; for a $k$-dimensional submanifold of $\RR^N$ there is no preferred normal vector, but there is a preferred \emph{tangent $k$-vector}. Let
$M \subseteq \RR^N$ be an oriented $k$-dimensional submanifold. For $x \in M$ let
\[
\xi(x) = \tau_1 \wedge \cdots \wedge \tau_k \in \Lambda^k\RR^N,
\]
where $(\tau_1, \ldots, \tau_k)$ is a positively oriented orthonormal basis of $T_xM$. By Proposition~\ref{multilinear-algebra:proposition-induced-inner-product}, applied to the oriented inner
product space $T_xM$, $\xi(x)$ does not depend on the choice of the basis (it is the volume element of $T_xM$, regarded in $\Lambda^k\RR^N$), and $|\xi(x)| = 1$. It is the \emph{orienting $k$-vector}
of $M$: it determines both the tangent space, as the set of $v$ with $v \wedge \xi(x) = 0$, and its orientation. A $k$-form $\omega$ on an open set of $\RR^N$ can be paired with it pointwise, via the
pairing $\langle\,,\rangle \colon \Lambda^k(\RR^N)^* \times \Lambda^k\RR^N \to \RR$ of Proposition~\ref{multilinear-algebra:proposition-dual-exterior}, for which
$\langle\omega_x, v_1 \wedge \cdots \wedge v_k\rangle = \omega_x(v_1, \ldots, v_k)$.

\begin{proposition}\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-orienting-vector}
Let $M \subseteq \RR^N$ be an oriented $k$-dimensional submanifold with orienting $k$-vector $\xi$, contained in an open set $U$, and let $\omega$ be a $k$-form on $U$. Then
\[
\omega|_M = \langle\omega, \xi\rangle\,\mathrm{vol}_M,
\]
where $\omega|_M$ is the pullback of $\omega$ by the inclusion and $\langle\omega, \xi\rangle$ is the function $x \mapsto \langle\omega_x, \xi(x)\rangle$ on $M$. Consequently, if $\omega|_M$ has compact support,
\[
\int_M\omega = \int_M\langle\omega, \xi\rangle\,d\mu_g = \int_M\langle\omega(x), \xi(x)\rangle\,d\mathcal{H}^k(x).
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Fix $x \in M$ and a positively oriented orthonormal basis $(\tau_1, \ldots, \tau_k)$ of $T_xM$. The space $\Lambda^kT^*_xM$ of alternating $k$-forms on the $k$-dimensional space $T_xM$ is one-dimensional,
spanned by $(\mathrm{vol}_M)_x$, so $(\omega|_M)_x = c\,(\mathrm{vol}_M)_x$ for a number $c$. Evaluating both sides on $(\tau_1, \ldots, \tau_k)$, and using $\mathrm{vol}_M(\tau_1, \ldots, \tau_k) = 1$
(Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-volume-form}) and the definition of the pullback,
\[
c = (\omega|_M)_x(\tau_1, \ldots, \tau_k) = \omega_x(\tau_1, \ldots, \tau_k) = \langle\omega_x, \tau_1 \wedge \cdots \wedge \tau_k\rangle = \langle\omega_x, \xi(x)\rangle .
\]
This proves the identity of forms. Integrating, $\int_M\omega = \int_M\langle\omega, \xi\rangle\,\mathrm{vol}_M = \int_M\langle\omega, \xi\rangle\,d\mu_g$ by Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-volume-form}
(the function $\langle\omega, \xi\rangle$ is continuous with compact support on $M$, since $\xi$ is continuous, as it can be computed from a local positively oriented frame by the Gram--Schmidt process), and
$\mu_g = \mathcal{H}^k$ on $M$ by Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-hausdorff-measure}.
\end{proof}

The same computation in a chart makes each factor visible. Let $x \colon \Omega \to M$ be a positively oriented parametrisation with $x_i = \partial x/\partial u^i$. Then $x_1 \wedge \cdots \wedge x_k$ is a
positive multiple of $\xi$ (Gram--Schmidt does not change the orientation), and its length is $\mathcal{J} = \sqrt{\det(\langle x_i, x_j\rangle)}$ by Proposition~\ref{multilinear-algebra:proposition-induced-inner-product}; so
$x_1 \wedge \cdots \wedge x_k = \mathcal{J}\,\xi \circ x$. On the other hand, by the definition of the integral of a form,
\[
\int_{x(\Omega)}\omega = \int_\Omega(x^*\omega)(\partial_1, \ldots, \partial_k)\,du = \int_\Omega\omega_{x(u)}(x_1, \ldots, x_k)\,du = \int_\Omega\langle\omega_{x(u)}, \xi(x(u))\rangle\,\mathcal{J}(u)\,du,
\]
and $\mathcal{J}\,du$ is the volume element (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-parametrised-volume}). So the form $\omega$ splits into two factors: the number $\langle\omega, \xi\rangle$, which measures how much of
$\omega$ is ``seen'' by the oriented tangent plane, and the volume $\mathcal{J}\,du$, which only depends on the metric.

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-orienting-vector}
\begin{enumerate}
\item For a hypersurface $M \subseteq \RR^{n+1}$ oriented by $\nu$ and $\omega = \iota_F\Omega$, $\langle\omega, \xi\rangle = \Omega(F, \tau_1, \ldots, \tau_n) = \det(F, \tau_1, \ldots, \tau_n) = \langle F, \nu\rangle$, because
$(\nu, \tau_1, \ldots, \tau_n)$ is a positive orthonormal basis of $\RR^{n+1}$; this is Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-flux} again.
\item For an oriented curve with unit tangent $T$, $\xi = T$ and $\int_C\omega = \int_C\omega(T)\,ds$: the line integral of a $1$-form is the integral of its tangential component with respect to arc length.
\item For a $2$-plane $M \subseteq \RR^4$ spanned by orthonormal $\tau_1, \tau_2$ and $\omega = dx^1 \wedge dx^2$, $\langle\omega, \xi\rangle = \det\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix} \tau_1^1 & \tau_2^1\\ \tau_1^2 & \tau_2^2\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$ is the factor by
which the orthogonal projection to the $(x^1, x^2)$-plane multiplies oriented areas on $M$. In general $\xi = \sum_I\xi^Ie_I$ and $|\langle dx^I, \xi\rangle| = |\xi^I| \leq 1$, with equality only for the
coordinate planes; this is the inequality behind \emph{calibrations} (Section~\ref{riemannian-geometry:section-minimal}).
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-currents}
\emph{Currents.} The proposition says that the functional $[\![M]\!] \colon \omega \mapsto \int_M\omega$ on compactly supported $k$-forms is
\[
[\![M]\!](\omega) = \int\langle\omega(x), \xi(x)\rangle\,d\mu(x), \qquad \mu = \mathcal{H}^k \text{ restricted to } M,
\]
an integral of the pairing of $\omega$ with a unit $k$-vector field against a measure. De Rham and Federer--Fleming call a continuous linear functional on compactly supported smooth $k$-forms a
\emph{$k$-current} \cite{federer-fleming-1960}. If a current $T$ has locally finite mass, that is, $\sup\{T(\omega) : |\omega| \leq 1, \operatorname{supp}\omega \subseteq K\} < \infty$ for every compact $K$, then it
extends to continuous forms, and the Riesz representation theorem for vector-valued functions (Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-riesz-vector}, with $H = \Lambda^k(\RR^N)^*$, identified with $\Lambda^k\RR^N$ by matching the orthonormal bases $dx^I$ and $e_I$)
writes it in exactly this form: $T(\omega) = \int\langle\omega, \xi\rangle\,d\mu$ with a Radon measure $\mu = \|T\|$, the \emph{mass measure}, and a Borel field $\xi$ of unit $k$-vectors. For $T = [\![M]\!]$ one recovers
$\mu = \mathcal{H}^k$ on $M$ and the orienting $k$-vector; for general currents $\xi$ need not be a simple wedge product and $\mu$ need not live on a manifold, and this is the generality in which geometric measure
theory solves Plateau's problem.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Divergence theorem]\label{riemannian-geometry:corollary-divergence}
Let $D \subseteq \RR^{n+1}$ be a compact subset that is a smooth $(n+1)$-manifold with boundary (Definition~\ref{differential-forms:definition-manifold-with-boundary}),
with the charts given by charts of $\RR^{n+1}$, for instance a closed ball. Let $\nu$ be the outward unit normal along $\partial D$, and $F$ a smooth vector field on a
neighbourhood of $D$, with divergence $\operatorname{div}F = \sum_i\partial_iF^i$. Then
\[
\int_D\operatorname{div}F\,d\lambda = \int_{\partial D}\langle F, \nu\rangle\,dA .
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By Proposition~\ref{multilinear-algebra:proposition-interior-antiderivation},
$\iota_F\Omega = \sum_i(-1)^{i-1}F^i\,dx^1 \wedge \cdots \wedge \widehat{dx^i} \wedge \cdots \wedge dx^{n+1}$, and
$d(\iota_F\Omega) = \sum_i\partial_iF^i\,\Omega = (\operatorname{div}F)\,\Omega$, as in the proof of Theorem~\ref{differential-forms:theorem-stokes}. Stokes' theorem gives
$\int_D(\operatorname{div}F)\,\Omega = \int_{\partial D}\iota_F\Omega$, where $D$ carries the standard orientation of $\RR^{n+1}$, so that the left side is
$\int_D\operatorname{div}F\,d\lambda$, and $\partial D$ the induced orientation. We check that the induced orientation is the one defined by $\nu$; then the right side is
$\int_{\partial D}\langle F, \nu\rangle\,dA$ by Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-flux}. In a positively oriented chart in which $D$ is
$\{x^{n+1} \geq 0\}$, the vector $w = -\partial_{n+1}$ points outward, and $\det(w, \partial_1, \ldots, \partial_n) = (-1)^{n+1}$ with respect to the chart; by
Definition~\ref{differential-forms:definition-manifold-with-boundary}, $(x^1, \ldots, x^n)$ is positively oriented on $\partial D$ exactly when $(-1)^{n+1} = 1$. So a
basis $(v_1, \ldots, v_n)$ of $T_p\partial D$ is positive for the induced orientation if and only if $(w, v_1, \ldots, v_n)$ is a positive basis of $\RR^{n+1}$. The same
holds with $\nu$ in place of $w$, because $w = c\nu + t$ with $c > 0$ and $t$ tangent to $\partial D$, so that $\det(w, v_1, \ldots, v_n) = c\det(\nu, v_1, \ldots, v_n)$.
By Proposition~\ref{differential-forms:proposition-orientation-normal} this is the orientation defined by $\nu$.
\end{proof}

The divergence theorem is the many-variable version of the fundamental theorem of calculus, and it gives integration by parts on a region with a boundary term. Write $\Delta_0 = \sum_i\partial_i^2$
for the Euclidean Laplacian with the sign of analysis (so $\Delta_0 = -\Delta$ for the Hodge Laplacian of Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-laplacian}), $\nabla f$ for the gradient, and
$\partial_\nu f = \langle\nabla f, \nu\rangle$ for the outward normal derivative.

\begin{corollary}[Integration by parts and Green's identities]\label{riemannian-geometry:corollary-green-identities}
Let $D$ and $\nu$ be as in Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-divergence}, and $f$, $g$ smooth functions on a neighbourhood of $D$. Then
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\displaystyle\int_D(\partial_if)\,g\,d\lambda = \int_{\partial D}fg\,\nu^i\,dA - \int_Df\,\partial_ig\,d\lambda$;
\item (first Green identity) $\displaystyle\int_D\bigl(g\,\Delta_0f + \langle\nabla g, \nabla f\rangle\bigr)\,d\lambda = \int_{\partial D}g\,\partial_\nu f\,dA$;
\item (second Green identity) $\displaystyle\int_D\bigl(g\,\Delta_0f - f\,\Delta_0g\bigr)\,d\lambda = \int_{\partial D}\bigl(g\,\partial_\nu f - f\,\partial_\nu g\bigr)\,dA$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
(1) Apply Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-divergence} to $F = fg\,e_i$: $\operatorname{div}F = \partial_i(fg) = (\partial_if)g + f\,\partial_ig$ and $\langle F, \nu\rangle = fg\,\nu^i$. (2) Apply it to $F = g\nabla f$:
$\operatorname{div}F = \sum_i\partial_i(g\,\partial_if) = \langle\nabla g, \nabla f\rangle + g\,\Delta_0f$ and $\langle F, \nu\rangle = g\,\partial_\nu f$. (3) Subtract from (2) the same identity with $f$ and $g$ exchanged; the
symmetric terms $\langle\nabla g, \nabla f\rangle$ cancel.
\end{proof}

For $n + 1 = 1$ and $D = [a, b]$, $\nu = -1$ at $a$ and $+1$ at $b$, and (1) is the integration by parts of one variable (Corollary~\ref{real-analysis:corollary-integration-rules}); when $f$ or $g$ vanishes
near $\partial D$, it is Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-integration-by-parts}. With $g = 1$, (2) says that the total flux of $\nabla f$ through the boundary is $\int_D\Delta_0f$; with $g = f$ it gives
$\int_D|\nabla f|^2 = \int_{\partial D}f\,\partial_\nu f - \int_Df\,\Delta_0f$, the starting point of the energy methods for the Laplace equation (Exercise~\ref{riemannian-geometry:exercise-dirichlet-uniqueness}).

Stokes' theorem for $1$-forms on surfaces gives the two other classical theorems of vector calculus, which Example~\ref{differential-forms:example-classical} mentioned without formulas.

\begin{corollary}[Green's theorem]\label{riemannian-geometry:corollary-green-theorem}
Let $D \subseteq \RR^2$ be a compact region with smooth boundary, as in Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-divergence}, with $\partial D$ oriented as the boundary of $D$ (so that $D$ lies to the left
of the direction of motion), and $P$, $Q$ smooth functions near $D$. Then
\[
\oint_{\partial D}P\,dx + Q\,dy = \iint_D\Bigl(\frac{\partial Q}{\partial x} - \frac{\partial P}{\partial y}\Bigr)dx\,dy .
\]
In particular the area of $D$ is $\frac12\oint_{\partial D}x\,dy - y\,dx$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{differential-forms:theorem-exterior-derivative}, $d(P\,dx + Q\,dy) = P_y\,dy \wedge dx + Q_x\,dx \wedge dy = (Q_x - P_y)\,dx \wedge dy$, and Stokes' theorem
(Theorem~\ref{differential-forms:theorem-stokes}) gives the formula, the integral of the $2$-form $(Q_x - P_y)\,dx \wedge dy$ over $D$ with its standard orientation being the Lebesgue integral. In the boundary
orientation, an outward vector $w$ followed by a positive tangent vector $T$ is a positive basis of $\RR^2$ (Definition~\ref{differential-forms:definition-manifold-with-boundary}), so $T$ is $w$ rotated by $+\pi/2$
and $D$ is on the left. For the area take $P = -y/2$, $Q = x/2$, with $Q_x - P_y = 1$.
\end{proof}

For the ellipse $x = a\cos t$, $y = b\sin t$, $0 \leq t \leq 2\pi$, the area formula gives $\frac12\int_0^{2\pi}(ab\cos^2t + ab\sin^2t)\,dt = \pi ab$. Green's theorem is also the divergence theorem in the plane:
if $T = (T^1, T^2)$ is the positive unit tangent of $\partial D$, the outward normal is $\nu = (T^2, -T^1)$, so for $F = (Q, -P)$ one has $\langle F, \nu\rangle = PT^1 + QT^2$ and $\operatorname{div}F = Q_x - P_y$: the flux of
$F$ out of $D$ is the circulation of $(P, Q)$ along $\partial D$.

\begin{corollary}[Stokes' theorem of vector calculus]\label{riemannian-geometry:corollary-kelvin-stokes}
Let $\Sigma \subseteq \RR^3$ be a surface oriented by a unit normal field $\nu$, and $S \subseteq \Sigma$ a compact region with smooth boundary (a $2$-manifold with boundary in the charts of $\Sigma$). Orient
$\partial S$ as the boundary of $S$: a tangent vector $T$ of $\partial S$ is positive when $\nu \times T$ points into $S$, that is, walking along $\partial S$ with one's head in the direction of $\nu$, the
region $S$ is on the left. Then for a smooth vector field $F$ on a neighbourhood of $S$,
\[
\iint_S\langle\operatorname{curl}F, \nu\rangle\,dA = \oint_{\partial S}F^1\,dx + F^2\,dy + F^3\,dz = \int_{\partial S}\langle F, T\rangle\,ds,
\]
where $T$ is the positive unit tangent and $ds$ the arc length.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Let $\alpha = F^1\,dx + F^2\,dy + F^3\,dz$ and $\iota \colon S \to \RR^3$ the inclusion. By Example~\ref{differential-forms:example-forms-r3},
$d\alpha = G^1\,dy \wedge dz + G^2\,dz \wedge dx + G^3\,dx \wedge dy$ with $G = \operatorname{curl}F$, and by Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-flux}, applied to the surface $\Sigma$,
$\iota^*d\alpha = \langle G, \nu\rangle\,\mathrm{vol}_S$. Since pullback commutes with $d$ (Theorem~\ref{differential-forms:theorem-exterior-derivative}), Stokes' theorem on the compact manifold with
boundary $S$ gives $\iint_S\langle G, \nu\rangle\,dA = \int_Sd(\iota^*\alpha) = \int_{\partial S}\iota^*\alpha$, and the last integral is the line integral of $\alpha$ along $\partial S$ in its boundary orientation
(Definition~\ref{differential-forms:definition-line-integral}), which is $\int\langle F, T\rangle\,ds$ in a parametrisation by arc length. It remains to describe the boundary orientation. By
Definition~\ref{differential-forms:definition-manifold-with-boundary}, $T$ is positive when $(w, T)$ is a positive basis of $T_pS$ for an outward vector $w$, and by
Proposition~\ref{differential-forms:proposition-orientation-normal} this means $\det(\nu, w, T) > 0$. For $w$ and $T$ unit and orthogonal (which we may assume), $(\nu, w, T)$ is then a positive orthonormal basis of
$\RR^3$, so $\nu \times T = -w$, which points into $S$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-stokes-hemisphere}
Let $S$ be the closed upper unit hemisphere, with the outward normal $\nu(x) = x$, and $F = (-y, x, 0)$, the rotation field, with $\operatorname{curl}F = (0, 0, 2)$. The boundary is the equator, and its
boundary orientation is counterclockwise seen from above: at $(1, 0, 0)$ the inward direction in $S$ is $(0, 0, 1)$, and $\nu \times T = (1, 0, 0) \times (0, 1, 0) = (0, 0, 1)$ for $T = (0, 1, 0)$. With
$\gamma(t) = (\cos t, \sin t, 0)$, the right side is $\int_0^{2\pi}(\sin^2t + \cos^2t)\,dt = 2\pi$. The left side is $\iint_S2z\,dA$, and by Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-flux-graph} (with
$F = (0, 0, 2)$) it is twice the area of the unit disc, $2\pi$. The same value is obtained for every surface with boundary the equator and the corresponding orientation, for instance the flat unit disc
with $\nu = (0, 0, 1)$, where Stokes' theorem is Green's theorem: the flux of a curl through a surface only depends on its boundary, because $\operatorname{curl}F\,\cdot\,d\vec\Sigma$ is an exact $2$-form.
\end{example}

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-areas}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{The sphere.} The map $x(\theta, \varphi) = r(\sin\theta\cos\varphi, \sin\theta\sin\varphi, \cos\theta)$ on $(0, \pi) \times (0, 2\pi)$ parametrises the sphere of
radius $r$ minus a closed half great circle. That half circle has area $0$: without its two endpoints it is the segment $\varphi = 0$ for the same formula on
$(0, \pi) \times (-\pi, \pi)$, a Lebesgue null set, and points have measure $0$. Here $E = r^2$, $F = 0$, $G = r^2\sin^2\theta$, so $dA = r^2\sin\theta\,d\theta\,d\varphi$
and the area is $\int_0^{2\pi}\int_0^\pi r^2\sin\theta\,d\theta\,d\varphi = 4\pi r^2$.
\item \emph{The torus of revolution.} For $0 < b < a$, $x(u, v) = ((a + b\cos u)\cos v, (a + b\cos u)\sin v, b\sin u)$ has $E = b^2$, $F = 0$ and
$G = (a + b\cos u)^2$, so the area is $\int_0^{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}b(a + b\cos u)\,du\,dv = 4\pi^2ab$: the length $2\pi b$ of the rotated circle times the length $2\pi a$
of the path of its centre.
\item \emph{Graphs.} The upper hemisphere is the graph of $\phi = \sqrt{1 - u^2 - v^2}$ over the open unit disc, with $1 + |\nabla\phi|^2 = 1/(1 - u^2 - v^2)$. In polar
coordinates its area is $\int_0^{2\pi}\int_0^1\rho(1 - \rho^2)^{-1/2}\,d\rho\,d\theta = 2\pi$, half of $4\pi$.
\item \emph{Spheres and balls.} For $F(x) = x$ on $\RR^{n+1}$, $\operatorname{div}F = n + 1$, and on the unit sphere $\nu(x) = x$ and $\langle F, \nu\rangle = 1$. By
Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-divergence}, $\mathrm{vol}(S^n) = (n + 1)\,\lambda(B^{n+1})$: for instance $2\pi = 2 \cdot \pi$ and
$4\pi = 3 \cdot \frac{4\pi}{3}$. With the volume $\lambda(B^m) = \pi^{m/2}/\Gamma(\frac m2 + 1)$ of the unit ball (Exercise~\ref{measure-theory:exercise-ball-volume}) this gives
$\mathrm{vol}(S^n) = 2\pi^{(n+1)/2}/\Gamma(\frac{n+1}2)$, using $\Gamma(s + 1) = s\Gamma(s)$: for instance $\mathrm{vol}(S^3) = 2\pi^2$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\section{Covariant derivatives along curves}\label{riemannian-geometry:section-along-curves}

A \emph{vector field along} a smooth curve $\gamma \colon I \to M$ is a smooth map $V \colon I \to TM$ with $V(t) \in T_{\gamma(t)}M$; for instance the velocity $\dot\gamma$, or $Y \circ \gamma$ for a vector field $Y$ on $M$.

\begin{proposition}\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-covariant-derivative-curves}
There is a unique operator $D_t$ sending vector fields along $\gamma$ to vector fields along $\gamma$ such that $D_t$ is $\RR$-linear, $D_t(fV) = \dot fV + fD_tV$ for smooth $f \colon I \to \RR$, and
$D_t(Y \circ \gamma) = \nabla_{\dot\gamma}Y$ for every vector field $Y$ defined near $\gamma(I)$. In coordinates, for $V = V^k\partial_k$,
\[
D_tV = \big(\dot V^k + \Gamma^k_{ij}(\gamma)\dot\gamma^iV^j\big)\partial_k.
\]
Moreover $\frac{d}{dt}\langle V, W\rangle = \langle D_tV, W\rangle + \langle V, D_tW\rangle$. A vector field along $\gamma$ is parallel in the sense of Definition~\ref{connections:definition-parallel} if and only if $D_tV = 0$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
In a chart, $V = V^k(\partial_k \circ \gamma)$, and the axioms force $D_tV = \dot V^k\partial_k + V^k\nabla_{\dot\gamma}\partial_k = (\dot V^k + \dot\gamma^iV^j\Gamma^k_{ij})\partial_k$, since $\nabla_{\dot\gamma}\partial_j = \dot\gamma^i\Gamma^k_{ij}\partial_k$. This
proves uniqueness. Conversely, the formula satisfies the axioms in each chart (for $Y = Y^k\partial_k$, $\frac{d}{dt}Y^k(\gamma) = \dot\gamma^i\partial_iY^k$ matches the coordinate expression of $\nabla_{\dot\gamma}Y$), and by uniqueness the
local definitions agree on overlaps. For the metric property, differentiate $\langle V, W\rangle = g_{jk}(\gamma)V^jW^k$: the derivative of $g_{jk}(\gamma)$ is $\dot\gamma^i\partial_ig_{jk}$, and by \eqref{riemannian-geometry:equation-metric-christoffel} the result equals
$\langle D_tV, W\rangle + \langle V, D_tW\rangle$. The last statement is the local expression of $\nabla_{\dot\gamma}s = 0$ in the coordinate frame.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-derivative-along-curves}
In the language of Chapter~\ref{connections}, $D_t$ is the pullback connection $\gamma^*\nabla$ on the bundle $\gamma^*TM$ over $I$, differentiated in the direction of
the vector field $d/dt$ on $I$. Indeed $(\gamma^*\nabla)_{d/dt}$ is $\RR$-linear, satisfies the Leibniz rule for functions of $t$, and sends $Y \circ \gamma$ to
$(\nabla Y)(\dot\gamma) = \nabla_{\dot\gamma}Y$ (Proposition~\ref{connections:proposition-pullback-tensor}(1)), so it equals $D_t$ by the uniqueness in
Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-covariant-derivative-curves}. This is the meaning of $\nabla_{\dot\gamma}\dot\gamma$ in
Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-geodesic}: it is $D_t\dot\gamma$. It could not be defined as $\nabla_XX$ for a vector field $X$, because the velocity
need not be the restriction of a vector field on $M$: the curve $\gamma(t) = (t^2, 0)$ passes through each point $(t^2, 0)$ twice, with the opposite velocities $(\pm2t, 0)$
(Remark~\ref{connections:remark-along-curves}). When $\dot\gamma$ does extend near $\gamma(t_0)$ to a vector field $X$, that is $X(\gamma(t)) = \dot\gamma(t)$ for $t$ near
$t_0$, then $D_t\dot\gamma = D_t(X \circ \gamma) = \nabla_{\dot\gamma}X = (\nabla_XX)(\gamma(t))$ there, whichever extension is chosen. For example, a unit speed great circle
$c$ on $S^2$ is the integral curve of the rotation field $X(x) = a \times x$ about its axis $a$, and $D_t\dot c = (\nabla_XX)(c)$.
\end{remark}

For a submanifold $M$ of $\RR^N$ with the induced metric there is a concrete description: regarding $V$ as a function $I \to \RR^N$, $D_tV$ is the orthogonal projection of the ordinary derivative $V^{\prime}(t)$
onto $T_{\gamma(t)}M$, and a curve is a geodesic exactly when its acceleration $\gamma^{\prime\prime}$ is normal to $M$ (Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-tangential-projection} below, with examples).

\begin{lemma}[Symmetry lemma]\label{riemannian-geometry:lemma-symmetry}
Let $f \colon (-\varepsilon, \varepsilon) \times (a, b) \to M$ be smooth, and write $D_s$, $D_t$ for the covariant derivatives along the curves $s \mapsto f(s, t)$ and $t \mapsto f(s, t)$. Then $D_s\partial_tf = D_t\partial_sf$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
In coordinates both sides equal $(\partial_s\partial_tf^k + \Gamma^k_{ij}\partial_sf^i\partial_tf^j)\partial_k$, by the symmetry $\Gamma^k_{ij} = \Gamma^k_{ji}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-geodesic-speed}
A curve $\gamma$ is a geodesic (Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-geodesic}) if and only if $D_t\dot\gamma = 0$. Geodesics have constant speed, and parallel transport along any curve is a linear isometry.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The coordinate expression of $D_t\dot\gamma$ is the geodesic equation. If $D_t\dot\gamma = 0$ then $\frac{d}{dt}|\dot\gamma|^2 = 2\langle D_t\dot\gamma, \dot\gamma\rangle = 0$. If $V$ and $W$ are parallel, $\langle V, W\rangle$ is constant by
Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-covariant-derivative-curves}.
\end{proof}

\section{Gradient, Hessian, divergence and Laplacian}\label{riemannian-geometry:section-gradient}

The differential $df$ of a function is defined on every manifold, but the gradient, the vector ``pointing in the direction of steepest ascent'', and the second derivative of $f$ as a symmetric bilinear
form are not: the first needs a metric to turn covectors into vectors, the second a connection to differentiate $df$. On a Riemannian manifold both are available, together with the divergence of a
vector field and the Laplacian of a function, and in Euclidean space they reduce to the familiar operators of vector calculus. In this section $(M, g)$ is a Riemannian manifold with Levi-Civita
connection $\nabla$, and $\langle\ ,\ \rangle = g$.

\begin{definition}\label{riemannian-geometry:definition-gradient-hessian}
Let $f$ be a smooth function on $M$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item The \emph{gradient} of $f$ is the vector field $\operatorname{grad}f$ with $\langle\operatorname{grad}f, X\rangle = df(X) = Xf$ for all vector fields $X$; it is the image of $df$ under the isomorphism
$T^*M \cong TM$ given by the metric (Proposition~\ref{vector-bundles:proposition-metrics}), that is, $\operatorname{grad}f = (df)^\sharp$ is obtained by raising the index of $df$
(Definition~\ref{multilinear-algebra:definition-raising-lowering}).
\item The \emph{Hessian} of $f$ is the covariant derivative of the $1$-form $df$, the tensor field $\operatorname{Hess}f = \nabla df$ of type $(0,2)$:
\[
\operatorname{Hess}f(X, Y) = (\nabla_Xdf)(Y) = X(Yf) - (\nabla_XY)f .
\]
\item The \emph{divergence} of a vector field $X$ is the trace of the endomorphism $v \mapsto \nabla_vX$ of each tangent space: $\operatorname{div}X = \sum_i\langle\nabla_{e_i}X, e_i\rangle$ for an orthonormal
basis $(e_i)$.
\item The \emph{Laplacian} of $f$ (with the sign of analysis) is $\Delta_gf = \operatorname{div}\operatorname{grad}f$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

The formula for the Hessian is the Leibniz rule for the induced connection on $1$-forms (Proposition~\ref{connections:proposition-induced-connections}(1)), $(\nabla_X\alpha)(Y) = X(\alpha(Y)) - \alpha(\nabla_XY)$, with
$\alpha = df$ and $df(Y) = Yf$ (Remark~\ref{differential-forms:remark-differential-function}). Without the correction term, $X(Yf)$ alone is not a tensor in $X$ and $Y$: it is not $C^\infty(M)$-linear in $Y$, as
$X(hYf) = (Xh)(Yf) + hX(Yf)$.

\begin{proposition}\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-gradient-hessian}
Let $f$ be smooth and $X$ a vector field on $M$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item In coordinates, $\operatorname{grad}f = \sum_{i,j}g^{ij}\partial_jf\,\partial_i$, $\operatorname{Hess}f(\partial_i, \partial_j) = \partial_i\partial_jf - \sum_k\Gamma^k_{ij}\partial_kf$,
$\operatorname{div}X = \frac{1}{\sqrt{\det g}}\sum_i\partial_i\bigl(\sqrt{\det g}\,X^i\bigr)$ and $\Delta_gf = \frac{1}{\sqrt{\det g}}\sum_{i,j}\partial_i\bigl(\sqrt{\det g}\,g^{ij}\partial_jf\bigr)$.
\item At a point where $df \neq 0$, $\operatorname{grad}f$ is orthogonal to the level set of $f$ through it, and among unit vectors $v$ the derivative $v(f)$ is largest for $v = \operatorname{grad}f/|\operatorname{grad}f|$,
with maximum value $|\operatorname{grad}f|$.
\item $\operatorname{Hess}f$ is symmetric, and $\operatorname{Hess}f(X, Y) = \langle\nabla_X\operatorname{grad}f, Y\rangle$; hence $\Delta_gf = \operatorname{tr}_g\operatorname{Hess}f = \sum_i\operatorname{Hess}f(e_i, e_i)$.
\item For a geodesic $\gamma$, $(f \circ \gamma)^{\prime\prime}(t) = \operatorname{Hess}f(\dot\gamma(t), \dot\gamma(t))$. At a critical point $p$ of $f$, $\operatorname{Hess}f_p(v, w) = \sum_{i,j}v^iw^j\partial_i\partial_jf(p)$ in any chart:
there the Hessian does not depend on the metric.
\item If $M$ is oriented, $d(\iota_X\mathrm{vol}_g) = (\operatorname{div}X)\,\mathrm{vol}_g$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(1) Writing $\operatorname{grad}f = \sum_iG^i\partial_i$, the definition gives $\sum_ig_{ij}G^i = \partial_jf$, so $G^i = \sum_jg^{ij}\partial_jf$. The Hessian formula is the definition with $\nabla_{\partial_i}\partial_j = \sum_k\Gamma^k_{ij}\partial_k$.
For the divergence, $\nabla_{\partial_i}X = \sum_k(\partial_iX^k + \sum_jX^j\Gamma^k_{ij})\partial_k$, so its trace is $\sum_i\partial_iX^i + \sum_{i,j}\Gamma^i_{ij}X^j$; the trace does not depend on the basis, so it may be computed in
the coordinate basis. By the formula of Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-levi-civita}, $\sum_i\Gamma^i_{ij} = \frac12\sum_{i,l}g^{il}\partial_jg_{il} = \frac12\operatorname{tr}(g^{-1}\partial_jg)$, the other two terms cancelling by
the symmetry of $g$. For an invertible matrix $A(t)$, $\det A(t + h) = \det A(t)\det\bigl(I + A(t)^{-1}(A(t + h) - A(t))\bigr)$ and $\det(I + hB) = 1 + h\operatorname{tr}B + O(h^2)$ by the Leibniz formula for the determinant,
so $\partial_j\det g = \det g\operatorname{tr}(g^{-1}\partial_jg)$ and $\sum_i\Gamma^i_{ij} = \partial_j\log\sqrt{\det g}$. Hence
$\operatorname{div}X = \sum_i\partial_iX^i + \sum_jX^j\partial_j\log\sqrt{\det g} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{\det g}}\sum_i\partial_i(\sqrt{\det g}\,X^i)$. The formula for $\Delta_g$ combines this with the one for the gradient.

(2) For $v$ tangent to the level set, $\langle\operatorname{grad}f, v\rangle = v(f) = 0$, since $f$ is constant along a curve in the level set. For $|v| = 1$, $v(f) = \langle\operatorname{grad}f, v\rangle \leq |\operatorname{grad}f|$ by the
Cauchy--Schwarz inequality, with equality exactly for $v = \operatorname{grad}f/|\operatorname{grad}f|$.

(3) $\operatorname{Hess}f(X, Y) - \operatorname{Hess}f(Y, X) = X(Yf) - Y(Xf) - (\nabla_XY - \nabla_YX)f = [X, Y]f - [X, Y]f = 0$, by torsion-freeness. Since the connection is metric,
$X\langle\operatorname{grad}f, Y\rangle = \langle\nabla_X\operatorname{grad}f, Y\rangle + \langle\operatorname{grad}f, \nabla_XY\rangle$, that is $X(Yf) = \langle\nabla_X\operatorname{grad}f, Y\rangle + (\nabla_XY)f$, which is the second
formula. Taking the trace in an orthonormal basis, $\sum_i\operatorname{Hess}f(e_i, e_i) = \sum_i\langle\nabla_{e_i}\operatorname{grad}f, e_i\rangle = \operatorname{div}\operatorname{grad}f$.

(4) $(f \circ \gamma)^{\prime} = df(\dot\gamma) = \langle\operatorname{grad}f \circ \gamma, \dot\gamma\rangle$, and differentiating with the metric property of $D_t$ (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-covariant-derivative-curves}),
$(f \circ \gamma)^{\prime\prime} = \langle\nabla_{\dot\gamma}\operatorname{grad}f, \dot\gamma\rangle + \langle\operatorname{grad}f, D_t\dot\gamma\rangle = \operatorname{Hess}f(\dot\gamma, \dot\gamma)$, as $D_t\dot\gamma = 0$. At a critical point the terms
$\Gamma^k_{ij}\partial_kf$ of (1) vanish.

(5) By Cartan's formula (Proposition~\ref{differential-forms:proposition-cartan}) and $d\,\mathrm{vol}_g = 0$, $d(\iota_X\mathrm{vol}_g) = L_X\mathrm{vol}_g$. In a positively oriented chart
$\iota_X\mathrm{vol}_g = \sum_i(-1)^{i-1}\sqrt{\det g}\,X^i\,dx^1 \wedge \cdots \wedge \widehat{dx^i} \wedge \cdots \wedge dx^n$ (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-volume-form} and
Proposition~\ref{multilinear-algebra:proposition-interior-antiderivation}), whose exterior derivative is $\sum_i\partial_i(\sqrt{\det g}\,X^i)\,dx^1 \wedge \cdots \wedge dx^n = (\operatorname{div}X)\,\mathrm{vol}_g$ by (1).
\end{proof}

By (4), at a critical point $p$ the second derivative test of calculus is coordinate free: if $\operatorname{Hess}f_p$ is positive definite, $f$ has a strict local minimum at $p$ (the test in any
chart, where $\operatorname{Hess}f_p$ is the matrix of second derivatives), and conversely at a local minimum it is positive semidefinite (apply the one-variable test to $f \circ \gamma$ for geodesics $\gamma$
through $p$). Away from critical points the Hessian depends on the metric through the Christoffel
symbols. A function is \emph{convex} along all geodesics exactly when $\operatorname{Hess}f \geq 0$, again by (4). The Hodge Laplacian of Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-laplacian} on functions is
$-\Delta_g$ (compare (1) with Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-adjoint}(5)): geometers prefer the sign that makes the Laplacian a non-negative operator, analysts the sign that gives $\sum_i\partial_i^2$ on
$\RR^n$.

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-gradient-hessian}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Euclidean space.} For $g = \sum_idx_i^2$ all Christoffel symbols vanish, and $\operatorname{grad}f = (\partial_1f, \ldots, \partial_nf)$, $\operatorname{Hess}f = (\partial_i\partial_jf)$,
$\operatorname{div}X = \sum_i\partial_iX^i$ and $\Delta_g = \sum_i\partial_i^2$.
\item \emph{Polar coordinates.} For $g = dr^2 + r^2d\theta^2$ on the plane minus the origin, $\det g = r^2$, $g^{rr} = 1$, $g^{\theta\theta} = r^{-2}$, so
\[
\operatorname{grad}f = f_r\,\partial_r + \frac{f_\theta}{r^2}\partial_\theta, \qquad \Delta_gf = \frac1r\partial_r(rf_r) + \frac{1}{r^2}f_{\theta\theta} = f_{rr} + \frac1rf_r + \frac{1}{r^2}f_{\theta\theta} .
\]
In terms of the unit vectors $e_r = \partial_r$, $e_\theta = r^{-1}\partial_\theta$ the gradient is $f_re_r + \frac1rf_\theta e_\theta$, the formula of vector calculus. On the round sphere with
$g = d\vartheta^2 + \sin^2\vartheta\,d\varphi^2$, the same computation gives $\Delta_gf = \frac{1}{\sin\vartheta}\partial_\vartheta(\sin\vartheta\,f_\vartheta) + \frac{1}{\sin^2\vartheta}f_{\varphi\varphi}$.
\item \emph{Conformal metrics.} For $g^{\prime} = e^{2u}g$, $\operatorname{grad}^{\prime}f = e^{-2u}\operatorname{grad}f$: the gradient depends on the metric, while $df$ does not. In dimension $2$ also
$\Delta_{g^{\prime}}f = e^{-2u}\Delta_gf$, since $\sqrt{\det g^{\prime}}\,g^{\prime ij} = \sqrt{\det g}\,g^{ij}$; so harmonic functions on a surface depend only on the conformal class of the metric. For the
hyperbolic half-plane, $\Delta_g = y^2(\partial_x^2 + \partial_y^2)$, and $y^s$ satisfies $\Delta_gy^s = s(s - 1)y^s$.
\item \emph{Distance from the origin} in $\RR^n$: for $r = |x|$ on $\RR^n \setminus \{0\}$, $\operatorname{grad}r = x/r$, a unit vector field, and
$\operatorname{Hess}r = \frac1r\bigl(I - \frac{xx^T}{r^2}\bigr)$: it vanishes in the radial direction, along which $r$ is linear, and equals $\frac1r$ times the identity on the tangent spaces of the spheres
$\{r = \text{const}\}$, whose curvature it measures. Hence $\Delta_gr = \frac{n-1}{r}$, and $\Delta_g(r^{2-n}) = 0$ for $n \geq 3$ (Exercise~\ref{riemannian-geometry:exercise-gradient-identities}); for $n = 3$, $-\frac{1}{4\pi r}$ is the fundamental solution of the Laplacian
(Chapter~\ref{elliptic-operators}).
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

The divergence theorem of Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-divergence} holds on any Riemannian manifold with boundary, with the divergence of the metric. Let $M$ be an oriented Riemannian manifold
with boundary and $\nu$ the outward unit normal along $\partial M$: the unit vector orthogonal to $T(\partial M)$ pointing out of $M$.

\begin{proposition}[Divergence theorem and Green's formula]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-riemannian-divergence}
Let $M$ be a compact oriented Riemannian manifold with boundary, with the induced metric and the boundary orientation on $\partial M$. For a vector field $X$ and smooth functions $f$, $h$,
\[
\int_M\operatorname{div}X\,\mathrm{vol}_g = \int_{\partial M}\langle X, \nu\rangle\,\mathrm{vol}_{\partial M}, \qquad
\int_M\bigl(h\,\Delta_gf + \langle\operatorname{grad}h, \operatorname{grad}f\rangle\bigr)\mathrm{vol}_g = \int_{\partial M}h\,\langle\operatorname{grad}f, \nu\rangle\,\mathrm{vol}_{\partial M} .
\]
In particular, if $\partial M = \emptyset$, then $\int_M\operatorname{div}X\,\mathrm{vol}_g = 0$ and $\int_Mh\,\Delta_gf\,\mathrm{vol}_g = -\int_M\langle\operatorname{grad}h, \operatorname{grad}f\rangle\,\mathrm{vol}_g$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-gradient-hessian}(5) and Stokes' theorem (Theorem~\ref{differential-forms:theorem-stokes}), $\int_M\operatorname{div}X\,\mathrm{vol}_g = \int_{\partial M}\iota_X\mathrm{vol}_g$. As
in the proof of Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-flux}, write $X = \langle X, \nu\rangle\nu + X^\top$ with $X^\top$ tangent to $\partial M$; then $\iota_{X^\top}\mathrm{vol}_g$ vanishes on $T(\partial M)$, because
$n$ vectors in the $(n-1)$-dimensional space $T_p(\partial M)$ are linearly dependent. And $\iota_\nu\mathrm{vol}_g$ restricted to $\partial M$ is $\mathrm{vol}_{\partial M}$: if $(e_2, \ldots, e_n)$ is an orthonormal basis of
$T_p(\partial M)$ that is positive for the boundary orientation, then $(\nu, e_2, \ldots, e_n)$ is a positive orthonormal basis of $T_pM$ (the outward vector comes first,
Definition~\ref{differential-forms:definition-manifold-with-boundary}, and $\nu$ differs from any outward vector by a positive multiple plus a tangent vector, as in the proof of
Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-divergence}), so $(\iota_\nu\mathrm{vol}_g)(e_2, \ldots, e_n) = 1$. Green's formula is the divergence theorem for $X = h\operatorname{grad}f$, with
$\operatorname{div}(h\operatorname{grad}f) = \langle\operatorname{grad}h, \operatorname{grad}f\rangle + h\,\Delta_gf$, by the Leibniz rule $\nabla_v(hY) = v(h)Y + h\nabla_vY$ inside the trace.
\end{proof}

\section{The exponential map and minimising geodesics}\label{riemannian-geometry:section-exponential}

Geodesics are the projections to $M$ of the integral curves of a vector field on $TM$, the \emph{geodesic spray}, which in the coordinates $(x, v)$ of $TM$ is $v^k\partial_{x^k} - \Gamma^k_{ij}(x)v^iv^j\partial_{v^k}$. By
Theorem~\ref{smooth-manifolds:theorem-flow}, the set $\mathcal{E} \subseteq TM$ of vectors $v$ for which $\gamma_v$ is defined on $[0, 1]$ is open, and $\exp \colon \mathcal{E} \to M$ is smooth. Since $\gamma_{cv}(t) = \gamma_v(ct)$, each
$\mathcal{E}_p = \mathcal{E} \cap T_pM$ is star-shaped about $0$, and $\exp_p(tv) = \gamma_v(t)$.

\begin{proposition}[The geodesic spray and the Sasaki metric]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-geodesic-spray}
Let $(M, g)$ be a Riemannian manifold, $\pi \colon TM \to M$, and $T(TM) = H \oplus V$ the splitting into the horizontal distribution of the Levi-Civita connection and the vertical subbundle
(Proposition~\ref{connections:proposition-horizontal}), with connection map $K$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item The vector field $G$ on $TM$ with $G(v) = v^h_v$, the horizontal lift of $v \in T_pM$ to the point $v$ itself, is smooth, and its integral curves are the velocity curves $t \mapsto \dot\gamma(t)$
of the geodesics. So the flow of $G$, the \emph{geodesic flow}, is $\Phi_t(v) = \dot\gamma_v(t)$.
\item $g_S(A, B) = g(d\pi\,A, d\pi\,B) + g(KA, KB)$ is a Riemannian metric on $TM$, the \emph{Sasaki metric}, for which $H \perp V$, $d\pi \colon H_v \to T_pM$ and $K \colon V_v \to T_pM$ are isometries; in
particular $\pi$ is a \emph{Riemannian submersion}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(1) In coordinates $(x, v)$ on $TM$, by Proposition~\ref{connections:proposition-horizontal}(1), $G(x, v) = (v, -\theta_x(v)v) = \sum_kv^k\partial_{x^k} - \sum_{i,j,k}\Gamma^k_{ij}v^iv^j\partial_{v^k}$, which is smooth.
A curve $u(t) = (x(t), v(t))$ is an integral curve if and only if $\dot x = v$ and $\dot v^k = -\sum\Gamma^k_{ij}v^iv^j$, that is, $\ddot x^k + \sum\Gamma^k_{ij}\dot x^i\dot x^j = 0$ with $v = \dot x$: the
geodesic equation of Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-geodesic}. Equivalently, by Proposition~\ref{connections:proposition-horizontal}(3), the curve $\dot\gamma$ over $\gamma$ is horizontal if
and only if it is parallel, $D_t\dot\gamma = 0$, and its projection has velocity $\dot\gamma$ exactly when it is an integral curve of $G$.

(2) $g_S$ is symmetric and smooth. If $g_S(A, A) = 0$ then $d\pi\,A = 0$, so $A$ is vertical, and $KA = 0$, so $A = 0$ ($K$ is injective on $V$, being the inverse of the vertical lift); hence $g_S$ is
positive definite. For $A \in H$ and $B \in V$, $KA = 0$ and $d\pi\,B = 0$, so $g_S(A, B) = 0$. The other statements follow from the definition.
\end{proof}

So geodesics are the projections of the integral curves of a single vector field on $TM$, so their properties (existence, uniqueness, smooth
dependence) are those of flows (Theorem~\ref{smooth-manifolds:theorem-flow}); on $T^*M$ the same flow is Hamiltonian (Chapter~\ref{symplectic-geometry}).

\begin{proposition}\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-exponential}
$d_0\exp_p = \id$, so $\exp_p$ is a diffeomorphism from a neighbourhood of $0$ onto a neighbourhood of $p$, giving \emph{normal coordinates} in which $g_{ij}(p) = \delta_{ij}$ and $\Gamma^k_{ij}(p) = 0$.
Geodesics through $p$ are the images of lines through $0$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since $\exp_p(tv) = \gamma_v(t)$, the differential of $\exp_p$ at $0$ sends $v$ to $\dot\gamma_v(0) = v$, and the inverse function theorem applies. In normal coordinates for an orthonormal basis of $T_pM$, $g_{ij}(p) = \delta_{ij}$, and the lines
$t \mapsto tv$ are geodesics, so the geodesic equation at $t = 0$ gives $\Gamma^k_{ij}(p)v^iv^j = 0$ for all $v$; as $\Gamma^k_{ij}$ is symmetric in $i$, $j$, it vanishes at $p$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Gauss lemma]\label{riemannian-geometry:theorem-gauss-lemma}
Let $v \in \mathcal{E}_p$ and $w \in T_pM$, and identify $T_v(T_pM)$ with $T_pM$. Then $\langle d_v\exp_p(v), d_v\exp_p(w)\rangle = \langle v, w\rangle$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since $\mathcal{E}_p$ is open and star-shaped, $f(s, t) = \exp_p(t(v + sw))$ is defined and smooth for $|s|$ small and $t \in [0, 1]$. Let $T = \partial_tf$ and $S = \partial_sf$. For fixed $s$, $t \mapsto f(s, t)$ is the geodesic with initial
velocity $v + sw$, so $D_tT = 0$ and $|T|^2 = |v + sw|^2$ for all $t$. Using Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-covariant-derivative-curves} and Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-symmetry},
\[
\partial_t\langle S, T\rangle = \langle D_tS, T\rangle + \langle S, D_tT\rangle = \langle D_sT, T\rangle = \tfrac12\partial_s|T|^2 = \tfrac12\partial_s|v + sw|^2 = \langle v + sw, w\rangle.
\]
At $s = 0$ this is $\langle v, w\rangle$. Also $f(s, 0) = p$, so $S(s, 0) = 0$. Integrating, $\langle S, T\rangle(0, 1) = \langle v, w\rangle$. Finally $T(0, 1) = \frac{d}{dt}\exp_p(tv)|_{t=1} = d_v\exp_p(v)$ and
$S(0, 1) = \frac{d}{ds}\exp_p(v + sw)|_{s=0} = d_v\exp_p(w)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Geodesics are locally minimising]\label{riemannian-geometry:theorem-local-minimisation}
Let $\varepsilon > 0$ be such that $\exp_p$ is a diffeomorphism from $B(0, \varepsilon) \subseteq T_pM$ onto an open set $U$ (a \emph{normal ball}).
\begin{enumerate}
\item For $|v| < \varepsilon$, every curve from $p$ to $\exp_p(v)$ has length $\geq |v|$, with equality if and only if it is a monotone reparametrisation of the radial geodesic $t \mapsto \exp_p(tv)$, $t \in [0, 1]$.
\item $d(p, \exp_p(v)) = |v|$ for $|v| < \varepsilon$, and $\exp_p(B(0, r))$ is the metric ball $\{x : d(p, x) < r\}$ for $0 < r \leq \varepsilon$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $x \in U$ let $\rho(x) = |\exp_p^{-1}(x)|$, a continuous function, smooth on $U \setminus \{p\}$. Let $c \colon [a, b] \to U \setminus \{p\}$ be a piecewise smooth curve and write $c(t) = \exp_p(\rho(t)u(t))$ with $\rho(t) = \rho(c(t)) > 0$ and $|u(t)| = 1$.
Then $\dot c = d\exp_p(\dot\rho u + \rho\dot u)$ at the point $\rho u$, with $\langle u, \dot u\rangle = 0$. By the Gauss lemma, $d\exp_p(u)$ has length $1$ and is orthogonal to $d\exp_p(\rho\dot u)$ (both at $\rho u$), so
\begin{equation}\label{riemannian-geometry:equation-length-estimate}
|\dot c|^2 = \dot\rho^2 + |d\exp_p(\rho\dot u)|^2 \geq \dot\rho^2, \qquad\text{hence}\qquad L(c) \geq \int_a^b|\dot\rho|\,dt \geq \rho(b) - \rho(a).
\end{equation}
Now let $c \colon [a, b] \to M$ go from $p$ to $q = \exp_p(v)$, $0 < |v| = r < \varepsilon$. Suppose first that $c$ stays in $U$. Let $a'$ be the last time with $c(a') = p$; applying \eqref{riemannian-geometry:equation-length-estimate} on $[a'', b]$ for $a'' \downarrow a'$ gives $L(c) \geq r$. If
$L(c) = r$, then $c$ is constant on $[a, a']$, and on $(a', b]$ equality holds in \eqref{riemannian-geometry:equation-length-estimate}: $\dot\rho \geq 0$ and $d\exp_p(\rho\dot u) = 0$, so $\dot u = 0$ ($d\exp_p$ being injective on $B(0, \varepsilon)$), $u$ is constant and $c$ is a monotone
reparametrisation of the radial geodesic. Conversely such reparametrisations have length $r$. If $c$ leaves $U$, choose $r < r' < \varepsilon$. The set $K = \exp_p(\bar B(0, r'))$ is compact, hence closed, and contains $p$; so $c$
leaves $K$, and by continuity $\rho(c(t))$ takes the value $r'$ at some first time $t_2$ with $c([a, t_2]) \subseteq K$. The first case applied to $c|_{[a, t_2]}$ gives $L(c) \geq r' > r$. This proves (1).

For (2), the radial geodesic has length $|v|$, so $d(p, \exp_p(v)) = |v|$ by (1). The ball $\exp_p(B(0, r))$ lies in the metric ball of radius $r$. Conversely, if $d(p, x) < r$, choose a curve $c$ from $p$ to $x$ with $L(c) < r' < r$; if
$x \notin \exp_p(B(0, r'))$, the argument above shows $L(c) \geq r'$, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-convex}
Every point also has a \emph{geodesically convex} neighbourhood, in which any two points are joined by a unique minimising geodesic, contained in the neighbourhood \cite[Chapter 3]{do-carmo-riemannian}.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Uniformly normal neighbourhoods]\label{riemannian-geometry:lemma-uniformly-normal}
Every $q \in M$ has a neighbourhood $W$ and a $\delta > 0$ such that for every $x \in W$, $\exp_x$ is defined on $B(0, \delta) \subseteq T_xM$, is a diffeomorphism from it onto an open set, and $\exp_x(B(0, \delta)) \supseteq W$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Consider $E \colon \mathcal{E} \to M \times M$, $E(v) = (\pi(v), \exp(v))$. In coordinates $(x, v)$ near $0_q \in TM$, its differential at $0_q$ is $\begin{pmatrix}I & 0\\ I & I\end{pmatrix}$, since $\exp(0_x) = x$ and $d_0\exp_x = \mathrm{id}$; so by the
inverse function theorem $E$ is a diffeomorphism from a neighbourhood $O$ of $0_q$ onto a neighbourhood of $(q, q)$. Shrinking, we may take $O = \{v \in T_xM : x \in W', |v| < \delta\}$ for a neighbourhood $W'$ of $q$ and some $\delta > 0$
(the norm is continuous on $TM$), and choose a neighbourhood $W \subseteq W'$ of $q$ with $W \times W \subseteq E(O)$. For $x \in W$, $\exp_x$ is injective on $B(0, \delta)$ with invertible differential, hence a diffeomorphism onto an open
set; and for $y \in W$, $(x, y) = E(v)$ with $v \in O$, that is $y = \exp_x(v)$ with $|v| < \delta$.
\end{proof}
