Let $M$ be a compact connected oriented Riemannian manifold of dimension $n$ with positive sectional curvature, and $F$ an isometry of $M$ that preserves the
orientation if $n$ is even and reverses it if $n$ is odd. Then $F$ has a fixed point.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Suppose not. The function $x \mapsto d(x, F(x))$ is continuous and positive on the compact $M$; let $x_0$ be a minimum point, $L = d(x_0, F(x_0)) > 0$, and
$\gamma \colon [0, L] \to M$ a minimising geodesic of unit speed from $x_0$ to $F(x_0)$ (Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-hopf-rinow}). An isometry maps geodesics to
geodesics, since in charts $y$ and $y \circ F$ the metric has the same coefficients (as in the proof of Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-bonnet-myers}), and it
preserves lengths of curves, hence distances.

\emph{Step 1: $d_{x_0}F(\dot\gamma(0)) = \dot\gamma(L)$.} Let $m = \gamma(L/2)$. Then
$d(m, F(m)) \leq d(m, F(x_0)) + d(F(x_0), F(m)) = L/2 + d(x_0, m) = L$, and $d(m, F(m)) \geq L$ by the choice of $x_0$. So the curve that follows $\gamma$ from $m$ to $F(x_0)$
and then $F \circ \gamma$ from $F(x_0)$ to $F(m)$ has unit speed and length $d(m, F(m))$; by Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-minimising-geodesic} it is a geodesic,
hence smooth at $F(x_0)$, where its two velocities are $\dot\gamma(L)$ and $d_{x_0}F(\dot\gamma(0))$.

\emph{Step 2: a closed-up parallel field.} Let $P \colon T_{x_0}M \to T_{F(x_0)}M$ be parallel transport along $\gamma$. It is a linear isometry
(Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-geodesic-speed}) and preserves orientations, as in the proof of
Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-holonomy-basic}(4). So $B = P^{-1} \circ d_{x_0}F$ is an orthogonal map of $T_{x_0}M$ with $\det B = 1$ if $F$ preserves the
orientation and $\det B = -1$ if it reverses it; in both cases of the theorem $\det B = (-1)^n$. As $\dot\gamma$ is parallel, $P(\dot\gamma(0)) = \dot\gamma(L)$, so $B$ fixes
$\dot\gamma(0)$ by Step 1, and preserves the orthogonal complement $E$ of $\dot\gamma(0)$, of dimension $m = n - 1$, with $\det(B|_E) = (-1)^{m+1}$. An orthogonal map $C$ of an
$m$-dimensional space with $\det C = (-1)^{m+1}$ has the eigenvalue $1$:
$\det(C - I) = \det C\,\det(I - C^T) = \det C\,\det(I - C) = (-1)^{m+1}(-1)^m\det(C - I) = -\det(C - I)$. So there is a unit vector $v \in E$ with $Bv = v$. Let $V$ be the parallel
field along $\gamma$ with $V(0) = v$; then $|V| = 1$, $V \perp \dot\gamma$, and $V(L) = Pv = d_{x_0}F(v)$.

\emph{Step 3: a shorter curve.} Let $f(s, t) = \exp_{\gamma(t)}(sV(t))$ and $x_s = \exp_{x_0}(sv)$. Then $f(s, 0) = x_s$ and, since $F$ maps geodesics to geodesics,
$f(s, L) = \exp_{F(x_0)}(s\,d_{x_0}F(v)) = F(x_s)$. By Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-first-variation} the first derivative of $E(f_s)$ at $s = 0$ is
$\langle V, \dot\gamma\rangle|_0^L = 0$. By Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-second-variation}, whose boundary term vanishes because the curves
$s \mapsto f(s, t)$ are geodesics, and since $D_tV = 0$,
\[
\frac{d^2}{ds^2}\Big|_{s=0}E(f_s) = -\int_0^LR(V, \dot\gamma, \dot\gamma, V)\,dt < 0,
\]
the integrand being the sectional curvature of the plane spanned by the orthonormal vectors $V$ and $\dot\gamma$. So $E(f_s) < E(\gamma)$ for small $s \neq 0$, and
$L(f_s) < L$ by Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-energy-length}. But $f_s$ joins $x_s$ to $F(x_s)$, so $L(f_s) \geq d(x_s, F(x_s)) \geq L$, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Synge]\label{riemannian-geometry:corollary-synge}
Let $M$ be a compact connected Riemannian manifold with positive sectional curvature.
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $n$ is even and $M$ is orientable, then $M$ is simply connected.
\item If $n$ is odd, then $M$ is orientable.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
(1) The sectional curvature $K(u, v) = R(u, v, v, u)$ is continuous and positive on the compact set of orthonormal pairs of tangent vectors, so $K \geq k$ for some $k > 0$,
and $\mathrm{Ric} \geq (n - 1)kg$. Let $\pi \colon \widetilde{M} \to M$ be a universal covering with the metric $\pi^*g$. As in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-bonnet-myers}, $\widetilde{M}$ is complete, hence compact by that theorem, and it has positive sectional curvature. Orient
$\widetilde{M}$ by pulling back a volume form $\mu$ of $M$ by the local diffeomorphism $\pi$. A deck transformation $\tau$ satisfies $\pi \circ \tau = \pi$, so it is an isometry
and $\tau^*\pi^*\mu = \pi^*\mu$: it preserves the orientation. By Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-weinstein}, $\tau$ has a fixed point, so it is the identity, by the
uniqueness of lifts (Proposition~\ref{homotopy:proposition-lifting-criterion}). Hence $\mathrm{Deck}(\pi)$ is trivial, and so is $\pi_1(M) \cong \mathrm{Deck}(\pi)$
(Proposition~\ref{homotopy:proposition-deck}).

(2) Suppose $M$ is not orientable, and let $p \colon \widehat{M} \to M$ be the orientation double covering (Definition~\ref{poincare-duality:definition-orientation-covering}),
which is connected by Proposition~\ref{poincare-duality:proposition-orientation-covering}(1). It is compact, a smooth manifold for which $p$ is a local diffeomorphism
(Example~\ref{smooth-manifolds:example-smooth-quotients}), and it has positive sectional curvature for $p^*g$. It is orientable: at a point $(x, \alpha)$, $p$ identifies the
local homology of $\widehat{M}$ with $H_n(M \mid x; \ZZ)$ (a sheet over a coordinate ball is mapped homeomorphically), and $(x, \alpha) \mapsto \alpha$ is a continuous choice of
generators, that is an orientation (Proposition~\ref{differential-forms:proposition-orientation}). The deck transformation $\tau(x, \alpha) = (x, -\alpha)$ is an isometry of
$p^*g$ without fixed points, and it reverses this orientation, because $p \circ \tau = p$ carries the generator $\alpha$ at $(x, \alpha)$ to $\alpha$ at $(x, -\alpha)$, where the
chosen generator is $-\alpha$. By Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-weinstein} with $n$ odd, $\tau$ has a fixed point, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{counterexample}\label{riemannian-geometry:counterexample-synge}
Every hypothesis of Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-weinstein} and Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-synge} is needed.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{The parity conditions in Weinstein's theorem.} The antipodal map $x \mapsto -x$ of the unit sphere $S^n$ is an isometry without fixed points. It is the restriction
of $-I$, of determinant $(-1)^{n+1}$, so it preserves the orientation of $S^n$ when $n$ is odd and reverses it when $n$ is even: exactly the cases excluded by the theorem.
\item \emph{Orientability in (1).} $\RR\PP^n = S^n/\{\pm1\}$ with the round metric has $K = 1$ and $\pi_1 = \ZZ/2$. For $n$ even it is not orientable
(Exercise~\ref{differential-forms:exercise-orientability-projective}).
\item \emph{Even dimension in (1).} For $n$ odd, $\RR\PP^n$ is orientable with $K = 1$ and $\pi_1 = \ZZ/2$; so are the lens spaces $S^3/(\ZZ/p)$
(Example~\ref{topological-manifolds:example-covering-actions}), with $\pi_1 = \ZZ/p$, as the group acts by unitary, hence orthogonal, maps.
\item \emph{Odd dimension in (2).} $\RR\PP^2$ has $K = 1$ and is not orientable.
\item \emph{Positive curvature, not just $K \geq 0$.} The flat torus $\RR^2/\ZZ^2$ is compact and orientable of even dimension, with $\pi_1 = \ZZ^2$. The product
$\RR\PP^2 \times S^1$ of the round $\RR\PP^2$ and a circle is compact of dimension $3$ with $K \geq 0$ (the curvature tensor of a Riemannian product is the sum of those of the factors, and the circle is flat), and it is not
orientable: by Exercise~\ref{riemannian-geometry:exercise-holonomy-product} its holonomy contains $h \times 1$ for $h \in \mathrm{Hol}(\RR\PP^2)$ with $\det h = -1$, which
exists by Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-holonomy-basic}(4).
\item \emph{Compactness in (1).} The sphere minus two antipodal points, $S^2 \setminus \{\pm e_3\}$, has $K = 1$ and is orientable of even dimension, but by stereographic
projection it is homeomorphic to $\RR^2 \setminus \{0\}$, with $\pi_1 = \ZZ$.
\item \emph{Compactness in (2).} Let $A = \mathrm{diag}(-1, -1, -1, 1)$ acting on $U = S^3 \setminus \{\pm e_4\}$. Its fixed points on $S^3$ are $\pm e_4$, so the group
$\{1, A\}$ acts freely, and properly as it is finite, by isometries; $\det A = -1$, so $A$ reverses the orientation of $S^3$ (defined by the outward normal, which $A$
preserves). The quotient $U/\{1, A\}$ has $K = 1$ (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-quotient-metric}) and dimension $3$, and it is not orientable: an
orientation would pull back to an orientation of the connected $U$ preserved by $A$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{counterexample}

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-synge}
$\RR\PP^2 \times \RR\PP^2$ carries no metric of positive sectional curvature. Its fundamental group $\ZZ/2 \times \ZZ/2$ (Proposition~\ref{homotopy:proposition-product}) is
nontrivial, so by Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-synge}(1) it could only be non-orientable. But then its orientation double covering would be a compact
orientable $4$-manifold of positive curvature whose fundamental group has order $2$, a subgroup of index $2$ of $\ZZ/2 \times \ZZ/2$
(Corollary~\ref{homotopy:corollary-lifting-consequences}), contradicting (1). Whether $S^2 \times S^2$ carries a metric of positive sectional curvature is a famous open question
of Hopf; Synge's theorem does not decide it, as $S^2 \times S^2$ is simply connected.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-comparison}
Theorems~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-cartan-hadamard} and~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-bonnet-myers} are the simplest \emph{comparison theorems}, which compare a manifold with a space of constant curvature
through its Jacobi fields. The Rauch comparison theorem \cite[Chapter 10]{do-carmo-riemannian} and the Bishop--Gromov volume comparison \cite{lee-riemannian} are their refinements, and they lead to the sphere theorems and to
Gromov's compactness theorem.
\end{remark}

A different kind of rigidity concerns straight lines. A \emph{ray} in a Riemannian manifold is a unit speed geodesic $\gamma \colon [0, \infty) \to M$ with
$d(\gamma(s), \gamma(t)) = |s - t|$ for all $s, t$, and a \emph{line} is a unit speed geodesic $\gamma \colon \RR \to M$ with the same property for all $s, t \in \RR$: it
minimises length between any two of its points.

\begin{proposition}\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-rays-lines}
Let $M$ be complete and connected.
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $M$ is not compact, every point is the starting point of a ray.
\item If there are a compact $C \subseteq M$ and points $p_k$, $q_k$ with $d(p_k, C) \to \infty$ and $d(q_k, C) \to \infty$ such that every curve from $p_k$ to $q_k$ meets
$C$, then $M$ contains a line.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(1) By Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-hopf-rinow} closed bounded sets are compact, so $M$ is unbounded: choose $q_k$ with $L_k = d(p, q_k) \to \infty$ and minimising
unit speed geodesics $\gamma_k(t) = \exp_p(tv_k)$, $t \in [0, L_k]$, $|v_k| = 1$. By compactness of the unit sphere a subsequence has $v_k \to v$; let $\gamma(t) = \exp_p(tv)$, defined for
all $t$. For fixed $s, t \geq 0$ and $k$ large, $d(\gamma_k(s), \gamma_k(t)) = |s - t|$, since a subarc of a minimising curve is minimising; by continuity of $\exp$ and of $d$,
$d(\gamma(s), \gamma(t)) = |s - t|$.

(2) Let $\sigma_k$ be a minimising unit speed geodesic from $p_k$ to $q_k$. It meets $C$; shift its parameter so that $\sigma_k(0) \in C$, and $\sigma_k$ is defined on
$[-a_k, b_k]$ with $a_k = d(p_k, \sigma_k(0)) \geq d(p_k, C)$ and $b_k \geq d(q_k, C)$, both tending to $\infty$. The unit tangent vectors at points of the compact $C$ form a compact
set, so a subsequence has $\dot\sigma_k(0) \to v \in T_xM$, $x \in C$. As in (1), $\gamma(t) = \exp_x(tv)$ satisfies $d(\gamma(s), \gamma(t)) = |s - t|$ for all $s, t \in \RR$.
\end{proof}

For example, on the flat cylinder $S^{n-1} \times \RR$ the curves $\{x\} \times \RR$ are lines, and (2) applies with $C = S^{n-1} \times \{0\}$. On the hyperbolic plane every
geodesic is a line: it is complete (see the discussion after Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-model-spaces}) with $K = -1$
(Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-conformal-metrics}(1)), so by Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-cartan-hadamard} each $\exp_p$ is a diffeomorphism, so the geodesic from $\gamma(s)$ through $\gamma(t)$ is the only one joining them, and the minimising geodesic given by
Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-hopf-rinow} must be it.

\begin{theorem}[Cheeger--Gromoll splitting theorem]\label{riemannian-geometry:theorem-splitting}
Let $M$ be a complete connected Riemannian manifold with $\mathrm{Ric} \geq 0$ that contains a line. Then $M$ is isometric to a Riemannian product $N \times \RR$, where $N$ is a
complete manifold with $\mathrm{Ric} \geq 0$, and the line corresponds to a curve $\{x\} \times \RR$.
\end{theorem}

This is \cite{cheeger-gromoll-1971}. The proof uses the \emph{Busemann functions} $b^\pm(x) = \lim_{t \to \infty}\bigl(t - d(x, \gamma(\pm t))\bigr)$ of the line $\gamma$. The
triangle inequality gives $b^+ + b^- \leq 0$, with equality on $\gamma$. The condition $\mathrm{Ric} \geq 0$ gives, through the comparison of the Laplacian of distance functions
with the Euclidean one, that $b^+$ and $b^-$ are subharmonic in a weak sense; so $b^+ + b^-$ is subharmonic, and by the maximum principle it vanishes identically. Hence
$b^+ = -b^-$ is harmonic and smooth, with $|\nabla b^+| = 1$, and the Bochner formula $0 = \frac12\Delta|\nabla b^+|^2 = |\mathrm{Hess}\,b^+|^2 + \mathrm{Ric}(\nabla b^+, \nabla b^+)$
(with the analysts' sign of the Laplacian) forces $\mathrm{Hess}\,b^+ = 0$: the gradient of $b^+$ is a parallel unit vector field, and its flow splits off the factor $\RR$.
The same paper shows that the universal covering of a compact manifold with $\mathrm{Ric} \geq 0$ is isometric to $N \times \RR^k$ with $N$ compact.

\begin{corollary}\label{riemannian-geometry:corollary-no-lines}
A complete connected manifold with positive definite Ricci curvature, for instance with $K > 0$, contains no line. In particular it satisfies the hypothesis of
Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-rays-lines}(2) for no compact $C$: outside a large compact set it cannot be separated into two unbounded pieces.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
If it contained a line, it would be isometric to $N \times \RR$ by Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-splitting}. In coordinates $(x, t)$ adapted to the product, the
metric is $g_N + dt^2$, and the Christoffel symbols with an index $t$ vanish (Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-levi-civita}); so $\nabla\partial_t = 0$,
$R(X, \partial_t)\partial_t = 0$ for all $X$, and $\mathrm{Ric}(\partial_t, \partial_t) = 0$, a contradiction. The second statement follows from
Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-rays-lines}(2).
\end{proof}

Both hypotheses of the splitting theorem are needed: the hyperbolic plane contains lines but is not a product, since a product $N \times \RR$ of dimension $2$ is flat, and
the flat cylinder shows that the factor $N$ need not be $\RR^{n-1}$. The paraboloid $z = x^2 + y^2$, which has $K > 0$, contains rays from every point but no line.

\section{Submanifolds and the second fundamental form}\label{riemannian-geometry:section-submanifolds}

Classical differential geometry studies curves and surfaces in $\RR^3$ through two quadratic forms: the \emph{first fundamental form}, the metric induced from
$\RR^3$, and the \emph{second fundamental form}, which measures how the surface bends in the ambient space. This section develops both for a submanifold of
any Riemannian manifold, proves the equations of Gauss and Codazzi that relate them to the curvature, and then specialises to surfaces in $\RR^3$, where Gauss's
equation becomes the Theorema Egregium. The order is: the Gauss formula, which defines the second fundamental form (Subsection~\ref{riemannian-geometry:subsection-gauss-formula}); the shape operator, the derivative of a normal field, and the curvatures of a hypersurface (Subsection~\ref{riemannian-geometry:subsection-shape-operator}); the equations of Gauss and Codazzi (Subsection~\ref{riemannian-geometry:subsection-gauss-codazzi}); totally geodesic and umbilical submanifolds (Subsection~\ref{riemannian-geometry:subsection-umbilical}); formulas in a parametrisation (Subsection~\ref{riemannian-geometry:subsection-coordinates-second-fundamental-form}); other ways of computing (Subsection~\ref{riemannian-geometry:subsection-computing-second-fundamental-form}); and the Gauss map of a hypersurface of Euclidean space, from which classical surface theory reads off all these curvatures (Subsection~\ref{riemannian-geometry:subsection-gauss-map}).

Throughout, $(\overline{M}, \bar{g})$ is a Riemannian manifold with Levi-Civita connection $\overline{\nabla}$ and curvature $\overline{R}$, and
$M \subseteq \overline{M}$ is a submanifold with the induced metric $g = \bar{g}|_{TM}$, the \emph{first fundamental form}; we write $\langle -, - \rangle$ for
both. For $p \in M$, $T_p\overline{M} = T_pM \oplus N_pM$ with $N_pM = (T_pM)^\perp$, the \emph{normal space}, and $v = v^\top + v^\perp$ is the corresponding
decomposition. The normal spaces form the \emph{normal bundle} $NM$, a vector bundle over $M$: near each point it has a smooth frame, obtained by applying
Gram--Schmidt to a frame of $T\overline{M}$ extending one of $TM$. For $\overline{M} = \RR^N$ with the Euclidean metric, $\overline{\nabla}_XY$ is the directional
derivative of the components of $Y$, and $\overline{R} = 0$.

\subsection{The Gauss formula}\label{riemannian-geometry:subsection-gauss-formula}

\begin{lemma}\label{riemannian-geometry:lemma-ambient-derivative}
Every vector field on an open subset of $M$ extends, near each point, to a vector field on an open subset of $\overline{M}$. For vector fields $X$, $Y$ on $M$ and
extensions $\overline{X}$, $\overline{Y}$, the value of $\overline{\nabla}_{\overline{X}}\overline{Y}$ at $p \in M$ depends only on $X$ and $Y$, and we denote it by
$\overline{\nabla}_XY$. Moreover $[\overline{X}, \overline{Y}]$ restricts on $M$ to $[X, Y]$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
In a slice chart $(x^1, \ldots, x^k, y^1, \ldots, y^m)$ of $\overline{M}$, in which $M$ is $\{y = 0\}$, a vector field $\sum_iY^i(x)\partial/\partial x^i$ on $M$ extends by
the same formula, with coefficients independent of $y$. The value of $\overline{\nabla}_{\overline{X}}\overline{Y}$ at $p$ depends only on $\overline{X}_p = X_p$, since a
connection is $C^\infty$-linear in its first argument, and it equals $D_t(\overline{Y} \circ \gamma)(0)$ for any curve $\gamma$ with $\dot\gamma(0) = X_p$
(Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-covariant-derivative-curves}); taking $\gamma$ in $M$, $\overline{Y} \circ \gamma = Y \circ \gamma$. Finally $X$ and
$\overline{X}$ are related by the inclusion $\iota$, and so are $Y$ and $\overline{Y}$, so $[X, Y]$ and $[\overline{X}, \overline{Y}]$ are $\iota$-related
(Lemma~\ref{lie-groups:lemma-related-fields}).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Gauss formula]\label{riemannian-geometry:theorem-gauss-formula}
For vector fields $X$, $Y$ tangent to $M$,
\[
\overline{\nabla}_XY = \nabla_XY + \mathrm{II}(X, Y),
\]
where $\nabla_XY = (\overline{\nabla}_XY)^\top$ is the Levi-Civita connection of $(M, g)$, and $\mathrm{II}(X, Y) = (\overline{\nabla}_XY)^\perp$, the \emph{second fundamental
form}, is a symmetric $C^\infty(M)$-bilinear map with values in the normal bundle.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The operator $\nabla^\top_XY = (\overline{\nabla}_XY)^\top$ is a connection on $TM$: it is $C^\infty$-linear in $X$, and
$\overline{\nabla}_X(fY) = (Xf)Y + f\overline{\nabla}_XY$ with $Y$ tangent gives the Leibniz rule. It is metric: for tangent $Y$ and $Z$,
$X\langle Y, Z\rangle = \langle\overline{\nabla}_XY, Z\rangle + \langle Y, \overline{\nabla}_XZ\rangle = \langle\nabla^\top_XY, Z\rangle + \langle Y, \nabla^\top_XZ\rangle$, as the normal parts are orthogonal to
$Y$ and $Z$. By Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-ambient-derivative} and the torsion-freeness of $\overline{\nabla}$,
$\overline{\nabla}_XY - \overline{\nabla}_YX = [X, Y]$, which is tangent. Its tangent part says that $\nabla^\top$ is torsion-free, so $\nabla^\top = \nabla$ by the uniqueness in
Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-levi-civita}; its normal part says $\mathrm{II}(X, Y) = \mathrm{II}(Y, X)$. Finally $\mathrm{II}$ is $C^\infty$-linear in $X$, and in $Y$
because $\mathrm{II}(X, fY) = ((Xf)Y + f\overline{\nabla}_XY)^\perp = f\,\mathrm{II}(X, Y)$.
\end{proof}

So the intrinsic connection of $M$ is the tangential part of the ambient one. For a submanifold of Euclidean space this is the familiar description of the covariant derivative as
``differentiate in $\RR^N$ and project back onto the tangent space'', which classical surface theory takes as the definition \cite[Chapter 4]{do-carmo-curves-surfaces}.

\begin{corollary}[Covariant derivative as tangential projection]\label{riemannian-geometry:corollary-tangential-projection}
Let $M \subseteq \RR^N$ be a submanifold with the induced metric, and for $p \in M$ let $P_p \colon \RR^N \to T_pM$ be the orthogonal projection. Regard vector fields on $M$, and vector fields along
curves in $M$, as $\RR^N$-valued functions.
\begin{enumerate}
\item For vector fields $X$, $Y$ on $M$, $\nabla_XY = P(X(Y))$, where $X(Y) = dY(X)$ is the derivative of the components of $Y$ in the direction $X$. So the Levi-Civita connection of $M$ is the
projection connection of Example~\ref{connections:example-connections}(4).
\item For a curve $\gamma \colon I \to M$ and a vector field $V$ along $\gamma$, $D_tV = P(V^{\prime}(t))$, the projection of the ordinary derivative of $V \colon I \to \RR^N$ onto $T_{\gamma(t)}M$.
\item $V$ is parallel along $\gamma$ if and only if $V^{\prime}(t) \perp T_{\gamma(t)}M$ for all $t$, and $\gamma$ is a geodesic if and only if its acceleration $\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t)$ is normal to $M$ for all $t$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
(1) In $\RR^N$ with the Euclidean metric all Christoffel symbols vanish, so $\overline{\nabla}_{\overline{X}}\overline{Y}$ is the derivative of the components of $\overline{Y}$ in the direction $\overline{X}$. At a point
$p \in M$ the direction $\overline{X}_p = X_p$ is tangent to $M$, so this derivative only involves the values of $\overline{Y}$ along a curve in $M$ with velocity $X_p$, where $\overline{Y} = Y$; hence
$\overline{\nabla}_XY = X(Y)$, and the Gauss formula (Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-gauss-formula}) gives $\nabla_XY = (X(Y))^\top = P(X(Y))$. The projection connection of the subbundle
$TM \subseteq M \times \RR^N$ is $\nabla_Xs = P(X(s))$ by definition.

(2) The operator $V \mapsto P(V^{\prime})$ sends vector fields along $\gamma$ to vector fields along $\gamma$ and is $\RR$-linear. It satisfies the Leibniz rule: $(fV)^{\prime} = f^{\prime}V + fV^{\prime}$, and $P(V) = V$
because $V(t)$ is tangent, so $P((fV)^{\prime}) = f^{\prime}V + fP(V^{\prime})$. For $V = Y \circ \gamma$, with $Y$ a vector field near $\gamma(I)$, the chain rule gives $(Y \circ \gamma)^{\prime}(t) = dY(\dot\gamma(t))$, the derivative of
$Y$ in the direction $\dot\gamma(t)$, so $P((Y \circ \gamma)^{\prime}) = \nabla_{\dot\gamma}Y$ by (1), whose value at a point depends only on the direction there. By the uniqueness in
Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-covariant-derivative-curves}, $P(V^{\prime}) = D_tV$.

(3) $D_tV = 0$ means $P(V^{\prime}) = 0$, that is, $V^{\prime}$ is normal; for $V = \dot\gamma$ this is the statement about geodesics.
\end{proof}

The formula $P(X(Y))$ looks extrinsic, since it uses the embedding; the point of the Levi-Civita theorem is that the result only depends on the metric of $M$. In classical surface theory one
defines the covariant derivative by the projection and then discovers, by writing it with the Christoffel symbols of the first fundamental form, that it is intrinsic; here the logic runs the other way,
and the corollary is a computation. The part of the acceleration that is thrown away is the second fundamental form: for a curve $\gamma$ in $M$,
\[
\gamma^{\prime\prime} = D_t\dot\gamma + \mathrm{II}(\dot\gamma, \dot\gamma),
\]
the acceleration measured inside $M$ plus the normal acceleration forced on the curve by the bending of $M$ (Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-gauss-formula}, applied along $\gamma$ as in (2)). A geodesic
is a curve with no acceleration inside $M$; it is accelerated only as much as is needed to stay on $M$. The same argument, with the covariant derivative $\overline{D}_t$ of $\overline{M}$ in place of
the ordinary derivative, shows for a submanifold of any Riemannian manifold $\overline{M}$ that $D_tV = (\overline{D}_tV)^\top$, and that a curve in $M$ is a geodesic of $M$ exactly when its acceleration in
$\overline{M}$ is normal to $M$.

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-tangential-projection}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Circles of latitude.} On the unit sphere $S^2$, with normal $\nu(x) = x$, let $\gamma(t) = (s\cos t, s\sin t, c)$ with $s = \sin\vartheta_0 > 0$ and $c = \cos\vartheta_0$. Then $\gamma^{\prime\prime} = (-s\cos t, -s\sin t, 0)$,
$\langle\gamma^{\prime\prime}, \gamma\rangle = -s^2$, and
\[
D_t\dot\gamma = \gamma^{\prime\prime} - \langle\gamma^{\prime\prime}, \gamma\rangle\gamma = (-sc^2\cos t, -sc^2\sin t, s^2c) = sc\,(-c\cos t, -c\sin t, s) .
\]
The vector $(-c\cos t, -c\sin t, s)$ is the unit tangent to the meridian pointing north. So $D_t\dot\gamma = 0$ only for $c = 0$: among the circles of latitude only the equator is a geodesic. The others curve
towards the nearer pole inside the sphere, with geodesic curvature $|D_t\dot\gamma|/|\dot\gamma|^2 = |c|/s = |\cot\vartheta_0|$, in accordance with
Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-geodesic-curvature} and the value given after it.
\item \emph{Great circles.} For $\gamma(t) = \cos(t)p + \sin(t)v$ with $p \perp v$ unit vectors, $\gamma^{\prime\prime} = -\gamma$ is normal to the sphere at $\gamma(t)$, so great circles are geodesics; by uniqueness of
geodesics with given initial velocity they are all of them (Exercise~\ref{riemannian-geometry:exercise-great-circles}).
\item \emph{The cylinder} $C = \{x^2 + y^2 = 1\} \subseteq \RR^3$, with normal $\nu = (x, y, 0)$. The helix $\gamma(t) = (\cos(at + t_0), \sin(at + t_0), bt + z_0)$ has $\gamma^{\prime\prime} = -a^2(\cos(at + t_0), \sin(at + t_0), 0)$, a
multiple of $\nu$; so helices are geodesics, including the horizontal circles ($b = 0$) and the vertical lines ($a = 0$). Every geodesic is of this form
(Exercise~\ref{riemannian-geometry:exercise-cylinder-geodesics}). Unrolling the cylinder onto the plane, which is a local isometry, the helices become straight lines.
\item \emph{Parallel transport} along a circle of latitude, which Example~\ref{connections:example-transport} computed with connection forms, is the solution of the linear equation $V^{\prime} \perp T_\gamma S^2$,
that is $V^{\prime} = \langle V^{\prime}, \gamma\rangle\gamma = -\langle V, \gamma^{\prime}\rangle\gamma$ (differentiate $\langle V, \gamma\rangle = 0$): the vector turns only as much as needed to remain tangent to the sphere.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

The Gauss formula does not need the ambient extensions of Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-ambient-derivative}, and it holds for immersions that are not injective, such
as a curve with self-intersections. Let $f \colon M \to \overline{M}$ be an immersion with the induced metric $g = f^*\bar g$, and let $\overline{\nabla}^f = f^*\overline{\nabla}$ be the
pullback connection on $f^*T\overline{M}$ (Proposition~\ref{connections:proposition-pullback-tensor}(1)): it differentiates sections of $f^*T\overline{M}$, such as $df(Y)$ for a
vector field $Y$ on $M$, in the direction of tangent vectors of $M$, and $\overline{\nabla}^f_X(s \circ f) = \overline{\nabla}_{df(X)}s$ for a vector field $s$ on $\overline{M}$.
The differential identifies $TM$ with the subbundle $df(TM)$ of $f^*T\overline{M}$, whose orthogonal complement $N_f$ is the normal bundle of $f$.

One often writes $\overline{\nabla}_{df(X)}(df(Y))$ for $\overline{\nabla}^f_X(df(Y))$. This is justified by
Remark~\ref{connections:remark-along-curves}: if near $p$ the section $df(Y)$ is of the form $\overline{Y} \circ f$ for a vector field $\overline{Y}$ on an open set of
$\overline{M}$, then $\overline{\nabla}^f_X(df(Y)) = \overline{\nabla}_{df(X)}\overline{Y}$, whatever the extension $\overline{Y}$; for an immersion such extensions exist near
every point, since $f$ is an embedding near each point. But $df(Y)$ itself is not a vector field on $\overline{M}$: it is defined only along $f$, and at a self-intersection
$f(p) = f(q)$ it has two values. For maps that are not immersions the abbreviation is even misleading: for a constant curve $c$ and a nonconstant field $V$ along it,
$\dot c = 0$ but $(c^*\overline{\nabla})_{d/dt}V = \dot V \neq 0$.

\begin{proposition}[Gauss formula for immersions]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-gauss-immersion}
For vector fields $X$, $Y$ on $M$,
\[
\overline{\nabla}^f_X\bigl(df(Y)\bigr) = df(\nabla_XY) + \mathrm{II}(X, Y),
\]
where $\nabla$ is the Levi-Civita connection of $g$ and $\mathrm{II}(X, Y) = (\overline{\nabla}^f_X(df(Y)))^\perp$ is a symmetric $C^\infty(M)$-bilinear map with values in $N_f$.
In particular $df(\nabla_XY) = (\overline{\nabla}^f_X(df(Y)))^\top$. For an embedding this is Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-gauss-formula}, and for
$\overline{M} = \RR^N$, $\overline{\nabla}^f_X(df(Y))$ is the derivative $X(df(Y))$ of the $\RR^N$-valued function $df(Y)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
\emph{$\overline{\nabla}^f$ is metric:} $X\langle\sigma, \tau\rangle = \langle\overline{\nabla}^f_X\sigma, \tau\rangle + \langle\sigma, \overline{\nabla}^f_X\tau\rangle$ for sections
$\sigma$, $\tau$ of $f^*T\overline{M}$. Both sides satisfy the same Leibniz rule when $\sigma$ or $\tau$ is multiplied by a function, and locally $f^*T\overline{M}$ has frames
$s_a \circ f$ pulled back from frames of $T\overline{M}$; for $\sigma = s \circ f$ and $\tau = t \circ f$ the identity is
$df(X)\langle s, t\rangle = \langle\overline{\nabla}_{df(X)}s, t\rangle + \langle s, \overline{\nabla}_{df(X)}t\rangle$ at $f(p)$.

\emph{Symmetry:} $\overline{\nabla}^f_X(df(Y)) - \overline{\nabla}^f_Y(df(X)) = df([X, Y])$. The difference of the two sides is $C^\infty(M)$-bilinear in $X$ and $Y$ (the terms with
derivatives of a function cancel, as for the torsion), so it suffices to take coordinate fields $X = \partial_i$, $Y = \partial_j$ of a chart $u$ of $M$. With a chart $y$ of
$\overline{M}$ and $f^a = y^a \circ f$, $df(\partial_j) = \sum_a\partial_jf^a\,(\partial_a \circ f)$ and
\[
\overline{\nabla}^f_{\partial_i}\bigl(df(\partial_j)\bigr) = \sum_a\Bigl(\partial_i\partial_jf^a + \sum_{b,c}\overline{\Gamma}^a_{bc}(f)\,\partial_if^b\,\partial_jf^c\Bigr)(\partial_a \circ f),
\]
which is symmetric in $i$ and $j$ since $\overline{\Gamma}^a_{bc} = \overline{\Gamma}^a_{cb}$; and $[\partial_i, \partial_j] = 0$.

\emph{Conclusion:} as in the proof of Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-gauss-formula}, $\nabla^{\prime}_XY = df^{-1}\bigl((\overline{\nabla}^f_X(df(Y)))^\top\bigr)$ is a connection
on $TM$; it is metric for $g$ because $\overline{\nabla}^f$ is metric and $N_f \perp df(TM)$, and torsion-free by the tangential part of the symmetry. So $\nabla^{\prime} = \nabla$
(Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-levi-civita}); the normal part of the symmetry gives $\mathrm{II}(X, Y) = \mathrm{II}(Y, X)$, and $\mathrm{II}$ is $C^\infty$-linear in $X$ and
in $Y$ as before. For an embedding and an extension $\overline{Y}$ of $Y$, $df(Y) = \overline{Y} \circ f$, so $\overline{\nabla}^f_X(df(Y)) = \overline{\nabla}_X\overline{Y}$. For
$\overline{M} = \RR^N$ the Christoffel symbols vanish.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The shape operator and the curvatures of a hypersurface}\label{riemannian-geometry:subsection-shape-operator}

\begin{definition}\label{riemannian-geometry:definition-shape-operator}
For a normal vector field $\nu$ along $M$, the \emph{shape operator} (or \emph{Weingarten map}) is $S_\nu(X) = -(\overline{\nabla}_X\nu)^\top$, and the \emph{normal
connection} is $\nabla^\perp_X\nu = (\overline{\nabla}_X\nu)^\perp$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Weingarten equation]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-weingarten}
For tangent $X$, $Y$ and normal $\nu$, $\langle S_\nu X, Y\rangle = \langle\mathrm{II}(X, Y), \nu\rangle$. In particular $S_\nu$ is self-adjoint on each tangent space, and
$\overline{\nabla}_X\nu = -S_\nu X + \nabla^\perp_X\nu$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Differentiate $\langle Y, \nu\rangle = 0$: $0 = \langle\overline{\nabla}_XY, \nu\rangle + \langle Y, \overline{\nabla}_X\nu\rangle = \langle\mathrm{II}(X, Y), \nu\rangle - \langle Y, S_\nu X\rangle$. Self-adjointness
follows from the symmetry of $\mathrm{II}$, and the last formula is the decomposition of $\overline{\nabla}_X\nu$ into its two parts.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}\label{riemannian-geometry:definition-hypersurface-curvatures}
Let $M$ be a hypersurface ($\dim\overline{M} = \dim M + 1$) with a unit normal field $\nu$, which exists locally, and globally if $M$ and $\overline{M}$ are orientable.
The \emph{scalar second fundamental form} is $h(X, Y) = \langle\mathrm{II}(X, Y), \nu\rangle$, so $\mathrm{II} = h\,\nu$, and the shape operator $S = S_\nu$ satisfies
$\langle SX, Y\rangle = h(X, Y)$. The eigenvalues $\kappa_1, \ldots, \kappa_n$ of the self-adjoint map $S$ on $T_pM$ are the \emph{principal curvatures}, its eigenvectors the
\emph{principal directions}, $H = \frac1n\operatorname{tr}S$ is the \emph{mean curvature}, and $\det S = \kappa_1 \cdots \kappa_n$ the \emph{Gauss--Kronecker
curvature}. Changing $\nu$ to $-\nu$ changes the signs of $h$, $S$, the $\kappa_i$ and $H$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Derivative of the unit normal]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-normal-derivative}
Let $M \subseteq \overline{M}$ be a hypersurface with unit normal field $\nu$, and $v, w \in T_pM$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\overline{\nabla}_v\nu$ is tangent to $M$. So $\nabla^\perp\nu = 0$, and the shape operator is the derivative of the normal itself, without projection: $S v = -\overline{\nabla}_v\nu$.
\item $h(v, w) = \langle\mathrm{II}(v, w), \nu\rangle = -\langle\overline{\nabla}_v\nu, w\rangle$. For $\overline{M} = \RR^{n+1}$, $\overline{\nabla}_v\nu = d\nu(v)$ is the derivative of $\nu$ regarded as a map $M \to \RR^{n+1}$, so
$h(v, w) = -\langle d\nu(v), w\rangle$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(1) Extend $\nu$ to a vector field near $p$, with coefficients independent of the normal coordinates of a slice chart, as in the proof of Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-ambient-derivative}; as there, $\overline{\nabla}_v\nu$ does not depend on the extension. Since $\overline{\nabla}$ is metric and $\langle\nu, \nu\rangle = 1$ on $M$,
$0 = v\langle\nu, \nu\rangle = 2\langle\overline{\nabla}_v\nu, \nu\rangle$. The normal space at $p$ is the line $\RR\nu(p)$, so the normal part of $\overline{\nabla}_v\nu$, which is $\langle\overline{\nabla}_v\nu, \nu\rangle\nu$, vanishes.
Hence $\nabla^\perp_v\nu = 0$ and $S v = -(\overline{\nabla}_v\nu)^\top = -\overline{\nabla}_v\nu$ (Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-shape-operator}).

(2) By the Weingarten equation (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-weingarten}) and (1), $h(v, w) = \langle\mathrm{II}(v, w), \nu\rangle = \langle S v, w\rangle = -\langle\overline{\nabla}_v\nu, w\rangle$. In $\RR^{n+1}$ the
Levi-Civita connection differentiates components, and along a curve in $M$ with velocity $v$ only the values of $\nu$ on $M$ enter.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-normal-derivative}
\emph{Reading the formula.} Many texts define the second fundamental form of a hypersurface directly as
\[
\mathrm{II}(v, w) = \langle\overline{\nabla}_vn, w\rangle ,
\]
for a unit normal $n$: tilt the normal in the direction $v$ and measure how much of the change points in the direction $w$. By the proposition this is our $h$ for the normal $\nu = -n$, or $-h$ for $\nu = n$; the sign is a
convention, and its effect is discussed in Remark~\ref{riemannian-geometry:remark-sign-conventions} below. Part (1) explains why such a definition makes sense: because $n$ has constant length, its derivative has no component
along $n$, and in a hypersurface it is therefore a tangent vector, so pairing it with tangent vectors loses nothing. The symmetry of this bilinear form is not obvious from the formula; it comes from the symmetry of
$\mathrm{II}$, that is, from the torsion-freeness of $\overline{\nabla}$ (Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-gauss-formula}).

In codimension $\geq 2$ the first part fails: a unit normal field $\nu$ still has $\langle\overline{\nabla}_v\nu, \nu\rangle = 0$, but $\overline{\nabla}_v\nu$ can have a component along the other normal directions, which is the normal connection
$\nabla^\perp_v\nu$. For example, along the unit circle $\gamma(t) = (\cos t, \sin t, 0)$ in $\RR^3$ the normal space is spanned by $r(t) = (\cos t, \sin t, 0)$ and $e_3$. For the unit normal field
$\nu(t) = \cos\phi(t)\,e_3 + \sin\phi(t)\,r(t)$, using $r^{\prime} = \dot\gamma$,
\[
\nu^{\prime} = \sin\phi\,\dot\gamma + \phi^{\prime}\bigl(\cos\phi\,r - \sin\phi\,e_3\bigr),
\]
so $S_\nu(\dot\gamma) = -\sin\phi\,\dot\gamma$, while $\nabla^\perp_{\dot\gamma}\nu = \phi^{\prime}(\cos\phi\,r - \sin\phi\,e_3)$ is nonzero as soon as $\nu$ rotates in the normal plane. Only the tangential part enters the second fundamental
form, through the Weingarten equation $\langle S_\nu X, Y\rangle = \langle\mathrm{II}(X, Y), \nu\rangle$.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-second-fundamental-form-notation}
Notations for the second fundamental form vary, and it may help to collect them. Many texts call the scalar form $h$ of a hypersurface itself the second fundamental form, write it $\mathrm{II}$, and
write $n$ for the unit normal; the choice of the unit normal is also called a \emph{co-orientation}. For tangent vectors $v$, $w$ at $p$, a tangent vector field $W$ extending $w$ and a unit normal field $\nu$,
\[
h(v, w) = \langle\mathrm{II}(v, w), \nu\rangle = \langle\overline{\nabla}_vW, \nu\rangle = -\langle\overline{\nabla}_v\nu, w\rangle = \langle S_\nu v, w\rangle .
\]
The first equality is the definition of $h$; the second is the Gauss formula (Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-gauss-formula}), as $\mathrm{II}(v, w)$ is the normal part of $\overline{\nabla}_vW$; the third comes from
differentiating $\langle W, \nu\rangle = 0$; and the last is the Weingarten equation (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-weingarten}). The symmetry $h(v, w) = h(w, v)$ comes from the torsion-freeness of
$\overline{\nabla}$, through the Gauss formula. Changing the co-orientation $\nu$ to $-\nu$ changes the signs of $h$ and $S_\nu$; for a hypersurface of an oriented manifold, a co-orientation is the same as an
orientation (Proposition~\ref{differential-forms:proposition-orientation-normal}).

For $M \subseteq \RR^{n+1}$, $\overline{\nabla}_v\nu = d\nu(v)$ is the derivative of the \emph{Gauss map} $\nu \colon M \to S^n$, regarded as an $\RR^{n+1}$-valued function on $M$, so that $d\nu$ is an
$\RR^{n+1}$-valued $1$-form (a $1$-form with values in the trivial bundle $M \times \RR^{n+1}$, Definition~\ref{connections:definition-bundle-valued-forms}). It is tangent to $M$, since
$\langle d\nu(v), \nu\rangle = \frac12v(|\nu|^2) = 0$; hence $h(v, w) = -\langle d\nu(v), w\rangle$ and $S = -d\nu$, the form in which classical surface theory states it
(Remark~\ref{riemannian-geometry:remark-sign-conventions}).

In higher codimension there is no preferred normal, and one keeps the vector-valued form $\mathrm{II}(v, w) = (\overline{\nabla}_vW)^\perp$, with values in the normal space. With $R(u, v, w, z) = \langle R(u, v)w, z\rangle$, the
Gauss equation of Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-gauss-codazzi}(1) reads
\[
\langle R(u, v)w, z\rangle = \langle\overline{R}(u, v)w, z\rangle + \langle\mathrm{II}(u, z), \mathrm{II}(v, w)\rangle - \langle\mathrm{II}(u, w), \mathrm{II}(v, z)\rangle,
\]
and for a hypersurface of $\RR^{n+1}$, where $\overline{R} = 0$ and $\mathrm{II} = h\,\nu$, it becomes $\langle R(u, v)w, z\rangle = h(u, z)h(v, w) - h(u, w)h(v, z)$
(Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-theorema-egregium} below), the generalisation of Gauss's Theorema Egregium. These are the formulas found in most references, with $\mathrm{II}$ in place of $h$ in the
last one.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The equations of Gauss and Codazzi}\label{riemannian-geometry:subsection-gauss-codazzi}

\begin{theorem}[Gauss and Codazzi equations]\label{riemannian-geometry:theorem-gauss-codazzi}
For vector fields $X, Y, Z, W$ tangent to $M$:
\begin{enumerate}
\item (Gauss) $R(X, Y, Z, W) = \overline{R}(X, Y, Z, W) + \langle\mathrm{II}(Y, Z), \mathrm{II}(X, W)\rangle - \langle\mathrm{II}(X, Z), \mathrm{II}(Y, W)\rangle$;
\item (Codazzi) $(\overline{R}(X, Y)Z)^\perp = (\nabla_X\mathrm{II})(Y, Z) - (\nabla_Y\mathrm{II})(X, Z)$, where
$(\nabla_X\mathrm{II})(Y, Z) = \nabla^\perp_X(\mathrm{II}(Y, Z)) - \mathrm{II}(\nabla_XY, Z) - \mathrm{II}(Y, \nabla_XZ)$.
\end{enumerate}
In particular, for orthonormal $u, v \in T_pM$ the sectional curvatures of the plane they span satisfy
$K(u, v) = \overline{K}(u, v) + \langle\mathrm{II}(u, u), \mathrm{II}(v, v)\rangle - |\mathrm{II}(u, v)|^2$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By the Gauss formula, $\overline{\nabla}_X\overline{\nabla}_YZ = \overline{\nabla}_X(\nabla_YZ) + \overline{\nabla}_X(\mathrm{II}(Y, Z))$, and applying the Gauss formula again to the tangent field
$\nabla_YZ$ and the Weingarten equation to the normal field $\mathrm{II}(Y, Z)$,
\[
\overline{\nabla}_X\overline{\nabla}_YZ = \nabla_X\nabla_YZ + \mathrm{II}(X, \nabla_YZ) - S_{\mathrm{II}(Y, Z)}X + \nabla^\perp_X(\mathrm{II}(Y, Z)).
\]
Also $\overline{\nabla}_{[X,Y]}Z = \nabla_{[X,Y]}Z + \mathrm{II}([X, Y], Z)$. Subtract the same expressions with $X$ and $Y$ exchanged, and use $[X, Y] = \nabla_XY - \nabla_YX$.
The tangential part gives $(\overline{R}(X, Y)Z)^\top = R(X, Y)Z - S_{\mathrm{II}(Y, Z)}X + S_{\mathrm{II}(X, Z)}Y$; pairing with $W$ and using the Weingarten equation gives (1). The
normal part gives
\[
(\overline{R}(X, Y)Z)^\perp = \nabla^\perp_X(\mathrm{II}(Y, Z)) + \mathrm{II}(X, \nabla_YZ) - \nabla^\perp_Y(\mathrm{II}(X, Z)) - \mathrm{II}(Y, \nabla_XZ) - \mathrm{II}(\nabla_XY - \nabla_YX, Z),
\]
which is (2) after regrouping. For the last statement take $X = W = u$ and $Y = Z = v$ in (1), recalling $K(u, v) = R(u, v, v, u)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Theorema Egregium]\label{riemannian-geometry:corollary-theorema-egregium}
Let $M^n \subseteq \RR^{n+1}$ be a hypersurface with unit normal $\nu$. Then
$R(X, Y, Z, W) = h(Y, Z)h(X, W) - h(X, Z)h(Y, W)$, and for orthonormal principal directions $e_i$, $e_j$ the sectional curvature is $K(e_i, e_j) = \kappa_i\kappa_j$. In
particular, for a surface in $\RR^3$ the Gaussian curvature is $K = \kappa_1\kappa_2 = \det S$: a quantity defined through the embedding is determined by the metric
alone, and so is preserved by isometries between surfaces.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Apply Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-gauss-codazzi}(1) with $\overline{R} = 0$ and $\mathrm{II} = h\nu$, $|\nu| = 1$. For principal directions,
$h(e_i, e_i) = \kappa_i$ and $h(e_i, e_j) = 0$ for $i \neq j$. For a surface the only plane is the tangent plane, and $\kappa_1\kappa_2$ is the determinant of $S$.
\end{proof}

Codazzi's equation for a hypersurface of $\RR^{n+1}$ says that $(\nabla_Xh)(Y, Z)$ is symmetric in all three arguments. Gauss's and Codazzi's equations are the
integrability conditions for the embedding: by Bonnet's theorem, a metric and a symmetric form on a simply connected surface satisfying them come from a surface
in $\RR^3$, unique up to rigid motions \cite[Chapter 4]{do-carmo-curves-surfaces}.

\subsection{Totally geodesic and totally umbilical submanifolds}\label{riemannian-geometry:subsection-umbilical}

\begin{definition}\label{riemannian-geometry:definition-umbilical}
A submanifold $M \subseteq \overline{M}$ is \emph{totally geodesic} if $\mathrm{II} = 0$, and \emph{totally umbilical} if $\mathrm{II}(X, Y) = g(X, Y)\,\eta$ for a normal vector field $\eta$; for a hypersurface
the second condition says $S = \lambda\,\id$, all principal curvatures being equal at each point. A point where $S$ is a multiple of the identity is an \emph{umbilic}.
\end{definition}

By the Gauss formula (Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-gauss-formula}), $M$ is totally geodesic if and only if every geodesic of $M$ is a geodesic of $\overline{M}$: a curve in $M$ has
$\overline{\nabla}_{\dot\gamma}\dot\gamma = \nabla_{\dot\gamma}\dot\gamma + \mathrm{II}(\dot\gamma, \dot\gamma)$, and $\mathrm{II}$ is determined by its values $\mathrm{II}(v, v)$ by polarisation.

\begin{proposition}\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-umbilical}
\begin{enumerate}
\item Affine hyperplanes of $\RR^{n+1}$ are totally geodesic, and spheres of radius $r$ are totally umbilical with $S = -\frac1r\id$ for the outward normal.
\item A connected totally umbilical hypersurface $M \subseteq \RR^{n+1}$, $n \geq 2$, is an open subset of a hyperplane or of a sphere.
\item In the half-space model of hyperbolic space, $|dx|^2/x_n^2$, the \emph{horospheres} $\{x_n = c\}$ are totally umbilical, with $S = \id$ for the normal $\nu = x_n\partial_n$; their induced metric
$|dx^{\prime}|^2/c^2$ is flat. The vertical hyperplanes $\{x_1 = c\}$ are totally geodesic.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(1) For a hyperplane the unit normal is constant, so $S = -d\nu = 0$. For the sphere $|x| = r$, $\nu(x) = x/r$, so $d\nu = \frac1r\id$ and $S = -\frac1r\id$
(Remark~\ref{riemannian-geometry:remark-second-fundamental-form-notation}).

(2) Let $S = \lambda\id$ with $\lambda$ a smooth function, so $d\nu(X) = -\lambda X$ for tangent $X$. For coordinate fields $X = \partial_i$, $Y = \partial_j$ of a parametrisation $x$,
$\partial_i\partial_j\nu = -\partial_i(\lambda\,\partial_jx) = -(\partial_i\lambda)\partial_jx - \lambda\partial_i\partial_jx$; exchanging $i$ and $j$ and subtracting gives
$(\partial_i\lambda)\partial_jx = (\partial_j\lambda)\partial_ix$. For $i \neq j$ the vectors $\partial_ix$, $\partial_jx$ are independent ($n \geq 2$), so $\partial_i\lambda = 0$: $\lambda$ is locally constant,
hence constant on the connected $M$. If $\lambda = 0$, $\nu$ is constant and $\langle x, \nu\rangle$ is constant (its differential is $\langle dx, \nu\rangle = 0$): $M$ lies in a hyperplane. If $\lambda \neq 0$,
$d(\nu + \lambda x) = -\lambda\,dx + \lambda\,dx = 0$, so $\nu + \lambda x = \lambda c$ is constant and $|x - c| = |\nu|/|\lambda| = 1/|\lambda|$: $M$ lies in a sphere. In both cases $M$ is contained in a hypersurface $N$, and the inclusion
$M \to N$ is smooth (Proposition~\ref{smooth-manifolds:proposition-restrict-codomain}) and an injective immersion between manifolds of the same dimension, hence open by the inverse function theorem.

(3) With $g_{ij} = \delta_{ij}x_n^{-2}$, the formula of Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-levi-civita} gives, for $i, j < n$, $\Gamma^n_{ij} = \frac12x_n^2(-\partial_ng_{ij}) = \delta_{ij}/x_n$ and
$\Gamma^k_{ij} = 0$ for $k < n$. So $\nabla_{\partial_i}\partial_j = \delta_{ij}x_n^{-1}\partial_n$ is normal to the horosphere: $\mathrm{II}(\partial_i, \partial_j) = \delta_{ij}x_n^{-1}\partial_n$, and with the unit
normal $\nu = x_n\partial_n$, $h(\partial_i, \partial_j) = \delta_{ij}x_n^{-1}x_ng(\partial_n, \partial_n) = \delta_{ij}x_n^{-2} = g(\partial_i, \partial_j)$. So $h = g$ and $S = \id$. The induced metric on
$x_n = c$ is $|dx^{\prime}|^2/c^2$, a constant multiple of the Euclidean one. For $\{x_1 = c\}$, the reflection $x_1 \mapsto 2c - x_1$ is an isometry fixing it pointwise; it maps a geodesic of
hyperbolic space tangent to the hyperplane to a geodesic with the same initial data, hence to itself, so such geodesics stay in the hyperplane, which is therefore totally geodesic.
\end{proof}

The horospheres illustrate the Gauss equation: an umbilical surface with principal curvatures $1$ in a space of curvature $-1$ has $K = -1 + 1 \cdot 1 = 0$ (Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-gauss-codazzi}),
and indeed it is flat. In $\RR^3$ an umbilical surface has $K = \lambda^2 \geq 0$, by the Theorema Egregium (Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-theorema-egregium}).

\subsection{Surfaces and hypersurfaces in coordinates}\label{riemannian-geometry:subsection-coordinates-second-fundamental-form}

\begin{proposition}[Surfaces in coordinates]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-surface-coordinates}
Let $x \colon \Omega \to \RR^3$ be a parametrisation of a surface $S$ (Remark~\ref{smooth-manifolds:remark-do-carmo}), with coordinates $(u^1, u^2) = (u, v)$,
$x_i = \partial x/\partial u^i$, and unit normal $\nu = x_1 \times x_2/|x_1 \times x_2|$. Let
\[
g_{ij} = \langle x_i, x_j\rangle = \begin{pmatrix} E & F\\ F & G\end{pmatrix}, \qquad h_{ij} = \langle\partial_i\partial_jx, \nu\rangle = -\langle x_i, \partial_j\nu\rangle.
\]
Then the first and second fundamental forms are $E\,du^2 + 2F\,du\,dv + G\,dv^2$ and $h_{11}\,du^2 + 2h_{12}\,du\,dv + h_{22}\,dv^2$, the matrix of $S$ in the basis
$(x_1, x_2)$ is $(g_{ij})^{-1}(h_{ij})$, and
\[
K = \frac{h_{11}h_{22} - h_{12}^2}{EG - F^2}, \qquad H = \frac{Eh_{22} - 2Fh_{12} + Gh_{11}}{2(EG - F^2)}.
\]
More generally, for a submanifold of $\RR^N$ parametrised by $x(u^1, \ldots, u^n)$, the decomposition
$\partial_i\partial_jx = \Gamma^k_{ij}x_k + \mathrm{II}(x_i, x_j)$ of the second derivatives into tangent and normal parts gives the Christoffel symbols and the second
fundamental form, and $R_{ijkl} = R(x_i, x_j, x_k, x_l) = \langle\mathrm{II}_{jk}, \mathrm{II}_{il}\rangle - \langle\mathrm{II}_{ik}, \mathrm{II}_{jl}\rangle$ with
$\mathrm{II}_{ij} = \mathrm{II}(x_i, x_j)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
In $\RR^N$, $\overline{\nabla}$ is the directional derivative, and the coordinate field $\partial/\partial u^j$ is $x_j$ along $S$, so
$\overline{\nabla}_{x_i}x_j = \partial_i\partial_jx$. The Gauss formula splits it as $\nabla_{x_i}x_j + \mathrm{II}(x_i, x_j)$, which gives the general statement, the tangent part
being $\Gamma^k_{ij}x_k$, and for surfaces $h_{ij} = \langle\partial_i\partial_jx, \nu\rangle$; differentiating $\langle x_i, \nu\rangle = 0$ gives the second expression. From
$\langle Sx_i, x_j\rangle = h_{ij}$, the matrix $A$ of $S$ satisfies $(g_{ij})A = (h_{ij})$. So $K = \det S = \det(h_{ij})/\det(g_{ij})$ and
$2H = \operatorname{tr}((g_{ij})^{-1}(h_{ij}))$, which are the formulas stated, using $(g_{ij})^{-1} = \frac{1}{EG - F^2}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix} G & -F\\ -F & E\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$. The
formula for $R_{ijkl}$ is Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-gauss-codazzi}(1) with $\overline{R} = 0$.
\end{proof}

For a hypersurface of $\RR^{n+1}$ the unit normal is a map $\nu \colon M \to S^n$, the \emph{Gauss map}, and since $T_{\nu(p)}S^n = \nu(p)^\perp = T_pM$ its
differential is an endomorphism of $T_pM$. The shape operator is \emph{minus} this differential, and the next proposition writes the relation between the matrices
of $d\nu$, of the first fundamental form and of the second in coordinates.

\begin{proposition}[Weingarten equations in coordinates]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-weingarten-coordinates}
Let $M \subseteq \RR^{n+1}$ be a hypersurface with unit normal $\nu$ and shape operator $S = S_\nu$, parametrised by $x(u^1, \ldots, u^n)$, with $x_i = \partial x/\partial u^i$,
$g_{ij} = \langle x_i, x_j\rangle$, $h_{ij} = h(x_i, x_j) = \langle\partial_i\partial_jx, \nu\rangle$, and $(g^{ij})$ the inverse matrix of $(g_{ij})$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item $d\nu = -S$: the derivative $\partial_i\nu = \partial(\nu \circ x)/\partial u^i$ is tangent to $M$ and equals $-S(x_i)$.
\item Write $\partial_i\nu = \sum_ka_{ki}\,x_k$, so that $A = (a_{ki})$ is the matrix of $d\nu$ in the basis $(x_1, \ldots, x_n)$. Then
\[
h_{ij} = -\sum_{k=1}^na_{ki}\,g_{kj}, \qquad\text{that is,}\qquad (h_{ij}) = -A^T(g_{ij}), \qquad A = -(g_{ij})^{-1}(h_{ij}), \qquad a_{ki} = -\sum_lg^{kl}h_{li},
\]
and the matrix of $S$ in the same basis is $-A = (g_{ij})^{-1}(h_{ij})$.
\item The Gauss--Kronecker curvature is $\det S = \det(h_{ij})/\det(g_{ij})$, and $\det(d\nu) = (-1)^n\det(h_{ij})/\det(g_{ij})$.
\item For a surface in $\RR^3$, with $E, F, G$ as in Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-surface-coordinates} and $\Delta = EG - F^2$,
\[
a_{11} = \frac{Fh_{12} - Gh_{11}}{\Delta}, \quad a_{12} = \frac{Fh_{22} - Gh_{12}}{\Delta}, \quad a_{21} = \frac{Fh_{11} - Eh_{12}}{\Delta}, \quad
a_{22} = \frac{Fh_{12} - Eh_{22}}{\Delta},
\]
and $K = \det(d\nu) = \det S = (h_{11}h_{22} - h_{12}^2)/\Delta$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(1) Differentiating $\langle\nu, \nu\rangle = 1$ gives $\langle\partial_i\nu, \nu\rangle = 0$, so $\partial_i\nu$ is tangent. In $\RR^{n+1}$, $\overline{\nabla}_{x_i}\nu$ is the
derivative $\partial_i\nu$ of the components of $\nu$, and by Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-shape-operator},
$S(x_i) = -(\overline{\nabla}_{x_i}\nu)^\top = -\partial_i\nu$.

(2) Differentiating $\langle x_j, \nu\rangle = 0$ in $u^i$ gives $h_{ij} = \langle\partial_i\partial_jx, \nu\rangle = -\langle x_j, \partial_i\nu\rangle$, and inserting
$\partial_i\nu = \sum_ka_{ki}x_k$ gives $h_{ij} = -\sum_ka_{ki}g_{kj} = -(A^T(g_{ij}))_{ij}$. Transposing, and using that $(g_{ij})$ and $(h_{ij})$ are symmetric,
$(h_{ij}) = -(g_{ij})A$, so $A = -(g_{ij})^{-1}(h_{ij})$. The matrix of $S = -d\nu$ is $-A$.

(3) $\det S = \det\bigl((g_{ij})^{-1}(h_{ij})\bigr) = \det(h_{ij})/\det(g_{ij})$, and $\det(d\nu) = \det(-S) = (-1)^n\det S$.

(4) Here $(g_{ij})^{-1} = \Delta^{-1}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix} G & -F\\ -F & E\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$, and multiplying out $-(g_{ij})^{-1}(h_{ij})$ gives the four entries.
For $n = 2$ the sign $(-1)^n$ is $+1$, and $K = \det S$ by Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-theorema-egregium}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-sign-conventions}
With $e = h_{11}$, $f = h_{12}$, $g = h_{22}$ (the letter $g$ here is not the metric), part (4) is the form in which the Weingarten equations appear in
\cite[Chapter 3]{do-carmo-curves-surfaces}, where one works with $d\nu$ itself and $\mathrm{II}_p(v) = -\langle d\nu_p(v), v\rangle$. The two conventions differ by the
sign $S = -d\nu$, which is the most common source of sign errors: if $a_{ki}$ denotes the matrix of $S$ instead of $d\nu$, the formula of (2) reads
$h_{ij} = +\sum_ka_{ki}g_{kj}$. For surfaces the sign does not affect $K$, because $\det(-S) = \det S$ for $2 \times 2$ matrices, but it changes the sign of the mean
curvature, and in odd dimension $n$ it changes the sign of the Gauss--Kronecker curvature. For example, for the unit sphere with the outward normal $\nu(p) = p$, the Gauss map
is the identity, $d\nu = \id$, $S = -\id$, and $(h_{ij}) = -(g_{ij})$, as in Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-surfaces}(1) below.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-surfaces}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Spheres.} For the sphere of radius $r$ in $\RR^{n+1}$ with outward normal $\nu(p) = p/r$, $\overline{\nabla}_X\nu = X/r$, so $S = -\frac1r\id$, $h = -\frac1rg$, every
direction is principal with $\kappa = -1/r$, and all sectional curvatures are $\kappa_i\kappa_j = 1/r^2$, as in Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-curvature}.
\item \emph{Cylinders.} For $\{x^2 + y^2 = 1\} \subseteq \RR^3$ with $\nu = (x, y, 0)$, $\overline{\nabla}_X\nu$ is the horizontal part of $X$, so the principal curvatures are
$-1$ (horizontal circles) and $0$ (vertical lines), $K = 0$ and $H = -\frac12$. The cylinder is locally isometric to the plane (unroll it), in accordance with the Theorema
Egregium, although it bends in $\RR^3$.
\item \emph{Graphs.} Let $S$ be the graph of $z = \phi(u, v)$ with $\phi(0) = 0$ and $d\phi(0) = 0$, parametrised by $x(u, v) = (u, v, \phi(u, v))$. At the origin
$x_1 = e_1$, $x_2 = e_2$, $\nu = e_3$, so $g_{ij} = \delta_{ij}$ and $h_{ij} = \partial_i\partial_j\phi(0)$: the second fundamental form is the Hessian of $\phi$, the principal
curvatures are its eigenvalues, and $K(0) = \det\operatorname{Hess}\phi(0)$. The paraboloid $z = u^2 + v^2$ has $K(0) = 4$, the saddle $z = u^2 - v^2$ has $K(0) = -4$: positive
curvature means that the surface lies locally on one side of its tangent plane, negative curvature that it crosses it.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

The last example only looked at a point where the tangent plane is horizontal. The general formula for graphs shows exactly how the second fundamental form is related to the second derivatives of
the function.

\begin{proposition}[Second fundamental form of a graph]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-graph-second-fundamental-form}
Let $U \subseteq \RR^n$ be open, $u \colon U \to \RR$ smooth, and $M \subseteq \RR^{n+1}$ the graph of $u$, parametrised by $x(y) = (y, u(y))$, $y = (y^1, \ldots, y^n) \in U$. Write
$u_i = \partial u/\partial y^i$, $u_{ij} = \partial^2u/\partial y^i\partial y^j$, $\nabla u = (u_1, \ldots, u_n)$, $\operatorname{Hess}u = (u_{ij})$ and $W = \sqrt{1 + |\nabla u|^2}$, and give $M$ the upward unit
normal $\nu = (-\nabla u, 1)/W$. In the basis $x_i = e_i + u_i\,e_{n+1}$ of the tangent space, with the notation of Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-weingarten-coordinates}:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $g_{ij} = \delta_{ij} + u_iu_j$, $\det(g_{ij}) = W^2$, and $g^{ij} = \delta_{ij} - u_iu_j/W^2$.
\item $h_{ij} = u_{ij}/W$: the matrix of the second fundamental form is the Hessian of $u$ divided by $W$.
\item The matrix of the shape operator is $W^{-1}\bigl(I - W^{-2}\,\nabla u\,\nabla u^T\bigr)\operatorname{Hess}u$, with $\nabla u$ a column, so
\[
nH = \operatorname{tr}S = \frac{W^2\Delta u - \sum_{i,j}u_iu_ju_{ij}}{W^3}, \qquad \det S = \frac{\det\operatorname{Hess}u}{W^{n+2}} ,
\]
where $\Delta u = \sum_iu_{ii}$.
\item At a point where $\nabla u = 0$: $g_{ij} = \delta_{ij}$, $h_{ij} = u_{ij}$, the matrix of $S$ is $\operatorname{Hess}u$, and the principal curvatures are the eigenvalues of the Hessian.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(1) $\langle x_i, x_j\rangle = \langle e_i, e_j\rangle + u_iu_j\langle e_{n+1}, e_{n+1}\rangle = \delta_{ij} + u_iu_j$, since $e_i \perp e_{n+1}$ for $i \leq n$. So $(g_{ij}) = I + aa^T$ with $a = \nabla u$. The
matrix $aa^T$ maps $a$ to $|a|^2a$ and vanishes on $a^\perp$, so the eigenvalues of $I + aa^T$ are $1 + |a|^2$ (once) and $1$ (with multiplicity $n - 1$), and $\det(g_{ij}) = 1 + |a|^2 = W^2$. Multiplying
out, $(I + aa^T)(I - aa^T/W^2) = I + aa^T - aa^T(1 + |a|^2)/W^2 = I$, which gives the inverse.

(2) $\langle x_i, \nu\rangle = (-u_i + u_i)/W = 0$ and $|\nu|^2 = (|\nabla u|^2 + 1)/W^2 = 1$, so $\nu$ is a unit normal, with positive last component. The second derivatives of the parametrisation are
$\partial_i\partial_jx = (0, \ldots, 0, u_{ij}) = u_{ij}\,e_{n+1}$, and $\langle e_{n+1}, \nu\rangle = 1/W$, so $h_{ij} = \langle\partial_i\partial_jx, \nu\rangle = u_{ij}/W$.

(3) By Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-weingarten-coordinates}(2) the matrix of $S$ is $(g^{ij})(h_{ij})$, which is the stated product by (1) and (2). Its trace is
$W^{-1}\bigl(\operatorname{tr}\operatorname{Hess}u - W^{-2}\operatorname{tr}(\nabla u\,\nabla u^T\operatorname{Hess}u)\bigr)$, and $\operatorname{tr}(\nabla u\,\nabla u^T\operatorname{Hess}u) = \nabla u^T\operatorname{Hess}u\,\nabla u = \sum_{i,j}u_iu_ju_{ij}$.
By Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-weingarten-coordinates}(3), $\det S = \det(h_{ij})/\det(g_{ij}) = W^{-n}\det\operatorname{Hess}u/W^2$, as $(h_{ij})$ is an $n \times n$ matrix multiplied by $W^{-1}$.

(4) Put $\nabla u = 0$, so $W = 1$, in (1)--(3).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-graph-hessian}
\emph{Second derivatives and the second fundamental form.} The proposition makes precise in what sense the second fundamental form ``is the second derivative''.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{The normal part of the second derivatives.} For any parametrisation, $h_{ij} = \langle\partial_i\partial_jx, \nu\rangle$ is the normal component of the second derivatives of $x$. Their tangential
component is not geometric: it equals $\sum_k\Gamma^k_{ij}x_k$ by the Gauss formula (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-gauss-immersion}), and it changes with the parametrisation even for a
plane, where $h = 0$. For a graph only the last coordinate has second derivatives, the vector $\partial_i\partial_jx = u_{ij}e_{n+1}$ is vertical, and the fraction of it that is normal is
$\langle e_{n+1}, \nu\rangle = 1/W$, the cosine of the angle between the tangent plane and the horizontal hyperplane. This is the factor in (2).
\item \emph{Two corrections, both of first order.} Passing from $\operatorname{Hess}u$ to the curvatures costs two corrections: the factor $1/W$ in $h$, and the inverse metric $g^{-1}$ in $S$. Both involve
only $\nabla u$. So $h$ exists, is bounded, or is continuous exactly where $\operatorname{Hess}u$ is, as long as $u$ is $C^1$; this is what decides the regularity of the second fundamental form of
submanifolds of finite regularity (Remark~\ref{smooth-manifolds:remark-regularity-losses}). For a curve, $n = 1$, (3) is the classical formula for the curvature of a graph,
\[
\kappa = \frac{u^{\prime\prime}}{(1 + (u^{\prime})^2)^{3/2}} .
\]
\item \emph{The second derivative alone is not the curvature.} The parabola $u(t) = t^2$ has $u^{\prime\prime} = 2$ everywhere, but $\kappa(t) = 2/(1 + 4t^2)^{3/2}$, which is largest at the vertex and tends to $0$: far
from the vertex the parabola is steep, and its bending per unit of arc length is much smaller than per unit of horizontal distance. In the other direction, the upper half of the circle of radius $r$, $u(t) = \sqrt{r^2 - t^2}$,
has $u^{\prime} = -t/u$, $1 + (u^{\prime})^2 = r^2/u^2$ and $u^{\prime\prime} = -r^2/u^3$, which is unbounded as $t \to \pm r$, while $\kappa = (-r^2/u^3)(u^3/r^3) = -1/r$ is constant: the blow-up of $u^{\prime\prime}$
only says that the tangent line becomes vertical, so that the circle stops being a graph over the horizontal axis. In the same way the Hessian of $u$ is not invariant under rotations of the graph, while $h$ is.
\item \emph{The Hessian over the tangent plane.} The factor and the correction disappear if one looks at $M$ from its own tangent plane. Given $p \in M$, apply a rigid motion of $\RR^{n+1}$ mapping $p$ to $0$ and
$T_pM$ to $\RR^n \times \{0\}$; it preserves inner products and second derivatives, hence $h$. Near $0$ the moved hypersurface is the graph of a function $u$ with $u(0) = 0$ and $\nabla u(0) = 0$ (by the implicit
function theorem, as in Proposition~\ref{smooth-manifolds:proposition-graph-submanifold}), and by (4) and Taylor's formula
\[
u(y) = \frac12\sum_{i,j}h_{ij}(p)\,y^iy^j + o(|y|^2) .
\]
So $h_p$ is the Hessian at $p$ of the height of $M$ above its tangent plane, measured along $\nu(p)$: the quadratic term with which $M$ leaves $T_pM$. Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-surfaces}(3) is the case
$n = 2$. A positive definite $h_p$ means that $M$ lies above its tangent plane near $p$, on the side of $\nu(p)$, and an indefinite one that it crosses it.
\item \emph{Two consistency checks.} For $n = 2$ the trace in (3) is the formula for $2H$ in the minimal surface equation (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-minimal-surface-equation} below), since
$(1 + p^2 + q^2)(u_{xx} + u_{yy}) - (p^2u_{xx} + 2pq\,u_{xy} + q^2u_{yy}) = (1 + q^2)u_{xx} - 2pq\,u_{xy} + (1 + p^2)u_{yy}$, and the determinant gives the classical
$K = (u_{xx}u_{yy} - u_{xy}^2)/(1 + u_x^2 + u_y^2)^2$. Beltrami's formula (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-beltrami-formula} below) is another form of the same principle: the Riemannian Hessian
on $M$ of each coordinate function of $\RR^{n+1}$ is the corresponding component of $\mathrm{II}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\subsection{Computing the second fundamental form}\label{riemannian-geometry:subsection-computing-second-fundamental-form}

Parametrisations, as in Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-weingarten-coordinates}, are only one way to compute the second fundamental form, and often not the most convenient. A hypersurface
is frequently given as a level set $\{f = c\}$, or as a level set of a distance function, or one works with an orthonormal frame adapted to it. Each description comes with its own formula. Throughout,
$\Sigma \subseteq \overline{M}$ is a hypersurface of a Riemannian manifold with unit normal $\nu$, and we keep the conventions of Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-normal-derivative} and Remark~\ref{riemannian-geometry:remark-second-fundamental-form-notation}:
\[
h(v, w) = -\langle\overline{\nabla}_v\nu, w\rangle = \langle S v, w\rangle .
\]
Many papers instead write $\mathrm{II}(v, w) = \langle\overline{\nabla}_v\nu, w\rangle$, which is $-h$; with that convention all the formulas below change sign, and the Gauss--Kronecker curvature changes by
$(-1)^n$, $n = \dim\Sigma$.

\paragraph{Level sets.} Let $f$ be a smooth function on $\overline{M}$, $c$ a regular value, and $\Sigma = f^{-1}(c)$, a hypersurface by the regular value theorem
(Theorem~\ref{smooth-manifolds:theorem-regular-value}), with tangent spaces $T_p\Sigma = \ker d_pf$. By Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-gradient-hessian}(2), $\nu = \operatorname{grad}f/|\operatorname{grad}f|$ is a
unit normal field along $\Sigma$, defined in fact on the open set where $df \neq 0$.

\begin{proposition}[Second fundamental form of a level set]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-level-set-second-fundamental-form}
With $\nu = \operatorname{grad}f/|\operatorname{grad}f|$, for $v, w \in T_p\Sigma$,
\[
h(v, w) = -\frac{\operatorname{Hess}f(v, w)}{|\operatorname{grad}f|} .
\]
Consequently $n H = -\bigl(\Delta_gf - \operatorname{Hess}f(\nu, \nu)\bigr)/|\operatorname{grad}f|$. If $\overline{M} = \RR^{n+1}$, the Gauss--Kronecker curvature is
\[
\det S = (-1)^n\frac{\det\bigl(\operatorname{Hess}f|_{T_p\Sigma}\bigr)}{|\nabla f|^n} = (-1)^{n+1}\frac{1}{|\nabla f|^{n+2}}\det\begin{pmatrix}\operatorname{Hess}f & \nabla f\\ (\nabla f)^T & 0\end{pmatrix},
\]
where $\operatorname{Hess}f = (\partial_i\partial_jf)$ is the $(n+1) \times (n+1)$ matrix of second derivatives and $\nabla f$ the column of first derivatives. For a surface in $\RR^3$, $K = -\det\begin{pmatrix}\operatorname{Hess}f & \nabla f\\ (\nabla f)^T & 0\end{pmatrix}/|\nabla f|^4$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write $G = \operatorname{grad}f$. On the open set where $G \neq 0$, $\nu = |G|^{-1}G$, and the Leibniz rule gives $\overline{\nabla}_v\nu = |G|^{-1}\overline{\nabla}_vG + v(|G|^{-1})\,G$. The second term is a
multiple of $G$, hence orthogonal to $w \in T_p\Sigma$. So, by Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-gradient-hessian}(3),
$h(v, w) = -\langle\overline{\nabla}_v\nu, w\rangle = -|G|^{-1}\langle\overline{\nabla}_vG, w\rangle = -|G|^{-1}\operatorname{Hess}f(v, w)$. Taking the trace over an orthonormal basis $e_1, \ldots, e_n$ of $T_p\Sigma$,
completed by $e_{n+1} = \nu$ to an orthonormal basis of $T_p\overline{M}$, gives $nH = \operatorname{tr}S = -|G|^{-1}\sum_{i \leq n}\operatorname{Hess}f(e_i, e_i)$, and
$\sum_{i \leq n}\operatorname{Hess}f(e_i, e_i) = \Delta_gf - \operatorname{Hess}f(\nu, \nu)$.

Now let $\overline{M} = \RR^{n+1}$, where $\operatorname{Hess}f$ is the matrix of second derivatives and $G = \nabla f$. In the orthonormal basis $(e_i)$ of $T_p\Sigma$ the matrix of $S$ is that of $h$, which is
$-|\nabla f|^{-1}$ times the matrix $A^{\prime}$ of $\operatorname{Hess}f|_{T_p\Sigma}$; so $\det S = (-1)^n\det A^{\prime}/|\nabla f|^n$. For the bordered determinant, let $Q$ be the orthogonal matrix with
columns $e_1, \ldots, e_{n+1}$. Conjugating the bordered matrix $B$ by $\operatorname{diag}(Q, 1)$, which does not change its determinant, replaces $\operatorname{Hess}f$ by $A = Q^T(\operatorname{Hess}f)Q$, whose upper left
$n \times n$ block is $A^{\prime}$, and $\nabla f$ by $Q^T\nabla f = |\nabla f|\,e_{n+1}$. In the resulting matrix the last row and the last column have a single nonzero entry $|\nabla f|$, in position
$n + 1$. Expanding along the last row and then along the last column gives $\det B = -|\nabla f|^2\det A^{\prime}$. Hence $\det A^{\prime} = -\det B/|\nabla f|^2$, which gives the formula. For $n = 2$, $\det S$
is the Gauss curvature $K$ (Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-theorema-egregium}).
\end{proof}

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-level-sets}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Spheres.} For $f(x) = |x|^2$ on $\RR^{n+1}$, $\nabla f = 2x$ and $\operatorname{Hess}f = 2I$, so on the sphere of radius $r$, with the outward normal, $h = -\frac{2}{2r}g = -\frac1rg$, as in
Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-surfaces}(1), and $nH = -(2(n+1) - 2)/2r$, that is, $H = -1/r$.
\item \emph{Ellipsoids.} For $f = x^2/a^2 + y^2/b^2 + z^2/c^2$, $\operatorname{Hess}f = \operatorname{diag}(2/a^2, 2/b^2, 2/c^2)$ and $\nabla f = (2x/a^2, 2y/b^2, 2z/c^2)$. For a diagonal matrix
$D = \operatorname{diag}(d_1, d_2, d_3)$ and a vector $u$, expanding the bordered determinant gives $\det\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}D & u\\ u^T & 0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr) = -(u_1^2d_2d_3 + u_2^2d_1d_3 + u_3^2d_1d_2)$. Here this is
$-\frac{16}{a^2b^2c^2}\bigl(\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} + \frac{z^2}{c^2}\bigr) = -\frac{16}{a^2b^2c^2}$ on the ellipsoid $f = 1$, and $|\nabla f|^4 = 16\bigl(\frac{x^2}{a^4} + \frac{y^2}{b^4} + \frac{z^2}{c^4}\bigr)^2$. So
\[
K = \frac{1}{a^2b^2c^2}\Bigl(\frac{x^2}{a^4} + \frac{y^2}{b^4} + \frac{z^2}{c^4}\Bigr)^{-2} .
\]
At the end $(a, 0, 0)$ of the first axis this is $a^2/(b^2c^2)$: for $a$ large and $b = c$ the ellipsoid is a long cigar, sharply curved at its tips.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\paragraph{Distance functions.} The formula simplifies when $|\operatorname{grad}f| = 1$. This happens for the distance $r(x) = |x|$ to the origin in $\RR^{n+1} \setminus \{0\}$, and more generally, on suitable open
sets, for the distance to a point in a Riemannian manifold or the signed distance to a hypersurface; these are the functions one meets in comparison geometry.

\begin{proposition}\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-distance-function}
Let $U \subseteq \overline{M}$ be open and $r \colon U \to \RR$ smooth with $|\operatorname{grad}r| = 1$, and put $N = \operatorname{grad}r$ and $A = \overline{\nabla}N$, the endomorphism $X \mapsto \overline{\nabla}_XN$ of each tangent space.
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\operatorname{Hess}r(N, X) = 0$ for all $X$, that is, $AN = 0$, and $A$ is self-adjoint. The integral curves of $N$ are geodesics.
\item The level sets $\Sigma_t = r^{-1}(t)$ are hypersurfaces with unit normal $N$, second fundamental form $h_t = -\operatorname{Hess}r|_{T\Sigma_t}$ and shape operator $S_t = -A|_{T\Sigma_t}$. So the Gauss--Kronecker
curvature of $\Sigma_t$ is $(-1)^n$ times the product of the eigenvalues of $\operatorname{Hess}r$ other than the eigenvalue $0$ in the direction $N$.
\item (\emph{Riccati equation}) $(\overline{\nabla}_NA)X + A^2X + \overline{R}(X, N)N = 0$ for every vector field $X$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(1) Differentiating $\langle N, N\rangle = 1$ gives $0 = 2\langle\overline{\nabla}_XN, N\rangle = 2\operatorname{Hess}r(X, N)$, and $\operatorname{Hess}r$ is symmetric (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-gradient-hessian}(3)), so $A$ is
self-adjoint and $\langle AN, X\rangle = \operatorname{Hess}r(N, X) = 0$. In particular $\overline{\nabla}_NN = AN = 0$.

(2) Every $t$ in the image of $r$ is a regular value, as $dr \neq 0$; the rest is Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-level-set-second-fundamental-form} with $|\operatorname{grad}r| = 1$, and
$\langle S_tX, Y\rangle = h_t(X, Y) = -\langle AX, Y\rangle$ for $X, Y$ tangent to $\Sigma_t$, where $AX$ is tangent to $\Sigma_t$ by (1) and self-adjointness. On $T_p\overline{M} = T_p\Sigma_t \oplus \RR N$, $A$ is
$-S_t \oplus 0$, so its eigenvalues are those of $-S_t$ and $0$.

(3) By definition of the induced connection, $(\overline{\nabla}_NA)X = \overline{\nabla}_N(AX) - A(\overline{\nabla}_NX)$. Since $\overline{\nabla}$ is torsion free, $\overline{\nabla}_NX = \overline{\nabla}_XN + [N, X] = AX + [N, X]$, so
\[
(\overline{\nabla}_NA)X = \overline{\nabla}_N\overline{\nabla}_XN - A^2X - \overline{\nabla}_{[N, X]}N .
\]
On the other hand $\overline{R}(N, X)N = \overline{\nabla}_N\overline{\nabla}_XN - \overline{\nabla}_X\overline{\nabla}_NN - \overline{\nabla}_{[N, X]}N = \overline{\nabla}_N\overline{\nabla}_XN - \overline{\nabla}_{[N, X]}N$ by (1).
Subtracting, $(\overline{\nabla}_NA)X = \overline{R}(N, X)N - A^2X = -\overline{R}(X, N)N - A^2X$.
\end{proof}

The Riccati equation says how the shape of the level sets evolves as one moves along the normal geodesics: the term $A^2$ focuses or defocuses, and the curvature of $\overline{M}$ acts as a force. It is
the first-order form of the Jacobi equation (Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-jacobi}), and the starting point of the comparison theorems of Rauch, Bishop and Gromov.

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-distance-functions}
\begin{enumerate}
\item For $r(x) = |x|$ on $\RR^{n+1} \setminus \{0\}$, $\nabla r = x/r$ and $\partial_i\partial_jr = (\delta_{ij} - x_ix_j/r^2)/r$, so $A = \frac1r(I - NN^T)$: it is $\frac1r$ on $N^\perp$ and $0$ on $N$. The level spheres
have $S_t = -\frac1t\,\id$, and the Riccati equation, with $\overline{R} = 0$, reads $\frac{d}{dt}\bigl(\frac1t\bigr) + \frac{1}{t^2} = 0$ on $N^\perp$.
\item In $\RR^{n+1}$, where $\overline{R} = 0$, the Riccati equation along a normal line is $A^{\prime} + A^2 = 0$, solved by $A(t) = A(0)(I + tA(0))^{-1}$ wherever the inverse exists, as one checks by
differentiating $A(t)(I + tA(0)) = A(0)$. In terms of $S_t = -A(t)$ on $N^\perp$, $S_t = S_0(I - tS_0)^{-1}$. This is the content of the next proposition, which does not need a distance function.
\item On the unit sphere $S^{n+1}$, the distance $r$ from the north pole is smooth with $|\operatorname{grad}r| = 1$ for $0 < r < \pi$, and its level sets are the spheres of latitude. By rotational symmetry
$A = a(r)\,\id$ on $N^\perp$, and with $\overline{R}(X, N)N = X$ for $X \perp N$ (constant curvature $1$), the Riccati equation is $a^{\prime} + a^2 + 1 = 0$, and $a = \cot r$ is the solution with $a \sim 1/r$ near the pole.
So the sphere of latitude at distance $r$ has $S = -\cot r\,\id$: it is umbilic, curved like a Euclidean sphere of radius $\tan r$, and totally geodesic for $r = \pi/2$, the equator.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\paragraph{Parallel hypersurfaces.} In Euclidean space one can move a hypersurface along its normals and follow the second fundamental form explicitly.

\begin{proposition}[Parallel hypersurfaces]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-parallel-hypersurfaces}
Let $\Sigma \subseteq \RR^{n+1}$ be a hypersurface with unit normal $\nu$ and shape operator $S$, and $t \in \RR$ such that $I - tS$ is invertible at every point (for instance $\Sigma$ compact and $|t|\max_i|\kappa_i| < 1$).
Then $x_t(p) = p + t\nu(p)$ is an immersion of $\Sigma$, $\nu$ is a unit normal of $x_t$, and in terms of $T_p\Sigma$:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $d_px_t = I - tS$, so the metric induced by $x_t$ is $g_t(v, w) = \langle(I - tS)v, (I - tS)w\rangle$ and, for $t$ in the interval around $0$ where $I - tS$ stays invertible, its volume form is
$\det(I - tS)\,\mathrm{vol}_\Sigma = \prod_i(1 - t\kappa_i)\,\mathrm{vol}_\Sigma$;
\item the shape operator of $x_t$ is $S_t = S(I - tS)^{-1}$, with principal curvatures $\kappa_i/(1 - t\kappa_i)$ and the same principal directions.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(1) As maps to $\RR^{n+1}$, $dx_t = dx + t\,d\nu$, and $d\nu = -S$ (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-weingarten-coordinates}(1)), so $d_px_t(v) = v - tSv \in T_p\Sigma$. This is injective, so $x_t$ is an
immersion with $d_px_t(T_p\Sigma) = T_p\Sigma$, and $\nu(p)$ is orthogonal to it. The formula for $g_t$ is the definition of the induced metric. For an orthonormal basis $(e_i)$ of $T_p\Sigma$ for $g$, positively oriented,
$\mathrm{vol}_{g_t}(e_1, \ldots, e_n) = \sqrt{\det(g_t(e_i, e_j))} = |\det(I - tS)|$, and $\det(I - tS) > 0$ on the interval in question, since it is continuous in $t$, nonzero, and equal to $1$ at $t = 0$.
In an orthonormal basis of principal directions $I - tS$ is diagonal with entries $1 - t\kappa_i$.

(2) The Gauss map of $x_t$ is again $\nu$, viewed as a function on $\Sigma$, so by (1) of Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-weingarten-coordinates} applied to $x_t$ (whose proof only uses that $\nu$ is a unit
normal), $d\nu = -S_t \circ dx_t$, that is, $-S = -S_t(I - tS)$. Hence $S_t = S(I - tS)^{-1}$, which commutes with $S$ and has the same eigenvectors, with eigenvalues $\kappa_i/(1 - t\kappa_i)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-steiner}
For a compact surface $\Sigma \subseteq \RR^3$ the area of the parallel surface is a polynomial in $t$, for small $|t|$:
\[
\operatorname{area}(x_t) = \int_\Sigma(1 - t\kappa_1)(1 - t\kappa_2)\,dA = \operatorname{area}(\Sigma) - 2t\int_\Sigma H\,dA + t^2\int_\Sigma K\,dA,
\]
and $\int_\Sigma K\,dA = 2\pi\chi(\Sigma)$ by the Gauss--Bonnet theorem of the next section. For the unit sphere with the outward normal, $H = -1$, $K = 1$, and the formula gives $4\pi(1 + t)^2$, the area of
the sphere of radius $1 + t$. Integrating in $t$ gives the volume of a thin shell around $\Sigma$, and this is Steiner's formula for the volume of the $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood of a convex body; Weyl's tube formula
is the analogue for submanifolds of any codimension. The coefficients, integrals of the elementary symmetric functions of the principal curvatures, are the \emph{quermassintegrals} or Lipschitz--Killing curvatures,
and the theory of normal cycles extends them to convex bodies and other singular sets, where they become measures (Chapter~\ref{symplectic-geometry}).
\end{remark}

\paragraph{Moving frames and the Gauss map.} Finally, in an adapted orthonormal frame the second fundamental form is part of the connection forms of $\RR^{n+1}$
(Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-hypersurface-frames}), and wedging these forms gives the Gauss--Kronecker curvature times the volume form. Geometrically this wedge product is the pullback of the volume
form of the sphere by the Gauss map.

\begin{proposition}\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-gauss-map-volume}
Let $\Sigma \subseteq \RR^{n+1}$ be a hypersurface with unit normal $\nu$, oriented so that $(\nu, v_1, \ldots, v_n)$ is a positive basis of $\RR^{n+1}$ whenever $(v_1, \ldots, v_n)$ is a positive basis of $T_p\Sigma$, and orient
$S^n$ by its outward normal in the same way. Let $(e_1, \ldots, e_n)$ be a positive orthonormal frame on $U \subseteq \Sigma$, with $e_{n+1} = \nu$ and forms $\omega^i$, $\omega^A_B$ as in
Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-hypersurface-frames}. Then on $U$
\[
