\nu^*\mathrm{vol}_{S^n} = \omega^1_{n+1} \wedge \cdots \wedge \omega^n_{n+1} = (-1)^n\,\omega^{n+1}_1 \wedge \cdots \wedge \omega^{n+1}_n = (-1)^n\det(h_{ij})\,\omega^1 \wedge \cdots \wedge \omega^n = (-1)^n(\det S)\,\mathrm{vol}_\Sigma .
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
At $p \in U$, $T_{\nu(p)}S^n = \nu(p)^\perp = T_p\Sigma$, and the two orientations agree, since both are defined by putting the normal vector $\nu(p)$ first. The vectors $e_i(p)$ form a positive orthonormal basis of this
space, so $\mathrm{vol}_{S^n}$ at $\nu(p)$ is $e^1 \wedge \cdots \wedge e^n$ with $e^i = \langle -, e_i(p)\rangle$, and
$(\nu^*\mathrm{vol}_{S^n})_p = (e^1 \circ d\nu) \wedge \cdots \wedge (e^n \circ d\nu)$. By definition $d\nu = de_{n+1} = \sum_A\omega^A_{n+1}e_A$, and $\omega^{n+1}_{n+1} = 0$ by skew-symmetry, so
$e^i \circ d\nu = \omega^i_{n+1}$. This is the first equality; the second is $\omega^i_{n+1} = -\omega^{n+1}_i$. By Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-hypersurface-frames}(2),
$\omega^{n+1}_i = \sum_jh_{ij}\omega^j$, and the wedge product of $n$ such forms is $\det(h_{ij})\,\omega^1 \wedge \cdots \wedge \omega^n$ (expand by multilinearity: the terms with a repeated $\omega^j$ vanish, and the others give the Leibniz formula for the determinant). Finally
$\omega^1 \wedge \cdots \wedge \omega^n = \mathrm{vol}_\Sigma$ for a positive orthonormal coframe, and $\det(h_{ij}) = \det S$ in an orthonormal frame.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-curvatura-integra}
For a compact connected hypersurface $\Sigma \subseteq \RR^{n+1}$ the proposition gives $\int_\Sigma\det S\,\mathrm{vol}_\Sigma = (-1)^n\int_\Sigma\nu^*\mathrm{vol}_{S^n} = (-1)^n\deg(\nu)\operatorname{vol}(S^n)$, where $\deg(\nu)$ is the degree of
the Gauss map (Exercise~\ref{de-rham:exercise-degree-de-rham}): the total Gauss--Kronecker curvature, Gauss's \emph{curvatura integra}, is a topological invariant. For a surface in $\RR^3$ Gauss--Bonnet then gives
$\deg(\nu) = \frac{1}{4\pi}\int_\Sigma K\,dA = \frac12\chi(\Sigma)$. In general $\Sigma$ bounds a compact region $X$, and Hopf showed that $\deg(\nu) = \chi(X)$, which is $\frac12\chi(\Sigma)$ when $n$ is even;
this is the heart of the proof of the Poincar\'e--Hopf theorem in \cite{milnor-differential-viewpoint}. For odd $n$, $\chi(\Sigma) = 0$ and the degree depends on the region $X$, not only on the topology of $\Sigma$. The same idea, a curvature form pulled back from the unit sphere bundle and integrated, is the core of Chern's intrinsic
proof of the Gauss--Bonnet--Chern theorem (Chapter~\ref{characteristic-classes}), and the pullback along the unit normals of a set is the definition of curvature measures through normal cycles.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The Gauss map}\label{riemannian-geometry:subsection-gauss-map}

Classical surface theory, as in \cite[Chapter 3]{do-carmo-curves-surfaces}, does not start from connections. It starts from a single map, the unit normal regarded as a point of the unit sphere, and
reads all the extrinsic curvature off its derivative. This subsection collects that point of view for hypersurfaces $M \subseteq \RR^{n+1}$ of Euclidean space. The main case is $n = 2$, surfaces in $\RR^3$,
but $n = 1$, curves in the plane, is already instructive. Most of the facts below have appeared in pieces earlier in this section; the point is to see all of them as properties of one map.

\begin{definition}\label{riemannian-geometry:definition-gauss-map}
Let $M \subseteq \RR^{n+1}$ be a hypersurface with a unit normal field $\nu$; such a field exists exactly when $M$ is orientable, and then it is smooth
(Proposition~\ref{differential-forms:proposition-orientation-normal}). The \emph{Gauss map} of $M$ is $\nu \colon M \to S^n$, $p \mapsto \nu(p)$. Its differential at $p$ maps $T_pM$ to
$T_{\nu(p)}S^n = \nu(p)^\perp = T_pM$, so $d\nu_p$ is an endomorphism of $T_pM$. The quadratic form
\[
\mathrm{II}_p(v) = -\langle d\nu_p(v), v\rangle, \qquad v \in T_pM,
\]
is the \emph{second fundamental form} of classical surface theory \cite[Section 3-2]{do-carmo-curves-surfaces}. For a unit vector $v$, $\mathrm{II}_p(v)$ is the \emph{normal curvature} of $M$ at $p$ in the
direction $v$.
\end{definition}

So in the classical language the second fundamental form \emph{is} the derivative of the Gauss map, turned into a quadratic form with the inner product. The tangent plane at $p$ is $\nu(p)^\perp$, so the
Gauss map is the same as the map $p \mapsto T_pM$, recorded by its unit normal; its derivative measures how fast the tangent plane turns, which is the intuitive meaning of curvature. A plane does not turn at
all, a sphere turns at the same rate in all directions, and a cylinder turns in one direction only. The next proposition translates the definition into the notation of this section and states the
classical properties.

\begin{proposition}\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-gauss-map-curvatures}
Let $M \subseteq \RR^{n+1}$ be a hypersurface with unit normal $\nu$, shape operator $S$, scalar second fundamental form $h$, principal curvatures $\kappa_1 \leq \cdots \leq \kappa_n$ at $p$ and mean
curvature $H$ (Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-hypersurface-curvatures}).
\begin{enumerate}
\item $d\nu_p = -S_p$. So $d\nu_p$ is self-adjoint, its eigenvalues are $-\kappa_1, \ldots, -\kappa_n$, and $\mathrm{II}_p(v) = h_p(v, v)$, with $h_p(v, w) = -\langle d\nu_p(v), w\rangle$ the associated symmetric
bilinear form. Moreover $\det d\nu_p = (-1)^n\det S_p$ and $\operatorname{tr}d\nu_p = -nH(p)$; for a surface, $n = 2$, $K = \det d\nu_p$ and $H = -\frac12\operatorname{tr}d\nu_p$.
\item (Meusnier) Let $\gamma \colon (-\varepsilon, \varepsilon) \to M$ be a curve with $\gamma(0) = p$ and $\gamma^{\prime}(0) = v$. Then $\langle\gamma^{\prime\prime}(0), \nu(p)\rangle = \mathrm{II}_p(v)$. So the normal component
of the acceleration of a curve in $M$ depends only on its velocity; for curves parametrised by arc length, the normal component of their curvature vector depends only on the tangent direction.
\item (Euler) For unit vectors $v$, the normal curvature $\mathrm{II}_p(v)$ takes values in $[\kappa_1, \kappa_n]$. Its maximum $\kappa_n$ is attained exactly at the unit eigenvectors of $S_p$ for $\kappa_n$,
and its minimum $\kappa_1$ exactly at those for $\kappa_1$. For $n = 2$, if $(e_1, e_2)$ is an orthonormal basis of principal directions,
\[
\mathrm{II}_p(\cos t\,e_1 + \sin t\,e_2) = \kappa_1\cos^2t + \kappa_2\sin^2t .
\]
\item (Area) Suppose $\det S_p \neq 0$. Then $\nu$ maps an open neighbourhood $U$ of $p$ diffeomorphically onto an open subset of $S^n$; it preserves orientation near $p$ if $(-1)^n\det S_p > 0$ and reverses
it if $(-1)^n\det S_p < 0$, for the orientations of Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-gauss-map-volume}. If $B_1, B_2, \ldots \subseteq U$ are open sets containing $p$ and contained in balls
around $p$ whose radii tend to $0$, then
\[
\lim_{k \to \infty}\frac{\operatorname{vol}(\nu(B_k))}{\operatorname{vol}(B_k)} = |\det S_p| .
\]
For a surface: $|K(p)|$ is the limit of the ratio of the area swept by the normal on the sphere to the area on the surface, and $\nu$ preserves orientation near $p$ if $K(p) > 0$ and reverses it if $K(p) < 0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(1) By Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-normal-derivative}, $d\nu(v) = \overline{\nabla}_v\nu$ is tangent and equals $-S v$, and $h(v, w) = -\langle d\nu(v), w\rangle$. The shape operator is
self-adjoint with eigenvalues $\kappa_i$ (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-weingarten} and Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-hypersurface-curvatures}), so $-S$ is self-adjoint
with eigenvalues $-\kappa_i$. On the $n$-dimensional space $T_pM$, $\det(-S) = (-1)^n\det S$ and $\operatorname{tr}(-S) = -\operatorname{tr}S = -nH$. For $n = 2$, $\det S = K$ by
Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-weingarten-coordinates}(4).

(2) For all $t$, $\gamma^{\prime}(t)$ is tangent to $M$ at $\gamma(t)$, so $\langle\gamma^{\prime}(t), \nu(\gamma(t))\rangle = 0$. Differentiating at $t = 0$ gives
$\langle\gamma^{\prime\prime}(0), \nu(p)\rangle + \langle v, d\nu_p(v)\rangle = 0$, that is, $\langle\gamma^{\prime\prime}(0), \nu(p)\rangle = \mathrm{II}_p(v)$.

(3) Choose an orthonormal basis $(e_i)$ of $T_pM$ with $S e_i = \kappa_ie_i$, which exists because $S$ is self-adjoint. For $v = \sum_iv_ie_i$ with $\sum_iv_i^2 = 1$, by (1),
$\mathrm{II}_p(v) = \langle S v, v\rangle = \sum_i\kappa_iv_i^2$, a weighted average of the $\kappa_i$ with weights $v_i^2 \geq 0$ summing to $1$. So $\kappa_1 \leq \mathrm{II}_p(v) \leq \kappa_n$. Equality
$\mathrm{II}_p(v) = \kappa_n$ means $\sum_i(\kappa_n - \kappa_i)v_i^2 = 0$, a sum of nonnegative terms, so $v_i = 0$ whenever $\kappa_i < \kappa_n$, that is, $v$ is an eigenvector for $\kappa_n$; similarly
for $\kappa_1$. For $n = 2$ and $v = \cos t\,e_1 + \sin t\,e_2$ the weights are $\cos^2t$ and $\sin^2t$.

(4) By (1), $\det d\nu_p = (-1)^n\det S_p \neq 0$, so by the inverse function theorem $\nu$ maps an open neighbourhood of $p$ diffeomorphically onto an open subset of $S^n$. Shrink it to a connected open
neighbourhood $U$ with compact closure on which $\det S$ has no zero; then $\det S$ has a constant sign on $U$. By Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-gauss-map-volume},
$\nu^*\mathrm{vol}_{S^n} = (-1)^n(\det S)\,\mathrm{vol}_M$, which is a positive multiple of the volume form of $M$ if $(-1)^n\det S > 0$ and a negative one otherwise; so $\nu|_U$ preserves, respectively reverses,
orientation. For a continuous function $f \geq 0$ with compact support in $\nu(U)$, Proposition~\ref{differential-forms:proposition-integral-well-defined} gives
\[
\int_{\nu(U)}f\,\mathrm{vol}_{S^n} = \pm\int_U\nu^*(f\,\mathrm{vol}_{S^n}) = \int_U(f \circ \nu)\,|\det S|\,\mathrm{vol}_M ,
\]
with the sign $\pm$ chosen according to the orientation behaviour, which exactly turns $(-1)^n\det S$ into $|\det S|$. For an open $B \subseteq U$, approximating the indicator function of the open set $\nu(B)$
from below by an increasing sequence of such functions and using monotone convergence on both sides gives $\operatorname{vol}(\nu(B)) = \int_B|\det S|\,d\mathrm{vol}_M$. Now let $\epsilon > 0$. By
continuity of $\det S$ there is $r > 0$ with $\bigl||\det S_q| - |\det S_p|\bigr| < \epsilon$ for $q \in U$ with $|q - p| < r$, and for large $k$ the set $B_k$ lies in this ball. Then
\[
\Bigl|\operatorname{vol}(\nu(B_k)) - |\det S_p|\operatorname{vol}(B_k)\Bigr| = \Bigl|\int_{B_k}\bigl(|\det S| - |\det S_p|\bigr)\,d\mathrm{vol}_M\Bigr| \leq \epsilon\operatorname{vol}(B_k) ,
\]
and dividing by $\operatorname{vol}(B_k) > 0$ gives the limit. For $n = 2$, $(-1)^2\det S = K$ by (1).
\end{proof}

Part (4) is how Gauss himself introduced the curvature of a surface in 1827: the \emph{curvatura integra} of a piece of surface is the area of its image under the Gauss map, counted with sign, and the
curvature at a point is its density with respect to area. Remark~\ref{riemannian-geometry:remark-curvatura-integra} is the global version of the same idea. That the limit only depends on the first
fundamental form, although its definition uses the embedding through $\nu$, is the content of the Theorema Egregium (Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-theorema-egregium}).

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-gauss-map-curves}
\emph{The Gauss map of a plane curve.} For $n = 1$ everything reduces to one angle. Let $\gamma \colon I \to \RR^2$ be parametrised by arc length on an interval $I$, so that $T = \gamma^{\prime}$ is a unit
vector, and write $T(s) = (\cos\theta(s), \sin\theta(s))$ with a smooth angle function $\theta$ (it exists on an interval by the lifting property of the covering $\RR \to S^1$, $\theta \mapsto e^{i\theta}$). Take
the normal $\nu = (-\sin\theta, \cos\theta)$, obtained by rotating $T$ by $+90^\circ$. The Gauss map is $s \mapsto \nu(s) \in S^1$, and
\[
\frac{d}{ds}\nu(s) = -\theta^{\prime}(s)\,(\cos\theta(s), \sin\theta(s)) = -\theta^{\prime}(s)\,T(s),
\]
so $d\nu = -\theta^{\prime}\id$ on the tangent line, and the only principal curvature is $\kappa = \theta^{\prime}$: the \emph{signed curvature} of the curve, the speed at which the tangent, or equally the normal,
turns per unit of length, positive when the curve turns to the left. Part (4) of the proposition becomes the statement that the length of the arc swept by $\nu$ on $S^1$ is approximately $|\kappa|$ times the
length of the piece of curve. For a closed curve of length $L$, $\int_0^L\kappa\,ds = \theta(L) - \theta(0)$ is $2\pi$ times the number of turns made by the tangent, the \emph{rotation index}; for a simple
closed curve it is $\pm1$, Hopf's theorem of turning tangents \cite{do-carmo-curves-surfaces}. This is the one-dimensional case of Remark~\ref{riemannian-geometry:remark-curvatura-integra}. For a graph $y = u(t)$,
$\tan\theta = u^{\prime}$, and differentiating $\theta = \arctan u^{\prime}$ with respect to arc length, $ds = \sqrt{1 + (u^{\prime})^2}\,dt$, recovers $\kappa = u^{\prime\prime}/(1 + (u^{\prime})^2)^{3/2}$ of
Remark~\ref{riemannian-geometry:remark-graph-hessian}.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-gauss-map}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Plane.} The Gauss map of an affine plane is constant, its image is a point, and $d\nu = 0$: $\mathrm{II} = 0$ and $K = H = 0$.
\item \emph{Sphere.} For the sphere of radius $r$ with outward normal, $\nu(p) = p/r$ is the dilation by $1/r$; its image is all of $S^2$, $d\nu = \frac1r\id$ and $K = \det d\nu = 1/r^2$. Here the ratio in
(4) is $1/r^2$ for every region, not only in the limit, since a dilation by $1/r$ multiplies areas by $1/r^2$; and $H = -\frac12\operatorname{tr}d\nu = -1/r$, as in Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-surfaces}(1).
\item \emph{Cylinder.} For $\{x^2 + y^2 = 1\}$ with $\nu(x, y, z) = (x, y, 0)$, the image of the Gauss map is the equator, a set of area $0$. The normal is constant along the vertical lines, so $d\nu$ kills
the vertical direction and has rank $1$: $K = 0$, while $H = -\frac12\operatorname{tr}d\nu = -\frac12$, as in Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-surfaces}(2). The cylinder bends in only one direction.
\item \emph{Paraboloid and saddle.} For the graph of $u$, the upward normal is $(-u_x, -u_y, 1)/W$, and its horizontal part $-(u_x, u_y)/W$ shows how $\nu$ moves near the north pole of $S^2$. For the
paraboloid $u = x^2 + y^2$ it is $-2(x, y)/W$: when $(x, y) = \epsilon(\cos t, \sin t)$ runs counterclockwise around a small circle, $-2\epsilon(\cos t, \sin t)/W$ runs counterclockwise too, and $\nu$
preserves orientation, as it must since $K(0) = 4 > 0$. For the saddle $u = xy$ it is $-(y, x)/W$, and $-\epsilon(\sin t, \cos t) = \epsilon(\cos(-t - \frac\pi2), \sin(-t - \frac\pi2))$ runs clockwise: $\nu$
reverses orientation, and $K(0) = -1 < 0$ (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-graph-second-fundamental-form}).
\item \emph{Torus.} For $R > r > 0$ let $x(\theta, \phi) = \bigl((R + r\cos\phi)\cos\theta, (R + r\cos\phi)\sin\theta, r\sin\phi\bigr)$, with outward normal
$\nu = (\cos\phi\cos\theta, \cos\phi\sin\theta, \sin\phi)$; so the Gauss map sends the point with parameters $(\theta, \phi)$ to the point of $S^2$ with longitude $\theta$ and latitude $\phi$. Since
$x_\theta = (R + r\cos\phi)(-\sin\theta, \cos\theta, 0)$ and $x_\phi = r(-\sin\phi\cos\theta, -\sin\phi\sin\theta, \cos\phi)$,
\[
d\nu(x_\theta) = \partial_\theta\nu = \frac{\cos\phi}{R + r\cos\phi}\,x_\theta, \qquad d\nu(x_\phi) = \partial_\phi\nu = \frac1r\,x_\phi ,
\]
so $x_\theta$ and $x_\phi$ are principal directions and $K = \det d\nu = \cos\phi/(r(R + r\cos\phi))$. The outer half, $\cos\phi > 0$, has $K > 0$; the inner half, $\cos\phi < 0$, has $K < 0$; and on the top
and bottom circles $\phi = \pm\frac\pi2$, $K = 0$ and $\nu = \pm e_3$ is constant. Every point of $S^2$ other than $\pm e_3$ is the image of exactly two points, $(\theta, \phi)$ on the outer half and
$(\theta + \pi, \pi - \phi)$ on the inner half; the Gauss map preserves orientation at the first and reverses it at the second, so the signed area of the image is $0$. Accordingly, with
$dA = |x_\theta||x_\phi|\,d\theta\,d\phi = r(R + r\cos\phi)\,d\theta\,d\phi$ ($x_\theta \perp x_\phi$),
$\int K\,dA = \int_0^{2\pi}\!\int_0^{2\pi}\cos\phi\,d\phi\,d\theta = 0$, in agreement with Remark~\ref{riemannian-geometry:remark-curvatura-integra}, since the torus has Euler characteristic $0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-gauss-map-points}
\emph{Classification of points of a surface.} The rank of $d\nu_p$ and the sign of $K(p)$ sort the points of a surface $M \subseteq \RR^3$ into four classes, all visible on the torus above.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Elliptic}, $K(p) > 0$: $\kappa_1$ and $\kappa_2$ have the same sign, $\mathrm{II}_p$ is definite, $\nu$ is a local diffeomorphism preserving orientation, and by the Taylor expansion of
Remark~\ref{riemannian-geometry:remark-graph-hessian}(4) $M$ lies on one side of its tangent plane near $p$, touching it only at $p$. Example: the outer half of the torus, every point of a sphere.
\item \emph{Hyperbolic}, $K(p) < 0$: the principal curvatures have opposite signs, $\nu$ reverses orientation, and $M$ crosses its tangent plane. By (3) the normal curvature vanishes exactly in the two
\emph{asymptotic directions} $\cos t\,e_1 + \sin t\,e_2$ with $\tan^2t = -\kappa_1/\kappa_2$, which separate the directions in which $M$ lies above the tangent plane from those in which it lies below.
Example: the saddle, the inner half of the torus.
\item \emph{Parabolic}, $K(p) = 0$ but $d\nu_p \neq 0$: one principal curvature vanishes, $d\nu_p$ has rank $1$, and the Gauss map squeezes a neighbourhood of $p$ towards a curve. Example: the cylinder, the
top and bottom circles of the torus.
\item \emph{Planar}, $d\nu_p = 0$: $\mathrm{II}_p = 0$. Example: the plane; but also the origin of $z = x^4 + y^4$, which lies on one side of its tangent plane, and of the \emph{monkey saddle}
$z = x^3 - 3xy^2$, which crosses it along three lines (Exercise~\ref{riemannian-geometry:exercise-gauss-map}). At a planar point the second order Taylor term vanishes, and only higher order terms decide the
shape.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\section{Global theorems on surfaces}\label{riemannian-geometry:section-surfaces-global}

This section collects the classical global theorems on surfaces, in the form in which they appear in \cite[Chapters 4 and 5]{do-carmo-curves-surfaces}:
the Gauss--Bonnet theorem for regions with boundary, Minding's theorem on surfaces of constant curvature, and the theorems of Hadamard, Liebmann and Hilbert on
surfaces in $\RR^3$. Throughout, $M$ is an oriented Riemannian surface and $J$ is the rotation by $+\pi/2$ in its tangent planes, as in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-gauss-bonnet}; for a surface in $\RR^3$ oriented by a unit normal $\nu$, $Jv = \nu \times v$
(Proposition~\ref{differential-forms:proposition-orientation-normal}).

\begin{definition}\label{riemannian-geometry:definition-geodesic-curvature}
Let $\alpha$ be a curve in $M$ parametrised by arc length, $|\alpha'| = 1$. Since $\langle D_s\alpha', \alpha'\rangle = \frac12\frac{d}{ds}|\alpha'|^2 = 0$, the covariant acceleration is
$D_s\alpha' = k_g\,J\alpha'$ for a function $k_g$, the \emph{geodesic curvature} of $\alpha$. So $\alpha$ is a geodesic if and only if $k_g = 0$. For a surface in $\RR^3$,
$D_s\alpha'$ is the tangential part of the acceleration $\alpha''$ (Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-gauss-formula}), and $k_g = \langle\alpha'', \nu \times \alpha'\rangle$.
\end{definition}

For example, a circle of latitude at height $z_0 \in (-1, 1)$ on the unit sphere has geodesic curvature $\pm z_0/(1 - z_0^2)^{1/2}$, and the equator, $z_0 = 0$, is a geodesic.

A \emph{simple region} is a compact set $R \subseteq M$ contained in the domain of a positively oriented chart $x \colon \Omega \to M$, whose preimage is homeomorphic to a closed
disc and bounded by a simple closed curve that is piecewise smooth with nonvanishing velocity. Its boundary $\partial R$ is parametrised by arc length so that $R$ lies on the left
(that is, $J\alpha'$ points into $R$), and at a vertex where the smooth arcs meet, the \emph{exterior angle} $\theta_i \in (-\pi, \pi)$ is the oriented angle from the incoming to the
outgoing velocity; the \emph{interior angle} is $\pi - \theta_i$.

\begin{theorem}[Theorem of turning tangents]\label{riemannian-geometry:theorem-turning-tangents}
Let $R$ be a simple region, $(e_1, e_2)$ a positively oriented orthonormal frame on a neighbourhood of $R$, and write the unit tangent of $\partial R$ on each smooth arc as
$\alpha' = \cos\varphi\,e_1 + \sin\varphi\,e_2$ with $\varphi$ continuous on the arc. Then the sum over the arcs of the changes of $\varphi$, plus the sum of the exterior angles,
equals $2\pi$.
\end{theorem}

This is Hopf's Umlaufsatz \cite{hopf-1935}; see \cite[Chapters 4 and 5]{do-carmo-curves-surfaces}. For a smooth convex curve in the Euclidean plane it says that the tangent turns
exactly once.

\begin{theorem}[Local Gauss--Bonnet theorem]\label{riemannian-geometry:theorem-local-gauss-bonnet}
Let $R$ be a simple region with smooth boundary arcs $\alpha_i$ and exterior angles $\theta_1, \ldots, \theta_k$. Then
\[
\sum_i\int_{\alpha_i}k_g\,ds + \iint_RK\,dA + \sum_{j=1}^k\theta_j = 2\pi.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Applying Gram--Schmidt to the coordinate fields of the chart gives a positively oriented orthonormal frame $(e_1, e_2)$ near $R$. Define the $1$-form $\omega$ by
$\nabla_Xe_1 = \omega(X)e_2$; then $\nabla_Xe_2 = -\omega(X)e_1$, and the computation in the proof of Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-gauss-bonnet} gives
$d\omega = -K\,dA$. On a smooth arc, $\alpha' = \cos\varphi\,e_1 + \sin\varphi\,e_2$ with $\varphi$ smooth (lift the map to the circle through the covering $\RR \to S^1$,
Proposition~\ref{homotopy:proposition-homotopy-lifting}), and $J\alpha' = -\sin\varphi\,e_1 + \cos\varphi\,e_2$. Differentiating,
\[
D_s\alpha' = \varphi'J\alpha' + \cos\varphi\,\nabla_{\alpha'}e_1 + \sin\varphi\,\nabla_{\alpha'}e_2 = \varphi'J\alpha' + \omega(\alpha')J\alpha',
\]
so $k_g = \varphi' + \omega(\alpha')$. Summing over the arcs, $\sum_i\int_{\alpha_i}k_g\,ds = \sum_i(\text{change of }\varphi\text{ on }\alpha_i) + \int_{\partial R}\omega$. The first sum is
$2\pi - \sum_j\theta_j$ by Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-turning-tangents}, and $\int_{\partial R}\omega = \iint_Rd\omega = -\iint_RK\,dA$ by Stokes' theorem in the chart, that
is, Green's theorem for a plane region bounded by a piecewise smooth simple closed curve \cite[Chapter 4]{do-carmo-curves-surfaces}
(Theorem~\ref{differential-forms:theorem-stokes} in the smooth case).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{riemannian-geometry:corollary-geodesic-triangles}
Let $T$ be a \emph{geodesic triangle}: a simple region bounded by three geodesic arcs, with interior angles $\phi_1, \phi_2, \phi_3$. Then
\[
\phi_1 + \phi_2 + \phi_3 - \pi = \iint_TK\,dA.
\]
So the angles of a geodesic triangle add up to more than $\pi$ where $K > 0$, to $\pi$ where $K = 0$, and to less than $\pi$ where $K < 0$. On the unit sphere the area of a geodesic
triangle is its angle excess; for instance the octant bounded by the three coordinate planes has three right angles and area $\frac{3\pi}{2} - \pi = \frac{\pi}{2}$, one eighth of $4\pi$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Here $k_g = 0$ on the sides and $\theta_j = \pi - \phi_j$, so Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-local-gauss-bonnet} gives
$\iint_TK\,dA + 3\pi - (\phi_1 + \phi_2 + \phi_3) = 2\pi$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Global Gauss--Bonnet theorem]\label{riemannian-geometry:theorem-global-gauss-bonnet}
Let $R \subseteq M$ be a compact region whose boundary consists of finitely many simple closed piecewise smooth curves $C_1, \ldots, C_m$, oriented with $R$ on the left, with
exterior angles $\theta_1, \ldots, \theta_k$ at their vertices. Then
\[
\iint_RK\,dA + \sum_i\int_{C_i}k_g\,ds + \sum_j\theta_j = 2\pi\chi(R).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Sketch of proof]
Choose a triangulation of $R$ fine enough that every triangle is a simple region, with $F$ triangles, $E$ edges and $V$ vertices, so that $\chi(R) = V - E + F$. Apply
Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-local-gauss-bonnet} to each triangle and add. Every interior edge is traversed twice in opposite directions, so the integrals of $k_g$ over
interior edges cancel, and those over boundary edges give the boundary term. The exterior angles of a triangle are $\pi$ minus its interior angles, and the interior
angles of all triangles add up to $2\pi$ at each of the $V_{\mathrm{int}}$ interior vertices, to $\pi - \theta_j$ at a corner of $\partial R$, and to $\pi$ at the other boundary
vertices. So the sum of all exterior angles of the triangles is $3\pi F - 2\pi V_{\mathrm{int}} - \pi V_\partial + \sum_j\theta_j$, where $V_\partial$ is the number of boundary
vertices, and adding the local formulas gives
$\iint_RK\,dA + \sum_i\int_{C_i}k_g\,ds + \sum_j\theta_j = -\pi F + 2\pi V_{\mathrm{int}} + \pi V_\partial$. Each triangle has three edges, each interior edge lies in two triangles
and each boundary edge in one, so $3F = 2E_{\mathrm{int}} + E_\partial$, and $E_\partial = V_\partial$ because the boundary is a union of closed curves; with these relations the right
side equals $2\pi(V - E + F)$. The omitted verification is the existence of such triangulations \cite[Chapter 4]{do-carmo-curves-surfaces}.
\end{proof}

For $R = M$ a closed surface there is no boundary, and the formula is Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-gauss-bonnet}, $\iint_MK\,dA = 2\pi\chi(M)$. For a disc in the
plane bounded by a smooth convex curve, $K = 0$ and $\chi = 1$, and the formula says that the total geodesic curvature of the boundary is $2\pi$: the tangent turns once. For the
northern cap of the unit sphere above the latitude $z_0$, the area $2\pi(1 - z_0)$ plus the total geodesic curvature $2\pi(1 - z_0^2)^{1/2} \cdot z_0/(1 - z_0^2)^{1/2} = 2\pi z_0$ of
the boundary circle gives $2\pi = 2\pi\chi(\text{disc})$.

Combining the global Gauss--Bonnet theorem, proved with a triangulation, with Chern's theorem, proved with a vector field, gives the Poincar\'e--Hopf theorem for surfaces without
using Theorem~\ref{characteristic-classes:theorem-euler}(3).

\begin{corollary}[Poincar\'e--Hopf for surfaces]\label{riemannian-geometry:corollary-poincare-hopf-surfaces}
Let $M$ be a compact oriented surface and $X$ a vector field on $M$ with isolated zeros. Then the sum of the indices of $X$ (Definition~\ref{characteristic-classes:definition-index}) is $\chi(M)$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Choose a metric (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-metric-exists}). By Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-global-gauss-bonnet} with $R = M$, $\iint_MK\,dA = 2\pi\chi(M)$. By
Theorem~\ref{characteristic-classes:theorem-chern} for the Levi-Civita connection on $TM$, $\int_Me(TM, \nabla) = \sum_z\operatorname{ind}_z(X)$, and $e(TM, \nabla) = \frac{1}{2\pi}K\,dA$
(Definition~\ref{characteristic-classes:definition-euler-form} and Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-frames-surfaces}(2)).
\end{proof}

For example, on the torus of revolution the vector field $\partial_v$ that rotates the torus about its axis has no zeros, and indeed $\chi = 0$; on the round sphere the rotation field has two
zeros, at the poles, each of index $1$ (in a chart at a pole it is $-y\partial_x + x\partial_y$, a rotation of the source $x\partial_x + y\partial_y$, by
Lemma~\ref{characteristic-classes:lemma-index}(2)).

Many metrics on surfaces are given as a function times the Euclidean metric, and for them the Gaussian curvature has a simple formula.

\begin{proposition}[Conformal metrics on surfaces]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-conformal-curvature}
Let $\Omega \subseteq \RR^2$ be open, $u \colon \Omega \to \RR$ smooth, and $g = e^{2u}(dx^2 + dy^2)$. Then the Gaussian curvature of $g$ is
\[
K = -e^{-2u}\Delta u, \qquad \Delta = \partial_x^2 + \partial_y^2 .
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Here $g_{ij} = e^{2u}\delta_{ij}$, and the formula of Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-levi-civita} gives
$\Gamma^k_{ij} = u_i\delta_{jk} + u_j\delta_{ik} - u_k\delta_{ij}$, where $u_1 = u_x$ and $u_2 = u_y$. So $\nabla_{\partial_x}\partial_x = u_x\partial_x - u_y\partial_y$ and
$\nabla_{\partial_y}\partial_x = u_y\partial_x + u_x\partial_y$. The fields $e_1 = e^{-u}\partial_x$, $e_2 = e^{-u}\partial_y$ form a positively oriented orthonormal frame, and
\[
\nabla_{\partial_x}e_1 = -u_xe^{-u}\partial_x + e^{-u}(u_x\partial_x - u_y\partial_y) = -u_y\,e_2, \qquad
\nabla_{\partial_y}e_1 = -u_ye^{-u}\partial_x + e^{-u}(u_y\partial_x + u_x\partial_y) = u_x\,e_2 .
\]
So the $1$-form $\omega$ with $\nabla_Xe_1 = \omega(X)e_2$ is $\omega = -u_y\,dx + u_x\,dy$, and $d\omega = \Delta u\,dx \wedge dy$. The computation in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-gauss-bonnet} gives $d\omega = -K\,dA = -Ke^{2u}\,dx \wedge dy$, whence the formula.
\end{proof}

This is also the case $n = 2$ of Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-conformally-flat}, proved there with moving frames; the proof above is the same computation written with the
Christoffel symbols.

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-conformal-metrics}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{The hyperbolic plane.} On $H = \{(x, y) : y > 0\}$ with $(dx^2 + dy^2)/y^2$, $u = -\log y$ and $\Delta u = 1/y^2$, so $K = -1$.
\item \emph{The Poincar\'e disc.} On the unit disc $\mathbb{D}$ with $4(dx^2 + dy^2)/(1 - x^2 - y^2)^2$, $u = \log2 - \log(1 - r^2)$ with $r^2 = x^2 + y^2$. For a radial function
$\Delta = \partial_r^2 + r^{-1}\partial_r$, and $\Delta\log(1 - r^2) = -4/(1 - r^2)^2$, so $K = -\frac{(1 - r^2)^2}4 \cdot \frac4{(1 - r^2)^2} = -1$. The \emph{Cayley map}
$w = i(1 + z)/(1 - z)$ is an isometry from the disc onto $H$: with $z = x + iy$ and $w$ written as a complex number, $|dw/dz| = 2/|1 - z|^2$ and
$\operatorname{Im}w = (1 - |z|^2)/|1 - z|^2$, so $|dw|/\operatorname{Im}w = 2|dz|/(1 - |z|^2)$.
\item \emph{The sphere.} In the chart of stereographic projection (Example~\ref{general-topology:example-stereographic}) the round metric of $S^2$ is
$4(dx^2 + dy^2)/(1 + x^2 + y^2)^2$, by a direct computation; here $\Delta\log(1 + r^2) = 4/(1 + r^2)^2$, and $K = 1$, as it should.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{theorem}[Minding; Cartan]\label{riemannian-geometry:theorem-minding}
Let $(M, g)$ and $(N, h)$ be Riemannian manifolds of the same dimension $n$ with the same constant sectional curvature $\kappa$, and let $p \in M$, $q \in N$ and a linear isometry
$I \colon T_pM \to T_qN$ be given. Then there are neighbourhoods $U \ni p$ and $V \ni q$ and an isometry $F \colon U \to V$ with $F(p) = q$ and $d_pF = I$. In particular two surfaces
with the same constant Gaussian curvature are locally isometric, and every surface of constant curvature $0$, $1$ or $-1$ is locally isometric to the plane, the unit sphere or the
hyperbolic plane.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $s_\kappa$ be the solution of $s'' + \kappa s = 0$ with $s(0) = 0$ and $s'(0) = 1$ (so $s_\kappa(t) = t$, $\sin(\sqrt\kappa t)/\sqrt\kappa$ or $\sinh(\sqrt{-\kappa}t)/\sqrt{-\kappa}$). We
first compute the differential of $\exp_p$. Let $v \in T_pM$ be in the domain of $\exp_p$, $v \neq 0$, $\ell = |v|$, $u = v/\ell$, and $\gamma(t) = \exp_p(tv)$; for $w \in T_pM$ write
$w = w_\parallel + w_\perp$ with $w_\parallel$ a multiple of $u$ and $w_\perp \perp u$, and let $P_t$ be parallel transport along $\gamma$. By
Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-jacobi}(2), $J(t) = d_{tv}\exp_p(tw)$ is the Jacobi field along $\gamma$ with $J(0) = 0$ and $D_tJ(0) = w$. The fields
$J_1(t) = tP_t(w_\parallel)$ and $J_2(t) = \ell^{-1}s_\kappa(\ell t)P_t(w_\perp)$ are Jacobi fields: $P_t(u) = \dot\gamma(t)/\ell$, so $J_1$ is a multiple of $t\dot\gamma$, with $D_t^2J_1 = 0$ and
$R(J_1, \dot\gamma)\dot\gamma = 0$; and $P_t(w_\perp) \perp \dot\gamma$, so by the curvature formula of Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-curvature},
$R(J_2, \dot\gamma)\dot\gamma = \kappa|\dot\gamma|^2J_2 = \kappa\ell^2J_2$, while $D_t^2J_2 = \ell s_\kappa''(\ell t)P_t(w_\perp) = -\kappa\ell^2J_2$. They have the initial values $J_1(0) = J_2(0) = 0$,
$D_tJ_1(0) = w_\parallel$ and $D_tJ_2(0) = w_\perp$, so $J = J_1 + J_2$ by uniqueness, and since $P_1$ is an isometry and $P_1(w_\parallel) \perp P_1(w_\perp)$,
\[
|d_v\exp_p(w)|^2 = |J(1)|^2 = |w_\parallel|^2 + \Bigl(\frac{s_\kappa(\ell)}{\ell}\Bigr)^2|w_\perp|^2.
\]
The same formula holds on $N$. Now choose $\varepsilon > 0$ such that $\exp_p$ and $\exp_q$ map the balls of radius $\varepsilon$ diffeomorphically onto open sets $U$ and $V$
(Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-exponential}), and let $F = \exp_q \circ I \circ (\exp_p|_{B_\varepsilon})^{-1} \colon U \to V$, a diffeomorphism with $F(p) = q$ and $d_pF = I$.
For $x = \exp_p(v)$ and $w \in T_pM$, the chain rule gives $d_xF(d_v\exp_p(w)) = d_{Iv}\exp_q(Iw)$, and since $I$ preserves lengths and orthogonality, the formula above gives the same
length for $d_v\exp_p(w)$ and $d_{Iv}\exp_q(Iw)$ (for $v = 0$ both differentials are the identity). As the $d_v\exp_p(w)$ fill $T_xM$, $d_xF$ preserves lengths, hence inner products, so $F$
is an isometry.
\end{proof}

For instance the cylinder and the cone (without its vertex) are locally isometric to the plane, and the pseudosphere, the surface of revolution of the tractrix, to the hyperbolic
plane. Minding's theorem is local: the flat torus $\RR^2/\ZZ^2$ is locally but not globally isometric to the plane.

\begin{lemma}\label{riemannian-geometry:lemma-elliptic-point}
A compact surface $S \subseteq \RR^3$ has a point where $K > 0$. More precisely, if $p_0 \in S$ maximises the distance $R$ to the origin, then $K(p_0) \geq 1/R^2$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The function $|x|^2$ on $S$ has a maximum $R^2 > 0$ at some $p_0$, since $S$ is compact (and not a point). Its differential $2\langle p_0, -\rangle$ vanishes on $T_{p_0}S$, so
$\nu = p_0/R$ is a unit normal at $p_0$. For a unit-speed curve $\alpha$ in $S$ with $\alpha(0) = p_0$, the function $|\alpha|^2$ has a maximum at $0$, so
$0 \geq (|\alpha|^2)''(0) = 2\langle\alpha''(0), p_0\rangle + 2|\alpha'(0)|^2$, that is, $\langle\alpha''(0), \nu\rangle \leq -1/R$. The normal part of $\alpha''(0)$ is
$\mathrm{II}(\alpha'(0), \alpha'(0))$ (Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-gauss-formula}, applied to the covariant acceleration of $\alpha$ in $\RR^3$), so
$h(w, w) \leq -1/R$ for every unit tangent vector $w$ at $p_0$. Both principal curvatures are therefore $\leq -1/R$, and $K(p_0) = \kappa_1\kappa_2 \geq 1/R^2$
(Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-theorema-egregium}).
\end{proof}

So there is no compact surface in $\RR^3$ with $K \leq 0$ everywhere; in particular the flat torus cannot be embedded isometrically in $\RR^3$ as a smooth surface.

\begin{lemma}\label{riemannian-geometry:lemma-proper-covering}
Let $f \colon X \to Y$ be a local homeomorphism from a compact Hausdorff space to a connected Hausdorff space. Then $f$ is surjective and a covering map with finite fibres.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
A local homeomorphism is open, and a continuous map from a compact space to a Hausdorff space is closed (Proposition~\ref{general-topology:proposition-compact-basic}); so $f(X)$ is
open, closed and nonempty, hence equal to $Y$. A fibre $f^{-1}(y)$ is discrete, because $f$ is injective near each point, and closed in the compact $X$, hence finite, say
$\{x_1, \ldots, x_k\}$. Choose pairwise disjoint open sets $U_i \ni x_i$ mapped homeomorphically onto open sets $V_i \ni y$. The set $f(X \setminus \bigcup_iU_i)$ is compact, hence
closed, and does not contain $y$, so $V = \bigcap_iV_i \setminus f(X \setminus \bigcup_iU_i)$ is an open neighbourhood of $y$ with $f^{-1}(V) = \bigsqcup_i(U_i \cap f^{-1}(V))$, each piece
mapped homeomorphically onto $V$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Hadamard]\label{riemannian-geometry:theorem-hadamard}
Let $S \subseteq \RR^3$ be a compact connected surface with Gaussian curvature $K > 0$ everywhere. Then $S$ is orientable, its Gauss map $\nu \colon S \to S^2$ (for the unit normal
making the second fundamental form negative definite) is a diffeomorphism, $S$ is diffeomorphic to $S^2$, and $\iint_SK\,dA = 4\pi$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
At each point $K = \det S_\nu > 0$, so the self-adjoint shape operator is definite, and exactly one of the two unit normals makes it negative definite; near each point this normal is
$\pm$ a smooth local normal with a locally constant sign, so it is a unit normal field, and $S$ is orientable (Proposition~\ref{differential-forms:proposition-orientation-normal}). For
tangent $X$, $\overline{\nabla}_X\nu$ is tangent, because $\langle\nu, \nu\rangle = 1$, so $d_p\nu(X) = \overline{\nabla}_X\nu = -S_\nu X$
(Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-weingarten}); as $T_{\nu(p)}S^2 = \nu(p)^\perp = T_pS$ and $\det(-S_\nu) = K > 0$, $d_p\nu$ is an isomorphism, and $\nu$ is a local
diffeomorphism (Theorem~\ref{smooth-manifolds:theorem-inverse-function-manifolds}). By Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-proper-covering} it is a covering map, and since $S^2$ is simply
connected (Corollary~\ref{homotopy:corollary-spheres}) and $S$ connected, it has one sheet (Corollary~\ref{homotopy:corollary-lifting-consequences}(3)): $\nu$ is a bijective local
diffeomorphism, hence a diffeomorphism. Orient $S$ by $\nu$ and $S^2$ by its outward normal; then $T_pS$ and $T_{\nu(p)}S^2$ are the same oriented plane, $\nu^*(dA_{S^2}) = \det(d\nu)\,dA_S = K\,dA_S$,
and $\iint_SK\,dA = \int_{S^2}dA = 4\pi$ (Proposition~\ref{differential-forms:proposition-integral-well-defined}).
\end{proof}

The value $4\pi$ agrees with the Gauss--Bonnet theorem, $2\pi\chi(S^2)$. Hadamard also proved that such a surface is the boundary of a convex body: it lies on one side of each
of its tangent planes \cite[Chapter 5]{do-carmo-curves-surfaces}.

\begin{theorem}[Liebmann]\label{riemannian-geometry:theorem-liebmann}
A compact connected surface in $\RR^3$ with constant Gaussian curvature $K$ is a round sphere of radius $1/\sqrt{K}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Sketch of proof]
By Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-elliptic-point} the constant $K$ is positive. With the unit normal $\nu$ of Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-hadamard} the
principal curvatures are negative; write them as $-\lambda_1 \leq -\lambda_2$, so that $\lambda_1 \geq \lambda_2 > 0$ and $\lambda_1\lambda_2 = K$. The continuous function $\lambda_1$
attains its maximum at some $p$, where $\lambda_2 = K/\lambda_1$ attains its minimum. \emph{Hilbert's lemma}, a computation with the Codazzi equation in principal coordinates, says
that at a non-umbilic point ($\lambda_1 > \lambda_2$) where $\lambda_1$ has a local maximum and $\lambda_2$ a local minimum, $K \leq 0$. So $p$ is umbilic and
$\lambda_1(p) = \sqrt K$. Hence $\lambda_1 \leq \sqrt K$ everywhere, while $\lambda_1^2 \geq \lambda_1\lambda_2 = K$; so $\lambda_1 = \lambda_2 = \sqrt K$ everywhere, every point is umbilic,
and $S_\nu = -\sqrt K\,\id$. Then $d\nu = -S_\nu = \sqrt K\,\id$, so $x - \nu/\sqrt K$ is constant on the connected surface $S$: every point lies at distance $1/\sqrt K$ from this
fixed point. The omitted verification is Hilbert's lemma \cite[Chapter 5]{do-carmo-curves-surfaces}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Hilbert]\label{riemannian-geometry:theorem-hilbert}
There is no complete surface of constant negative Gaussian curvature isometrically immersed in $\RR^3$. In particular the hyperbolic plane cannot be realised as a complete
surface in $\RR^3$.
\end{theorem}

This is \cite{hilbert-1901}; see \cite[Chapter 5]{do-carmo-curves-surfaces} for a complete proof. In outline: on such a surface the asymptotic directions (where $h(w, w) = 0$) give
coordinates in which the metric has the form $du^2 + 2\cos\theta\,du\,dv + dv^2$, and the Gauss equation becomes the sine-Gordon equation $\theta_{uv} = \sin\theta$; these
coordinates cover the whole hyperbolic plane, whose area is infinite, while the Gauss--Bonnet theorem bounds the area of every coordinate quadrilateral by $2\pi$, a contradiction.
Incomplete surfaces of constant negative curvature do exist, such as the pseudosphere; and by Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-minding} each is locally isometric to
the hyperbolic plane.

\section{Minimal surfaces}\label{riemannian-geometry:section-minimal}

A soap film spanning a wire takes the shape that minimises its area. Its surface is therefore a critical point of the area, and the first variation formula below
identifies these critical points: they are the hypersurfaces with mean curvature $H = 0$, the \emph{minimal} ones. We give the classical examples, show that a minimal
graph has the least area among all surfaces with the same boundary, compute the second variation, and prove Bernstein's theorem: a minimal graph over the whole plane is
a plane. Throughout, $M \subseteq \RR^{n+1}$ is a hypersurface with unit normal $\nu$, shape operator $S = -d\nu$ and mean curvature $H = \frac1n\operatorname{tr}S$
(Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-hypersurface-curvatures} and Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-weingarten-coordinates}), and we write
$|S|^2 = \operatorname{tr}(S^2) = \sum_i\kappa_i^2$.

\begin{proposition}[First and second variation of area]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-variation-area}
Let $x \colon \Omega \to \RR^{n+1}$ be a parametrisation of $M$, $\phi$ a smooth function with compact support $K \subseteq \Omega$, and $x_s = x + s\phi\nu$ (with
$\nu = \nu \circ x$). For small $|s|$ the matrix $g(s)_{ij} = \langle\partial_ix_s, \partial_jx_s\rangle$ is positive definite on $K$; let
$A(s) = \int_K\sqrt{\det g(s)}\,du$, the area of the piece $x_s(K)$. Then, with $dA = \sqrt{\det g}\,du$ and $|\nabla\phi|^2 = \sum_{i,j}g^{ij}\partial_i\phi\,\partial_j\phi$,
\[
A^{\prime}(0) = -n\int_KH\phi\,dA, \qquad\text{and, if } H = 0, \qquad A^{\prime\prime}(0) = \int_K\bigl(|\nabla\phi|^2 - |S|^2\phi^2\bigr)\,dA .
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write $x_i = \partial_ix$ and $\nu_i = \partial_i\nu$. Then $\partial_ix_s = x_i + s(\partial_i\phi\,\nu + \phi\nu_i)$. Since $\langle x_i, \nu\rangle = 0$, $\langle\nu_i, \nu\rangle = 0$
and $|\nu| = 1$,
\[
g(s)_{ij} = g_{ij} + s\phi\bigl(\langle x_i, \nu_j\rangle + \langle\nu_i, x_j\rangle\bigr) + s^2\bigl(\partial_i\phi\,\partial_j\phi + \phi^2\langle\nu_i, \nu_j\rangle\bigr).
\]
By Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-weingarten-coordinates}, $\langle x_i, \nu_j\rangle = -h_{ij}$, and $\nu_i = -\sum_kS^k_ix_k$ with $(S^k_i) = g^{-1}h$ the
matrix of $S$, so $\langle\nu_i, \nu_j\rangle = (S^Tg\,S)_{ij} = (hg^{-1}h)_{ij}$. Hence $g(s) = g(I + sY + s^2Z)$ with $Y = -2\phi\,g^{-1}h$ and
$Z = g^{-1}(\nabla\phi\,\nabla\phi^T) + \phi^2(g^{-1}h)^2$, where $\nabla\phi$ is the column $(\partial_i\phi)$; in particular $g(s)$ is positive definite on $K$ for small $|s|$. For a
matrix $X$, $\det(I + X) = 1 + \operatorname{tr}X + \frac12\bigl((\operatorname{tr}X)^2 - \operatorname{tr}(X^2)\bigr) + O(|X|^3)$ (expand the characteristic polynomial, or use
the eigenvalues), and $\sqrt{1 + \epsilon} = 1 + \frac\epsilon2 - \frac{\epsilon^2}8 + O(\epsilon^3)$. With $X = sY + s^2Z$ this gives
\[
\sqrt{\det g(s)} = \sqrt{\det g}\,\Bigl(1 + \frac s2\operatorname{tr}Y + s^2\Bigl(\frac12\operatorname{tr}Z + \frac18(\operatorname{tr}Y)^2 - \frac14\operatorname{tr}(Y^2)\Bigr) + O(s^3)\Bigr),
\]
uniformly on $K$. Now $\operatorname{tr}Y = -2\phi\operatorname{tr}S = -2nH\phi$, $\operatorname{tr}(Y^2) = 4\phi^2|S|^2$ and $\operatorname{tr}Z = |\nabla\phi|^2 + \phi^2|S|^2$. So the
coefficient of $s$ is $-nH\phi$, and if $H = 0$ the coefficient of $s^2$ is $\frac12(|\nabla\phi|^2 + \phi^2|S|^2) - \phi^2|S|^2 = \frac12(|\nabla\phi|^2 - |S|^2\phi^2)$. Integrating
over $K$ gives $A^{\prime}(0)$ and $A^{\prime\prime}(0)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-second-variation-general}
\emph{The second variation without $H = 0$, and the Jacobi operator.} The computation in the proof gives, without assuming $H = 0$, the coefficient of $s^2$ as
$\frac12\operatorname{tr}Z + \frac18(\operatorname{tr}Y)^2 - \frac14\operatorname{tr}(Y^2) = \frac12(|\nabla\phi|^2 - |S|^2\phi^2 + n^2H^2\phi^2)$, so
\[
A^{\prime\prime}(0) = \int_K\bigl(|\nabla\phi|^2 - |S|^2\phi^2 + n^2H^2\phi^2\bigr)\,dA .
\]
For the sphere of radius $r$ in $\RR^3$ and $\phi = 1$ (the computation is local, so it applies to the whole sphere), $|S|^2 = 2/r^2$ and $n^2H^2 = 4/r^2$, so $A^{\prime\prime}(0) = \frac{2}{r^2} \cdot 4\pi r^2 = 8\pi$,
in agreement with $A(s) = 4\pi(r + s)^2$. When $H = 0$, Green's formula (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-riemannian-divergence}, applied on a compact domain with smooth boundary
containing the support of $\phi$) gives $\int|\nabla\phi|^2 = -\int\phi\,\Delta\phi$, hence
\[
A^{\prime\prime}(0) = -\int_K\phi\,L\phi\,dA, \qquad L = \Delta + |S|^2,
\]
the \emph{Jacobi operator}, or stability operator, of the minimal hypersurface: $M$ is \emph{stable} (the area does not decrease to second order under compactly supported normal variations) if and only if
$-L$ is nonnegative on such $\phi$. For instance a minimal graph is stable, since it minimises area (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-graphs-minimise} below).
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}\label{riemannian-geometry:definition-minimal}
A hypersurface $M \subseteq \RR^{n+1}$ is \emph{minimal} if $H = 0$ everywhere. A minimal hypersurface is \emph{stable} if $A^{\prime\prime}(0) \geq 0$ for all variations as in
Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-variation-area}.
\end{definition}

By the proposition, $M$ is minimal if and only if $A^{\prime}(0) = 0$ for every such variation: if $H(u_0) \neq 0$, a function $\phi \geq 0$ supported where $H$ has the sign of
$H(u_0)$, with $\phi(u_0) > 0$, gives $A^{\prime}(0) \neq 0$. The word ``minimal'' refers to this critical point property; a minimal surface need not minimise area. For a
minimal surface in $\RR^3$ the principal curvatures are $\pm\kappa$, so $|S|^2 = 2\kappa^2 = -2K$, and the second variation is $\int_K(|\nabla\phi|^2 + 2K\phi^2)\,dA$.

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-minimal-surfaces}
We use Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-surface-coordinates}.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Planes} are minimal, with $S = 0$.
\item \emph{The catenoid}, the surface of revolution of the catenary, $x(u, v) = (\cosh v\cos u, \cosh v\sin u, v)$. Here $E = G = \cosh^2v$, $F = 0$, the unit normal is
$\nu = (\cos u, \sin u, -\sinh v)/\cosh v$, and $h_{11} = -1$, $h_{12} = 0$, $h_{22} = 1$. So $H = (Eh_{22} + Gh_{11})/(2EG) = 0$, and $K = -1/\cosh^4v$.
\item \emph{The helicoid}, swept out by a line rotating about an axis while moving along it, $x(u, v) = (v\cos u, v\sin u, u)$. Here $E = 1 + v^2$, $F = 0$, $G = 1$,
$\nu = (-\sin u, \cos u, -v)/\sqrt{1 + v^2}$, and $h_{11} = h_{22} = 0$, $h_{12} = 1/\sqrt{1 + v^2}$. So $H = 0$ and $K = -1/(1 + v^2)^2$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{proposition}[Minimal surface equation]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-minimal-surface-equation}
Let $u \colon \Omega \to \RR$ be smooth on an open $\Omega \subseteq \RR^2$, write $p = u_x$, $q = u_y$ and $W = \sqrt{1 + p^2 + q^2}$, and give the graph of $u$ the upward unit
normal $\nu = (-p, -q, 1)/W$. Then its mean curvature is
\[
2H = \frac{(1 + q^2)u_{xx} - 2pq\,u_{xy} + (1 + p^2)u_{yy}}{W^3} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\Bigl(\frac pW\Bigr) + \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\Bigl(\frac qW\Bigr).
\]
So the graph is minimal if and only if $u$ solves the \emph{minimal surface equation} $(1 + q^2)u_{xx} - 2pq\,u_{xy} + (1 + p^2)u_{yy} = 0$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For $x(x, y) = (x, y, u(x, y))$: $E = 1 + p^2$, $F = pq$, $G = 1 + q^2$, $EG - F^2 = W^2$, and $h_{11} = u_{xx}/W$, $h_{12} = u_{xy}/W$, $h_{22} = u_{yy}/W$, since the second
derivatives of $x$ are $(0, 0, u_{xx})$, $(0, 0, u_{xy})$, $(0, 0, u_{yy})$. The formula for $H$ in
Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-surface-coordinates} gives the first expression. For the second,
$\partial_x(p/W) = (u_{xx}W^2 - p(pu_{xx} + qu_{xy}))/W^3$ and $\partial_y(q/W) = (u_{yy}W^2 - q(pu_{xy} + qu_{yy}))/W^3$, whose sum is the first expression, as
$W^2 - p^2 = 1 + q^2$ and $W^2 - q^2 = 1 + p^2$.
\end{proof}

For example \emph{Scherk's surface}, the graph of $u = \log\cos y - \log\cos x$ over the square $|x|, |y| < \pi/2$, is minimal: $p = \tan x$, $q = -\tan y$, $u_{xy} = 0$, and
$(1 + q^2)u_{xx} + (1 + p^2)u_{yy} = \sec^2y\sec^2x - \sec^2x\sec^2y = 0$. It tends to $\pm\infty$ on the sides of the square, and cannot be extended to a larger domain.

\begin{proposition}[Minimal graphs minimise area]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-graphs-minimise}
Let $\Omega \subseteq \RR^2$ be open and convex, $u \colon \Omega \to \RR$ a solution of the minimal surface equation, and $G \subseteq \Omega \times \RR$ a compact piece of its
graph that is a surface with boundary, oriented by the upward normal. Let $\Sigma \subseteq \Omega \times \RR$ be a compact oriented surface with boundary
(Definition~\ref{differential-forms:definition-manifold-with-boundary}) with $\partial\Sigma = \partial G$ as oriented curves. Then $\mathrm{Area}(\Sigma) \geq \mathrm{Area}(G)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Extend the unit normal of the graph to the unit vector field $N(x, y, z) = (-p, -q, 1)/W$ on $\Omega \times \RR$, independent of $z$, and let
$\omega = \iota_N(dx \wedge dy \wedge dz)$. As in the proof of Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-divergence},
$d\omega = (\operatorname{div}N)\,dx \wedge dy \wedge dz$, and $\operatorname{div}N = -\partial_x(p/W) - \partial_y(q/W) = -2H = 0$ by
Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-minimal-surface-equation}. The open set $\Omega \times \RR$ is convex, so $\omega = d\eta$ for a $1$-form $\eta$ by the Poincar\'e lemma
(Theorem~\ref{de-rham:theorem-homotopy-invariance}). By Stokes' theorem (Theorem~\ref{differential-forms:theorem-stokes}),
$\int_\Sigma\omega = \int_{\partial\Sigma}\eta = \int_{\partial G}\eta = \int_G\omega$. By Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-flux}, on a surface with unit normal $n$
defining its orientation, $\omega$ restricts to $\langle N, n\rangle\,dA$. On $G$, $n = N$, so $\int_G\omega = \mathrm{Area}(G)$; on $\Sigma$, $\langle N, n\rangle \leq 1$, so
$\int_\Sigma\omega \leq \mathrm{Area}(\Sigma)$.
\end{proof}

The form $\omega$ is a \emph{calibration}: a closed form whose restriction to every surface is at most the area form, with equality on the graph. In particular minimal graphs
are stable, since the surfaces $x_s(K)$ of Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-variation-area} stay in $\Omega \times \RR$ and have the same boundary as the graph for
small $|s|$. The comparison only involves surfaces inside the cylinder $\Omega \times \RR$ over a convex domain, where the calibration is defined; a minimal surface
that is not a graph, such as the catenoid, need not have this property.

The minimal surface equation is a nonlinear elliptic equation, and its entire solutions are much more rigid than harmonic functions: $x^2 - y^2$ is harmonic on all of
$\RR^2$ without being affine, but the only minimal graphs over the whole plane are planes. The proof below is Nitsche's \cite{nitsche-1957}; it reduces the statement to
Liouville's theorem through a theorem of J\"orgens on the Monge--Amp\`ere equation.

\begin{lemma}[J\"orgens]\label{riemannian-geometry:lemma-jorgens}
Let $\varphi \colon \RR^2 \to \RR$ be smooth with $\varphi_{xx} > 0$ and $\varphi_{xx}\varphi_{yy} - \varphi_{xy}^2 = 1$ everywhere. Then the Hessian of $\varphi$ is constant.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write $a = \varphi_{xx}$, $b = \varphi_{xy}$, $c = \varphi_{yy}$, so $a > 0$ and $ac - b^2 = 1$; then $c > 0$, the Hessian $\mathcal{H}$ is positive definite, and
$a + c \geq 2\sqrt{ac} \geq 2$.

\emph{Step 1: the Lewy map.} Let $L \colon \RR^2 \to \RR^2$, $L(z) = z + \nabla\varphi(z)$. For $z_1, z_2 \in \RR^2$,
$\langle\nabla\varphi(z_2) - \nabla\varphi(z_1), z_2 - z_1\rangle = \int_0^1\langle\mathcal{H}(z_1 + t(z_2 - z_1))(z_2 - z_1), z_2 - z_1\rangle\,dt \geq 0$, so
\[
|L(z_2) - L(z_1)|^2 = |z_2 - z_1|^2 + 2\langle\nabla\varphi(z_2) - \nabla\varphi(z_1), z_2 - z_1\rangle + |\nabla\varphi(z_2) - \nabla\varphi(z_1)|^2 \geq |z_2 - z_1|^2 .
\]
So $L$ is injective, and its image is closed: if $L(z_k) \to w$, then $(z_k)$ is a Cauchy sequence, $z_k \to z$ and $L(z) = w$. Its differential $I + \mathcal{H}$ is positive
definite, hence invertible, so $L$ is a local diffeomorphism (Theorem~\ref{real-analysis:theorem-inverse-function}) and its image is open. As $\RR^2$ is connected, $L$ is a
diffeomorphism of $\RR^2$ onto itself.

\emph{Step 2: a holomorphic function.} Write $(\xi, \eta) = L(x, y)$ and $\zeta = \xi + i\eta$, and define $F(\zeta) = w + iv$ with $w = x - \varphi_x$ and $v = \varphi_y - y$,
where $(x, y) = L^{-1}(\xi, \eta)$; $F$ is smooth. From $d(\xi, \eta) = (I + \mathcal{H})\,d(x, y)$ and $d(w, v) = N\,d(x, y)$ with
$N = \bigl(\begin{smallmatrix} 1 - a & -b\\ b & c - 1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$, the Jacobian matrix of $(w, v)$ with respect to $(\xi, \eta)$ is
\[
N(I + \mathcal{H})^{-1} = \frac1{2 + a + c}\begin{pmatrix} 1 - a & -b\\ b & c - 1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix} 1 + c & -b\\ -b & 1 + a\end{pmatrix}
= \frac1{2 + a + c}\begin{pmatrix} c - a & -2b\\ 2b & c - a\end{pmatrix},
\]
using $\det(I + \mathcal{H}) = 1 + a + c + ac - b^2 = 2 + a + c$ and $ac - b^2 = 1$ to simplify the entries. This matrix satisfies the Cauchy--Riemann equations, so $F$ is an
entire function (Proposition~\ref{complex-analysis:proposition-cauchy-riemann}), with
\[
F^{\prime} = \frac{(c - a) + 2ib}{2 + a + c}, \qquad |F^{\prime}|^2 = \frac{(a + c)^2 - 4(ac - b^2)}{(a + c + 2)^2} = \frac{a + c - 2}{a + c + 2} < 1 .
\]

\emph{Step 3: Liouville.} The derivative $F^{\prime}$ is entire (Theorem~\ref{complex-analysis:theorem-analytic}) and bounded, hence constant
(Corollary~\ref{complex-analysis:corollary-liouville}). So $\rho = |F^{\prime}|^2 < 1$ is constant, hence so is $a + c = 2(1 + \rho)/(1 - \rho)$, and then the real and imaginary parts
of $F^{\prime}$ show that $c - a$ and $b$ are constant. So $a$, $b$ and $c$ are constant.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Bernstein]\label{riemannian-geometry:theorem-bernstein}
Let $u \colon \RR^2 \to \RR$ be a smooth solution of the minimal surface equation on the whole plane. Then $u$ is affine, $u(x, y) = \alpha x + \beta y + \gamma$: an entire
minimal graph is a plane.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
With the notation of Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-minimal-surface-equation}, write $\mathcal{M} = (1 + q^2)u_{xx} - 2pq\,u_{xy} + (1 + p^2)u_{yy}$, and
consider the smooth functions
\[
A = \frac{1 + p^2}{W}, \qquad B = \frac{pq}{W}, \qquad C = \frac{1 + q^2}{W}.
\]
A direct computation, using $p_x = u_{xx}$, $p_y = q_x = u_{xy}$ and $q_y = u_{yy}$, gives
\[
\frac{\partial A}{\partial y} - \frac{\partial B}{\partial x} = -\frac{q\mathcal{M}}{W^3}, \qquad \frac{\partial C}{\partial x} - \frac{\partial B}{\partial y} = -\frac{p\mathcal{M}}{W^3}
\]
(for the first, both $W^3\partial_yA$ and $W^3\partial_xB$ are polynomials in $p, q, u_{xx}, u_{xy}, u_{yy}$, and their difference is $-q\mathcal{M}$; the second follows by
exchanging $x$ and $y$). As $\mathcal{M} = 0$, the $1$-forms $A\,dx + B\,dy$ and $B\,dx + C\,dy$ are closed on $\RR^2$, hence exact by the Poincar\'e lemma
(Theorem~\ref{de-rham:theorem-homotopy-invariance}): there are smooth $P$, $Q$ with $dP = A\,dx + B\,dy$ and $dQ = B\,dx + C\,dy$. Then $P\,dx + Q\,dy$ is closed, since
$P_y = B = Q_x$, so it is $d\varphi$ for a smooth $\varphi$. Its Hessian is
\[
\varphi_{xx} = A = \frac{1 + p^2}{W} > 0, \qquad \varphi_{xy} = B, \qquad \varphi_{yy} = C, \qquad \varphi_{xx}\varphi_{yy} - \varphi_{xy}^2 = \frac{(1 + p^2)(1 + q^2) - p^2q^2}{W^2} = 1 .
\]
By Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-jorgens}, $A$, $B$ and $C$ are constant. Then $A + C = (2 + p^2 + q^2)/W = W + 1/W$ is constant, and so is $W \geq 1$, since
$t \mapsto t + 1/t$ is injective on $[1, \infty)$. Hence $p^2 = AW - 1$ and $q^2 = CW - 1$ are constant, and so are $p$ and $q$, being continuous on the connected plane with
constant square. So $u$ has constant gradient and is affine.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-bernstein-problem}
The \emph{Bernstein problem} asks whether an entire minimal graph over $\RR^n$, a solution of $\operatorname{div}(\nabla u/\sqrt{1 + |\nabla u|^2}) = 0$ on $\RR^n$, must be
affine. The answer is yes for $n \leq 7$ (De Giorgi for $n = 3$, Almgren for $n = 4$, Simons \cite{simons-1968} for $n \leq 7$) and no for $n \geq 8$: Bombieri, De Giorgi and
Giusti \cite{bombieri-de-giorgi-giusti-1969} constructed a non-affine entire minimal graph over $\RR^8$, related to the area-minimising cone over
$S^3 \times S^3 \subseteq \RR^8$. The existence of minimal surfaces spanning a given curve, Plateau's problem, is a separate and deep theory.
\end{remark}

\emph{Minimal hypersurfaces in a Riemannian manifold.} So far the ambient space was $\RR^{n+1}$. In a curved ambient manifold $\overline{M}$ a new phenomenon appears: the normal
geodesics leaving a hypersurface converge or diverge according to the curvature of $\overline{M}$, exactly as nearby geodesics do in the Jacobi equation
(Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-jacobi}), and this changes the area of the pushed hypersurface to second order. The relevant curvature is the average over the
tangent directions of the sectional curvatures of the planes containing the normal: for an orthonormal basis $e_1, \ldots, e_n$ of $T_pM$ and the unit normal $\nu$,
\[
\overline{\mathrm{Ric}}(\nu, \nu) = \sum_{a=1}^n\overline{R}(e_a, \nu, \nu, e_a) = \sum_{a=1}^n\overline{K}(e_a, \nu),
\]
by Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-curvature-tensor} applied to the orthonormal basis $e_1, \ldots, e_n, \nu$ of $T_p\overline{M}$ (the term with $\nu$ vanishes). The
second variation formula below is the form in which the area variation is usually quoted.

\begin{proposition}[Second variation of area in a Riemannian manifold]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-variation-area-riemannian}
Let $\overline{M}$ be a Riemannian manifold of dimension $n + 1$, with Levi-Civita connection $\overline{\nabla}$ and curvature $\overline{R}$, and let $M \subseteq \overline{M}$ be a
hypersurface with unit normal field $\nu$, shape operator $S$ and mean curvature $H$ (Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-hypersurface-curvatures}). Let
$x \colon \Omega \to M$ be a parametrisation, $\Omega \subseteq \RR^n$ open, and let $F \colon \Omega \times (-\varepsilon, \varepsilon) \to \overline{M}$ be smooth with $F(u, 0) = x(u)$,
with $F(u, s) = x(u)$ for $u$ outside a compact set $K \subseteq \Omega$, and with $\partial_sF(u, 0) = \phi(u)\nu(x(u))$ for a smooth function $\phi$. Let
$g(s)_{ij} = \langle\partial_iF, \partial_jF\rangle$, which is positive definite on $K$ for small $|s|$, and $A(s) = \int_K\sqrt{\det g(s)}\,du$. Then $A^{\prime}(0) = -n\int_KH\phi\,dA$,
and if $H = 0$,
\[
A^{\prime\prime}(0) = \int_K\Bigl(|\nabla\phi|^2 - \bigl(|S|^2 + \overline{\mathrm{Ric}}(\nu, \nu)\bigr)\phi^2\Bigr)\,dA .
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Step 1: the setting.} Write $F_i = \partial_iF$ and $T = \partial_sF$, vector fields along $F$, and $D_s$, $D_i$ for the covariant derivatives along the curves
$s \mapsto F(u, s)$ and $u^i \mapsto F(u, s)$ (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-covariant-derivative-curves}). Two facts will be used: $D_sF_i = D_iT$ by the symmetry
lemma (Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-symmetry}), and $D_sD_iT - D_iD_sT = \overline{R}(T, F_i)T$ by Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-curvature-families}. At $s = 0$ we
write $x_i = \partial_ix$, $h_{ij} = h(x_i, x_j)$, and $Z = D_sT|_{s=0}$, the acceleration of the curves $s \mapsto F(u, s)$; for $u \notin K$ these curves are constant, so $T$ and $Z$
vanish there.

\emph{Step 2: derivatives of the metric.} By the metric property of $D_s$ and the symmetry lemma,
\[
g^{\prime}_{ij} = \langle D_iT, F_j\rangle + \langle F_i, D_jT\rangle, \qquad g^{\prime\prime}_{ij} = \langle D_sD_iT, F_j\rangle + \langle F_i, D_sD_jT\rangle + 2\langle D_iT, D_jT\rangle .
\]

\emph{Step 3: derivatives of the area density.} For a smooth family of invertible matrices, $(\det g)^{\prime} = \det g\,\operatorname{tr}(g^{-1}g^{\prime})$: indeed
$\det g(s + t) = \det g(s)\det\bigl(I + g(s)^{-1}(g(s + t) - g(s))\bigr)$ and $\det(I + X) = 1 + \operatorname{tr}X + O(|X|^2)$. Also $(g^{-1})^{\prime} = -g^{-1}g^{\prime}g^{-1}$,
obtained by differentiating $g^{-1}g = I$. Hence
\[
\frac{(\sqrt{\det g})^{\prime}}{\sqrt{\det g}} = \frac12\operatorname{tr}(g^{-1}g^{\prime}), \qquad
\frac{(\sqrt{\det g})^{\prime\prime}}{\sqrt{\det g}} = \frac14\bigl(\operatorname{tr}(g^{-1}g^{\prime})\bigr)^2 + \frac12\operatorname{tr}(g^{-1}g^{\prime\prime}) - \frac12\operatorname{tr}\bigl((g^{-1}g^{\prime})^2\bigr).
\]
For a symmetric bilinear form $B$ on $T_pM$, $\sum_{i,j}g^{ij}B(x_i, x_j) = \sum_aB(e_a, e_a)$ for any orthonormal basis $(e_a)$, the trace of $B$ with respect to the metric; we use
this repeatedly.

\emph{Step 4: the values at $s = 0$.} Since $|\nu| = 1$, $D_i\nu = \overline{\nabla}_{x_i}\nu$ is orthogonal to $\nu$, so it is tangent, and $D_i\nu = -Sx_i$ by
Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-shape-operator}. So $D_iT = (\partial_i\phi)\nu - \phi\,Sx_i$, and
\[
\langle D_iT, x_j\rangle = -\phi h_{ij}, \qquad \langle D_iT, D_jT\rangle = \partial_i\phi\,\partial_j\phi + \phi^2\langle Sx_i, Sx_j\rangle .
\]
Hence $g^{-1}g^{\prime}$ is the matrix of $-2\phi S$, with trace $-2nH\phi$, and $\operatorname{tr}((g^{-1}g^{\prime})^2) = 4\phi^2|S|^2$. This already gives
$A^{\prime}(0) = \int_K\frac12(-2nH\phi)\,dA = -n\int_KH\phi\,dA$, differentiating under the integral sign (the integrand is smooth on $K \times (-\varepsilon^{\prime}, \varepsilon^{\prime})$). Further,
$\sum g^{ij}\langle D_iT, D_jT\rangle = |\nabla\phi|^2 + \phi^2\sum_a|Se_a|^2 = |\nabla\phi|^2 + \phi^2|S|^2$, as $S$ is self-adjoint. Finally
$D_sD_iT = D_iZ + \phi^2\overline{R}(\nu, x_i)\nu$ at $s = 0$, and
\[
\sum_{i,j}g^{ij}\langle\overline{R}(\nu, x_i)\nu, x_j\rangle = \sum_a\overline{R}(\nu, e_a, \nu, e_a) = -\sum_a\overline{R}(e_a, \nu, \nu, e_a) = -\overline{\mathrm{Ric}}(\nu, \nu).
\]
So $\frac12\operatorname{tr}(g^{-1}g^{\prime\prime}) = \sum g^{ij}\langle D_iZ, x_j\rangle - \phi^2\,\overline{\mathrm{Ric}}(\nu, \nu) + |\nabla\phi|^2 + \phi^2|S|^2$.

\emph{Step 5: the term with $Z$.} Write $Z = Z^\top + f\nu$ with $Z^\top$ tangent to $M$. Then $\langle D_i(f\nu), x_j\rangle = f\langle D_i\nu, x_j\rangle = -fh_{ij}$, whose trace is
$-nHf$. By the Gauss formula (Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-gauss-formula}), the tangential part of $D_iZ^\top = \overline{\nabla}_{x_i}Z^\top$ is $\nabla_{x_i}Z^\top$, so the trace
of $\langle D_iZ^\top, x_j\rangle$ is $\operatorname{div}_MZ^\top$ (Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-gradient-hessian}(3)). So $\sum g^{ij}\langle D_iZ, x_j\rangle = \operatorname{div}_MZ^\top - nHf$.

\emph{Step 6: conclusion.} Now let $H = 0$. Collecting Steps 3--5,
\[
\frac{(\sqrt{\det g})^{\prime\prime}(0)}{\sqrt{\det g}} = 0 + \operatorname{div}_MZ^\top - \phi^2\,\overline{\mathrm{Ric}}(\nu, \nu) + |\nabla\phi|^2 + \phi^2|S|^2 - 2\phi^2|S|^2 .
\]
The vector field $Z^\top$ vanishes outside $K$, so $\int_K\operatorname{div}_MZ^\top\,dA = 0$ by the divergence theorem
(Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-riemannian-divergence}, applied on a compact domain with smooth boundary in $x(\Omega)$ containing $x(K)$ in its interior). Differentiating
twice under the integral sign gives the formula. (For the variation $F(u, s) = \exp_{x(u)}(s\phi(u)\nu(x(u)))$, which exists for small $|s|$ as at the beginning of
Section~\ref{riemannian-geometry:section-variation}, the curves $s \mapsto F(u, s)$ are geodesics, $Z = 0$, and Step 5 is not needed.)
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-variation-area-riemannian}
\emph{Reading the formula: what each term means.} Write $Q(\phi) = \int_K(|\nabla\phi|^2 - (|S|^2 + \overline{\mathrm{Ric}}(\nu, \nu))\phi^2)\,dA$ for the second variation. Its three terms
describe three different effects of pushing $M$ by the amount $\phi$ in the normal direction.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{$|\nabla\phi|^2$: tilting.} Pushing different points by different amounts tilts the hypersurface, and a tilted piece has more area. For a flat piece of a hyperplane in
$\RR^{n+1}$ this is exact: the pushed hypersurface is the graph of $s\phi$, whose area element is $\sqrt{1 + s^2|\nabla\phi|^2} = 1 + \frac{s^2}2|\nabla\phi|^2 + O(s^4)$, with second
derivative $|\nabla\phi|^2$ at $s = 0$. This term is always $\geq 0$, and it is the only one for a hyperplane in $\RR^{n+1}$, which is therefore stable.
\item \emph{$-|S|^2\phi^2$: the shape of $M$.} Push a piece of a hypersurface in $\RR^{n+1}$ by the constant amount $s$: then $\partial_i(x + s\nu) = x_i - sSx_i$, so the area element is
multiplied by $\det(I - sS) = \prod_i(1 - s\kappa_i) = 1 - s\sum_i\kappa_i + s^2\sum_{i<j}\kappa_i\kappa_j + O(s^3)$. If $H = 0$, then
$\sum_{i<j}\kappa_i\kappa_j = \frac12((\sum_i\kappa_i)^2 - \sum_i\kappa_i^2) = -\frac12|S|^2$, and the second derivative is $-|S|^2$. Geometrically, a minimal surface curves equally in
opposite directions ($\kappa_2 = -\kappa_1$ in $\RR^3$, like a saddle); pushing it expands it in one principal direction and contracts it in the other, the first-order effects cancel,
and the second-order effect is a loss of area.
\item \emph{$-\overline{\mathrm{Ric}}(\nu, \nu)\phi^2$: the curvature of the ambient space.} The pushed hypersurface consists of points on the normal geodesics. If the ambient sectional
curvatures $\overline{K}(e_a, \nu)$ are positive, these geodesics converge, as the meridians do when one pushes the equator of a sphere towards a pole, and the area decreases; the
amount is the sum $\sum_a\overline{K}(e_a, \nu) = \overline{\mathrm{Ric}}(\nu, \nu)$ over the $n$ tangent directions (Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-equator-unstable} below
computes it exactly). In a space of constant curvature $\kappa$, $\overline{\mathrm{Ric}}(\nu, \nu) = n\kappa$ (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-ricci-constant-curvature}),
so $Q(\phi) = \int_K(|\nabla\phi|^2 - (|S|^2 + n\kappa)\phi^2)\,dA$.
\end{enumerate}

\emph{The Jacobi operator and the notations of the literature.} Green's formula (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-riemannian-divergence}, on a compact domain with smooth
boundary containing $x(K)$) gives $\int|\nabla\phi|^2\,dA = -\int\phi\,\Delta\phi\,dA$ for compactly supported $\phi$, where $\Delta = \operatorname{div}\operatorname{grad}$ is the Laplacian of $M$
with its induced metric (the \emph{Laplace--Beltrami operator}). Hence
\[
Q(\phi) = -\int_K\bigl(\Delta\phi + |S|^2\phi + \overline{\mathrm{Ric}}(\nu, \nu)\phi\bigr)\phi\,dA = -\int_K\phi\,L\phi\,dA, \qquad L = \Delta + |S|^2 + \overline{\mathrm{Ric}}(\nu, \nu),
\]
the \emph{Jacobi operator}, or stability operator, of $M$. A minimal hypersurface is \emph{stable} if $Q(\phi) \geq 0$ for all compactly supported $\phi$. Both curvature terms make stability
harder; positive Ricci curvature of $\overline{M}$ pushes towards instability, negative towards stability. The literature uses several equivalent conventions, and it helps to have a
dictionary:
\begin{enumerate}
\item The second fundamental form is often written $A$, and $|A|^2 = \sum_{i,j}h_{ij}h^{ij} = \sum_i\kappa_i^2$ is our $|S|^2$ (the norm of $h$ and of $S$ agree, as $S$ is $h$ with an
index raised). The variation function is often written $u$, the Laplacian $\Delta_M$, and the area element $d\mu$ or $d\omega$.
\item Many authors define the Jacobi operator with the opposite sign, $L = -\Delta_M - |A|^2 - \overline{\mathrm{Ric}}(\nu, \nu)$, so that $Q(u) = \int_Mu\,Lu$ and stability means that $L$ is a
nonnegative operator (its eigenvalues on compactly supported functions are $\geq 0$). This is the same quadratic form as ours.
\item Some authors use the geometer's Laplacian $\Delta = -\operatorname{div}\operatorname{grad}$, which is a nonnegative operator; then $L$ is written $\Delta - |A|^2 - \overline{\mathrm{Ric}}(\nu, \nu)$.
\end{enumerate}
To translate a formula, check it on the hyperplane in $\RR^{n+1}$: there $Q(u) = \int|\nabla u|^2 \geq 0$.

\emph{Curves.} For $n = 1$, $M$ is a curve in a surface $\overline{M}$, $A$ is its length, and $S$ is its geodesic curvature, so $H = 0$ means that $M$ is a geodesic and then $S = 0$. Since
$\overline{\mathrm{Ric}}(\nu, \nu) = \overline{K}$ for a surface (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-ricci-constant-curvature}), the formula becomes $\int(\phi^{\prime 2} - K\phi^2)\,dt$ for
a unit speed geodesic $\gamma$. This is the index form $I(V, V)$ of Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-second-variation} for $V = \phi\nu$: the unit normal $\nu$ is parallel along
$\gamma$ (its derivative is orthogonal to $\nu$ and, as $\langle D_t\nu, \dot\gamma\rangle = -\langle\nu, D_t\dot\gamma\rangle = 0$, to $\dot\gamma$), so $D_tV = \phi^{\prime}\nu$ and
$R(V, \dot\gamma, \dot\gamma, V) = K\phi^2$. So the second variation of area generalises the second variation of geodesics, and the three terms above correspond to those of
Remark~\ref{riemannian-geometry:remark-reading-second-variation}.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-equator-unstable}
\emph{The equator of a sphere.} Let $M = S^n = \{x_{n+2} = 0\}$ be the equator of the unit sphere $\overline{M} = S^{n+1} \subseteq \RR^{n+2}$, with the constant unit normal
$\nu = e_{n+2}$. The derivative of the constant field $\nu$ in $\RR^{n+2}$ is zero, so its tangential projection $\overline{\nabla}_X\nu$ vanishes
(Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-tangential-projection}): $S = 0$, the equator is totally geodesic and in particular minimal. As
$\overline{\mathrm{Ric}} = n\overline{g}$ (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-ricci-constant-curvature}), the proposition with $\phi = 1$ (the computation is pointwise, so it applies on all of
$S^n$) predicts $A^{\prime\prime}(0) = -n\operatorname{vol}(S^n)$ for the variation by normal geodesics. Directly: the normal geodesic from $p$ is the great circle
$s \mapsto \cos s\,p + \sin s\,e_{n+2}$, so $F_s(p) = \cos s\,p + \sin s\,e_{n+2}$ maps $S^n$ onto the parallel sphere $\{x_{n+2} = \sin s\}$, with $dF_s = \cos s \cdot \id$ on tangent
vectors. So $g(s) = \cos^2s\,g$, $A(s) = \cos^ns\operatorname{vol}(S^n)$, and $A^{\prime\prime}(0) = -n\operatorname{vol}(S^n)$, as predicted. The equator is a minimal hypersurface, but it is a
maximum of the area among the parallel spheres, not a minimum.
\end{example}

\begin{corollary}\label{riemannian-geometry:corollary-ricci-unstable}
Let $\overline{M}$ be a Riemannian manifold with $\overline{\mathrm{Ric}}(v, v) > 0$ for all $v \neq 0$, and let $M \subseteq \overline{M}$ be a compact minimal hypersurface without boundary
with a unit normal field $\nu$. Then pushing $M$ along its normal geodesics, $F_s(p) = \exp_p(s\nu(p))$, decreases its area to second order: $M$ is not stable.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Cover $M$ by finitely many parametrisations and take a subordinate partition of unity $(\rho_k)$ (Proposition~\ref{smooth-manifolds:theorem-partitions-of-unity}). The area of
$F_s(M)$, computed with the metric $F_s^*\overline{g}$, is $A(s) = \sum_k\int\rho_k\sqrt{\det g(s)}\,du$, each term in the chart of $\rho_k$. Steps 3 to 6 of the proof of
Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-variation-area-riemannian} compute $(\sqrt{\det g})^{\prime\prime}(0)$ pointwise, with $\phi = 1$ and $Z = 0$ (the curves are geodesics),
as $-(|S|^2 + \overline{\mathrm{Ric}}(\nu, \nu))\sqrt{\det g}$. Since $\sum_k\rho_k = 1$,
\[
A^{\prime\prime}(0) = -\int_M\bigl(|S|^2 + \overline{\mathrm{Ric}}(\nu, \nu)\bigr)\,dA < 0 .
\]
\end{proof}

For example, in the round sphere $S^{n+1}$ every compact minimal hypersurface with a unit normal is unstable, as the equator of
Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-equator-unstable} illustrates. The proof uses a global unit normal field (that is, $M$ is \emph{two-sided}), since it pushes every point of $M$ to the same side. For a one-sided hypersurface, such as the
image of the equator $S^2 \subseteq S^3$ in $\RR\PP^3$, which is a totally geodesic copy of $\RR\PP^2$, the variation with $\phi = 1$ is not defined and the argument does not apply.

\section{The Laplace--Beltrami operator}\label{riemannian-geometry:section-laplace-beltrami}

The Laplacian $\Delta_g = \operatorname{div}\operatorname{grad}$ of Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-gradient-hessian}, called the \emph{Laplace--Beltrami operator} when one wants to
stress that it lives on a Riemannian manifold, has already appeared several times: in coordinates (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-gradient-hessian}(1)),
\[
\Delta_gf = \frac{1}{\sqrt{\det g}}\sum_{i,j}\partial_i\bigl(\sqrt{\det g}\,g^{ij}\partial_jf\bigr),
\]
as the trace of the Hessian (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-gradient-hessian}(3)), and in the Jacobi operator of a minimal hypersurface
(Remark~\ref{riemannian-geometry:remark-variation-area-riemannian}). This section collects its basic properties: it is symmetric and nonpositive, it measures how $f$ differs from its
averages, it detects minimal submanifolds, and on spheres its eigenfunctions are the spherical harmonics. We use the analysts' sign, so that $\Delta_g = \sum_i\partial_i^2$ on
$\RR^n$; the Hodge Laplacian of Section~\ref{riemannian-geometry:section-hodge-star} below is $-\Delta_g$ on functions. Throughout, $(M, g)$ is a Riemannian manifold of dimension $n$
and we write $\Delta$ for $\Delta_g$.

\begin{proposition}[Green's identities]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-laplacian-symmetric}
Let $M$ be oriented and without boundary, and let $f$, $h$ be smooth functions, one of which has compact support. Then
\[
\int_Mf\,\Delta h\,\mathrm{vol}_g = -\int_M\langle\operatorname{grad}f, \operatorname{grad}h\rangle\,\mathrm{vol}_g = \int_Mh\,\Delta f\,\mathrm{vol}_g .
\]
In particular $\int_M\Delta f\,\mathrm{vol}_g = 0$ and $\int_Mf\,\Delta f\,\mathrm{vol}_g = -\int_M|\operatorname{grad}f|^2\,\mathrm{vol}_g \leq 0$ for compactly supported $f$: the operator $\Delta$ is
\emph{symmetric} and \emph{nonpositive}. Moreover $\Delta$ is the only operator $P \colon C^\infty(M) \to C^\infty(M)$ with $\int_MfPh\,\mathrm{vol}_g = -\int_M\langle\operatorname{grad}f, \operatorname{grad}h\rangle\,\mathrm{vol}_g$
for all $h$ and all compactly supported $f$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The vector field $X = f\operatorname{grad}h$ has compact support and $\operatorname{div}X = \langle\operatorname{grad}f, \operatorname{grad}h\rangle + f\Delta h$, by the Leibniz rule
$\nabla_v(fY) = v(f)Y + f\nabla_vY$ inside the trace. By Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-gradient-hessian}(5), $(\operatorname{div}X)\,\mathrm{vol}_g = d(\iota_X\mathrm{vol}_g)$ is
the exterior derivative of a compactly supported form, so its integral vanishes by Stokes' theorem (Theorem~\ref{differential-forms:theorem-stokes}) with $\partial M = \emptyset$. This is
the first equality; the second follows by exchanging $f$ and $h$, as the middle term is symmetric. For the special cases take $h = 1$, so that $\int\Delta f = \int f\,\Delta1 = 0$, and $h = f$. For uniqueness, if $P$ has the property, then $u = Ph - \Delta h$ is smooth and $\int_Mfu\,\mathrm{vol}_g = 0$ for all
compactly supported $f$. Taking $f = \rho u$ for a bump function $\rho \geq 0$ gives $\int_M\rho u^2\,\mathrm{vol}_g = 0$, so $u = 0$ where $\rho > 0$; as $\rho$ is arbitrary, $u = 0$.
\end{proof}

The identity $\int f\Delta h = \int h\Delta f$ is the analogue for $\Delta$ of the symmetry of a symmetric matrix, with the $L^2$ inner product $(f, h) = \int_Mfh\,\mathrm{vol}_g$ in place of
the dot product; $\int f\Delta f \leq 0$ says that $\Delta$ is ``negative semidefinite''. As for symmetric matrices, this has consequences for eigenvalues.

\begin{corollary}\label{riemannian-geometry:corollary-laplacian-compact}
Let $M$ be compact, oriented and without boundary.
\begin{enumerate}
\item A function with $\Delta f \geq 0$ everywhere (a \emph{subharmonic} function), in particular a \emph{harmonic} function, $\Delta f = 0$, is locally constant.
\item If $\Delta f = -\lambda f$ with $f \neq 0$, then $\lambda = \int|\operatorname{grad}f|^2/\int f^2 \geq 0$, and $\lambda = 0$ only for locally constant $f$. So the eigenvalues of $-\Delta$
are nonnegative.
\item Eigenfunctions $\Delta f_1 = -\lambda_1f_1$, $\Delta f_2 = -\lambda_2f_2$ with $\lambda_1 \neq \lambda_2$ are orthogonal: $\int_Mf_1f_2\,\mathrm{vol}_g = 0$.
\item If the equation $\Delta u = h$ has a solution, then $\int_Mh\,\mathrm{vol}_g = 0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
All functions have compact support. (1) $\int\Delta f = 0$ and $\Delta f \geq 0$ force $\Delta f = 0$; then $\int|\operatorname{grad}f|^2 = -\int f\Delta f = 0$, so $df = 0$ and $f$ is constant
on each connected component. (2) $\lambda\int f^2 = -\int f\Delta f = \int|\operatorname{grad}f|^2$; if $\lambda = 0$ then $df = 0$. (3) $-\lambda_1\int f_1f_2 = \int f_2\Delta f_1 =
\int f_1\Delta f_2 = -\lambda_2\int f_1f_2$. (4) $\int h = \int\Delta u = 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-laplacian-eigen}
\begin{enumerate}
\item On the circle $\RR/2\pi\ZZ$ with $g = d\theta^2$, $\Delta = d^2/d\theta^2$; the functions $\cos k\theta$ and $\sin k\theta$ are eigenfunctions of $-\Delta$ with eigenvalue $k^2$, and part (3)
of the corollary is the orthogonality of the Fourier basis. On the flat torus $\RR^n/2\pi\ZZ^n$ the functions $\cos\langle k, x\rangle$ and $\sin\langle k, x\rangle$, $k \in \ZZ^n$, have eigenvalue $|k|^2$.
\item On the round sphere $S^n$, the restriction of a linear function $\langle a, x\rangle$ is an eigenfunction of $-\Delta$ with eigenvalue $n$
(Exercise~\ref{riemannian-geometry:exercise-height-sphere}); more eigenfunctions are found in Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-spherical-harmonics} below.
\item On a noncompact manifold part (1) fails: $x$ is a nonconstant harmonic function on $\RR^n$. Compactness enters through the absence of boundary terms in Green's identities.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\emph{What the Laplacian measures.} On $\RR$, $f^{\prime\prime}(p) > 0$ means that $f$ bends upwards, so that $\frac12(f(p + r) + f(p - r)) > f(p)$ for small $r$: the average of $f$ over the
two points at distance $r$ exceeds $f(p)$, and $f^{\prime\prime}(p) = \lim_{r\to0}\frac{2}{r^2}\bigl(\frac12(f(p + r) + f(p - r)) - f(p)\bigr)$. The Laplacian is the same quantity in higher
dimension, with the two points replaced by a small sphere.

\begin{proposition}[The Laplacian as a mean value]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-laplacian-mean}
Let $p \in M$, identify $T_pM$ with $\RR^n$ by an orthonormal basis, let $S \subseteq T_pM$ be the unit sphere with its Riemannian measure $\sigma$, and for small $r > 0$ let
$m_f(r) = \frac{1}{\sigma(S)}\int_Sf(\exp_p(rv))\,d\sigma(v)$, the average of $f$ over the points at distance $r$ from $p$ in all directions. Then
\[
