m_f(r) = f(p) + \frac{r^2}{2n}\Delta f(p) + O(r^3), \qquad\text{so}\qquad \Delta f(p) = \lim_{r\to0}\frac{2n}{r^2}\bigl(m_f(r) - f(p)\bigr).
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The map $(r, v) \mapsto f(\exp_p(rv))$ is smooth on $[0, r_0] \times S$ for small $r_0$, as $\exp$ is smooth on an open set containing $0 \in T_pM$. For fixed $v$, the curve
$\gamma(r) = \exp_p(rv)$ is the geodesic with $\gamma(0) = p$ and $\dot\gamma(0) = v$, so by Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-gradient-hessian}(4) and Taylor's formula,
$f(\exp_p(rv)) = f(p) + r\,df_p(v) + \frac{r^2}2\operatorname{Hess}f_p(v, v) + R(r, v)$ with $|R(r, v)| \leq Cr^3$, where $C$ bounds the third derivative in $r$ on the compact set
$[0, r_0] \times S$. Now average over $v \in S$. The orthogonal maps of $\RR^n$ preserve $S$ and are isometries of it, so they preserve $\sigma$. The reflection
$v_i \mapsto -v_i$ shows $\int_Sv_i\,d\sigma = 0$ and $\int_Sv_iv_j\,d\sigma = 0$ for $i \neq j$; the coordinate permutations show that $\int_Sv_i^2\,d\sigma$ does not depend on $i$, and as
$\sum_iv_i^2 = 1$ on $S$ it equals $\sigma(S)/n$. Writing $df_p(v) = \sum_ia_iv_i$ and $\operatorname{Hess}f_p(v, v) = \sum_{i,j}b_{ij}v_iv_j$, the averages are $0$ and
$\frac1n\sum_ib_{ii} = \frac1n\Delta f(p)$ (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-gradient-hessian}(3)).
\end{proof}

So $\Delta f(p) > 0$ means that near $p$ the values of $f$ are on average larger than $f(p)$: $p$ lies in a ``valley'' of $f$. A harmonic function equals its averages up to order
$r^3$; on $\RR^2$ it equals them exactly (the mean value property of harmonic functions, Chapter~\ref{complex-analysis}). At a local maximum, $\operatorname{Hess}f_p \leq 0$ (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-gradient-hessian}(4)),
so $\Delta f(p) \leq 0$; hence a function with $\Delta f > 0$ everywhere has no local maximum. This explains the heat equation $\partial_tu = \Delta u$: the temperature rises at points
whose surroundings are warmer on average.

\emph{The Laplacian of the coordinates of a submanifold.} For a submanifold $M \subseteq \RR^N$ with the induced metric, the coordinates $x^1, \ldots, x^N$ restrict to functions on $M$,
and we write $\Delta x = (\Delta x^1, \ldots, \Delta x^N)$, a map $M \to \RR^N$. Let $\mathrm{II}$ be the second fundamental form (Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-gauss-formula}) and
$\vec{H} = \frac1n\sum_a\mathrm{II}(e_a, e_a)$ the \emph{mean curvature vector}, a normal vector; for a hypersurface with unit normal $\nu$, $\vec{H} = H\nu$
(Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-hypersurface-curvatures}).

\begin{proposition}[Beltrami's formula]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-beltrami-formula}
For a submanifold $M^n \subseteq \RR^N$, $\operatorname{Hess}x^k(X, Y) = \langle\mathrm{II}(X, Y), e_k\rangle$ for each coordinate $x^k$, and
\[
\Delta x = n\vec{H} .
\]
In particular $M$ is minimal ($\vec{H} = 0$) if and only if its coordinate functions are harmonic on $M$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For a tangent vector field $Y$, regarded as a map $M \to \RR^N$, $Yx^k = \langle Y, e_k\rangle$ is the $k$-th component of $Y$. Differentiating along a tangent field $X$,
$X(Yx^k) = \langle D_XY, e_k\rangle$, where $D_XY$ is the ordinary derivative of the vector-valued function $Y$ along $X$. By the Gauss formula and
Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-tangential-projection}, $D_XY = \nabla_XY + \mathrm{II}(X, Y)$, so
$\operatorname{Hess}x^k(X, Y) = X(Yx^k) - (\nabla_XY)x^k = \langle D_XY - \nabla_XY, e_k\rangle = \langle\mathrm{II}(X, Y), e_k\rangle$. Taking the trace,
$\Delta x^k = \sum_a\langle\mathrm{II}(e_a, e_a), e_k\rangle = n\langle\vec{H}, e_k\rangle$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-beltrami-formula}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{The sphere.} For the unit sphere $S^n \subseteq \RR^{n+1}$ and tangent fields $X$, $Y$, differentiating $\langle Y, x\rangle = 0$ gives $\langle D_XY, x\rangle = -\langle Y, X\rangle$, so
$\mathrm{II}(X, Y) = -\langle X, Y\rangle x$, $\vec{H} = -x$ and $\Delta x = -nx$: each coordinate function is an eigenfunction of $-\Delta$ with eigenvalue $n$, as in
Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-laplacian-eigen}(2).
\item \emph{The catenoid} $x(u, v) = (\cosh v\cos u, \cosh v\sin u, v)$ has $E = G = \cosh^2v$, $F = 0$ (Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-minimal-surfaces}), so in these conformal coordinates
$\Delta = \cosh^{-2}v\,(\partial_u^2 + \partial_v^2)$ (Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-gradient-hessian}(3)). The coordinates $\cosh v\cos u$, $\cosh v\sin u$ and $v$ are harmonic for
$\partial_u^2 + \partial_v^2$ (for the first, $\partial_u^2$ gives $-\cosh v\cos u$ and $\partial_v^2$ gives $+\cosh v\cos u$), confirming that the catenoid is minimal.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{corollary}\label{riemannian-geometry:corollary-no-compact-minimal}
There is no compact oriented minimal submanifold $M \subseteq \RR^N$ without boundary and of positive dimension.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-beltrami-formula} the coordinate functions are harmonic on $M$, hence constant on each component of $M$
(Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-laplacian-compact}(1)). So each component of $M$ is a point, which contradicts $\dim M > 0$.
\end{proof}

So a soap film always needs a wire: a closed soap bubble is not a minimal surface, and indeed it has constant mean curvature $H \neq 0$, balancing the pressure difference
between inside and outside.

\emph{Spherical coordinates and spherical harmonics.} On $\RR^n \setminus \{0\}$ write $x = r\theta$ with $r = |x| > 0$ and $\theta \in S^{n-1}$, and let $\theta^1, \ldots, \theta^{n-1}$ be local
coordinates on $S^{n-1}$. The map $(r, \theta) \mapsto r\theta$ sends $\partial_r$ to $\theta$, a unit vector, and $\partial_{\theta^a}$ to $r\,\partial_a\theta$, which is orthogonal to $\theta$
(differentiate $|\theta|^2 = 1$). So the Euclidean metric is
\[
g = dr^2 + r^2g_{S^{n-1}},
\]
with $\sqrt{\det g} = r^{n-1}\sqrt{\det g_{S^{n-1}}}$, $g^{rr} = 1$, $g^{ra} = 0$ and $g^{ab} = r^{-2}g_{S^{n-1}}^{ab}$.

\begin{proposition}\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-laplacian-polar}
For a smooth function $f$ on $\RR^n \setminus \{0\}$,
\[
\Delta f = \frac{\partial^2f}{\partial r^2} + \frac{n-1}{r}\frac{\partial f}{\partial r} + \frac{1}{r^2}\Delta_{S^{n-1}}f,
\]
where $\Delta_{S^{n-1}}f$ is the Laplacian of the round unit sphere applied to $\theta \mapsto f(r\theta)$ for fixed $r$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Insert the metric above into the coordinate formula for $\Delta$. The terms with $i = j = r$ give $r^{1-n}\partial_r(r^{n-1}\partial_rf) = \partial_r^2f + \frac{n-1}r\partial_rf$, and the terms with
$i, j$ among the $\theta^a$ give $r^{-2}$ times the coordinate formula for $\Delta_{S^{n-1}}$, since the factor $r^{n-1}$ in $\sqrt{\det g}$ does not depend on $\theta$ and cancels.
\end{proof}

For $n = 2$ this is the formula $f_{rr} + \frac1rf_r + \frac1{r^2}f_{\theta\theta}$ of Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-gradient-hessian}(2), and for radial functions it is
Exercise~\ref{riemannian-geometry:exercise-gradient-identities}.

\begin{corollary}[Spherical harmonics]\label{riemannian-geometry:corollary-spherical-harmonics}
Let $P$ be a homogeneous polynomial of degree $k$ on $\RR^n$ with $\Delta P = 0$, and $Y = P|_{S^{n-1}}$. Then
\[
\Delta_{S^{n-1}}Y = -k(k + n - 2)\,Y .
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By homogeneity $P(r\theta) = r^kY(\theta)$. By Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-laplacian-polar},
$0 = \Delta P = \bigl(k(k - 1) + (n - 1)k\bigr)r^{k-2}Y + r^{k-2}\Delta_{S^{n-1}}Y$, and $k(k - 1) + (n - 1)k = k(k + n - 2)$.
\end{proof}

For $k = 1$ the eigenvalue of $-\Delta_{S^{n-1}}$ is $n - 1 = \dim S^{n-1}$, as in Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-beltrami-formula}(1). For $k = 2$ and $n = 3$, the harmonic
polynomials $xy$, $yz$, $zx$, $x^2 - y^2$ and $x^2 + y^2 - 2z^2$ give eigenfunctions on $S^2$ with eigenvalue $6$; in physics these are the orbitals with angular momentum $\ell = 2$, and
the eigenvalue $\ell(\ell + 1)$ is the square of the angular momentum.

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-laplacians}
\emph{Other Laplacians.} The formula $\Delta f = \operatorname{tr}_g\operatorname{Hess}f = \sum_a(\nabla^2f)(e_a, e_a)$ makes sense for any tensor field $T$ in place of $f$:
$\nabla^*\nabla T = -\sum_a(\nabla_{e_a}\nabla_{e_a}T - \nabla_{\nabla_{e_a}e_a}T)$ is the \emph{rough} or \emph{connection Laplacian} (with the geometers' sign). On differential forms there is
also the Hodge Laplacian $d\delta + \delta d$ of the next section, which equals $-\Delta$ on functions; on forms of positive degree the two differ by a curvature term (the
Weitzenb\"ock formula), which is the source of the Bochner vanishing theorems mentioned there.
\end{remark}

\section{The Hodge star and the Laplacian}\label{riemannian-geometry:section-hodge-star}

\begin{definition}\label{riemannian-geometry:definition-hodge-star}
Let $(M, g)$ be an oriented Riemannian $n$-manifold. The metric induces inner products on $\Lambda^kT^*_pM$ (Proposition~\ref{multilinear-algebra:proposition-induced-inner-product}), the volume form
$\mathrm{vol}_g$ (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-volume-form}), and the \emph{Hodge star} $* \colon \Omega^k(M) \to \Omega^{n-k}(M)$, characterised pointwise by $\alpha \wedge *\beta = \langle\alpha, \beta\rangle\,\mathrm{vol}_g$
(Theorem~\ref{multilinear-algebra:theorem-hodge-star}); it satisfies $** = (-1)^{k(n-k)}$ on $k$-forms. In coordinates $\mathrm{vol}_g = \sqrt{\det(g_{ij})}\,dx^1 \wedge \cdots \wedge dx^n$ in a positively
oriented chart.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}\label{riemannian-geometry:definition-laplacian}
On a compact oriented Riemannian manifold define the $L^2$ inner product on $\Omega^k(M)$ by
\[
(\alpha, \beta) = \int_M\alpha \wedge *\beta = \int_M\langle\alpha, \beta\rangle\,\mathrm{vol}_g,
\]
the \emph{codifferential} $\delta = (-1)^{n(k+1)+1}*d* \colon \Omega^k(M) \to \Omega^{k-1}(M)$, and the \emph{Hodge Laplacian} $\Delta = d\delta + \delta d$. A form is \emph{harmonic} if $\Delta\alpha = 0$; the space of
harmonic $k$-forms is $\mathcal{H}^k(M)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-adjoint}
On a compact oriented Riemannian manifold:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $(d\alpha, \beta) = (\alpha, \delta\beta)$ for $\alpha \in \Omega^{k-1}$, $\beta \in \Omega^k$: the codifferential is the formal adjoint of $d$;
\item $\Delta$ is self-adjoint and non-negative: $(\Delta\alpha, \alpha) = \|d\alpha\|^2 + \|\delta\alpha\|^2$;
\item $\Delta\alpha = 0$ if and only if $d\alpha = 0$ and $\delta\alpha = 0$;
\item $*\Delta = \Delta*$, so $*$ maps harmonic $k$-forms isomorphically to harmonic $(n-k)$-forms;
\item $\Delta$ on functions is $-\sum_{i}\partial_i^2$ in normal coordinates at a point, and in general
$\Delta f = -\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det g}}\sum_{i,j}\partial_i(\sqrt{\det g}\,g^{ij}\partial_jf)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(1) By Stokes' theorem (Theorem~\ref{differential-forms:theorem-stokes}), $0 = \int_Md(\alpha \wedge *\beta) = \int_Md\alpha \wedge *\beta + (-1)^{k-1}\int_M\alpha \wedge d{*}\beta$. Using $** = (-1)^{k(n-k)}$ one
rewrites $d*\beta = \pm *{*}d{*}\beta$, and the sign in the definition of $\delta$ is chosen exactly so that the identity becomes $(d\alpha, \beta) = (\alpha, \delta\beta)$.

(2) $(\Delta\alpha, \beta) = (d\delta\alpha, \beta) + (\delta d\alpha, \beta) = (\delta\alpha, \delta\beta) + (d\alpha, d\beta)$, which is symmetric; taking $\beta = \alpha$ gives the formula. (3) If $\Delta\alpha = 0$ then
$\|d\alpha\|^2 + \|\delta\alpha\|^2 = 0$, so both vanish; the converse is clear. (4) follows from the definition of $\delta$ and $** = \pm 1$: $*d\delta = \delta d*$ and $*\delta d = d\delta*$ up to the signs, which
cancel. (5) is a computation with the coordinate formula for $*$ and $d$, using $\mathrm{vol}_g = \sqrt{\det g}\,dx^1\wedge\cdots\wedge dx^n$; in normal coordinates at $p$ the first derivatives of $g$ vanish, so
$\Delta f(p) = -\sum_i\partial_i^2f(p)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-weitzenbock}
The Levi-Civita connection gives a second Laplacian, the \emph{connection Laplacian} $\nabla^*\nabla$, and the two differ by curvature: the \emph{Weitzenb\"ock formula} states
$\Delta = \nabla^*\nabla + \mathcal{R}$, where $\mathcal{R}$ is an endomorphism built from the curvature tensor; on $1$-forms $\mathcal{R} = \mathrm{Ric}$. Since $(\nabla^*\nabla\alpha, \alpha) = \|\nabla\alpha\|^2 \geq 0$,
this gives vanishing theorems: if $\mathrm{Ric} > 0$ on a compact oriented manifold, every harmonic $1$-form vanishes, hence $b_1(M) = 0$ by the Hodge theorem (Bochner's theorem,
Chapter~\ref{hodge-theorem}, where the formula on $1$-forms is proved). For proofs see \cite[Chapter 6]{warner-foundations} and \cite[Chapter 4]{lee-riemannian}.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-harmonic-forms}
On a compact connected oriented manifold the harmonic $0$-forms are the constants, since $\Delta f = 0$ implies $df = 0$; by (4) the harmonic $n$-forms are the multiples of $\mathrm{vol}_g$. On the flat torus
$\RR^n/\ZZ^n$, the harmonic forms are exactly the forms with constant coefficients in the coordinates $dx^i$, a space of dimension $\binom{n}{k}$, which matches $b_k(T^n)$
(Exercise~\ref{de-rham:exercise-torus}).
\end{example}

\section{Isometries, Killing fields and space forms}\label{riemannian-geometry:section-killing}

The symmetries of a Riemannian manifold are its isometries, and the infinitesimal symmetries are the vector fields whose flows consist of isometries, the Killing
fields. This section shows that both are rigid: an isometry is determined by its value and its differential at one point, a Killing field by its value and its
covariant derivative at one point, and so the Killing fields form a Lie algebra of dimension at most $n(n + 1)/2$. The spaces with the most symmetry are those of
constant curvature, and the theorem of Killing and Hopf classifies them: a complete simply connected manifold of constant curvature is Euclidean space, a round
sphere or hyperbolic space, and every complete manifold of constant curvature is a quotient of one of these. At the end we state two rigidity theorems of a more
difficult kind, Cheng's maximal diameter theorem and the sphere theorem. Throughout, $M$ has dimension $n$.

A smooth map $F \colon (N, h) \to (M, g)$ between Riemannian manifolds of the same dimension is a \emph{local isometry} if $F^*g = h$, that is, if every
$d_xF$ is a linear isometry. By the inverse function theorem (Theorem~\ref{smooth-manifolds:theorem-inverse-function-manifolds}) it is then a local diffeomorphism,
and it restricts to isometries between small open sets.

\begin{lemma}\label{riemannian-geometry:lemma-local-isometries}
Let $F \colon N \to M$ be a local isometry.
\begin{enumerate}
\item $F$ maps geodesics to geodesics: if $v \in T_xN$ is in the domain of $\exp_x$, then $d_xF(v)$ is in the domain of $\exp_{F(x)}$ and
$F(\exp_x(v)) = \exp_{F(x)}(d_xF(v))$.
\item If $N$ is connected and $F^{\prime} \colon N \to M$ is another local isometry with $F^{\prime}(x_0) = F(x_0)$ and $d_{x_0}F^{\prime} = d_{x_0}F$ at one point $x_0$, then
$F^{\prime} = F$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(1) Let $x \in N$, and choose an open $U \ni x$ that $F$ maps diffeomorphically onto an open $V$, and a chart $y$ on $V$. Then $y \circ F$ is a chart on $U$, and
since $F^*g = h$, the coefficients of $h$ in the chart $y \circ F$ are the same functions as those of $g$ in the chart $y$. So the Christoffel symbols
(Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-levi-civita}) and the geodesic equations (Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-geodesic}) are the same, and a curve
$c$ in $U$ is a geodesic if and only if $F \circ c$ is one. Being a geodesic is a local condition, so $F \circ \gamma$ is a geodesic whenever $\gamma$ is. For $v$ in the
domain of $\exp_x$, the curve $F \circ \gamma_v$ is a geodesic defined on $[0, 1]$ with initial velocity $d_xF(v)$; by uniqueness it is the geodesic
$\gamma_{d_xF(v)}$, which is therefore defined on $[0, 1]$, and evaluating at $t = 1$ gives the formula.

(2) Let $A = \{x \in N : F^{\prime}(x) = F(x),\ d_xF^{\prime} = d_xF\}$, which contains $x_0$. It is closed, as $F$, $F^{\prime}$ and their differentials are continuous (in charts,
the Jacobian matrices are continuous). It is open: if $x \in A$ and $\exp_x$ maps a ball $B(0, \varepsilon)$ diffeomorphically onto a neighbourhood $W$ of $x$
(Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-exponential}), then by (1), $F(\exp_x v) = \exp_{F(x)}(d_xFv) = F^{\prime}(\exp_x v)$ for $|v| < \varepsilon$, so
$F = F^{\prime}$ on $W$, and hence also $dF = dF^{\prime}$ on $W$. As $N$ is connected, $A = N$.
\end{proof}

For example, for a geodesic $\gamma$ and an isometry $F$, $F \circ \gamma$ is the geodesic with initial velocity $dF(\dot\gamma(0))$; this is the argument of
Exercise~\ref{riemannian-geometry:exercise-great-circles}, which finds the geodesics of the sphere as fixed sets of reflections.

\begin{definition}\label{riemannian-geometry:definition-killing}
A vector field $X$ on $M$ with local flow $\Phi$ (Theorem~\ref{smooth-manifolds:theorem-flow}) is a \emph{Killing field} if every $\Phi_t$ is an isometry from its
domain onto its image: $\langle d\Phi_t(v), d\Phi_t(w)\rangle = \langle v, w\rangle$ whenever $\Phi_t$ is defined at the base point of $v$ and $w$. For vector fields
$X$, $Y$, $Z$ we put
\[
(L_Xg)(Y, Z) = X\langle Y, Z\rangle - \langle[X, Y], Z\rangle - \langle Y, [X, Z]\rangle .
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-killing}
The expression $L_Xg$ is a symmetric tensor, and $(L_Xg)_p(v, w) = \frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}\langle d_p\Phi_t(v), d_p\Phi_t(w)\rangle$ for $v, w \in T_pM$ (the \emph{Lie derivative}
of $g$). Moreover $(L_Xg)(Y, Z) = \langle\nabla_YX, Z\rangle + \langle Y, \nabla_ZX\rangle$. Consequently the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $X$ is a Killing field;
\item $X\langle Y, Z\rangle = \langle[X, Y], Z\rangle + \langle Y, [X, Z]\rangle$ for all vector fields $Y$, $Z$;
\item for every $p$, the endomorphism $v \mapsto \nabla_vX$ of $T_pM$ is skew-adjoint: $\langle\nabla_vX, w\rangle + \langle v, \nabla_wX\rangle = 0$.
\end{enumerate}
In coordinates, (3) reads $\nabla_iX_j + \nabla_jX_i = 0$, where $\nabla_iX_j = \langle\nabla_{\partial_i}X, \partial_j\rangle$ (\emph{Killing's equation}).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since $\nabla$ is metric and torsion-free, $X\langle Y, Z\rangle = \langle\nabla_XY, Z\rangle + \langle Y, \nabla_XZ\rangle$ and $[X, Y] = \nabla_XY - \nabla_YX$;
substituting gives $(L_Xg)(Y, Z) = \langle\nabla_YX, Z\rangle + \langle Y, \nabla_ZX\rangle$. The right side depends only on the values of $Y$ and $Z$ at each point and
is symmetric, so $L_Xg$ is a symmetric tensor.

Fix $p$ and vector fields $Y$, $Z$ near $p$. Let $V(t) = d\Phi_{-t}(Y_{\Phi_t(p)})$ and $W(t) = d\Phi_{-t}(Z_{\Phi_t(p)})$, curves in $T_pM$ with $V(0) = Y_p$,
$W(0) = Z_p$, and by Proposition~\ref{smooth-manifolds:proposition-bracket}, $V^{\prime}(0) = [X, Y]_p$ and $W^{\prime}(0) = [X, Z]_p$. For $a, b \in T_pM$ put
$G(t, a, b) = \langle d_p\Phi_t(a), d_p\Phi_t(b)\rangle$, smooth in $t$ and bilinear in $(a, b)$. Since $d\Phi_t(V(t)) = Y_{\Phi_t(p)}$,
\[
\langle Y, Z\rangle(\Phi_t(p)) = G(t, V(t), W(t)),
\]
and differentiating at $t = 0$ (the derivative of $t \mapsto \Phi_t(p)$ is $X_p$),
$X_p\langle Y, Z\rangle = \partial_tG(0, Y_p, Z_p) + \langle[X, Y]_p, Z_p\rangle + \langle Y_p, [X, Z]_p\rangle$. So $(L_Xg)_p(Y_p, Z_p) = \partial_tG(0, Y_p, Z_p)$, and
every pair of tangent vectors at $p$ is of the form $(Y_p, Z_p)$.

(1) $\Rightarrow$ (2): if $X$ is Killing, $G(t, a, b) = \langle a, b\rangle$ does not depend on $t$, so $L_Xg = 0$. (2) $\Rightarrow$ (1): let $v, w \in T_pM$ and
$f(t) = G(t, v, w)$, on the interval of $t$ for which $\Phi_t(p)$ is defined. For $t_0$ in it, $\Phi_{t_0 + s} = \Phi_s \circ \Phi_{t_0}$ near $p$, so
$f(t_0 + s) = \langle d\Phi_s(v^{\prime}), d\Phi_s(w^{\prime})\rangle$ with $v^{\prime} = d_p\Phi_{t_0}(v)$ and $w^{\prime} = d_p\Phi_{t_0}(w)$, and
$f^{\prime}(t_0) = (L_Xg)(v^{\prime}, w^{\prime}) = 0$. So $f$ is constant, equal to $f(0) = \langle v, w\rangle$. (2) $\Leftrightarrow$ (3) is the formula
$(L_Xg)(Y, Z) = \langle\nabla_YX, Z\rangle + \langle Y, \nabla_ZX\rangle$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-killing-fields}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Euclidean space.} On $\RR^n$, $\nabla_vX = DX(x)v$, so $X$ is a Killing field if and only if the matrix $DX(x)$ is skew-symmetric for every $x$. This holds for
$X(x) = Ax + b$ with $A^T = -A$, whose flow $\Phi_t(x) = e^{tA}x + \int_0^te^{sA}b\,ds$ consists of rigid motions ($e^{tA}$ is orthogonal, since
$(e^{tA})^Te^{tA} = e^{-tA}e^{tA} = I$). Conversely, if $\partial_iX^j + \partial_jX^i = 0$ everywhere, then $T_{kij} = \partial_k\partial_iX^j$ is symmetric in
$k, i$ (Theorem~\ref{real-analysis:theorem-schwarz}) and antisymmetric in $i, j$, and a tensor with these two properties vanishes:
$T_{kij} = -T_{kji} = -T_{jki} = T_{jik} = T_{ijk} = -T_{ikj} = -T_{kij}$. So $DX$ is constant and $X(x) = Ax + b$.
\item \emph{Spheres.} For $A \in \mathfrak{so}(n + 1)$ the field $X(x) = Ax$ is tangent to $S^n$, as $\langle Ax, x\rangle = 0$, and its flow $e^{tA} \in \mathrm{SO}(n + 1)$
restricts to isometries of $S^n$; so $X$ is a Killing field on $S^n$. For $n = 2$ and $A = L_a$ (Example~\ref{lie-groups:example-so3}) it is the field
$x \mapsto a \times x$ of the rotations about the axis $a$.
\item \emph{Lie groups.} On a Lie group with a bi-invariant metric (Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-metrics}) the left-invariant fields are Killing fields: the
flow of $X$ is $\Phi_t(g) = g\exp(tX)$ (Theorem~\ref{lie-groups:theorem-exponential}, translated by $L_g$), which is right multiplication by $\exp(tX)$, an isometry.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{proposition}\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-killing-jacobi}
Let $X$ be a Killing field and $\gamma \colon I \to M$ a geodesic. Then $X \circ \gamma$ is a Jacobi field along $\gamma$, with $D_t(X \circ \gamma) = \nabla_{\dot\gamma}X$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $[a, b] \subseteq I$. The domain of the flow is open and contains $\{0\} \times M$ (Theorem~\ref{smooth-manifolds:theorem-flow}), so by compactness of
$\gamma([a, b])$ there is $\varepsilon > 0$ such that $\Phi_s$ is defined on a neighbourhood of $\gamma([a, b])$ for $|s| < \varepsilon$. Each $\Phi_s$ is an isometry onto
its image, so by Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-local-isometries}(1), $f(s, t) = \Phi_s(\gamma(t))$ is a family of geodesics $t \mapsto f(s, t)$. By
Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-jacobi}(1), $\partial_sf(0, t) = X(\gamma(t))$ is a Jacobi field on $[a, b]$; as $[a, b]$ is arbitrary, it is one on $I$.
The formula for $D_t$ is Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-covariant-derivative-curves}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}\label{riemannian-geometry:theorem-killing-algebra}
Let $M$ be connected, and $p \in M$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item A Killing field $X$ with $X_p = 0$ and $\nabla_vX = 0$ for all $v \in T_pM$ vanishes identically.
\item The Killing fields form a Lie subalgebra $\mathfrak{iso}(M, g)$ of the Lie algebra $\mathfrak{X}(M)$ of vector fields
(Proposition~\ref{smooth-manifolds:proposition-bracket}), and the linear map $X \mapsto (X_p, \nabla X_p)$, where $\nabla X_p$ is the endomorphism $v \mapsto \nabla_vX$
of $T_pM$, is injective with values in $T_pM \oplus \mathfrak{so}(T_pM)$. Hence $\dim\mathfrak{iso}(M, g) \leq n + \frac{n(n-1)}{2} = \frac{n(n + 1)}{2}$.
\item The bound is attained by $\RR^n$ and by $S^n$, whose Killing fields are exactly those of Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-killing-fields}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(1) Let $Z$ be the set of points $q$ with $X_q = 0$ and $\nabla X_q = 0$; it is closed and contains $p$. If $q \in Z$ and $\exp_q$ maps $B(0, \varepsilon)$
diffeomorphically onto $W$, then for $|v| < \varepsilon$, $J(t) = X(\exp_q(tv))$ is a Jacobi field (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-killing-jacobi}) with
$J(0) = X_q = 0$ and $D_tJ(0) = \nabla_vX = 0$, hence $J = 0$ and $X(\exp_qv) = J(1) = 0$. So $X = 0$ on $W$, hence also $\nabla X = 0$ on $W$, and $Z$ is open. As $M$
is connected, $Z = M$.

(2) The map is linear, and it takes values in $T_pM \oplus \mathfrak{so}(T_pM)$ by Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-killing}(3); by (1) it is
injective, which gives the dimension bound. Killing fields form a vector space, since $L_Xg$ is linear in $X$. For the bracket, let $X$ and $W$ satisfy
Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-killing}(2). Then for all $Y$, $Z$,
\[
\begin{aligned}
XW\langle Y, Z\rangle &= X\langle[W, Y], Z\rangle + X\langle Y, [W, Z]\rangle \\
&= \langle[X, [W, Y]], Z\rangle + \langle[W, Y], [X, Z]\rangle + \langle[X, Y], [W, Z]\rangle + \langle Y, [X, [W, Z]]\rangle .
\end{aligned}
\]
Exchanging $X$ and $W$ and subtracting, the two middle terms cancel, and by the Jacobi identity $[X, [W, Y]] - [W, [X, Y]] = [[X, W], Y]$, so
$[X, W]\langle Y, Z\rangle = \langle[[X, W], Y], Z\rangle + \langle Y, [[X, W], Z]\rangle$: $[X, W]$ is a Killing field.

(3) On $\RR^n$ the fields $Ax + b$ with $A$ skew form a space of dimension $n(n - 1)/2 + n$, and by
Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-killing-fields}(1) they are all the Killing fields. On $S^n$ the fields $x \mapsto Ax$, $A \in \mathfrak{so}(n + 1)$, form a space of
dimension $\dim\mathfrak{so}(n + 1) = n(n + 1)/2$: the map $A \mapsto (x \mapsto Ax)$ is injective because $S^n$ spans $\RR^{n+1}$. By (2) there are no other Killing
fields on $S^n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-myers-steenrod}
The isometries of $M$ form a group $\mathrm{Isom}(M, g)$. By the theorem of Myers and Steenrod it is a Lie group, compact if $M$ is compact, and its Lie algebra is
identified with the Lie algebra of complete Killing fields (on a compact manifold every Killing field is complete); so
$\dim\mathrm{Isom}(M, g) \leq n(n + 1)/2$ \cite[Chapter VI]{kobayashi-nomizu}. For instance $\mathrm{Isom}(\RR^n)$ is the group of rigid motions and
$\mathrm{Isom}(S^n) = \mathrm{O}(n + 1)$ (Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-model-isometries} below), both of the maximal dimension. A manifold whose isometry
group has maximal dimension has constant curvature \cite[Chapter VI]{kobayashi-nomizu}.
\end{remark}

Curvature restricts the Killing fields of a compact manifold, by an integration argument of Bochner.

\begin{theorem}[Bochner]\label{riemannian-geometry:theorem-bochner-killing}
Let $M$ be compact and oriented, and $X$ a Killing field. Then, with $|\nabla X|^2 = \sum_i|\nabla_{e_i}X|^2$ for an orthonormal basis $(e_i)$,
\[
\int_M\bigl(|\nabla X|^2 - \mathrm{Ric}(X, X)\bigr)\,\mathrm{vol}_g = 0 .
\]
Hence if $\mathrm{Ric} \leq 0$ everywhere, every Killing field is parallel ($\nabla X = 0$); and if $\mathrm{Ric}$ is negative definite everywhere, every Killing field is zero,
and $\mathrm{Isom}(M, g)$ is finite.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $f = \frac12|X|^2$ and $p \in M$, and take normal coordinates at $p$ (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-exponential}), with
$\partial_i = e_i$ orthonormal at $p$. The $i$-th coordinate line $\gamma_i(t) = \exp_p(te_i)$ is a unit speed geodesic, so $\partial_i^2f(p) = (f \circ \gamma_i)^{\prime\prime}(0)$,
and $J = X \circ \gamma_i$ is a Jacobi field with $D_tJ(0) = \nabla_{e_i}X$ (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-killing-jacobi}). Hence
\[
(f \circ \gamma_i)^{\prime\prime} = \frac{d}{dt}\langle D_tJ, J\rangle = |D_tJ|^2 + \langle D_t^2J, J\rangle = |D_tJ|^2 - \langle R(J, \dot\gamma_i)\dot\gamma_i, J\rangle,
\]
which at $t = 0$ is $|\nabla_{e_i}X|^2 - R(X, e_i, e_i, X)$. Summing over $i$, and using $R(X, e_i, e_i, X) = R(e_i, X, X, e_i)$
(Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-curvature-symmetries}) and Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-curvature-tensor}, Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-adjoint}(5)
gives
\[
\Delta f(p) = -\sum_i\partial_i^2f(p) = \mathrm{Ric}(X, X) - |\nabla X|^2 .
\]
By Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-adjoint}(2), $\int_M\Delta f\,\mathrm{vol}_g = (\Delta f, 1) = (f, \Delta1) = 0$, which is the integral formula. If
$\mathrm{Ric} \leq 0$, the integrand $|\nabla X|^2 - \mathrm{Ric}(X, X)$ is a non-negative continuous function with integral $0$, so it vanishes: $\nabla X = 0$ and
$\mathrm{Ric}(X, X) = 0$; if $\mathrm{Ric}$ is negative definite, the latter forces $X = 0$. Then the compact Lie group $\mathrm{Isom}(M, g)$ has Lie algebra $0$
(Remark~\ref{riemannian-geometry:remark-myers-steenrod}), so it is discrete and compact, hence finite.
\end{proof}

For the flat torus $\RR^n/\ZZ^n$ the Killing fields are therefore the parallel fields, the constant fields $\sum_ic^i\partial_i$, whose flows are the translations; and a
compact surface of constant curvature $-1$ has only finitely many isometries.

We now turn to spaces of constant curvature. Recall that $s_\kappa(t)$ is $t$, $\sin(\sqrt\kappa t)/\sqrt\kappa$ or $\sinh(\sqrt{-\kappa}t)/\sqrt{-\kappa}$ for
$\kappa = 0$, $\kappa > 0$ or $\kappa < 0$, and that the proof of Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-minding} shows: if $M$ has constant sectional curvature $\kappa$,
$v \neq 0$ lies in the domain of $\exp_p$ and $w = w_\parallel + w_\perp$ is the decomposition of $w \in T_pM$ into a multiple of $v$ and a vector orthogonal to $v$, then
\begin{equation}\label{riemannian-geometry:equation-exp-constant-curvature}
|d_v\exp_p(w)|^2 = |w_\parallel|^2 + \Bigl(\frac{s_\kappa(|v|)}{|v|}\Bigr)^2|w_\perp|^2.
\end{equation}
Multiplying a metric by a constant $c > 0$ does not change the Levi-Civita connection (the Koszul formula is homogeneous) or $R(X, Y)Z$, and it multiplies the
sectional curvatures by $1/c$, since an orthonormal basis for $cg$ is $(u, v)/\sqrt c$ for an orthonormal basis $(u, v)$ for $g$.

\begin{definition}\label{riemannian-geometry:definition-model-spaces}
For $n \geq 2$ and $\kappa \in \RR$ the \emph{model space} $M^n_\kappa$ is: Euclidean space $\RR^n$ if $\kappa = 0$; the sphere $S^n(r) = \{x \in \RR^{n+1} : |x| = r\}$ of
radius $r = 1/\sqrt\kappa$ with the induced metric if $\kappa > 0$; and the \emph{hyperbolic space} $\{x \in \RR^n : x^n > 0\}$ with the metric
$|\kappa|^{-1}(dx_1^2 + \cdots + dx_n^2)/(x^n)^2$ if $\kappa < 0$.
\end{definition}

All three are complete, connected and simply connected with constant sectional curvature $\kappa$. For $\RR^n$ this is clear. The sphere $S^n(r)$ has curvature
$1/r^2 = \kappa$ (Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-surfaces}(1)), is complete, being compact (Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-hopf-rinow}), and is simply
connected for $n \geq 2$ (Corollary~\ref{homotopy:corollary-spheres}). Hyperbolic space is simply connected, being a convex subset of $\RR^n$, and it has constant curvature $\kappa$ by
Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-hyperbolic-curvature}(1) and the remark on scaling that follows it. It is complete because its isometries act transitively. Indeed the maps
$T(x) = \lambda x + b$ with $\lambda > 0$ and $b = (b_1, \ldots, b_{n-1}, 0)$ satisfy $T^*\bigl(|dx|^2/x_n^2\bigr) = \lambda^2|dx|^2/(\lambda x_n)^2 = |dx|^2/x_n^2$, so they are isometries, and they map
$p = (0, \ldots, 0, 1)$ to any point. The domain of $\exp_p$ is open, so it contains the ball of radius $2\delta$ in $T_pM$ for some $\delta > 0$; by
Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-local-isometries}(1), applied to these isometries, the same holds at every point. So every unit speed geodesic, starting anywhere, is defined at least on
$(-2\delta, 2\delta)$. If a unit speed geodesic $\gamma$ were defined on $[0, a)$ with $a < \infty$ and could not be extended, the geodesic with initial velocity $\dot\gamma(a - \delta)$ would be
defined on $(-2\delta, 2\delta)$ and would agree with $t \mapsto \gamma(a - \delta + t)$ where both are defined, by uniqueness of geodesics; so it would extend $\gamma$ to $[0, a + \delta)$. Hence
geodesics are defined for all time, and hyperbolic space is complete by Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-hopf-rinow}.

\begin{theorem}[Killing--Hopf]\label{riemannian-geometry:theorem-killing-hopf}
Let $M$ and $N$ be complete, connected and simply connected Riemannian manifolds of the same dimension $n \geq 2$, with the same constant sectional curvature
$\kappa$. For $p \in M$, $q \in N$ and a linear isometry $I \colon T_pM \to T_qN$ there is a unique isometry $F \colon M \to N$ with $F(p) = q$ and $d_pF = I$.
In particular $M$ is isometric to the model space $M^n_\kappa$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Uniqueness is Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-local-isometries}(2). By Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-hopf-rinow}, $\exp_p$ and $\exp_q$ are defined on
the whole tangent spaces. The key observation is the following. Let $U \subseteq T_pM$ be an open set on which $\exp_p$ is a local diffeomorphism, and
$F = \exp_q \circ I \circ \psi$ for a smooth local inverse $\psi$ of $\exp_p|_U$. For $x = \exp_p(v)$, $v \in U$, and $w \in T_pM$ the chain rule gives
$d_xF(d_v\exp_p(w)) = d_{Iv}\exp_q(Iw)$. The linear isometry $I$ preserves lengths and maps $w_\parallel$ and $w_\perp$ to the components of $Iw$ along and orthogonal to
$Iv$, so by \eqref{riemannian-geometry:equation-exp-constant-curvature} on $M$ and on $N$ the vectors $d_v\exp_p(w)$ and $d_{Iv}\exp_q(Iw)$ have the same length (for $v = 0$ both differentials are the identity). As
$d_v\exp_p$ is onto $T_xM$, $d_xF$ preserves lengths, hence inner products: $F$ is a local isometry.

\emph{The case $\kappa \leq 0$.} By Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-cartan-hadamard}, $\exp_p$ and $\exp_q$ are diffeomorphisms, so
$F = \exp_q \circ I \circ \exp_p^{-1} \colon M \to N$ is a diffeomorphism, a local isometry by the observation, hence an isometry; and $F(p) = q$, $d_pF = I$ as
$d_0\exp = \id$.

\emph{The case $\kappa > 0$.} Multiplying both metrics by $\kappa$ we may assume $\kappa = 1$. Let $S = S^n$ be the unit sphere and $o \in S$. We show: for every linear
isometry $J \colon T_oS \to T_pM$ there is an isometry $G \colon S \to M$ with $G(o) = p$ and $d_oG = J$. Applying this to $M$ with $J$ and to $N$ with $I \circ J$ gives
isometries $G_M$ and $G_N$, and $F = G_N \circ G_M^{-1}$ has the required properties.

For a unit vector $u \perp o$, the great circle $c(t) = \cos t\,o + \sin t\,u$ has acceleration $c^{\prime\prime} = -c$, which is normal to $S$, so
$D_tc^{\prime} = (c^{\prime\prime})^\top = 0$ (Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-gauss-formula}): $c$ is the geodesic with initial velocity $u$. Hence
$\exp_o(v) = \cos|v|\,o + \sin|v|\,v/|v|$ for $v \neq 0$. It maps $B = B(0, \pi)$ bijectively onto $S \setminus \{-o\}$:
every $y \neq -o$ is uniquely $\cos\theta\,o + \sin\theta\,u$ with $\theta = \arccos\langle y, o\rangle \in [0, \pi)$ and, if $\theta > 0$, a unit vector $u \perp o$.
By \eqref{riemannian-geometry:equation-exp-constant-curvature}, with $s_1(\ell) = \sin\ell > 0$ for $0 < \ell < \pi$, $d_v\exp_o$ is injective for $v \in B$; so $\exp_o \colon B \to S \setminus \{-o\}$ is a bijective local
diffeomorphism, hence a diffeomorphism. By the observation (with the roles of $M$ and $N$ played by $S$ and $M$),
$G_1 = \exp_p \circ J \circ (\exp_o|_B)^{-1} \colon S \setminus \{-o\} \to M$ is a local isometry with $G_1(o) = p$ and $d_oG_1 = J$. Choose $o^{\prime} \in S$ with
$o^{\prime} \neq \pm o$, and let $G_2 = \exp_{p^{\prime}} \circ J^{\prime} \circ (\exp_{o^{\prime}}|_B)^{-1} \colon S \setminus \{-o^{\prime}\} \to M$ with
$p^{\prime} = G_1(o^{\prime})$ and $J^{\prime} = d_{o^{\prime}}G_1$, again a local isometry. The open set $W = S \setminus \{-o, -o^{\prime}\}$ contains $o^{\prime}$, and it
is connected: stereographic projection from $-o$ (Example~\ref{general-topology:example-stereographic}) maps it homeomorphically onto $\RR^n$ minus a point, which is
connected for $n \geq 2$ (Lemma~\ref{topological-manifolds:lemma-punctured-connected}). On $W$ the local isometries $G_1$ and $G_2$ agree at $o^{\prime}$ together with
their differentials, so $G_1 = G_2$ on $W$ by Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-local-isometries}(2). Since $-o \neq -o^{\prime}$, they glue to a local isometry
$G \colon S \to M$. By Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-proper-covering}, $G$ is a covering map ($S$ is compact and $M$ connected and Hausdorff). As $M$ is simply
connected and $S$ is path-connected, the monodromy action on a fibre is transitive and $\pi_1(M)$ is trivial, so each fibre is a single point
(Corollary~\ref{homotopy:corollary-lifting-consequences}(3)). So $G$ is a bijective local isometry, that is, an isometry, with $G(o) = p$ and $d_oG = J$.

Finally, the model space $M^n_\kappa$ satisfies the hypotheses, so $M$ is isometric to it.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{riemannian-geometry:corollary-model-isometries}
\begin{enumerate}
\item The model spaces are \emph{frame-homogeneous}: for any two points $p, q \in M^n_\kappa$ and any linear isometry $I \colon T_pM^n_\kappa \to T_qM^n_\kappa$ there is a
unique isometry $F$ of $M^n_\kappa$ with $F(p) = q$ and $d_pF = I$.
\item The isometries of $\RR^n$ are the maps $x \mapsto Ax + b$ with $A \in \mathrm{O}(n)$ and $b \in \RR^n$.
\item The isometries of $S^n(r)$ are the restrictions of the maps $A \in \mathrm{O}(n + 1)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
(1) is Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-killing-hopf} with $M = N = M^n_\kappa$. (2) These maps are isometries, since $d(Ax + b) = A$ preserves lengths. Given
an isometry $F$, $A = d_0F$ is an orthogonal matrix and $x \mapsto Ax + F(0)$ agrees with $F$ at $0$ together with its differential, so it equals $F$ by
Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-local-isometries}(2). (3) Restrictions of orthogonal maps are isometries of $S^n(r)$. Given an isometry $F$ and $o \in S^n(r)$,
let $A$ be the linear map with $A(o) = F(o)$ and $A = d_oF$ on $T_oS^n(r) = o^\perp$; it maps the orthonormal basis formed by $o/r$ and an orthonormal basis of $o^\perp$
to an orthonormal basis, since $d_oF$ is an isometry onto $F(o)^\perp$, so $A \in \mathrm{O}(n + 1)$. Then $A|_{S^n(r)}$ and $F$ agree at $o$ together with their
differentials, so they are equal.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Space forms]\label{riemannian-geometry:corollary-space-forms}
Let $M$ be a complete connected Riemannian manifold of dimension $n \geq 2$ with constant sectional curvature $\kappa$. Then $M$ is isometric to $M^n_\kappa/\Gamma$ for
a group $\Gamma \cong \pi_1(M)$ of isometries of $M^n_\kappa$ acting freely, such that the projection $M^n_\kappa \to M^n_\kappa/\Gamma$ is a covering map, with the metric for
which this projection is a local isometry.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Let $\pi \colon \widetilde{M} \to M$ be a universal covering (Theorem~\ref{homotopy:theorem-universal-cover}; manifolds are locally path-connected and locally simply
connected). $\widetilde{M}$ is a connected manifold (Proposition~\ref{topological-manifolds:proposition-constructions}(4)) with a unique smooth structure making $\pi$ a local
diffeomorphism (Example~\ref{smooth-manifolds:example-smooth-quotients}), and $\tilde g = \pi^*g$ makes $\pi$ a local isometry, so $\widetilde{M}$ has constant curvature
$\kappa$. It is complete: let $\tilde\gamma$ be a maximal geodesic of $\widetilde{M}$ with $\tilde\gamma(0) = \tilde x$. Then $\pi \circ \tilde\gamma$ is a geodesic
(Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-local-isometries}(1)), which extends to a geodesic $\gamma$ defined on $\RR$ by completeness of $M$. Let $\hat\gamma$ be the lift of
$\gamma$ starting at $\tilde x$ (Proposition~\ref{homotopy:proposition-homotopy-lifting}). Near each time, $\hat\gamma$ is $\gamma$ followed by a local inverse of $\pi$, which is a
local isometry, so $\hat\gamma$ is a geodesic; it has the initial velocity of $\tilde\gamma$, so it extends $\tilde\gamma$ to $\RR$, and $\tilde\gamma$ was defined on all of
$\RR$. By Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-killing-hopf} there is an isometry $\Theta \colon M^n_\kappa \to \widetilde{M}$.

The deck transformations $\tau$ of $\pi$ satisfy $\pi \circ \tau = \pi$, so $\tau^*\tilde g = \tau^*\pi^*g = \pi^*g = \tilde g$: they are isometries. The group
$\mathrm{Deck}(\pi)$ is isomorphic to $\pi_1(M)$ and acts transitively on each fibre of $\pi$ (Proposition~\ref{homotopy:proposition-deck}), and freely, since a deck
transformation with a fixed point is a lift of $\pi$ agreeing with the identity at one point, hence the identity by the uniqueness in
Proposition~\ref{homotopy:proposition-lifting-criterion}.
So the fibres of $\pi$ are the orbits, and $\pi$ induces a bijection $\widetilde{M}/\mathrm{Deck}(\pi) \to M$, a homeomorphism because $\pi$ is open. The group
$\Gamma = \Theta^{-1}\,\mathrm{Deck}(\pi)\,\Theta$ acts on $M^n_\kappa$ by isometries, freely, and $\pi \circ \Theta$ identifies $M^n_\kappa/\Gamma$ with $M$; the projection is the
covering map $\pi \circ \Theta$, a local isometry.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-space-forms}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Flat manifolds.} The flat tori $\RR^n/\ZZ^n$ and the flat Klein bottle (Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-quotient-metrics}) are complete flat manifolds,
quotients of $\RR^n$.
\item \emph{Spherical space forms.} $\RR\PP^n = S^n/\{\pm1\}$ and the lens spaces $L(p, q) = S^3/(\ZZ/p)$ (Example~\ref{topological-manifolds:example-covering-actions})
have constant curvature $1$, because the maps $(z_1, z_2) \mapsto (\zeta^kz_1, \zeta^{qk}z_2)$ are unitary, hence orthogonal on $\RR^4 = \CC^2$, and so isometries of
$S^3$. In particular, in dimension $3$ there are infinitely many spherical space forms, with fundamental groups $\ZZ/p$ for all $p$.
\item \emph{Hyperbolic surfaces.} By the Gauss--Bonnet theorem (Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-gauss-bonnet}) a compact orientable surface with a metric of
constant curvature $-1$ has genus $g \geq 2$, and its area is $4\pi(g - 1)$; by Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-space-forms} it is a quotient of the hyperbolic
plane. That every genus $g \geq 2$ actually occurs is shown below (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-hyperbolic-surfaces}).
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\emph{A closed surface with a hyperbolic metric.} The flat torus is obtained from a Euclidean square by gluing opposite sides; its four corners come together at one point, where the four
right angles add up to $2\pi$, and this is why the flat metric extends across that point. A surface of genus $2$ is obtained from an octagon by gluing its sides in pairs, and again all eight
corners come together at one point. A Euclidean octagon will not do, as its angles add up to $6\pi$; but in the hyperbolic plane polygons are ``thinner'', the angle sum of a geodesic polygon
being smaller than in the Euclidean plane by its area, and there is a regular octagon with all angles equal to $\pi/4$, whose angles add up to exactly $2\pi$. We construct it in the disc model of
the hyperbolic plane, where geodesics and angles are easy to see.

\begin{lemma}[Regular polygons in the hyperbolic plane]\label{riemannian-geometry:lemma-regular-polygons}
Let $\mathbb{D} = \{z \in \CC : |z| < 1\}$ with the metric $g_{\mathbb{D}} = 4|dz|^2/(1 - |z|^2)^2$, of constant curvature $-1$
(Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-hyperbolic-curvature}(2)).
\begin{enumerate}
\item The rotations $z \mapsto e^{i\theta}z$ and the conjugation $z \mapsto \bar z$ are isometries of $\mathbb{D}$. Let $C$ be a circle with centre $c$ and radius $s$ such that $|c|^2 = 1 + s^2$, that is, a
circle meeting the unit circle at right angles. Then the inversion $J_C(z) = c + s^2(z - c)/|z - c|^2$ maps $\mathbb{D}$ onto itself and is an isometry of $\mathbb{D}$; its fixed points in $\mathbb{D}$ are the
points of the arc $C \cap \mathbb{D}$; and this arc, parametrised by arc length, is a geodesic.
\item The angle between two tangent vectors at a point of $\mathbb{D}$ for the metric $g_{\mathbb{D}}$ is their Euclidean angle, and $\mathbb{D}$ is complete.
\item Let $n \geq 3$, $0 < \rho < 1$, and $v_k = \rho e^{2\pi ik/n}$ for $k \in \ZZ/n\ZZ$. For each $k$ there is exactly one circle $C_k$ as in (1) through $v_k$ and $v_{k+1}$. The arcs of the $C_k$
from $v_k$ to $v_{k+1}$ are geodesic segments of the same length, and they bound a \emph{regular geodesic $n$-gon} $P_\rho$, invariant under the rotation by $2\pi/n$ and under conjugation, all of whose
interior angles are equal to the angle $\alpha \in (0, \pi)$ with
\[
\tan\frac{\alpha}{2} = \frac{1 - \rho^2}{1 + \rho^2}\cot\frac{\pi}{n} .
\]
As $\rho$ increases from $0$ to $1$, $\alpha$ decreases strictly and continuously from $\frac{(n - 2)\pi}{n}$, the angle of a Euclidean regular $n$-gon, to $0$. In particular the interior angles equal
$\frac{2\pi}{n}$ if and only if $n > 4$ and $\rho^2 = \cos\frac{2\pi}{n}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(1) Rotations and conjugation preserve $|z|$ and $|dz|$. For the inversion write $w = z - c$; note $|c| > 1$, so $c \notin \overline{\mathbb{D}}$. The map $w \mapsto w/|w|^2$ has differential
$\frac{1}{|w|^2}\bigl(I - 2\frac{ww^T}{|w|^2}\bigr)$, a reflection times $1/|w|^2$, so $J_C^*|dz|^2 = s^4|dz|^2/|w|^4$. Next, expanding $|J_C(z)|^2|w|^2 = |c|^2|w|^2 + 2s^2\operatorname{Re}(\bar cw) + s^4$ and
using $|w|^2 = |z|^2 - 2\operatorname{Re}(\bar cz) + |c|^2$, $\operatorname{Re}(\bar cw) = \operatorname{Re}(\bar cz) - |c|^2$ and $1 - |c|^2 = -s^2$,
\[
\bigl(1 - |J_C(z)|^2\bigr)|w|^2 = -s^2|w|^2 - 2s^2\operatorname{Re}(\bar cz) + 2s^2|c|^2 - s^4 = -s^2|z|^2 + s^2|c|^2 - s^4 = s^2\bigl(1 - |z|^2\bigr).
\]
Hence $J_C$ maps $\mathbb{D}$ into $\mathbb{D}$; it is an involution of $\CC \setminus \{c\}$, so it maps $\mathbb{D}$ onto $\mathbb{D}$; and
\[
J_C^*g_{\mathbb{D}} = \frac{4s^4|dz|^2}{|w|^4} \cdot \frac{|w|^4}{s^4(1 - |z|^2)^2} = g_{\mathbb{D}} .
\]
$J_C(z) = z$ means $s^2/|w|^2 = 1$, that is, $|z - c| = s$. Let $p \in C \cap \mathbb{D}$ and let $u$ be a unit tangent vector of $C$ at $p$. Since $J_C$ fixes $C \cap \mathbb{D}$ pointwise, $d_pJ_C(u) = u$,
and by Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-local-isometries}(1) the geodesic $\gamma$ with $\dot\gamma(0) = u$ satisfies $J_C \circ \gamma = \gamma$: it stays in the fixed set $C \cap \mathbb{D}$. As
$\dot\gamma \neq 0$, on a small interval $\gamma$ is a parametrisation with constant speed of an open sub-arc of $C \cap \mathbb{D}$ around $p$; so near $p$ the parametrisation of $C \cap \mathbb{D}$ by
arc length differs from $\gamma$ by a change of parameter $t \mapsto \pm t + \mathrm{const}$, and is a geodesic near $p$. As $p$ was arbitrary, it is a geodesic.

(2) $g_{\mathbb{D}}$ is the Euclidean inner product multiplied at each point by the positive number $4/(1 - |z|^2)^2$, which does not change angles. The isometries of (1) act transitively on
$\mathbb{D}$: for $c > 1$ real and $s^2 = c^2 - 1$, $J_C(0) = c - s^2/c = 1/c$, which takes every value in $(0, 1)$, and rotations then reach every point. So the argument given above for the half-space
shows that $\mathbb{D}$ is complete.

(3) A circle through $v_0$ and $v_1$ has its centre on the perpendicular bisector of the segment $v_0v_1$, which is the line through $0$ in the direction $e^{i\pi/n}$, as $|v_0| = |v_1|$. So
$c = de^{i\pi/n}$ with $d \in \RR$, and the conditions $|c|^2 = 1 + s^2$ and $|v_0 - c|^2 = s^2$ give $\rho^2 - 2\rho d\cos\frac{\pi}{n} + d^2 = d^2 - 1$, that is,
\[
d = \frac{1 + \rho^2}{2\rho\cos(\pi/n)} ,
\]
which determines $C_0$ uniquely ($\cos\frac{\pi}{n} > 0$ as $n \geq 3$). The rotation $R$ by $2\pi/n$ maps $v_k$ to $v_{k+1}$ and circles orthogonal to the unit circle to such circles, so by uniqueness
$R(C_k) = C_{k+1}$; likewise conjugation maps $C_0$ to $C_{-1}$, since it fixes $v_0$ and maps $v_1$ to $v_{-1}$. By (1) the arcs from $v_k$ to $v_{k+1}$ are geodesic segments, and $R$, an isometry, maps
each to the next, so they have the same length; and the polygon $P_\rho$ they bound is invariant under $R$ and conjugation. Hence all interior angles are equal, and the one at $v_0 = \rho$ is bisected by
the real axis, so $\frac{\alpha}{2}$ is the angle at $v_0$ between the real axis and the tangent of $C_0$, measured on the side of $0$; by (2) we may compute it in Euclidean geometry. The tangent of
$C_0$ at $v_0$ is perpendicular to the radius $r = c - v_0$, so $\frac{\alpha}{2}$ equals the angle between $r$ and the imaginary axis, whose tangent is $\operatorname{Re}r/\operatorname{Im}r$. Here
\[
\operatorname{Re}r = d\cos\frac{\pi}{n} - \rho = \frac{1 - \rho^2}{2\rho} > 0, \qquad \operatorname{Im}r = d\sin\frac{\pi}{n} = \frac{1 + \rho^2}{2\rho}\tan\frac{\pi}{n} > 0,
\]
which gives the formula. As $\rho$ runs from $0$ to $1$, $\frac{1 - \rho^2}{1 + \rho^2}$ decreases strictly and continuously from $1$ to $0$, so $\tan\frac{\alpha}{2}$ decreases from
$\cot\frac{\pi}{n} = \tan(\frac{\pi}{2} - \frac{\pi}{n})$ to $0$, and $\alpha$ from $\pi - \frac{2\pi}{n}$ to $0$. Finally $\alpha = \frac{2\pi}{n}$ means $\frac{1 - \rho^2}{1 + \rho^2} = \tan^2\frac{\pi}{n}$,
that is, $\rho^2 = \frac{1 - \tan^2(\pi/n)}{1 + \tan^2(\pi/n)} = \cos\frac{2\pi}{n}$, which lies in $(0, 1)$ exactly when $n > 4$.
\end{proof}

For $n = 8$ the octagon with angles $\frac{\pi}{4}$ has $\rho^2 = \cos\frac{\pi}{4} = 2^{-1/2}$: its vertices lie at Euclidean distance $\rho = 2^{-1/4} \approx 0.84$ from the centre. For $n = 4$ the required angle $\frac{\pi}{2}$ is the Euclidean one, which no hyperbolic square attains: the torus is flat, not hyperbolic.

\begin{proposition}[Closed hyperbolic surfaces]\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-hyperbolic-surfaces}
For every $g \geq 2$ there is a compact surface homeomorphic to the closed orientable surface $\Sigma_g$ of genus $g$ with a Riemannian metric of constant curvature $-1$. Its area is $4\pi(g - 1)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Sketch of proof]
Let $n = 4g > 4$. By Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-regular-polygons}(3) there is a regular geodesic $n$-gon $P \subseteq \mathbb{D}$ with interior angles $\frac{2\pi}{n}$ and all sides of the same
length. Number its sides $\sigma_1, \ldots, \sigma_n$ counterclockwise and label them, in blocks of four, by the word
\[
a_1b_1a_1^{-1}b_1^{-1}\,a_2b_2a_2^{-1}b_2^{-1} \cdots a_gb_ga_g^{-1}b_g^{-1}:
\]
in the $j$-th block, the sides $\sigma_{4j-3}$ (labelled $a_j$) and $\sigma_{4j-1}$ (labelled $a_j^{-1}$) are to be glued, and so are $\sigma_{4j-2}$ and $\sigma_{4j}$, each with the orientation that the
exponent indicates relative to the counterclockwise direction.

\emph{The side pairings are isometries.} $\mathbb{D}$ is complete (Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-regular-polygons}(2)), simply connected (it is convex) and of constant curvature $-1$, so by
Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-killing-hopf} with $M = N = \mathbb{D}$ it is frame-homogeneous. For a pair of glued sides $\sigma$, $\sigma^{\prime}$ with midpoints $m$, $m^{\prime}$, let $T$ be the
isometry of $\mathbb{D}$ with $T(m) = m^{\prime}$ whose differential maps the unit tangent of $\sigma$ at $m$ to the unit tangent of $\sigma^{\prime}$ at $m^{\prime}$ prescribed by the gluing, and the inward
unit normal of $P$ at $m$ to the outward unit normal at $m^{\prime}$; such a linear isometry exists, as it maps an orthonormal basis to an orthonormal basis. By
Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-local-isometries}(1), $T$ maps geodesics to geodesics, and $\sigma$, $\sigma^{\prime}$ are geodesic segments of the same length centred at $m$, $m^{\prime}$; so
$T(\sigma) = \sigma^{\prime}$, and $T(P)$ lies on the other side of $\sigma^{\prime}$. Let $X$ be the quotient of $P$ by the relation identifying each $x \in \sigma$ with $T(x) \in \sigma^{\prime}$, for
the $2g$ pairs of sides.

\emph{Omitted verifications.} (a) $X$ is homeomorphic to $\Sigma_g$: this is the standard description of $\Sigma_g$ as a $4g$-gon with the above edge word \cite{hatcher-at}, and in particular all $n$
vertices of $P$ become a single point of $X$. (b) $X$ has a smooth structure and a metric for which every point has a neighbourhood isometric to an open subset of $\mathbb{D}$, compatibly with the interior
of $P$. At interior points of $P$ this is clear. At a point $x$ of a side $\sigma$, a half-disc of $P$ around $x$ and the image under $T^{-1}$ of a half-disc of $P$ around $T(x)$ lie on opposite sides of
$\sigma$ and together form a disc in $\mathbb{D}$. At the vertex, the $n$ corner sectors of $P$, each of angle $\frac{2\pi}{n}$, moved by suitable composites of the pairings $T$, fit together cyclically
around one vertex of $P$ without overlapping and fill a disc, because their angles add up to $n \cdot \frac{2\pi}{n} = 2\pi$; we omit the bookkeeping of which composites occur. This is the case of a
single vertex cycle of Poincar\'e's polygon theorem. Since curvature is computed locally, $X$ has constant curvature $-1$.

\emph{Area.} $P$ is a simple region in the chart $\mathbb{D}$ (it is star-shaped with respect to $0$), its sides are geodesics, so their geodesic curvature vanishes, $K = -1$, and its exterior angles are
$\pi - \frac{2\pi}{n}$. The local Gauss--Bonnet theorem (Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-local-gauss-bonnet}) gives $-\operatorname{Area}(P) + n(\pi - \frac{2\pi}{n}) = 2\pi$, so
$\operatorname{Area}(P) = (n - 4)\pi = 4\pi(g - 1)$. The boundary of $P$ has measure zero, so $X$ has the same area, in accordance with Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-space-forms}(3).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-hyperbolic-surfaces}
\begin{enumerate}
\item The construction explains the dichotomy of Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-gauss-bonnet}. The angle sum of a geodesic $n$-gon of area $A$ in a surface of constant curvature $\kappa$ is
$(n - 2)\pi + \kappa A$ by the local Gauss--Bonnet theorem; gluing a $4g$-gon whose vertices all become one point requires the angle sum $2\pi$, so $\kappa A = (4 - 4g)\pi$: the curvature must be zero for $g = 1$
(the square of the flat torus) and negative for $g \geq 2$.
\item By Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-space-forms}, $X$ is isometric to $\mathbb{D}/\Gamma$ for a group $\Gamma \cong \pi_1(\Sigma_g)$ of isometries of $\mathbb{D}$ acting freely;
the side pairings $T$ are the natural candidates for generators of $\Gamma$, and the copies $\gamma(P)$ for a picture of the hyperbolic plane tiled by octagons, eight of them meeting at each vertex
for $g = 2$, as the plane is tiled by the squares of the flat torus, with $\Gamma = \ZZ^2$, for $g = 1$. Proving this is the rest of Poincar\'e's polygon theorem, which we do not develop.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

In even dimension the positive curvature case is very restricted.

\begin{corollary}\label{riemannian-geometry:corollary-even-spherical}
Let $n$ be even and $M$ complete and connected with constant curvature $\kappa > 0$. Then $M$ is isometric to $S^n(r)$ or to $\RR\PP^n(r) = S^n(r)/\{\pm1\}$, with
$r = 1/\sqrt\kappa$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By Corollaries~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-space-forms} and~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-model-isometries}(3), $M = S^n(r)/\Gamma$ for a subgroup
$\Gamma \subseteq \mathrm{O}(n + 1)$ acting freely on the sphere. Every $A \in \mathrm{SO}(n + 1)$ has the eigenvalue $1$, because $n + 1$ is odd:
$\det(A - I) = \det(A - AA^T) = \det A\,\det(I - A^T) = \det(I - A) = (-1)^{n+1}\det(A - I) = -\det(A - I)$. A unit eigenvector, scaled to length $r$, is a fixed point of
$A$ on $S^n(r)$, so $\Gamma \cap \mathrm{SO}(n + 1) = \{I\}$, and $\det \colon \Gamma \to \{\pm1\}$ is injective. If $\Gamma \neq \{I\}$ it is $\{I, A\}$ with
$\det A = -1$ and $A^2 = I$; then $A$ is diagonalisable with eigenvalues $\pm1$ (its minimal polynomial divides $x^2 - 1$), and the eigenvalue $1$ is excluded since the
action is free, so $A = -I$.
\end{proof}

Finally we state two rigidity theorems for positive curvature, whose proofs use comparison geometry beyond this chapter. The first says that the diameter bound of
the Bonnet--Myers theorem (Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-bonnet-myers}) is attained only by the round sphere.

\begin{theorem}[Cheng's maximal diameter theorem]\label{riemannian-geometry:theorem-maximal-diameter}
Let $M$ be complete and connected of dimension $n$, with $\mathrm{Ric} \geq (n - 1)\kappa g$ for some $\kappa > 0$, and with diameter $\pi/\sqrt\kappa$. Then $M$ is
isometric to the sphere $S^n(1/\sqrt\kappa)$.
\end{theorem}

This is \cite{cheng-1975}. For example $\RR\PP^n$ with the metric of constant curvature $1$ has $\mathrm{Ric} = (n - 1)g$ but diameter $\pi/2$, and a product
$S^2 \times S^2$ of unit spheres has $\mathrm{Ric} = g$ and diameter $\sqrt2\,\pi$, less than the bound $\pi/\sqrt{1/3} = \sqrt3\,\pi$ given by
Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-bonnet-myers} with $n = 4$ and $3\kappa = 1$.

\begin{theorem}[Sphere theorem]\label{riemannian-geometry:theorem-sphere}
Let $M$ be complete and simply connected with sectional curvatures in $(\frac14, 1]$. Then $M$ is homeomorphic to $S^n$ (Rauch, Berger, Klingenberg), and in fact
diffeomorphic to $S^n$ (Brendle and Schoen).
\end{theorem}

The topological statement is \cite[Chapter 13]{do-carmo-riemannian}; the differentiable one is \cite{brendle-schoen-2009}, which proves more generally that a compact
manifold with pointwise $\frac14$-pinched curvature is diffeomorphic to a spherical space form. The constant $\frac14$ is optimal: the complex projective space $\CC\PP^m$
with the Fubini--Study metric (Chapter~\ref{kahler}) has sectional curvatures filling $[\frac14, 1]$ after scaling, it is compact and simply connected, and for $m \geq 2$
it is not homeomorphic to a sphere, since $H^2(\CC\PP^m; \ZZ) \cong \ZZ$. By Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-bonnet-myers}, the hypothesis of the sphere theorem
already forces $M$ to be compact.

\section{Geometric structures}\label{riemannian-geometry:section-geometric-structures}

In Klein's Erlangen programme (1872) a geometry is a space $X$ together with a group $G$ of transformations of $X$, and the geometry studies the properties of figures in $X$ that are invariant under $G$:
Euclidean geometry is $\RR^n$ with its isometries, affine geometry is $\RR^n$ with the affine maps, projective geometry is $\RR\PP^n$ with the projective transformations. A \emph{$(G, X)$-structure} on a manifold
$M$ (Ehresmann, Thurston) is a way of making $M$ look locally like $X$, with changes of coordinates in $G$. Constant curvature metrics are the first example: by Killing--Hopf they are exactly the structures modelled on
the model spaces with their isometry groups, and the space forms of Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-space-forms} are the complete ones. This section sets up the general language: the developing map and
the holonomy, completeness, and an example of a compact manifold with an incomplete structure. References are \cite[Chapter 3]{thurston-1997} and \cite{goldman-2022}.

\begin{definition}\label{riemannian-geometry:definition-geometry}
A \emph{geometry} $(G, X)$ is a connected smooth manifold $X$ together with a group $G$ of diffeomorphisms of $X$ which is \emph{rigid}: if $g, h \in G$ agree on a nonempty open subset of $X$, then $g = h$.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-geometries}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Constant curvature.} $(\mathrm{Isom}(M^n_\kappa), M^n_\kappa)$ for the model spaces of Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-model-spaces}: Euclidean, spherical and hyperbolic geometry. Rigidity holds for the
isometries of any connected Riemannian manifold: two isometries that agree on an open set agree at a point together with their differentials, hence everywhere (Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-local-isometries}(2)).
\item \emph{Affine geometry.} $(\mathrm{Aff}(\RR^n), \RR^n)$, with $\mathrm{Aff}(\RR^n) = \{x \mapsto Ax + b : A \in \GL_n(\RR), b \in \RR^n\}$. If $Ax + b = A^{\prime}x + b^{\prime}$ on an open set $U$, then $A - A^{\prime}$ vanishes on all differences
$x - y$ with $x, y \in U$, which span $\RR^n$; so $A = A^{\prime}$ and $b = b^{\prime}$. The subgroup of \emph{similarities} $x \mapsto \lambda Ax + b$, $\lambda > 0$, $A \in \mathrm{O}(n)$, gives \emph{similarity geometry}, between
Euclidean and affine geometry.
\item \emph{Projective geometry.} $(\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}(\RR), \RR\PP^n)$, where $[A]$ acts by $[v] \mapsto [Av]$. If $[A]$ and $[B]$ agree on a nonempty open set, then $C = A^{-1}B$ has every vector of a nonempty open cone
$V \subseteq \RR^{n+1}$ as an eigenvector. Choose a basis $v_0, \ldots, v_n$ of $\RR^{n+1}$ in $V$ (an open set spans) with $Cv_i = \lambda_iv_i$; for $i \neq j$ and small $\varepsilon$, $v_i + \varepsilon v_j \in V$, and
$C(v_i + \varepsilon v_j) = \lambda_iv_i + \varepsilon\lambda_jv_j$ is a multiple of $v_i + \varepsilon v_j$ only if $\lambda_i = \lambda_j$. So $C$ is a scalar and $[A] = [B]$. Affine geometry is contained in projective geometry: $\RR^n$ is
the open set $\{[x : 1]\}$ of $\RR\PP^n$, and the affine maps are the projective transformations preserving it, $[x : 1] \mapsto [Ax + b : 1]$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{definition}\label{riemannian-geometry:definition-gx-structure}
Let $(G, X)$ be a geometry and $M$ a manifold of the dimension of $X$. A \emph{$(G, X)$-atlas} on $M$ is a family of charts $\varphi_i \colon U_i \to X$, homeomorphisms of open sets $U_i$ covering $M$ onto open subsets
of $X$, such that for every connected component $C$ of every $U_i \cap U_j$ there is a $g \in G$ with $\varphi_i = g \circ \varphi_j$ on $C$. A \emph{$(G, X)$-structure} is a maximal $(G, X)$-atlas, and a
\emph{$(G, X)$-manifold} is a manifold with a $(G, X)$-structure. A \emph{$(G, X)$-map} $f \colon M \to N$ between $(G, X)$-manifolds is a local homeomorphism that is, in charts, the restriction of elements of $G$: for
charts $\varphi$ of $M$ and $\psi$ of $N$, $\psi \circ f \circ \varphi^{-1}$ agrees with an element of $G$ near each point where it is defined. In particular a $(G, X)$-map $M \to X$ is a local homeomorphism whose restrictions
to small open sets are charts.
\end{definition}

By rigidity the element $g$ in the definition is unique. Every $(G, X)$-atlas is contained in a unique maximal one, the charts compatible with all of its charts, by the argument for smooth atlases. Since $G$
consists of diffeomorphisms, a $(G, X)$-atlas is a smooth atlas, so a $(G, X)$-manifold is a smooth manifold and $(G, X)$-maps are local diffeomorphisms.

\begin{lemma}\label{riemannian-geometry:lemma-gx-constructions}
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $p \colon \widetilde{M} \to M$ is a covering map and $M$ is a $(G, X)$-manifold, there is a unique $(G, X)$-structure on $\widetilde{M}$ for which $p$ is a $(G, X)$-map.
\item If $\Omega \subseteq X$ is open and $\Gamma \subseteq G$ is a subgroup preserving $\Omega$ and acting on it by a covering space action (Definition~\ref{topological-manifolds:definition-covering-action}) with $\Omega/\Gamma$ Hausdorff, then
$\Omega/\Gamma$ has a unique $(G, X)$-structure for which the projection $\Omega \to \Omega/\Gamma$ is a $(G, X)$-map.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(1) The charts $\varphi \circ p|_V$, for open sets $V \subseteq \widetilde{M}$ mapped homeomorphically by $p$ onto the domain of a chart $\varphi$, cover $\widetilde{M}$. On a connected component $C$ of the overlap of two of them,
$\varphi \circ p|_V$ and $\psi \circ p|_{V^{\prime}}$ are $\varphi$ and $\psi$ composed with the same map $p|_C$, and $\varphi = g \circ \psi$ on the connected set $p(C)$. Any structure for which $p$ is a $(G, X)$-map contains
these charts, which gives uniqueness.

(2) By Theorem~\ref{topological-manifolds:theorem-covering-action} the projection $\pi \colon \Omega \to \Omega/\Gamma$ is a covering map; it is open, so $\Omega/\Gamma$ is locally Euclidean, and it is Hausdorff by assumption and second
countable as the image of a second countable space under an open map. Take as charts the inverses $\sigma \colon \pi(U) \to U$ of the restrictions $\pi|_U$ to the open sets $U$ with $\gamma U \cap U = \emptyset$ for $\gamma \neq e$.
For two of them, $\sigma$ and $\sigma^{\prime}$, and $y$ in the overlap, $\sigma(y)$ and $\sigma^{\prime}(y)$ lie in the same orbit, so $\sigma(y) = \gamma(y)\sigma^{\prime}(y)$ for a unique $\gamma(y) \in \Gamma$. Near $y$, the map
$\gamma(y) \circ \sigma^{\prime}$ is again a local inverse of $\pi$ taking the value $\sigma(y)$ at $y$, so it coincides with $\sigma$ near $y$; hence $y \mapsto \gamma(y)$ is locally constant, thus constant on each connected component
of the overlap, and $\sigma = \gamma \circ \sigma^{\prime}$ there. Uniqueness is as in (1).
\end{proof}

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-gx-structures}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Flat tori and the flat Klein bottle} are $(\mathrm{Isom}(\RR^n), \RR^n)$-manifolds by Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-gx-constructions}(2), as quotients of $\RR^n$ by groups of isometries acting by covering space actions
(Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-space-forms}). The same holds for all space forms $M^n_\kappa/\Gamma$.
\item \emph{Hopf manifolds.} For $n \geq 2$, the group $\Gamma = \{x \mapsto 2^kx : k \in \ZZ\}$ of similarities acts on $\Omega = \RR^n \setminus \{0\}$ freely and properly (a compact subset of $\Omega$ lies in some
$\{a \leq |x| \leq b\}$ with $a > 0$, and only finitely many $2^k$ map this annulus to a set meeting it), so by Theorem~\ref{topological-manifolds:theorem-proper-free} and the lemma, the \emph{Hopf manifold}
$H^n = (\RR^n \setminus \{0\})/\Gamma$ is a similarity manifold, hence an affine manifold. It is compact and diffeomorphic to $S^{n-1} \times S^1$, by $x \mapsto (x/|x|, \log_2|x| \bmod 1)$.
\item \emph{Affine structures are projective structures}, because $\RR^n \subseteq \RR\PP^n$ and $\mathrm{Aff}(\RR^n) \subseteq \mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}(\RR)$ as in Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-geometries}(3), with
the maps agreeing; so an affine atlas is a projective atlas.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{proposition}\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-constant-curvature-gx}
Let $n \geq 2$ and $\kappa \in \RR$. A Riemannian metric of constant sectional curvature $\kappa$ on an $n$-manifold $M$ is the same as an $(\mathrm{Isom}(M^n_\kappa), M^n_\kappa)$-structure: for such a metric, the
isometries of open subsets of $M$ onto open subsets of $M^n_\kappa$ form a $(G, X)$-structure, and conversely the charts of such a structure pull the metric of $M^n_\kappa$ back to a well-defined metric of curvature $\kappa$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $M$ have constant curvature $\kappa$, $p \in M$, $q \in M^n_\kappa$ and $I \colon T_pM \to T_qM^n_\kappa$ a linear isometry. Choose $\varepsilon > 0$ such that $\exp_p$ maps $B(0, \varepsilon) \subseteq T_pM$ diffeomorphically onto an open set
$U \ni p$ (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-exponential}) and, if $\kappa > 0$, $\varepsilon < \pi/\sqrt\kappa$, so that $\exp_q$ is injective on $B(0, \varepsilon) \subseteq T_qM^n_\kappa$ (for $\kappa \leq 0$ it is injective
everywhere, Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-cartan-hadamard}; for the sphere $S^n(r)$ the geodesics from $q$ first meet again at $-q$, at distance $\pi r$, as in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-killing-hopf}). By the key observation in that proof, $F = \exp_q \circ I \circ (\exp_p|_{B(0, \varepsilon)})^{-1} \colon U \to M^n_\kappa$ is a local isometry, and it is injective, so it is an isometry
of $U$ onto an open subset of $M^n_\kappa$. These maps cover $M$. If $F_1$ and $F_2$ are two of them and $C$ is a connected component of the overlap of their domains, $F_1 \circ F_2^{-1}$ is an isometry between connected open
subsets of $M^n_\kappa$; at a point $y$ it agrees, together with its differential, with an isometry $g$ of $M^n_\kappa$ (Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-model-isometries}(1)), hence $F_1 \circ F_2^{-1} = g$ on the connected
set $F_2(C)$ by Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-local-isometries}(2), and $F_1 = g \circ F_2$ on $C$. Conversely, given an $(\mathrm{Isom}(M^n_\kappa), M^n_\kappa)$-atlas, the metrics $\varphi_i^*g_\kappa$ agree on overlaps because
$\varphi_i = g \circ \varphi_j$ with $g^*g_\kappa = g_\kappa$; the resulting metric is locally isometric to $M^n_\kappa$, so it has constant curvature $\kappa$. The two constructions are inverse to each other: the charts of the
structure obtained from the metric $\varphi_i^*g_\kappa$ are the isometries onto open subsets of $M^n_\kappa$, which include the $\varphi_i$.
\end{proof}

The central construction of the theory extends the charts along paths. On a simply connected manifold the extensions fit together into a single map to $X$; in general they do so on the universal cover,
and going around a loop changes the extension by an element of $G$.

\begin{theorem}[Developing map and holonomy]\label{riemannian-geometry:theorem-developing-map}
Let $M$ be a connected $(G, X)$-manifold, $\pi \colon \widetilde{M} \to M$ a universal covering with the structure of Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-gx-constructions}(1), and $\mathrm{Deck}(\pi) \cong \pi_1(M)$ its group of
deck transformations.
\begin{enumerate}
\item There is a $(G, X)$-map $\mathrm{dev} \colon \widetilde{M} \to X$, the \emph{developing map}, and a homomorphism $\mathrm{hol} \colon \mathrm{Deck}(\pi) \to G$, the \emph{holonomy}, such that
\[
\mathrm{dev} \circ \tau = \mathrm{hol}(\tau) \circ \mathrm{dev} \qquad \text{for all } \tau \in \mathrm{Deck}(\pi).
\]
\item Every $(G, X)$-map $D \colon \widetilde{M} \to X$ is $g \circ \mathrm{dev}$ for a unique $g \in G$, and then its holonomy is $\tau \mapsto g\,\mathrm{hol}(\tau)g^{-1}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\emph{The space of germs of charts.} Two charts $\varphi$, $\psi$ defined near $x \in M$ have the same \emph{germ} at $x$ if they agree on a neighbourhood of $x$; write $[\varphi]_x$ for the germ. Let $P$ be the set of all germs of charts
at all points, $q \colon P \to M$ the base point map, and give $P$ the topology with basis the sets $N(\varphi, V) = \{[\varphi]_y : y \in V\}$ for charts $\varphi$ and open sets $V$ in their domains (an intersection of two such sets
is a union of such sets, as two charts with the same germ at $y$ agree near $y$). The group $G$ acts on $P$ by $g \cdot [\varphi]_x = [g \circ \varphi]_x$, mapping $N(\varphi, V)$ to $N(g \circ \varphi, V)$, so by homeomorphisms
commuting with $q$; and the \emph{evaluation} $E \colon P \to X$, $E([\varphi]_x) = \varphi(x)$, satisfies $E(g \cdot \xi) = gE(\xi)$.

\emph{$q$ is a covering map.} Let $\varphi \colon U \to X$ be a chart with $U$ connected. Every germ at a point $y \in U$ is $[g \circ \varphi]_y$ for some $g$: for a chart $\psi$ at $y$, $\psi = g \circ \varphi$ on the connected
component of the overlap containing $y$. And $[g \circ \varphi]_y = [h \circ \varphi]_y$ implies that $g$ and $h$ agree on an open subset of $X$, so $g = h$ by rigidity. Hence $q^{-1}(U)$ is the disjoint union of the open sets
$N(g \circ \varphi, U)$, $g \in G$, each mapped homeomorphically onto $U$ by $q$: the inverse $y \mapsto [g \circ \varphi]_y$ is continuous, since it maps open subsets $V$ of $U$ to basic open sets $N(g \circ \varphi, V)$. On the sheet
$N(\varphi, U)$, $E \circ (q|_{N(\varphi, U)})^{-1} = \varphi$.

\emph{Construction.} Fix $\tilde x_0 \in \widetilde{M}$, $x_0 = \pi(\tilde x_0)$ and a chart $\varphi_0$ at $x_0$. Since $\widetilde{M}$ is simply connected and locally path-connected, the lifting criterion
(Proposition~\ref{homotopy:proposition-lifting-criterion}) gives a unique continuous $s \colon \widetilde{M} \to P$ with $q \circ s = \pi$ and $s(\tilde x_0) = [\varphi_0]_{x_0}$. Put $\mathrm{dev} = E \circ s$. Near any point $\tilde x$,
$s$ takes values in one sheet $N(\varphi, U)$ with $\varphi \colon U \to X$ a chart on a connected neighbourhood of $\pi(\tilde x)$; then $s = (q|_{N(\varphi, U)})^{-1} \circ \pi$ there, and $\mathrm{dev} = \varphi \circ \pi$, which is a
chart of $\widetilde{M}$ when restricted to an open set mapped homeomorphically by $\pi$. So $\mathrm{dev}$ is a $(G, X)$-map.

\emph{Holonomy.} Let $\tau$ be a deck transformation. Then $s \circ \tau$ is a lift of $\pi \circ \tau = \pi$, and $s(\tau\tilde x_0)$ lies in the fibre of $q$ over $x_0$, which is $\{g \cdot s(\tilde x_0) : g \in G\}$ with $g$ unique by the
previous step. Define $\mathrm{hol}(\tau)$ by $s(\tau\tilde x_0) = \mathrm{hol}(\tau) \cdot s(\tilde x_0)$. The map $\tilde x \mapsto \mathrm{hol}(\tau) \cdot s(\tilde x)$ is also a lift of $\pi$, and it agrees with $s \circ \tau$ at $\tilde x_0$;
by uniqueness of lifts, $s \circ \tau = \mathrm{hol}(\tau) \cdot s$. Applying $E$ gives $\mathrm{dev} \circ \tau = \mathrm{hol}(\tau) \circ \mathrm{dev}$. For two deck transformations,
$s \circ \tau\sigma = \mathrm{hol}(\tau) \cdot (s \circ \sigma) = \mathrm{hol}(\tau)\mathrm{hol}(\sigma) \cdot s$, and evaluating at $\tilde x_0$ gives $\mathrm{hol}(\tau\sigma) = \mathrm{hol}(\tau)\mathrm{hol}(\sigma)$.

(2) Every point has a connected neighbourhood $V$ on which $D$ and $\mathrm{dev}$ are both charts of $\widetilde{M}$, so $D = g_V \circ \mathrm{dev}$ on $V$ for some $g_V \in G$. The element $g_V$ is locally constant: if $V \cap V^{\prime} \neq \emptyset$, then $g_V$ and $g_{V^{\prime}}$ agree on the
nonempty open set $\mathrm{dev}(V \cap V^{\prime})$, so they are equal. As $\widetilde{M}$ is connected, $D = g \circ \mathrm{dev}$ for a single $g$, unique by rigidity. Then
$D \circ \tau = g\,\mathrm{hol}(\tau) \circ \mathrm{dev} = g\,\mathrm{hol}(\tau)g^{-1} \circ D$.
\end{proof}

For a Riemannian manifold of constant curvature, the developing map extends a local isometry with the model space along all paths: it is the map $\Theta^{-1}$ of the proof of
Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-space-forms}, when $M$ is complete. In general $\mathrm{dev}$ need not be injective or surjective, and this is measured by completeness.

\begin{definition}\label{riemannian-geometry:definition-complete-gx}
A connected $(G, X)$-manifold is \emph{complete} if its developing map $\mathrm{dev} \colon \widetilde{M} \to X$ is a covering map. By Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-developing-map}(2) this does not depend on the choices.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}\label{riemannian-geometry:proposition-complete-gx}
Let $(G, X)$ be a geometry with $X$ simply connected, and $M$ a connected $(G, X)$-manifold.
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $M$ is complete, then $\mathrm{dev}$ is a homeomorphism, $\mathrm{hol}$ is injective, $\Gamma = \mathrm{hol}(\mathrm{Deck}(\pi)) \cong \pi_1(M)$ acts on $X$ by a covering space action, and $\mathrm{dev}$ induces an isomorphism of
$(G, X)$-manifolds $M \cong X/\Gamma$. Conversely the quotients $X/\Gamma$ of Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-gx-constructions}(2), with $\Omega = X$, are complete.
\item If $G$ preserves a Riemannian metric on $X$ and $M$ is compact, then $M$ is complete.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(1) A covering map from a connected space onto the simply connected, locally path-connected space $X$ is a homeomorphism: the monodromy action on a fibre is transitive and $\pi_1(X)$ is trivial
(Corollary~\ref{homotopy:corollary-lifting-consequences}(3)), as in the proof of Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-killing-hopf}. If $\mathrm{hol}(\tau) = e$, then $\mathrm{dev} \circ \tau = \mathrm{dev}$, so $\tau = \mathrm{id}$, and
$\mathrm{hol}$ is injective. The homeomorphism $\mathrm{dev}$ conjugates $\mathrm{Deck}(\pi)$ to $\Gamma$, and $\mathrm{Deck}(\pi)$ acts by a covering space action (each point has a neighbourhood mapped injectively by $\pi$), so $\Gamma$ does too,
and $\mathrm{dev}$ induces a homeomorphism $M \cong \widetilde{M}/\mathrm{Deck}(\pi) \to X/\Gamma$ (Proposition~\ref{homotopy:proposition-deck}); in charts it is the identity, so it is an isomorphism of $(G, X)$-manifolds. Conversely, for
$M = X/\Gamma$ the projection $X \to M$ is a universal covering, $X$ being simply connected, and the identity of $X$ is a $(G, X)$-map, hence a developing map, which is a covering.

(2) The charts pull the $G$-invariant metric $g_X$ back to a well-defined metric on $M$, as in the proof of Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-constant-curvature-gx}, and $\pi$ pulls this metric back to $\widetilde{M}$; since
$\mathrm{dev}$ is locally a chart composed with $\pi$, it is a local isometry $\widetilde{M} \to X$. As $M$ is compact it is complete (Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-hopf-rinow}), so $\widetilde{M}$ is complete, as in the proof of
Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-space-forms}. A local isometry from a complete connected Riemannian manifold to a connected one is a covering map \cite[Chapter 7]{do-carmo-riemannian}, so $\mathrm{dev}$ is a covering.
\end{proof}

Applied to the model spaces, (2) and (1) give again the space forms of Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-space-forms}, for compact $M$. Without an invariant metric, compactness does not imply completeness.

\begin{example}\label{riemannian-geometry:example-hopf-incomplete}
The Hopf manifold $H^n$, $n \geq 3$, of Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-gx-structures}(2) is a compact affine manifold that is not complete. Here $\Omega = \RR^n \setminus \{0\}$ is simply connected (it deformation retracts onto
$S^{n-1}$), so the projection $\Omega \to H^n$ is a universal covering, and the inclusion $\Omega \to \RR^n$ is a $(G, X)$-map, hence a developing map by Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-developing-map}(2). It is not surjective,
so it is not a covering map. The holonomy is the group $\Gamma \cong \ZZ$ generated by $x \mapsto 2x$. Accordingly, the affine group preserves no Riemannian metric on $\RR^n$, and the straight lines of $H^n$, the images of affine
lines in $\Omega$, run into the missing origin in finite affine time: the geodesics of the flat affine connection are not defined for all time. For $n = 2$ see Exercise~\ref{riemannian-geometry:exercise-hopf-torus}.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}\label{riemannian-geometry:remark-geometrisation}
\emph{Uniformisation and geometrisation.} Every closed surface carries a complete structure of constant curvature, spherical for $S^2$ and $\RR\PP^2$, Euclidean for the torus and the Klein bottle, and hyperbolic for the surfaces of
negative Euler characteristic; by Gauss--Bonnet (Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-gauss-bonnet}) the sign of the curvature is that of $\chi$. Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-hyperbolic-surfaces}
constructs the hyperbolic structures in the orientable case, and the general statement is a form of the uniformisation theorem for Riemann surfaces (Chapter~\ref{complex-manifolds}). Deforming a hyperbolic structure deforms its
holonomy $\pi_1(\Sigma_g) \to \mathrm{Isom}^+(\mathbb{H}^2) = \mathrm{PSL}_2(\RR)$, and the hyperbolic structures on $\Sigma_g$, up to isotopy, form the Teichm\"uller space, of dimension $6g - 6$ \cite{goldman-2022}. In dimension $3$,
Thurston singled out eight geometries $(G, X)$, among them the three constant curvature ones, and conjectured that every closed $3$-manifold can be cut along spheres and tori into pieces carrying complete
