structures modelled on them \cite{thurston-1997}; Perelman proved this \emph{geometrisation conjecture}, and with it the Poincar\'e conjecture.
\end{remark}

\section{Exercises}\label{riemannian-geometry:section-exercises}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-hodge-star-signs}
Verify that $\delta = -*d*$ on $k$-forms when $n$ is even, and compute $\delta$ on $1$-forms in $\RR^3$, identifying it with minus the divergence.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-conformal}
Show that in dimension $n = 2k$ the Hodge star on $k$-forms depends only on the conformal class of the metric, and that $*^2 = (-1)^k$ on $\Omega^k$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
Under $g \mapsto \lambda g$ with $\lambda > 0$, the inner product on $k$-forms scales by $\lambda^{-k}$ and the volume form by $\lambda^{n/2} = \lambda^k$, so $*$ is unchanged by the characterisation
$\alpha \wedge *\beta = \langle\alpha,\beta\rangle\mathrm{vol}$. The sign is $(-1)^{k(n-k)} = (-1)^{k^2} = (-1)^k$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-laplacian-sphere}
Compute the Laplacian on functions on the round sphere $S^2$ in spherical coordinates, and verify that the restrictions of linear functions of $\RR^3$ are eigenfunctions with eigenvalue $2$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
With $x = (\sin\vartheta\cos\varphi, \sin\vartheta\sin\varphi, \cos\vartheta)$ the metric is $d\vartheta^2 + \sin^2\vartheta\,d\varphi^2$, and Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-gradient-hessian}(2) gives
$\Delta f = \frac{1}{\sin\vartheta}\partial_\vartheta(\sin\vartheta\,f_\vartheta) + \frac{1}{\sin^2\vartheta}f_{\varphi\varphi}$. For $f = \cos\vartheta$:
$\frac{1}{\sin\vartheta}\partial_\vartheta(-\sin^2\vartheta) = -2\cos\vartheta$. For $f = \sin\vartheta\cos\varphi$: the first term is
$\frac{\cos\varphi}{\sin\vartheta}(\cos^2\vartheta - \sin^2\vartheta)$ and the second $-\frac{\cos\varphi}{\sin\vartheta}$, with sum $\frac{\cos\varphi}{\sin\vartheta}(-2\sin^2\vartheta) = -2\sin\vartheta\cos\varphi$; the
same computation applies to $\sin\vartheta\sin\varphi$. So $\Delta x_i = -2x_i$, eigenvalue $2$ for $-\Delta$, in accordance with Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-beltrami-formula}(1).
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-laplacian-composition}
Show that $\Delta(\phi \circ f) = \phi^{\prime}(f)\,\Delta f + \phi^{\prime\prime}(f)\,|\operatorname{grad}f|^2$ for smooth $f \colon M \to \RR$ and $\phi \colon \RR \to \RR$. Deduce that if $f$ is harmonic and
$\phi$ is convex, then $\phi \circ f$ is subharmonic, and that on a compact oriented manifold without boundary a harmonic function $f$ has $\int_M|\operatorname{grad}f|^2e^{f}\,\mathrm{vol}_g = 0$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
$\operatorname{grad}(\phi \circ f) = \phi^{\prime}(f)\operatorname{grad}f$, since $d(\phi \circ f) = \phi^{\prime}(f)\,df$. By the Leibniz rule for the divergence
(as in the proof of Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-laplacian-symmetric}), $\operatorname{div}(\phi^{\prime}(f)\operatorname{grad}f) = \phi^{\prime}(f)\Delta f + \langle\operatorname{grad}\phi^{\prime}(f), \operatorname{grad}f\rangle$, and
$\operatorname{grad}\phi^{\prime}(f) = \phi^{\prime\prime}(f)\operatorname{grad}f$. If $\Delta f = 0$ and $\phi^{\prime\prime} \geq 0$, then $\Delta(\phi \circ f) = \phi^{\prime\prime}(f)|\operatorname{grad}f|^2 \geq 0$. For $\phi = \exp$,
$\int_M\Delta(e^f)\,\mathrm{vol}_g = 0$ by Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-laplacian-symmetric} gives the last statement, and hence $\operatorname{grad}f = 0$: another proof of
Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-laplacian-compact}(1) for harmonic functions.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-spherical-harmonic-quadratic}
Show that $x_n^2 - \frac1n|x|^2$ is a harmonic polynomial on $\RR^n$, and deduce that $Y = x_n^2 - \frac1n$ satisfies $\Delta_{S^{n-1}}Y = -2nY$ on the unit sphere $S^{n-1}$. Check that
$\int_{S^{n-1}}Y\,d\sigma = 0$, as Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-laplacian-compact}(4) requires.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
$\Delta(x_n^2) = 2$ and $\Delta|x|^2 = \sum_i\Delta(x_i^2) = 2n$, so $\Delta(x_n^2 - \frac1n|x|^2) = 2 - 2 = 0$; the polynomial is homogeneous of degree $2$ and restricts to $Y$ on the sphere. By
Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-spherical-harmonics} with $k = 2$, $\Delta_{S^{n-1}}Y = -2n\,Y$. By the symmetry argument in the proof of
Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-laplacian-mean}, $\int x_n^2\,d\sigma = \sigma(S^{n-1})/n$, so $\int Y\,d\sigma = 0$; and indeed $Y = -\frac{1}{2n}\Delta_{S^{n-1}}Y$ has integral $0$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-bi-invariant}
Let $G$ be a compact Lie group with a bi-invariant metric. Show that the geodesics through $e$ are the one-parameter subgroups, and that $\nabla_XY = \frac12[X, Y]$ for left-invariant fields.
\end{exercise}
\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-great-circles}
Show that the geodesics of the round sphere $S^n \subseteq \RR^{n+1}$ are the great circles $\gamma(t) = \cos(t)p + \sin(t)v$ with $|p| = |v| = 1$ and $v \perp p$ (up to affine reparametrisation).
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
The reflection $\rho$ of $\RR^{n+1}$ fixing the plane spanned by $p$ and $v$ pointwise preserves $S^n$ and its metric, so it is an isometry, and it maps geodesics to geodesics. The geodesic $\gamma_v$ with $\gamma_v(0) = p$, $\dot\gamma_v(0) = v$
is mapped to a geodesic with the same initial data, hence to itself by uniqueness; so $\gamma_v$ lies in the fixed set of $\rho$ on $S^n$, the great circle through $p$ and $v$. Having constant speed $1$
(Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-geodesic-speed}) and starting at $p$ with velocity $v$, it is $\cos(t)p + \sin(t)v$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-holonomy-product}
Show that the holonomy group of a Riemannian product $M_1 \times M_2$ at $(p_1, p_2)$ is $\mathrm{Hol}_{p_1}(M_1) \times \mathrm{Hol}_{p_2}(M_2)$, acting on $T_{p_1}M_1 \oplus T_{p_2}M_2$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
The Levi-Civita connection of the product metric is the direct sum of those of the factors, since the direct sum connection is metric and torsion-free. So a vector $(v_1, v_2)$ is transported along a curve $(c_1, c_2)$ to
$(P_{c_1}v_1, P_{c_2}v_2)$. Loops at $(p_1, p_2)$ are exactly the pairs of loops at $p_1$ and $p_2$, which gives the claim.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-trivial-holonomy}
Let $(M, g)$ be connected with $\mathrm{Hol}_p(g) = \{1\}$. Show that $M$ is flat and that $TM$ is trivial.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
By Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-holonomy-principle}, each vector of an orthonormal basis of $T_pM$ extends to a parallel vector field $E_i$; the $E_i$ are orthonormal everywhere (parallel transport is an isometry), so they
trivialise $TM$. For parallel fields $R(X, Y)E_i = \nabla_X\nabla_YE_i - \nabla_Y\nabla_XE_i - \nabla_{[X,Y]}E_i = 0$, so $R = 0$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-surface-revolution-area}
Let $f, h \colon (a, b) \to \RR$ be smooth with $f > 0$ and $(f^{\prime}, h^{\prime})$ nowhere zero, and let $S$ be the surface of revolution
$x(t, \theta) = (f(t)\cos\theta, f(t)\sin\theta, h(t))$, assuming that the profile curve $t \mapsto (f(t), h(t))$ is injective and a homeomorphism onto its image. Show that the area of $S$ is $2\pi\int_a^bf(t)\sqrt{f^{\prime}(t)^2 + h^{\prime}(t)^2}\,dt$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
Here $x_t = (f^{\prime}\cos\theta, f^{\prime}\sin\theta, h^{\prime})$ and $x_\theta = (-f\sin\theta, f\cos\theta, 0)$, so $E = f^{\prime 2} + h^{\prime 2}$, $F = 0$ and $G = f^2$.
By Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-parametrised-volume}(2), $dA = f\sqrt{f^{\prime 2} + h^{\prime 2}}\,dt\,d\theta$ on the image of
$(a, b) \times (0, 2\pi)$, and the missing meridian $\theta = 0$ has area $0$ (it is a segment in the chart given by $\theta \in (-\pi, \pi)$). Integrating over $\theta$
gives the formula.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-archimedes}
Show that the area of the part of the unit sphere $S^2$ between the planes $z = c$ and $z = d$, for $-1 \leq c < d \leq 1$, is $2\pi(d - c)$: it depends only on the
distance between the planes (Archimedes).
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
Apart from the poles and a meridian, which have area $0$, the sphere is the surface of revolution of Exercise~\ref{riemannian-geometry:exercise-surface-revolution-area}
with $t = z \in (-1, 1)$, $f(z) = \sqrt{1 - z^2}$ and $h(z) = z$. Then $f^{\prime} = -z/\sqrt{1 - z^2}$ and $f\sqrt{f^{\prime 2} + 1} = \sqrt{1 - z^2} \cdot (1 - z^2)^{-1/2} = 1$, so
the area of the zone is $2\pi\int_c^d1\,dz = 2\pi(d - c)$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-killing-hyperbolic}
On the upper half plane $H = \{z = x + iy : y > 0\}$ with the metric $(dx^2 + dy^2)/y^2$, show that the Killing fields form a Lie algebra of dimension $3$, spanned by
$\partial_x$, $x\partial_x + y\partial_y$ and $(x^2 - y^2)\partial_x + 2xy\,\partial_y$. (Show first that the maps $z \mapsto (az + b)/(cz + d)$ with $a, b, c, d$ real and
$ad - bc = 1$ are isometries of $H$.)
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
For $\phi(z) = (az + b)/(cz + d)$ one computes $\operatorname{Im}\phi(z) = y/|cz + d|^2$ and $\phi^{\prime}(z) = 1/(cz + d)^2$, so $\phi$ maps $H$ to itself. As a map of
$\RR^2 = \CC$, its differential at $z$ is multiplication by the complex number $\phi^{\prime}(z)$, which multiplies Euclidean lengths by $|\phi^{\prime}(z)| = 1/|cz + d|^2$.
The hyperbolic length of $v \in T_zH$ is $|v|/y$, and that of its image is $|\phi^{\prime}(z)||v|/\operatorname{Im}\phi(z) = |v|/y$: $\phi$ is an isometry. The three fields generate the one-parameter groups
$z \mapsto z + t$, $z \mapsto e^tz$ and $z \mapsto z/(1 - tz)$ (for $z/(1 - tz)$, the derivative in $t$ at $t = 0$ is $z^2 = (x^2 - y^2) + 2ixy$), which are of the form above
(for the last one, with $a = d = 1$, $b = 0$, $c = -t$, defined on all of $H$ since $1 - tz \neq 0$ for $y > 0$); so they are Killing fields. They are linearly independent,
and by Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-killing-algebra} the Killing algebra has dimension at most $2 \cdot 3/2 = 3$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-catenoid-helicoid}
Show that the helicoid and the catenoid of Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-minimal-surfaces} are locally isometric: in the parameters $(u, w)$, with $v = \sinh w$ on the
helicoid, both metrics are $\cosh^2w\,(du^2 + dw^2)$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
The helicoid has the metric $(1 + v^2)\,du^2 + dv^2$. Substituting $v = \sinh w$, $dv = \cosh w\,dw$ and $1 + v^2 = \cosh^2w$ gives $\cosh^2w\,(du^2 + dw^2)$. The catenoid,
with $v$ renamed $w$, has $E = G = \cosh^2w$ and $F = 0$. So the map sending the point with parameters $(u, \sinh w)$ on the helicoid to the point with parameters $(u, w)$ on
the catenoid preserves the metric: it is a local isometry from the helicoid onto the catenoid, which winds the helicoid infinitely often around the catenoid, as the catenoid is
$2\pi$-periodic in $u$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-no-compact-minimal}
Show that a minimal surface in $\RR^3$ has Gaussian curvature $K \leq 0$, and deduce that there is no compact minimal surface in $\RR^3$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
If $H = 0$, the principal curvatures are $\kappa$ and $-\kappa$, so $K = -\kappa^2 \leq 0$ (Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-theorema-egregium}). A compact surface in
$\RR^3$ has a point with $K > 0$ by Lemma~\ref{riemannian-geometry:lemma-elliptic-point}.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-stereographic-metric}
Let $F \colon \RR^n \to S^n \subseteq \RR^{n+1}$, $F(x) = \bigl(2x, |x|^2 - 1\bigr)/(|x|^2 + 1)$, be the inverse of the stereographic projection from the north pole. Show that the pullback of the
round metric is $4(dx_1^2 + \cdots + dx_n^2)/(1 + |x|^2)^2$, and deduce once more from Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-hyperbolic-curvature}(3) that $S^n$ has constant curvature $1$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
Let $s = 1 + |x|^2$, so $F = (2x/s, 1 - 2/s)$ and $ds = 2\sum_kx_k\,dx_k = 2\,x \cdot dx$. Then $dF = \bigl(2\,dx/s - 2x\,ds/s^2,\; 2\,ds/s^2\bigr)$, and the pullback metric is
\[
|dF|^2 = \frac{4|dx|^2}{s^2} - \frac{8(x \cdot dx)\,ds}{s^3} + \frac{4|x|^2ds^2}{s^4} + \frac{4ds^2}{s^4} = \frac{4|dx|^2}{s^2} - \frac{16(x \cdot dx)^2}{s^3} + \frac{4s \cdot 4(x \cdot dx)^2}{s^4} = \frac{4|dx|^2}{s^2},
\]
where $|dx|^2 = \sum_kdx_k^2$ and products of $1$-forms mean symmetric products. So $F$ is an isometry from $\RR^n$ with this metric onto $S^n$ minus the north pole, and the curvature of $S^n$ is
that of Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-hyperbolic-curvature}(3).
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-metrics-height}
Let $f$ be a positive function on an interval $I$ and $g = f(x_n)^2(dx_1^2 + \cdots + dx_n^2)$ on $\{x \in \RR^n : x_n \in I\}$, $n \geq 3$. Show that in the frame $e_i = f^{-1}\partial_i$
the sectional curvatures are
\[
K(e_i, e_j) = -\frac{f^{\prime2}}{f^4}\quad(i, j < n), \qquad K(e_i, e_n) = \frac{f^{\prime2} - ff^{\prime\prime}}{f^4}\quad(i < n),
\]
and that $g$ has constant curvature if and only if $f(t) = 1/(a + bt)$ for constants $a$, $b$, the curvature being $-b^2$. Which metric is obtained for $a = 0$, $b = 1$?
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
Apply Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-conformally-flat} with $u = \log f(x_n)$: $u_i = \delta_{in}f^{\prime}/f$, $u_{ik} = \delta_{in}\delta_{kn}(f^{\prime}/f)^{\prime}$, so
$\alpha_i = \delta_{in}\mu\,dx_n$ with $\mu = (f^{\prime}/f)^{\prime} - (f^{\prime}/f)^2$, and $|\nabla u|^2 = (f^{\prime}/f)^2$. For $i, j < n$, $\alpha_i = \alpha_j = 0$ and
$\Omega^i_j = -(f^{\prime}/f)^2dx_i \wedge dx_j = -(f^{\prime2}/f^4)\,\omega^i \wedge \omega^j$. For $i < n$, $\Omega^i_n = -\mu\,dx_i \wedge dx_n - (f^{\prime}/f)^2dx_i \wedge dx_n = -(f^{\prime}/f)^{\prime}dx_i \wedge dx_n$, and
$-(f^{\prime}/f)^{\prime}/f^2 = (f^{\prime2} - ff^{\prime\prime})/f^4$. Since every $\Omega^i_j$ is a multiple of $\omega^i \wedge \omega^j$, the curvature is constant $c$ if and only if both expressions equal
$c$ (Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-structure-equations}(4)). The first forces $c = -b^2 \leq 0$ and $f^{\prime}/f^2 = \pm b$, that is $(1/f)^{\prime} = \mp b$, so $f = 1/(a + bt)$ after renaming
$b$; and for this $f$, $f^{\prime} = -b/(a + bt)^2$, $f^{\prime\prime} = 2b^2/(a + bt)^3$, $f^{\prime2} - ff^{\prime\prime} = -b^2/(a + bt)^4$, so the second expression is $-b^2$ as well. For $a = 0$, $b = 1$ we get
$f = 1/x_n$: the upper half-space model of hyperbolic space.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-heisenberg}
On $\RR^3$ consider the metric $g = dx^2 + dy^2 + (dz - x\,dy)^2$ (a left-invariant metric on the Heisenberg group). With the coframe $\omega^1 = dx$, $\omega^2 = dy$, $\omega^3 = dz - x\,dy$, find the
connection forms, and show that the sectional curvatures of the planes spanned by pairs of frame vectors are $K(e_1, e_2) = -\frac34$ and $K(e_1, e_3) = K(e_2, e_3) = \frac14$. Compute the Ricci and
scalar curvatures.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
$d\omega^1 = d\omega^2 = 0$ and $d\omega^3 = -dx \wedge dy = -\omega^1 \wedge \omega^2$. Try $\omega^1_2 = a\,\omega^3$, $\omega^1_3 = b\,\omega^2$, $\omega^2_3 = c\,\omega^1$. The first structure equation for
$i = 1, 2, 3$ gives $(a - b)\,\omega^2 \wedge \omega^3 = 0$, $-(a + c)\,\omega^1 \wedge \omega^3 = 0$ and $-(b - c)\,\omega^1 \wedge \omega^2 = -\omega^1 \wedge \omega^2$, so $a = b = \frac12$, $c = -\frac12$; by
uniqueness these are the connection forms. Then
\[
\Omega^1_2 = d\omega^1_2 + \omega^1_3 \wedge \omega^3_2 = -\tfrac12\omega^1 \wedge \omega^2 + \tfrac12\omega^2 \wedge \tfrac12\omega^1 = -\tfrac34\omega^1 \wedge \omega^2,
\]
$\Omega^1_3 = d\omega^1_3 + \omega^1_2 \wedge \omega^2_3 = \tfrac12\omega^3 \wedge (-\tfrac12\omega^1) = \tfrac14\omega^1 \wedge \omega^3$ and
$\Omega^2_3 = d\omega^2_3 + \omega^2_1 \wedge \omega^1_3 = -\tfrac12\omega^3 \wedge \tfrac12\omega^2 = \tfrac14\omega^2 \wedge \omega^3$. Each $\Omega^i_j$ is a multiple of $\omega^i \wedge \omega^j$, so $R(e_k, e_l, e_j, e_i) = 0$
unless $\{k, l\} = \{i, j\}$, and $\mathrm{Ric}(e_i, e_j) = \sum_kR(e_k, e_i, e_j, e_k)$ is diagonal, with $\mathrm{Ric}(e_1, e_1) = K_{12} + K_{13} = -\frac12$, $\mathrm{Ric}(e_2, e_2) = -\frac12$,
$\mathrm{Ric}(e_3, e_3) = \frac12$, and scalar curvature $-\frac12$. So this homogeneous metric has sectional curvatures of both signs.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-warped-products}
Let $(\theta^a)_{a < n}$ be an orthonormal coframe on an open subset of the round $S^{n-1}$, $n \geq 3$, with connection forms $\theta^a_b$, so that $d\theta^a_b + \sum_c\theta^a_c \wedge \theta^c_b = \theta^a \wedge \theta^b$.
On $(0, L) \times S^{n-1}$ consider $g = dr^2 + f(r)^2g_{S^{n-1}}$ with $f > 0$, and the coframe $\omega^a = f\theta^a$, $\omega^n = dr$. Show that $\omega^a_b = \theta^a_b$ and $\omega^a_n = f^{\prime}\theta^a$, and that
\[
K(e_a, e_n) = -\frac{f^{\prime\prime}}{f}, \qquad K(e_a, e_b) = \frac{1 - f^{\prime2}}{f^2} .
\]
Check that $f(r) = r$, $\sin r$ and $\sinh r$ give constant curvature $0$, $1$ and $-1$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
$d\omega^a = f^{\prime}dr \wedge \theta^a + f\,d\theta^a = -\frac{f^{\prime}}{f}\omega^a \wedge \omega^n - f\sum_b\theta^a_b \wedge \theta^b$, and with the proposed forms
$-\sum_b\omega^a_b \wedge \omega^b - \omega^a_n \wedge \omega^n = -f\sum_b\theta^a_b \wedge \theta^b - \frac{f^{\prime}}{f}\omega^a \wedge \omega^n$, which agrees; also $d\omega^n = 0$ and
$-\sum_a\omega^n_a \wedge \omega^a = \sum_af^{\prime}\theta^a \wedge f\theta^a = 0$. So these are the connection forms. Then
$\Omega^a_b = d\theta^a_b + \sum_c\theta^a_c \wedge \theta^c_b + \omega^a_n \wedge \omega^n_b = \theta^a \wedge \theta^b - f^{\prime2}\theta^a \wedge \theta^b = \frac{1 - f^{\prime2}}{f^2}\omega^a \wedge \omega^b$, and
$\Omega^a_n = d(f^{\prime}\theta^a) + \sum_c\theta^a_c \wedge f^{\prime}\theta^c = f^{\prime\prime}dr \wedge \theta^a + f^{\prime}\bigl(d\theta^a + \sum_c\theta^a_c \wedge \theta^c\bigr) = -\frac{f^{\prime\prime}}{f}\omega^a \wedge \omega^n$, using the
first structure equation of the sphere. For $f = r$: $0$ and $(1 - 1)/r^2 = 0$; for $f = \sin r$: $1$ and $(1 - \cos^2r)/\sin^2r = 1$; for $f = \sinh r$: $-1$ and $(1 - \cosh^2r)/\sinh^2r = -1$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-gbc-dimension-four}
Let $M$ be a compact oriented $4$-manifold with a metric of constant curvature $\kappa$. Show that $\chi(M) = 3\kappa^2\operatorname{vol}(M)/(4\pi^2)$, and deduce that $T^4$ and $S^1 \times S^3$ carry no metric of
constant curvature $\kappa \neq 0$. Check the formula directly for the product $S^2 \times S^2$ of two unit spheres, which does not have constant curvature: compute $\mathrm{Pf}(\Omega)$ in a product
frame and show that $\frac{1}{4\pi^2}\int\mathrm{Pf}(\Omega) = 4 = \chi(S^2 \times S^2)$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
The first statement is Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-gauss-bonnet-chern}(2) with $m = 2$, $(2m-1)!! = 3$. By the K\"unneth formula $\chi(T^4) = \chi(S^1)^4 = 0$ and
$\chi(S^1 \times S^3) = 0$, while $3\kappa^2\operatorname{vol}/(4\pi^2) > 0$ for $\kappa \neq 0$. For $S^2 \times S^2$ take orthonormal coframes $\omega^1, \omega^2$ of the first factor and $\omega^3, \omega^4$ of the
second, pulled back. The connection forms of the factors, placed in two diagonal blocks, are skew-symmetric and satisfy the first structure equation, so they are the connection forms of
the product. The curvature is block diagonal, $\Omega^1_2 = \omega^1 \wedge \omega^2$, $\Omega^3_4 = \omega^3 \wedge \omega^4$ and $\Omega^1_3 = \Omega^1_4 = \Omega^2_3 = \Omega^2_4 = 0$, so
$\mathrm{Pf}(\Omega) = \Omega^1_2 \wedge \Omega^3_4 = dA_1 \wedge dA_2$, with integral $(4\pi)^2$, and $16\pi^2/(4\pi^2) = 4 = \chi(S^2)^2$. (The plane spanned by $e_1$ and $e_3$ has curvature $0$, the
one spanned by $e_1$ and $e_2$ curvature $1$.)
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-hyperbolic-models}
This exercise relates three descriptions of hyperbolic space, each with its natural chart: the upper half-space $H^n = \{x \in \RR^n : x_n > 0\}$ with the metric
$|dx|^2/x_n^2$, where $|dx|^2 = dx_1^2 + \cdots + dx_n^2$ and the chart is the inclusion $H^n \subseteq \RR^n$; the ball $B^n = \{|y| < 1\}$ with the metric $4|dy|^2/(1 - |y|^2)^2$, where the chart
is the identity of $B^n$; and the hyperboloid $L = \{x \in \RR^{n+1} : x_1^2 + \cdots + x_n^2 - x_{n+1}^2 = -1,\ x_{n+1} > 0\}$, where the chart is the projection
$p(x) = (x_1, \ldots, x_n)$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item ($n = 2$, starting from the half-plane.) Show that the Cayley map $C(z) = i\frac{1 + z}{1 - z}$ is a diffeomorphism from the unit disc onto the upper half-plane, with inverse
$w \mapsto \frac{w - i}{w + i}$, and compute $C^*\bigl(|dw|^2/(\operatorname{Im}w)^2\bigr)$, finding the metric $4|dz|^2/(1 - |z|^2)^2$ of the disc. So the disc model is the half-plane model
transported by $C$.
\item (Any $n$.) Let $\sigma(x) = -e_n + 2(x + e_n)/|x + e_n|^2$, the inversion in the sphere of radius $\sqrt2$ centred at $-e_n$. Show that $\sigma$ maps $H^n$ diffeomorphically onto $B^n$ and
that $\sigma^*\bigl(4|dy|^2/(1 - |y|^2)^2\bigr) = |dx|^2/x_n^2$.
\item Show that $p$ maps $L$ diffeomorphically onto $\RR^n$, that the restriction to $L$ of the Lorentz form $dx_1^2 + \cdots + dx_n^2 - dx_{n+1}^2$ is positive definite, and that in the chart $p$, with
coordinates $u = p(x)$, it is $|du|^2 - (u \cdot du)^2/(1 + |u|^2)$, where $u \cdot du = \sum_iu_i\,du_i$.
\item Show that the map $y \mapsto x = \bigl(2y, 1 + |y|^2\bigr)/(1 - |y|^2)$ is a diffeomorphism from $B^n$ onto $L$, inverse to $x \mapsto (x_1, \ldots, x_n)/(1 + x_{n+1})$ (the projection from
the point $(0, \ldots, 0, -1)$), which pulls the Lorentz metric of $L$ back to $4|dy|^2/(1 - |y|^2)^2$. Conclude that the three models are isometric, and that all have constant curvature $-1$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
(1) $C$ is holomorphic on $\{z \neq 1\}$ with $C^{\prime}(z) = 2i/(1 - z)^2$. Writing $\frac{1 + z}{1 - z} = \frac{(1 + z)(1 - \bar z)}{|1 - z|^2}$ and $(1 + z)(1 - \bar z) = 1 - |z|^2 + (z - \bar z)$, we get
$\operatorname{Im}C(z) = \operatorname{Re}\frac{1 + z}{1 - z} = \frac{1 - |z|^2}{|1 - z|^2}$, which is positive exactly for $|z| < 1$. Solving $w = i(1 + z)/(1 - z)$ for $z$ gives $z = (w - i)/(w + i)$, defined
on the half-plane, and $|(w - i)/(w + i)| < 1$ there since $w$ is closer to $i$ than to $-i$; so $C$ is a bijection onto the half-plane with holomorphic, hence smooth, inverse. The differential of
a holomorphic map is multiplication by the complex derivative, which by the Cauchy--Riemann equations (Proposition~\ref{complex-analysis:proposition-cauchy-riemann}) multiplies lengths by its modulus;
so $C^*|dw|^2 = |C^{\prime}(z)|^2|dz|^2 = 4|dz|^2/|1 - z|^4$, and
\[
C^*\frac{|dw|^2}{(\operatorname{Im}w)^2} = \frac{4|dz|^2}{|1 - z|^4} \cdot \frac{|1 - z|^4}{(1 - |z|^2)^2} = \frac{4|dz|^2}{(1 - |z|^2)^2} .
\]

(2) Write $v = x + e_n$. The inversion $J(x) = c + r^2(x - c)/|x - c|^2$, here with $c = -e_n$ and $r^2 = 2$, is an involution of $\RR^n \setminus \{c\}$, and its differential is
$dJ = \frac{r^2}{|v|^2}\bigl(I - 2\frac{vv^T}{|v|^2}\bigr)$, a reflection times $r^2/|v|^2$ (compare Example~\ref{vector-bundles:example-cocycles}); so $J^*|dy|^2 = r^4|dx|^2/|v|^4 = 4|dx|^2/|x + e_n|^4$.
Moreover
\[
|\sigma(x)|^2 = 1 - \frac{4\langle v, e_n\rangle}{|v|^2} + \frac{4}{|v|^2} = 1 - \frac{4x_n}{|x + e_n|^2}, \qquad\text{so}\qquad 1 - |\sigma(x)|^2 = \frac{4x_n}{|x + e_n|^2} .
\]
Hence $\sigma$ maps $H^n$ into $B^n$, and, being an involution of $\RR^n \setminus \{-e_n\}$ mapping $\{x_n > 0\}$ into $\{|y| < 1\}$ and (by the same formula read backwards) $\{|y| < 1\}$ into
$\{x_n > 0\}$, it is a diffeomorphism $H^n \to B^n$. Finally
$\sigma^*\bigl(4|dy|^2/(1 - |y|^2)^2\bigr) = 4 \cdot \frac{4|dx|^2}{|x + e_n|^4} \cdot \frac{|x + e_n|^4}{16x_n^2} = \frac{|dx|^2}{x_n^2}$.

(3) The inverse of $p|_L$ is $u \mapsto (u, (1 + |u|^2)^{1/2})$, smooth on $\RR^n$, so $p$ is a diffeomorphism and $p$ is a chart of the submanifold $L$. On $L$, $x_{n+1} = (1 + |u|^2)^{1/2}$ and
$dx_{n+1} = (u \cdot du)/(1 + |u|^2)^{1/2}$, so the Lorentz form restricts to $|du|^2 - (u \cdot du)^2/(1 + |u|^2)$. By the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality, $(u \cdot \xi)^2 \leq |u|^2|\xi|^2 < (1 + |u|^2)|\xi|^2$ for
$\xi \neq 0$, so this is positive definite.

(4) Let $t = 1 - |y|^2$, so $x^{\prime} = (x_1, \ldots, x_n) = 2y/t$ and $x_{n+1} = (1 + |y|^2)/t = 2/t - 1$. Then $|x^{\prime}|^2 - x_{n+1}^2 = (4|y|^2 - (1 + |y|^2)^2)/t^2 = -1$ and $x_{n+1} > 0$, so the point is on $L$;
and $x^{\prime}/(1 + x_{n+1}) = (2y/t)/(2/t) = y$, which shows that the two maps are inverse to each other (the second one maps $L$ into $B^n$, as $|x^{\prime}|^2 = x_{n+1}^2 - 1 < (1 + x_{n+1})^2$). With
$dt = -2\,y \cdot dy$,
\[
\begin{aligned}
|dx^{\prime}|^2 - dx_{n+1}^2 &= \Bigl|\frac{2\,dy}{t} - \frac{2y\,dt}{t^2}\Bigr|^2 - \frac{4\,dt^2}{t^4} = \frac{4|dy|^2}{t^2} - \frac{8(y \cdot dy)\,dt}{t^3} + \frac{4(|y|^2 - 1)\,dt^2}{t^4}\\
&= \frac{4|dy|^2}{t^2} + \frac{16(y \cdot dy)^2}{t^3} - \frac{16(y \cdot dy)^2}{t^3} = \frac{4|dy|^2}{(1 - |y|^2)^2} .
\end{aligned}
\]
One can also do this computation in the chart $p$ of (3), with the metric $|du|^2 - (u \cdot du)^2/(1 + |u|^2)$ and the map $u = 2y/t$. Written with indices, the pulled back metric is
$\sum_{k,l}J_{ki}J_{lj}\bigl(\delta_{kl} - u_ku_l/(1 + |u|^2)\bigr)$, where $J_{ki} = \partial u_k/\partial y_i$, and expanding it term by term is long; organised as products of matrices it is short. With $r = |y|$,
\[
J = \frac2t\Bigl(I + \frac{2yy^T}{t}\Bigr), \qquad J^TJ = \frac{4}{t^2}\Bigl(I + \frac{4yy^T}{t} + \frac{4r^2yy^T}{t^2}\Bigr) = \frac{4}{t^2}\Bigl(I + \frac{4yy^T}{t^2}\Bigr),
\]
using $t + r^2 = 1$; and $J^Tu = \frac{4(1 + r^2)}{t^3}y$ and $1 + |u|^2 = \frac{(1 + r^2)^2}{t^2}$. So the matrix of the pulled back metric is
\[
J^T\Bigl(I - \frac{uu^T}{1 + |u|^2}\Bigr)J = J^TJ - \frac{(J^Tu)(J^Tu)^T}{1 + |u|^2} = \frac{4}{t^2}I + \frac{16yy^T}{t^4} - \frac{16yy^T}{t^4} = \frac{4}{(1 - |y|^2)^2}I,
\]
as before. So the ball, the half-space and the hyperboloid are isometric (by (2) and (4)), and they have constant curvature $-1$ by Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-hyperbolic-curvature}.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-dirichlet-uniqueness}
Let $D$ be as in Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-divergence}, and $u$ a smooth function near $D$ with $\Delta_0u = 0$ on $D$ and $u = 0$ on $\partial D$. Show that $u = 0$ on $D$. Deduce that a solution of
the Dirichlet problem $\Delta_0u = f$ on $D$, $u = g$ on $\partial D$, is unique.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
By Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-green-identities}(2) with $f = g = u$, $\int_D|\nabla u|^2\,d\lambda = \int_{\partial D}u\,\partial_\nu u\,dA - \int_Du\,\Delta_0u\,d\lambda = 0$. So $\nabla u = 0$ on $D$ and $u$ is
constant on each connected component of $D$. Each component $C$ meets $\partial D$: otherwise $C$ would be a compact manifold without boundary, open in $\RR^{n+1}$ (its points are interior points of $D$, and $C$
is open in $D$) and closed, hence empty or all of $\RR^{n+1}$, which is not compact. As $u = 0$ on $\partial D$, $u = 0$ on $C$. For the Dirichlet problem, the difference of two solutions satisfies the
hypotheses.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-stokes-paraboloid}
Let $S = \{z = 1 - x^2 - y^2,\ z \geq 0\}$ with the upward normal, and $F = (-y, x, z)$. Verify Stokes' theorem (Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-kelvin-stokes}) by computing both sides, using
Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-flux-graph} for the flux of $\operatorname{curl}F$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
$\operatorname{curl}F = (\partial_yF_3 - \partial_zF_2, \partial_zF_1 - \partial_xF_3, \partial_xF_2 - \partial_yF_1) = (0, 0, 2)$. By Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-flux-graph}, with $h = 1 - u^2 - v^2$ on the unit disc, the
flux of $(0, 0, 2)$ is $\iint2\,du\,dv = 2\pi$. The boundary is the unit circle in the plane $z = 0$; the upward normal and the rule of Corollary~\ref{riemannian-geometry:corollary-kelvin-stokes} orient it
counterclockwise seen from above, $\gamma(t) = (\cos t, \sin t, 0)$, and $\oint F^1\,dx + F^2\,dy + F^3\,dz = \int_0^{2\pi}(\sin^2t + \cos^2t + 0)\,dt = 2\pi$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-cylinder-geodesics}
Show that every geodesic of the cylinder $C = \{x^2 + y^2 = 1\} \subseteq \RR^3$ is one of the helices of Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-tangential-projection}(3), and that the points $(1, 0, 0)$ and
$(1, 0, 1)$ are joined by infinitely many geodesics. Which of them is the shortest?
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
At the point $(\cos t_0, \sin t_0, z_0)$ the tangent plane of $C$ is spanned by $(-\sin t_0, \cos t_0, 0)$ and $(0, 0, 1)$, so every initial velocity is $v = a(-\sin t_0, \cos t_0, 0) + b(0, 0, 1)$, which is the initial
velocity of the helix with parameters $a$, $b$, $t_0$, $z_0$. That helix is a geodesic (Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-tangential-projection}(3)), and a geodesic is determined by its initial position and
velocity (Definition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:definition-geodesic}), so every geodesic is a helix. For $k \in \ZZ$ the helix $\gamma_k(t) = (\cos(2\pi kt), \sin(2\pi kt), t)$, $0 \leq t \leq 1$, goes from $(1, 0, 0)$
to $(1, 0, 1)$ winding $k$ times around the cylinder, with length $(4\pi^2k^2 + 1)^{1/2}$. The shortest is the vertical segment, $k = 0$, of length $1$. On the plane obtained by unrolling the cylinder,
the $\gamma_k$ are the segments from $(0, 0)$ to the different preimages $(2\pi k, 1)$ of the end point.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-gradient-identities}
On a Riemannian manifold, prove the Leibniz rules $\operatorname{grad}(fh) = f\operatorname{grad}h + h\operatorname{grad}f$, $\operatorname{div}(fX) = f\operatorname{div}X + \langle\operatorname{grad}f, X\rangle$ and
$\Delta_g(fh) = f\Delta_gh + h\Delta_gf + 2\langle\operatorname{grad}f, \operatorname{grad}h\rangle$. On $\RR^n \setminus \{0\}$ show that a radial function $u = \phi(r)$ has $\Delta_gu = \phi^{\prime\prime}(r) + \frac{n-1}{r}\phi^{\prime}(r)$, and
deduce that $r^{2-n}$ ($n \geq 3$) and $\log r$ ($n = 2$) are harmonic.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
The first identity follows from $d(fh) = f\,dh + h\,df$. For the second, $\nabla_v(fX) = v(f)X + f\nabla_vX$, and taking traces gives $\langle\operatorname{grad}f, X\rangle + f\operatorname{div}X$. The third is the second with
$X = \operatorname{grad}h$ and $f$, plus the same with the roles exchanged, using the first. For $u = \phi(r)$, $\operatorname{grad}u = \phi^{\prime}(r)\operatorname{grad}r$, and by the second identity and
Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-gradient-hessian}(4), $\Delta_gu = \langle\operatorname{grad}\phi^{\prime}(r), \operatorname{grad}r\rangle + \phi^{\prime}(r)\Delta_gr = \phi^{\prime\prime}(r)|\operatorname{grad}r|^2 + \phi^{\prime}(r)\frac{n-1}{r}$,
with $|\operatorname{grad}r| = 1$. For $\phi = r^{2-n}$, $\phi^{\prime\prime} + \frac{n-1}{r}\phi^{\prime} = (2-n)r^{-n}\bigl((1-n) + (n-1)\bigr) = 0$; for $\phi = \log r$ and $n = 2$, $-r^{-2} + r^{-1} \cdot r^{-1} = 0$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-height-sphere}
Let $a \in \RR^{n+1}$ and $f(x) = \langle a, x\rangle$ on the round unit sphere $S^n$. Show that $\operatorname{grad}f = a - f(x)x$, that $\operatorname{Hess}f = -f\,g$, and hence $\Delta_gf = -nf$. Where are the
critical points of $f$, and what does the Hessian say about them?
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
For $X$ tangent to $S^n$ at $x$, $Xf = \langle a, X\rangle = \langle a - \langle a, x\rangle x, X\rangle$, and $a - \langle a, x\rangle x$ is tangent, as it is orthogonal to $x$; this is the gradient. For a unit speed great
circle $\gamma(t) = \cos(t)x + \sin(t)v$, a geodesic (Exercise~\ref{riemannian-geometry:exercise-great-circles}), $f(\gamma(t)) = \cos(t)f(x) + \sin(t)\langle a, v\rangle$, so
$\operatorname{Hess}f(v, v) = (f \circ \gamma)^{\prime\prime}(0) = -f(x)$ for every unit $v \in T_xS^n$ (Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-gradient-hessian}(4)). A symmetric bilinear form is determined by its
quadratic form, so $\operatorname{Hess}f = -f\,g$, and its trace is $-nf$. The gradient vanishes where $a$ is a multiple of $x$, that is at $x = \pm a/|a|$ (for $a \neq 0$), where $f = \pm|a|$ and
$\operatorname{Hess}f = \mp|a|g$: the maximum and the minimum of $f$. So the restrictions of linear functions are eigenfunctions of the Laplacian, with eigenvalue $n$ for the Hodge Laplacian $-\Delta_g$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-hyperboloid-curvature}
Use Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-level-set-second-fundamental-form} to show that the one-sheeted hyperboloid $x^2 + y^2 - z^2 = 1$ has Gauss curvature $K = -1/(x^2 + y^2 + z^2)^2$. Where is $|K|$
largest?
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
For $f = x^2 + y^2 - z^2$, $\operatorname{Hess}f = \operatorname{diag}(2, 2, -2)$ and $\nabla f = (2x, 2y, -2z)$. With the expansion of Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-level-sets}(2), the bordered determinant is
$-\bigl(4x^2 \cdot 2 \cdot (-2) + 4y^2 \cdot 2 \cdot (-2) + 4z^2 \cdot 2 \cdot 2\bigr) = 16(x^2 + y^2 - z^2) = 16$ on the surface, and $|\nabla f|^4 = 16(x^2 + y^2 + z^2)^2$. So
$K = -16/(16(x^2 + y^2 + z^2)^2)$. On the surface $x^2 + y^2 + z^2 = 1 + 2z^2 \geq 1$, so $|K|$ is largest, equal to $1$, on the waist circle $z = 0$, and $K \to 0$ far out, where the hyperboloid approaches its
asymptotic cone.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-parallel-surface-curvature}
Let $\Sigma \subseteq \RR^3$ be a surface with mean curvature $H$ and Gauss curvature $K$, and $x_t$ a parallel surface as in Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-parallel-hypersurfaces}. Show that its Gauss and
mean curvatures are
\[
K_t = \frac{K}{1 - 2tH + t^2K}, \qquad H_t = \frac{H - tK}{1 - 2tH + t^2K} .
\]
Deduce \emph{Bonnet's theorem}: if $K$ is a positive constant, the parallel surface at distance $t = 1/\sqrt K$ has constant mean curvature $-\frac12\sqrt K$, and if $H$ is a nonzero constant, the
parallel surface at distance $t = 1/(2H)$ has constant Gauss curvature $4H^2$, at the points where $x_t$ is an immersion.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
In a basis of principal directions $S_t$ is diagonal with entries $\kappa_i/(1 - t\kappa_i)$. The denominator of both products is $(1 - t\kappa_1)(1 - t\kappa_2) = 1 - t(\kappa_1 + \kappa_2) + t^2\kappa_1\kappa_2 = 1 - 2tH + t^2K$. So
$K_t = \kappa_1\kappa_2/(1 - 2tH + t^2K)$, and $2H_t = \frac{\kappa_1}{1 - t\kappa_1} + \frac{\kappa_2}{1 - t\kappa_2} = \frac{\kappa_1 + \kappa_2 - 2t\kappa_1\kappa_2}{1 - 2tH + t^2K}$. If $K$ is constant and $t = 1/\sqrt K$, then $t^2K = 1$, the
denominator is $2(1 - tH)$, and $H_t = (H - \sqrt K)/(2(1 - H/\sqrt K)) = -\frac12\sqrt K$. If $H \neq 0$ is constant and $t = 1/(2H)$, the denominator is $t^2K$, and $K_t = K/(t^2K) = 4H^2$. In both cases
the denominator is nonzero exactly where $x_t$ is an immersion, since it is $\det(I - tS)$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-hopf-torus}
Let $H^2 = (\CC \setminus \{0\})/\langle z \mapsto 2z\rangle$, an affine (indeed similarity) torus, as in Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-gx-structures}(2) with $n = 2$ and $\RR^2 = \CC$. Show that $\exp \colon \CC \to \CC \setminus \{0\}$
composed with the projection is a universal covering $\CC \to H^2$, that $\mathrm{dev} = \exp \colon \CC \to \CC$ is a developing map, and compute the holonomy. Conclude that $\mathrm{dev}$ is neither injective nor surjective, that
$\mathrm{hol}$ is not injective, and that $H^2$ is not complete.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
$\exp \colon \CC \to \CC \setminus \{0\}$ is a covering map with deck group $\{z \mapsto z + 2\pi ik\}$, and the projection $\CC \setminus \{0\} \to H^2$ is a covering (Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-gx-structures}(2)). The
composite $p$ is a covering: it is the quotient of $\CC$ by the group generated by $\tau_1(z) = z + \log 2$ and $\tau_2(z) = z + 2\pi i$, since $e^w$ and $e^z$ lie in the same $\langle 2\rangle$-orbit if and only if
$w - z \in \ZZ\log 2 + 2\pi i\ZZ$; this group of translations acts by a covering space action, and $\CC$ is simply connected, so $p$ is a universal covering with deck group $\ZZ^2$
(Theorem~\ref{topological-manifolds:theorem-covering-action}). With the structure pulled back from $H^2$, the map $\exp \colon \CC \to \CC$ is, near each point, a local inverse of the projection $\CC \setminus \{0\} \to H^2$ (a chart of $H^2$)
composed with $p$, so it is a $(G, X)$-map, hence a developing map by Theorem~\ref{riemannian-geometry:theorem-developing-map}(2). From $\exp(z + \log 2) = 2\exp(z)$ and $\exp(z + 2\pi i) = \exp(z)$, $\mathrm{hol}(\tau_1)$ is
$x \mapsto 2x$ and $\mathrm{hol}(\tau_2) = \mathrm{id}$. So $\mathrm{hol}$ is not injective, $\mathrm{dev}$ is not injective, and its image $\CC \setminus \{0\}$ is not all of $\CC$; a covering of the connected space $\CC$ by the
nonempty space $\CC$ would be surjective, so $\mathrm{dev}$ is not a covering, and $H^2$ is not complete. This also shows that the invariant metric cannot be dropped from Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-complete-gx}(2), even
in dimension $2$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-graph-curvatures}
Use Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-graph-second-fundamental-form}.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Show that the curvature of the parabola $y = t^2$, with the upward normal, is $2/(1 + 4t^2)^{3/2}$. Where is it largest, and what is the radius of the osculating circle there?
\item Let $u(y) = \sqrt{1 - |y|^2}$ on the open unit ball of $\RR^n$, whose graph is the upper unit hemisphere. Show that the matrix of the shape operator for the upward normal is $-I$, so that all
principal curvatures are $-1$, in agreement with Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-surfaces}(1), although $\operatorname{Hess}u$ is not a multiple of the identity.
\item Compute the Gauss curvature of the saddle $z = xy$. Where is it smallest, and what happens far from the origin?
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
(1) Here $n = 1$, $u^{\prime} = 2t$, $u^{\prime\prime} = 2$, and the matrix of $S$ is $g^{-1}h = (1 + 4t^2)^{-1}\cdot 2(1 + 4t^2)^{-1/2} = 2/(1 + 4t^2)^{3/2}$. It decreases in $|t|$, so it is largest at the vertex,
where it is $2$ and the osculating circle has radius $\frac12$.

(2) Write $u = \sqrt{1 - |y|^2}$, so $u_i = -y^i/u$ and $u_{ij} = -\delta_{ij}/u - y^iy^j/u^3$, that is, $\operatorname{Hess}u = -u^{-1}(I + yy^T/u^2)$ with $y$ a column. Then
$W^2 = 1 + |y|^2/u^2 = 1/u^2$, $W = 1/u$, and $I - W^{-2}\nabla u\,\nabla u^T = I - yy^T$. So the matrix of $S$ is
\[
u\,(I - yy^T)\cdot\bigl(-u^{-1}(I + u^{-2}yy^T)\bigr) = -\Bigl(I + yy^T\,\frac{1 - u^2 - |y|^2}{u^2}\Bigr) = -I ,
\]
using $yy^Tyy^T = |y|^2yy^T$ and $u^2 + |y|^2 = 1$. On the upper hemisphere the upward normal is the outward one, and Example~\ref{riemannian-geometry:example-surfaces}(1) gives $S = -\id$. The Hessian has the
eigenvalue $-1/u$ on $y^\perp$ and $-1/u^3$ along $y$; the factors $1/W$ and $g^{-1}$ exactly compensate for the steepness of the hemisphere near its boundary.

(3) With $u = xy$: $u_x = y$, $u_y = x$, $u_{xx} = u_{yy} = 0$, $u_{xy} = 1$, so $\det\operatorname{Hess}u = -1$ and $K = \det S = -1/(1 + x^2 + y^2)^2$ by part (3) of the proposition. It is negative
everywhere, smallest, equal to $-1$, at the origin, and tends to $0$ at infinity: far from the origin the saddle flattens out, although its Hessian is the same everywhere.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{riemannian-geometry:exercise-gauss-map}
\begin{enumerate}
\item Show that a connected hypersurface of $\RR^{n+1}$ whose Gauss map is constant is an open subset of an affine hyperplane.
\item Let $C = \{(x, y, z) : z = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2},\ z > 0\}$ be the cone without its vertex, with the upward normal. Show that the image of its Gauss map is a circle, find the kernel of $d\nu$, and deduce
$K = 0$.
\item Let $M \subseteq \RR^3$ be a surface and $(e_1, e_2)$ any orthonormal basis of $T_pM$. Show that $H(p) = \frac{1}{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}\mathrm{II}_p(\cos t\,e_1 + \sin t\,e_2)\,dt$: the mean curvature is
the average of the normal curvatures.
\item Show that the origin is a planar point of the graphs of $x^4 + y^4$ and of $x^3 - 3xy^2$. Show that the first lies above its tangent plane near $0$ and has $K \geq 0$, while the second takes both signs
on every neighbourhood of $0$, vanishes on three lines through $0$, and has $K < 0$ at every other point.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
(1) Let $\nu = \nu_0$ be constant. For a curve $\gamma$ in $M$, $\frac{d}{dt}\langle\gamma(t), \nu_0\rangle = \langle\gamma^{\prime}(t), \nu_0\rangle = 0$, as $\gamma^{\prime}(t)$ is tangent and $\nu_0$ normal at
$\gamma(t)$. A connected manifold is path connected, and a continuous path between two points can be replaced by a piecewise smooth one, so $\langle x, \nu_0\rangle = c$ is constant on $M$, and $M$ lies in the
hyperplane $P = \{\langle x, \nu_0\rangle = c\}$. The inclusion $M \to P$ is an injective immersion between manifolds of the same dimension $n$, hence a local diffeomorphism by the inverse function theorem,
hence an open map, and $M$ is open in $P$.

(2) Here $u = \rho = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$, $\nabla u = (x, y)/\rho$, $|\nabla u| = 1$ and $W = \sqrt2$, so $\nu = (-x/\rho, -y/\rho, 1)/\sqrt2$. Its image is the circle $\{z = 1/\sqrt2\}$ of $S^2$, at latitude
$45^\circ$. The normal depends only on $(x, y)/\rho$, so it is constant along each ray $t \mapsto t(x, y, \rho)$ of the cone, and $d\nu$ vanishes on the radial direction. So $d\nu$ has rank at most $1$ and
$K = \det d\nu = 0$. Like the cylinder, the cone is locally isometric to the plane: cut it along a ray and unroll it.

(3) If $(e_1^{\prime}, e_2^{\prime})$ is an orthonormal basis of principal directions, then $e_1 = \cos\alpha\,e_1^{\prime} + \sin\alpha\,e_2^{\prime}$ and $e_2 = \mp\sin\alpha\,e_1^{\prime} \pm \cos\alpha\,e_2^{\prime}$ for some
$\alpha$ and a choice of signs, so $\cos t\,e_1 + \sin t\,e_2 = \cos(\alpha \pm t)\,e_1^{\prime} + \sin(\alpha \pm t)\,e_2^{\prime}$. Since the integrand has period $2\pi$, the substitution $s = \alpha \pm t$ shows
that the average is the same for both bases. In the principal basis, by Euler's formula, it is $\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}(\kappa_1\cos^2s + \kappa_2\sin^2s)\,ds = \frac12(\kappa_1 + \kappa_2) = H(p)$, as
$\int_0^{2\pi}\cos^2s\,ds = \int_0^{2\pi}\sin^2s\,ds = \pi$.

(4) For both functions $u(0) = 0$, $\nabla u(0) = 0$ and $\operatorname{Hess}u(0) = 0$, so $h = 0$ at the origin by Proposition~\ref{riemannian-geometry:proposition-graph-second-fundamental-form}(4): the
tangent plane is $\{z = 0\}$ and the origin is planar. For $u = x^4 + y^4$, $u > 0$ away from $0$, and $\det\operatorname{Hess}u = 12x^2 \cdot 12y^2 \geq 0$, so $K = \det\operatorname{Hess}u/W^4 \geq 0$. For
$u = x^3 - 3xy^2 = \mathrm{Re}(x + iy)^3$, in polar coordinates $u = r^3\cos3\phi$, which takes both signs on every circle and vanishes on the three lines $\phi = \frac\pi6, \frac\pi2, \frac{5\pi}6$ (mod $\pi$).
Here $u_{xx} = 6x$, $u_{yy} = -6x$, $u_{xy} = -6y$, so $\det\operatorname{Hess}u = -36(x^2 + y^2) < 0$ away from $0$, and $K < 0$ there.
\end{solution}
